Sermons and Exhortations to Monks, by Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Sponheim, later of Wurzburg. Of these sermons there are two books: the first is called the Book of Homilies, the second the Book of Sermons.

Ottomar Luscinius of Strasbourg, on the homilies of Johannes Trithemius to monks: a decastich

If for anyone it is not enough, Christ, to follow
Your gentle laws of justice, unless he also recalls the monks:
Serapion, Paul, and Anthony, who long ago explored the path,
A path rough with countless hard places;
How each strove to show, beneath a harsh cloak, a body tormented,
And did not allow down to be in his bed;
How fasts drive out the swollen pride of the soul,
And brief, half-sleeping rest sustains the limbs:
These men, silent, taught this by their deeds; but fitter than they is
Trithemius, who is strong in tongue and hand.

ROYAL
LIBRARY
OF MUNICH.

8

--- Page 009 ---
With the grace and privilege of the Imperial Majesty.

Maximilian, by divine clemency favoring him elected Emperor of the Romans, ever Augustus, and king of Germany, Hungary, Dalmatia, Croatia, etc., archduke of Austria, duke of Burgundy and Brabant, and count palatine, etc., to the honorable and devout man beloved by us, Johannes Trithemius, abbot of St. James the Greater at Wurzburg: imperial grace and every good thing.

Since you have humbly explained to us that you have composed various editions of both old and new authors, gathered, not without great labor, vigils, and diligence, and that you desired with all your mind that they be brought forth into the light; and since our beloved master Johannes Haselberg of Constance, beloved also of the Holy Roman Empire, has offered with a most ready mind to be the minister of this undertaking, and has therefore procured at great expense the forms by which he may print the aforesaid works: therefore, wishing provision to be made for the indemnity of the said Master Johannes and for the adornment of your honor, lest your works, composed by you with great labors and vigils, lie hidden as if covered with rust, but rather shine forth more and more from day to day, we have therefore given and granted, and by the tenor of the present letters do give and grant, to the aforesaid Master Johannes Haselberg of Constance full power and authority of every kind.

We decree that he may print, and sell after printing, wherever he pleases, whatever among the treatises and little works has been issued by you, the aforesaid Trithemius, abbot of St. James the Greater at Wurzburg, and has been reviewed and approved by the honorable and faithful man beloved by us, Conrad Peutinger, doctor and our counselor. And we forbid all and singular typographers and printers of books, of whatever condition, under the penalty written below, commanding most strictly that, for ten years after such works or similar ones have been published by the said Master Johannes, they not dare or presume to print, publish, or publish what has been printed by others, buy, or sell them throughout the whole Roman Empire and in our hereditary lands.

We further command all and singular our beloved faithful subjects and those of the Holy Roman Empire, of whatever status, condition, dignity, and office, whether ecclesiastical or secular, under penalty of our indignation and the gravest indignation of the Holy Roman Empire, and of ten marks of pure gold, to be applied, as often as the contrary has been done, one part to our treasury and the other part to the aforesaid Master Johannes Haselberg, that they assist the same Master Johannes of Constance with their favor and aid in their places and lands, and not permit the said works and treatises to be printed, or those printed elsewhere to be sold. Rather, they are immediately to declare in our name that all who presume to do anything against this our grace and prohibition have incurred the sentence of this indignation of ours and of the Holy Roman Empire, carrying out our express will in this matter.

In testimony whereof, by these letters furnished with the impression of our seal on the back. Given in our town of Innsbruck on the tenth day of the month of November, in the year of the Lord 1513, in the twenty-ninth year of our Roman reign and the twenty-fifth of our Hungarian reign.

By the king personally.

At the command of the lord emperor himself.

N. Ziegler, and the chancery, through Paul Oberstein.

Letter

John Trithemius, called abbot of Sponheim, to the reverend father and his teacher, Lord John of Colenhausen, abbot of the monastery of Saints Marcellinus and Peter, martyrs, in Seligenstadt, and formerly my predecessor at Sponheim: reverence and honor.

I do not know, teacher ever venerable to me, under what unlucky star that monastery of Sponheim was built, for rarely does an abbot die in it; rather, almost all, overcome by weariness, have for nearly seventy years now, as I have found from the annals, resigned of their own accord. Nor are we entirely ignorant why you too, following the example of your predecessors, deserted us poor men before the proper time: we rightly blame both our poverty and the rebellious rashness of certain men. Now, alas, I myself experience the cause that moved your mind to leave the abbacy of Sponheim, altogether barren and unfruitful, over which you presided for thirteen years after the reform, not without continual anxiety of mind and infinite labors. I pass over the injuries of very many brothers that you endured; all these, indeed, are daily doubled upon me.

The book of my exhortations to the brothers, which you instructed me to send to your paternity, and which I recently composed by lamplight amid the raging occupations of worldly affairs, is unworthy of your reading; nevertheless, I was unwilling to resist the command of a father. Therefore I ask your gentleness kindly to apply the file of correction: if anything is lacking, add it; cut away what is superfluous; and what is unsuitable either hand over to the flames or at least set in order. Farewell, sweetest father, mindful of me. From Sponheim, the sixth day of the month of August, in the year of the Christians 1386.

--- Page 012 ---

All Things. I.
Folio III.

It belongs to Christian warfare, brothers, and especially to that of monks, to be subject to the varied calamities of this world and, like gold in the furnace, to be continually exercised and tested by tribulations. For it is the task of monks, and of Christian soldiers, to transcend the common little rule by a higher manner of living, and to obey not only the Lord’s precepts but also his counsels. It is the task of monks utterly to despise riches, honors, and all the pleasures of the present world for love of God, and, having overcome carnal desires, to be constantly free for God alone. For a monk is so called as one solitary, from unity; even by the very word of his name he is admonished to contempt of the world. Therefore the office of a monk is to stand with himself in solitude, clean and purified of vices, and sweetly to love Christ above all things.

For the goal of monks, to which regular discipline is directed, is Christ; whoever refuses to imitate him is neither truly a monk nor a Christian. Therefore, my brothers, we ought to know that whoever does not follow our Lord and Savior in true patience and humility gains nothing for salvation from being called Christian or monk. For he himself says in the holy Gospel: “Not everyone who says to me, Lord, Lord, shall enter the kingdom of heaven, but he who does the will of my Father who is in heaven, he shall enter the kingdom of heaven.” And what the will of God is, the holy Apostle has disclosed, saying: “This is the will of God, your sanctification.” But since the necessary consummation of this sanctification commanded for us requires great endurance of evils in all things and humility, because no one is holy by his own powers, we must, my brothers, keep continual consideration of our calling within ourselves, so that we may never grow sluggish in idleness...

[segment 4]
Homily 1, fol. II.

In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ. Here begins the first book of expositions to the monks, by Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Sponheim and afterward of Wurzburg: On the Spiritual Warfare of Monks.

First Homily.

Beloved brothers, Christian converts, and especially monks, must be subject to the various calamities of this world and, like gold in the furnace, be more fully refined and proved by tribulations. For monks belong to the Christian warfare, since they pass beyond the ordinary rule of living in a higher way, and obey not only the Lord’s precepts but also his counsels. It belongs to monks utterly to despise divine honors and all pleasures of the present world for love of God, and, having overcome carnal desires, to devote themselves continually to God alone. For “monk” is so called as though “solitary,” from oneness; and by the very title of his name he is admonished to contempt of the world. Therefore the office of monks is to be purified from all contagion in the solitude of the world, and to love Christ sweetly above all things.

For Christ is the goal of monks, toward which regular discipline is directed; whoever refuses to imitate him is not even a monk, nor indeed a Christian. Therefore, my brothers, we ought to know that whoever does not follow the Lord and Savior in true patience and humility gains nothing for salvation from being called Christian or monk. For he himself says in the holy Gospel: “Not everyone who says to me, Lord, Lord, shall enter into the kingdom of heaven, but he who does the will of my Father who is in heaven: he shall enter into the kingdom of heaven.” And how great the will of God is, the holy Apostle discloses, saying: “This is the will of God, your sanctification.” But since the necessary fulfillment of this precept of sanctification demands from us great tolerance of evils in all things and humility, and since no one is holy by his own strength, we must, my brothers, keep constant consideration of our vocation within ourselves, lest, growing numb through most destructive idleness, we be overcome in the soldier’s contest by the enemy, who, like a roaring lion, goes about seeking the incautious and languishing man whom he may devour.

For we are soldiers of God against the adversary, fighting a threefold battle: namely, against the flesh, the world, and the devil. The war is two-edged, and the struggle very hard against the one whom nature has made familiar by long endurance. Therefore, dearly beloved brothers, let us be armed with every kind of virtue, since he who is adorned with the splendor of good works is not easily conquered by the adversary. First let us arm ourselves with the most steadfast faith of the holy and undivided Trinity; its devout and sincere confession by mind and mouth drives far away all the wicked contrivances and deceits of demons, and, defeating the greatest enemy, overthrows him and utterly weakens him. Then let us take hold of the most fervent love of God and neighbor, which is fearful to demons above all things, especially if it has been rooted in true Christian humility. Let us not place confidence in ourselves, because he is called blessed who is always fearful; but he who is hard in mind will fall into evil.

Let us learn our leader, Christ, who says in the Gospel: “I am the way, the truth, and the life, because no one comes to the Father except through me.” Therefore, brothers, attend carefully to what we read in the holy Scriptures concerning our Savior, because whoever says that he remains in Christ, that is, in the way of truth, should strive to walk just as he walked. But it was necessary that he first suffer the reproach of the cross and so enter into his glory, by which we, his soldiers, unworthy though we are, are admonished that it is necessary for us to enter the kingdom of God through many tribulations. Therefore the Lord himself, who reigns, admonished us in the Gospel, saying: “Whoever wishes to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross daily, and follow me.” Behold, Christ is set before us as the way to eternal life; although he is Lord of the heavenly and worldly powers, for our sake he emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, and in that form, willingly and not by compulsion, bearing our infirmities, he was obedient to the Father even unto death, death on a cross. And so, if it was necessary for the Lord of the whole universe first to suffer, it certainly cannot be that we, his imitators, wicked and most vile sinners, ought not to suffer many things.
