*All things. I.*
*Folio IIII.*

...that you also carefully keep the monastic ordinances which you promised. “Make vows,” says Scripture, “and fulfill them.” And again another Scripture says: “If you have vowed anything to God, do not delay to fulfill it.” For an unfaithful and foolish promise displeases him, and it is much better not to vow than, after a vow, not to fulfill what was promised. And Saint Augustine admonishes us how necessary it is to fulfill promises made to God, saying: There are certain things which we owe to the Lord even without vowing them. But after we promise them to the Lord, we are necessarily constrained to fulfill them.

But now, my brothers, I ask you carefully to consider what you have vowed to the Lord your God, and diligently to weigh under what necessity of fulfilling your promises you have been placed. For you vowed to your God, not to a man, obedience and the conversion of your morals according to the Rule of our most holy lawgiver Benedict, and perpetual stability in this order of religion. The obedience which you promised to God you owe to his vicar, a man, whom from the Rule you profess you have learned to hold the place of Christ in the monastery. But what the conversion of morals ought to be according to the Rule is plainly seen in the fourth chapter, headed “On the Instruments of Good Works,” in which you do not fail to recognize the very pattern of all monastic perfection.

Indeed, all of you have heard what our divine father commanded to be done concerning novices, and you know that this was done in the case of each one of you at the beginning of his profession. For thus he says: “The one to be received, in the oratory before all, shall promise concerning his stability, the conversion of his morals, and obedience, before God and his saints, so that, if he should ever act otherwise, he may know that he is to be condemned by God, whom he mocks.” Consider now, therefore, brothers, under how great a necessity of regular observance you have been placed, since unless by deed you fulfill what you promised when you received the monastic habit, you will have to be judged before the almighty Lord not as servants of God, not as friends, not as monks, but as mockers.

Therefore always be mindful of the things which the Rule commands you, according to which you promised to form your morals; and labor like true soldiers of Jesus Christ, constantly keeping watch in the fear of the Lord, since, as Scripture says, “unless you hold yourselves steadfastly in the fear of the Lord, your house will quickly be overthrown.” Consider your vocation: because the Lord has called you not to idleness, not to the pleasures of this world, nor to pursuing the desires of the flesh, but rather to continual labor, to abstinence from all delights, and to the constant mortification of the flesh under the image of the Crucified, so that you may bear his marks in your hearts.

Certainly you have now put your hands to the plow of holy heavenly agriculture, in which you are commanded to sweat in continual labor, and not only never to look back, but, by always advancing from virtue to virtue, to pant toward the summit of highest perfection. For to live as a monk is nothing other than for a man to withdraw from fellowship and, in solitude, actually to please God alone through the desire of most fervent love. Hence Scripture says: “It is good for a man when he has borne the yoke of the Lord from his youth; he shall sit solitary and be silent, because he shall raise himself above himself.”

Therefore, if you wish to be perfect, you who by regular institution are bound to love perfection, begin from the way of life which Christ taught; and you who have already despised all things for love of him, do not neglect humbly to conquer, overcome, and despise even yourselves. The servant of Christ has nothing except Christ, loves nothing, cares for nothing, seeks nothing except Christ, so that he may be perfect and pleasing to the one whose soldiery he has voluntarily professed. But the monk who loves anything besides Christ cannot be perfect, since true love is not divided, nor is the mind truly nourished by diverse and contrary things.

For this reason the Lord says: “If you wish to be perfect, go and sell all that you have and give to the poor; and come, follow me.” You indeed, most beloved brothers, as I trust, have already left the world and whatever you had in the world; and not only all the worldly possessions which you owned and could have had, but you have also laid aside every desire of having. And you have come to this monastery of the divine bishop Martin, in which the poverty of temporal goods is great, and this alone has been given to us in place of riches: not to find occasions for avarice. But just as punishment does not make a martyr, but the cause does, so want does not make a poor man blessed, but voluntary poverty for the love of Christ

[segment 6]
*Homily I. Fol. III.*

...that you keep the monastic ordinances which you have promised. “Vow,” says Scripture, “and render.” And again another Scripture says: “If you have vowed anything to God, do not delay to pay it. For an unfaithful and foolish promise displeases him, and it is much better not to vow than, after a vow, not to render what was promised.” And Saint Augustine warns us how necessary it is to render to God what has been promised, saying: “There are certain things which we owe the Lord even without vowing them, and there are certain things which we do not owe unless we have vowed them. But after we promise them to the Lord, we are necessarily constrained to render them.”

And now, my brothers, I ask you carefully to consider what you have vowed to the Lord your God, and diligently to weigh under what necessity of rendering what you promised you have been placed. For you have vowed to your God, not to man, obedience and the conversion of your conduct according to the Rule of our most holy lawgiver Benedict, and perpetual stability in this order of religion. The obedience which you promised to God you will show to the Lord’s vicar, whom you acknowledge, in the monastery and by the Rule which you profess, to bear the place of Christ. What sort of conversion of conduct ought to be according to the Rule, you see plainly set out in chapter four, “On the Instruments of Good Works,” where you find the summary of the whole of monastic perfection briefly comprised in a few words.

Indeed, all of you have heard what our blessed father commanded to be done concerning novices, and you know that it was done in each one of you at the beginning of your profession. For he says thus: “The one to be received, in the oratory before all, promises concerning his stability, the conversion of his conduct, and obedience, before God and his saints, so that if at any time he should act otherwise, he may know that he will be condemned by God, whom he mocks.” Consider now, therefore, brothers, under how great a necessity you have been placed for the observance of the Rule, since unless you fulfill in deed what you promised in receiving the monastic habit, you will be judged before the almighty Lord not as servants of God, not as friends, not as monks, but as mockers.

Therefore always be mindful of the things which the Rule commands you, according to which you promised to shape your conduct. Labor like true soldiers of Jesus Christ, always watching in fear of the Lord, since, as Scripture says, unless you hold yourselves steadfastly in fear of the Lord, your house will quickly be overthrown. Consider your calling, because God has not called you to idleness, nor to the pleasures of this world, nor to pursue the desires of the flesh, but rather to continual labor, to abstinence from all delights, and to the constant mortification of the flesh under the image of the Crucified, so that, as far as you are able, you may bear his signs marked in your hearts.

You have set your hands, therefore, to the plow of holy heavenly agriculture, in which you are commanded to sweat continually in labor, and not only never to look back, but always, by advancing from virtue to virtue, to strive toward the highest summit of perfection. For what else is it to live as a monk than to avoid the company of men, and in solitude to please God alone actively through the desire of most fervent love? Hence Scripture says: “It is good for a man when he has borne the yoke of the Lord from his youth. He will sit solitary and keep silence, because he has lifted himself above himself.”

Therefore, if you wish to be perfect, you who by regular institution are bound to love perfection, begin with the way of life which Christ taught; and since for love of him you have already renounced all other things, do not neglect humbly to conquer, overcome, and despise even yourselves. The servant of Christ has nothing besides Christ; he loves nothing, cares for nothing, seeks nothing except Christ, so that he may be perfect and please him whose justice he has chosen and professed. But the monk who has anything besides Christ cannot be perfect, since true love is not divided, nor is the mind truly united to diverse and contrary things.

Therefore the Lord says: “If you wish to be perfect, go and sell all that you have and give to the poor, and come, follow me.” You, indeed, most beloved brothers, as I trust, have now left the world and whatever you had in the world; and you have laid aside not only ownership of all the things which you possessed and could have possessed, but also every desire to possess. And you have come to this monastery of the blessed Martin, where the poverty of temporal goods is great, and this alone has been given to us in place of riches: not to find occasions for avarice. Yet just as punishment does not make a martyr, but the cause does, so want does not make a blessed poor man, but voluntary patience in that very poverty for love of Christ.

[segment 7]
On the Progress of Spiritual Warfare.

in that poverty itself, patience. For the Lord in the Gospel did not proclaim every poor person blessed and to be granted the kingdom of heaven, but, as you know, he announced that only the poor in spirit are blessed. Contempt for riches is not sufficient for salvation, unless the one who renounces gold also strives with all his strength, through patience and humility, constantly to imitate the Savior. For it is easier to despise the world than to despise and deny oneself.

Therefore whoever wishes to imitate the Son of God and attain evangelical perfection must become a despiser not only of the world and temporal things, but also of his own self. For the Savior says in the holy Gospel: “Anyone who has not renounced all that he possesses cannot be my disciple.” And lest we should think this sufficient for perfection, he speaks again more openly concerning contempt of oneself, saying: “If anyone wishes to follow me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. For whoever wishes to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for my sake and the Gospel’s will save it.” Christ was made obedient to the Father even unto death. If you wish to imitate him as you have promised, brothers, it is necessary that you deny yourselves to yourselves. For he cannot be a monk who has not in every respect renounced his own will entirely.

You who before God and all his saints have promised to live according to the monastic Rule may no longer claim your own will for yourselves, since what has once been consecrated to God must not afterwards be called back to human uses. For you serve as soldiers not for yourselves but for Christ the King; therefore neither to will nor not to will remains in your power. For you are dead, as holy Theophilus says, and your life is hidden with Christ in God; but when Christ, your life, appears, then you also will appear with him in glory. If you desire to rejoice with him without end, you must now in this present time serve as vigorous soldiers for love of him. For no one will be crowned with Christ except the one who, striving lawfully, has persevered even to the end, since he cannot conquer who does not know how to persevere.

On the Progress of Spiritual Warfare: Second Homily

As often, most beloved brothers, as the memory of that event comes back to mind, when you appointed me, unworthy as I was, as abbot over you, though I protested and resisted, so often I am wholly shaken within myself by the bitterness of my heart, and even unwillingly I am compelled to shed undeserved tears. For whether this was done not because of my merits but because of yours, I do not know. May almighty God grant that this appointment of me was not made because of my demerits or yours. Why did you wish it so, brothers? You believed me, young, unlearned, and by no means fit for the soldiery which we profess, to be rich in God; so that now it is altogether necessary for me at the same time both to teach nothing and to learn, since neither perfection of life commends me as a teacher, nor does consideration of age yet grant authority to the necessity of my office.

In these three years I have sufficiently learned by experience in how many miseries I am entangled, while I myself alone am compelled to bear the weaknesses of you all, and I, unhappy man, am supported by no one. Who will deliver me from the multitude of these vanities into which I have been called back, I who entered the monastery in order to flee beneath all the care and anxiety even of the present life? Since, however, it is necessary to bear the burden placed upon my shoulders, I ask you, brothers, earnestly stretch out to me the helping hands of your prayers, so that in your midst, by the Lord’s gift, I may deserve to conduct myself in such a way that I do not depart from the condition of spiritual warfare which the Rule has prescribed for us.

For we are professors of the same warfare, nor does my office alter the vow by which, together with all the sheep, it has made me, as shepherd, bound by solemn profession in the enclosure of this life. Therefore, when I speak to you about the spiritual warfare which, by God’s mercy, we have professed, my intention is, as far as the Lord Jesus will deign to grant, to shake both myself out of the feathers of sloth and fear into the fervor of holy newness, and all of you, according to my strength, to stir up to the custody of the military order.

For we are frail and weak, and we do not long remain stable in holy readiness unless by daily exercises we continually renew the fervor of the spirit. For by reason of my office I am bound to set before you in common those things that can conduce to your salvation; it belongs to you to listen patiently in all things to me as I speak, as the shepherd of your souls. For although, as a young man, I am not fit to teach so great a multitude of elders, nevertheless, since you appointed me shepherd over you, I am compelled, under the necessity of salvation, to feed the flock which I have undertaken to govern.

What is mine to do I gladly do, and will do according to my strength as long as I live in this anxious care of pastoral charge: young as I am, I will teach you elders, and without interruption I will show how you ought to serve as soldiers for him into whose arms you have sworn. For the dreadful threat of our lawgiver does not escape me, when he says: “Let the abbot know that whatever lack of profit the father of the household may find in the sheep is laid upon the fault of the shepherd.” And what follows concerns you, my brothers, if, while I preach and admonish, contempt for the word of God arises. Again he says that the shepherd will be free only if every diligence has been bestowed on a disobedient flock, and every care has been applied to their diseased actions; then their shepherd, absolved in the judgment of the Lord, may say with the prophet to the Lord: “I have not hidden your justice in my heart; I have spoken your truth and your salvation. But they, despising, have spurned me.” And then at last, for the disobedient sheep entrusted to his care, death itself, prevailing over them, will be their punishment.

Here no covering of excuse is left; no place of escape is granted; rather, the danger of both parties is publicly described. For I, holding the place of abbot, if I have not announced to you the justice of God, if I have not corrected you when you sin according to the Rule, have denied my office, and whatever is found less than sanctified in you will be imputed wholly to me. It is mine, by reason of the pastoral office, to say what is expedient for the salvation of your souls; yours, however, is humbly to hear what I have pronounced in the name of the Savior, and, as far as each one is able, faithfully to fulfill it with a great heart.

You have heard my danger if I do not cry out; you have also heard yours if you despise me as I preach the word of God to you. Therefore, so that by the Savior’s mercy we may all come through safely, let each one of us diligently pursue what is prescribed for him in this spiritual warfare, taking the greatest care that we not, having become deaf hearers of the truth, lie to the Lord. Let us diligently remember the vow which we made, and let us preserve inviolate until death the holy order which we profess. Let us hear our king speaking in the Gospel to his soldiers: “If anyone serves me, let him follow me; and where I am, there also my servant will be.”

The ministers of the eternal king are priests and Levites, and all cloistered monks, bound by a double reason to great holiness, since we have at the same time received the order of clerics and profess the Rule of monastic purity. Therefore let us follow our commander, whom we serve, along the harsh road of all the virtues; and let us arm ourselves with right faith, firm hope, and the most fervent love of God and neighbor, in which the perfection of all holiness is comprehended. For whoever is girded with the defense of charity is never overcome by the adversary. For in charity the warfare of Christ truly grows sweet, and when its fervor has begun to burn in the soldier’s heart, he fears no adversities.

For charity, as the divine Gregory says, is never idle, but accomplishes great things if it is true; but if it refuses to work, it was not perfect charity. Therefore, cloistered soldiers, you who promised that you would serve the eternal king forever, cast away now the works of darkness, and put on charity toward God and neighbor, which is the strongest armor of salvation for all. If anyone has been clothed in it, he will fear no labors, however hard, and no opposition of this world. He will not fear hunger, thirst, persecutions by men, insults, mockeries, prisons, chains, or any hard and adverse things whatever; but everyone who has once perfectly tasted the charity of God will be unconquered against all things.

[segment 8]
*Second Homily.*

Fol. V.

...and all of you, according to your strength, toward the keeping of the military order. For we are frail and weak, and we have not long stood firm in our holy purpose, unless by daily exercises we continually renew the fervor of the spirit. For by reason of my office I am constrained to set before you in common those things that can conduce to your salvation; it belongs to you to listen patiently in all things to me as I speak, as the shepherd of your souls. For although I, a young man, am not fit to teach so great a multitude of elders, nevertheless, because I call myself shepherd over you, who have been committed to me, I must under necessity feed the flock that I have undertaken to govern.

What is mine to do, I willingly both do and shall do according to my strength, so long as I remain vigorous in this solicitude of pastoral care. Though young, I shall teach you elders, and I shall show you without interruption how you ought to serve as soldiers for him in whose army you have sworn. For the dreadful warning of our lawgiver does not escape me, when he says: “Let the abbot know that whatever the father of the household finds lacking in usefulness among the sheep falls upon the shepherd’s fault.”

And what follows concerns you, if, while I preach and admonish, the word of God is treated with contempt. “Only then,” he says again, “will he be free, if all diligence has been applied by the shepherd to the restless or disobedient flock, and all care has been shown for their diseased actions; then their shepherd, absolved in the judgment of the Lord, may say with the prophet to the Lord: ‘I have not hidden your righteousness in my heart; I have spoken your truth and your salvation; but they, despising it, have scorned me.’ And then at last for the disobedient sheep under his care, death itself shall be their prevailing punishment.”

Here no veil of excuse is left; no place is offered for escape, but the danger of both parties is openly described. For if I, holding the place of abbot, do not announce to you the righteousness of God, if I do not correct you when you sin according to the Rule, I have denied my office, and it will be imputed to me as a crime whatever is found lacking in holiness among you. Therefore, by reason of the pastoral office, it is mine to say what is expedient for the salvation of your souls; yours, however, is humbly to receive what I have proclaimed in the name of the Savior, and faithfully to fulfill it with a great heart, each one as far as he is able.

You have heard my danger if I do not cry out; you have also heard yours if you despise me when I preach the word of God to you. Therefore, so that by the mercy of the Savior we may all escape safely, let each one of us earnestly pursue what is commanded him in this spiritual warfare, taking the utmost care that we do not become deaf hearers of the truth and be measured accordingly. Let us very carefully remember what we have vowed, and let us preserve inviolate until death the holy order that we profess.

Let us hear our king saying in the Gospel to his soldiers: “If anyone serves me, let him follow me; and where I am, there also my servant shall be.” We are servants of the eternal king, priests and enclosed monks, and by a twofold reason we are bound to great holiness, since we have at once received the order of clerics and profess the rule of monastic purity. Let us therefore follow our commander, for whom we serve, along the path of the openness of all virtues, arming ourselves with right faith, firm hope, and the most fervent love of God and neighbor, in which the perfection of all holiness is comprehended. For whoever is girded with the defense of charity is never overcome by the adversary. For in charity truly Christ’s sweet service consists; and when it begins to burn in the heart of his soldier, it fears no adversities. For charity, as Gregory says, is never idle; rather, if it is true, it does great works. But if it refuses to work, it was not perfect charity.

Therefore, enclosed soldiers, you who have promised that you will serve the eternal king forever, cast away now the works of darkness, and put on the charity of God and neighbor, which is the strongest armor of salvation for all. If anyone has been clothed in it, he will fear no labors, however hard, nor any contrary things of this world. He does not fear hunger, thirst, persecutions for the Lord, reproach, mockeries, prisons, chains, or whatever hard and adverse things may come; rather, everyone who has once perfectly tasted the charity of God will remain unconquered against whatever raging things of the world.

[segment 9]
*On the Progress of Spiritual Warfare*

Whatever things there have been in the world pass away. The true lover of God rejoices in tribulations, is glad in adversities, and exults in persecutions for the sake of justice. He follows his king, seated in heaven; there he dwells by the desire of his soul, where he does not doubt that his Savior reigns.

Whatever pleasure and delights are perceived in the world, if you fervently burn in true charity for Christ, it begets in you, as though from very useful dung, a hatred of them; for he loves nothing earthly, nothing transitory, nothing perishable, but only him to whom he has devoted himself by the single intention of his mind. For the true soldier of Christ thinks nothing of the affairs of the world; he cares not for the flesh but for the mind, since he is not ignorant of what is written: “No one serving as a soldier for God entangles himself in secular affairs, so that he may please him to whom he has approved himself.” Indeed, whoever serves an earthly emperor always obeys his commands in all things; at once, without contradiction, they go at the order of their superior. All follow one leader, and whatever he commands in the king’s name, the whole army obeys without contradiction. You also, soldiers of the king of the ages, who in his place and by his command have appointed me your leader, hear the command of your emperor Jesus Christ through my mouth, and do what he commands us in the holy Gospel. Hear what he commands, and consider how you ought to serve as soldiers in the observance of regular discipline. “He who ministers to me,” he says, “let him follow me.”

Listen, cloistered monk, bearing the duties of military service; pay attention. Christ, to whom you serve as soldier, speaks to you; the heavenly emperor, whom you serve at the altar, addresses you. If you wish to be my minister, you must follow the virtue of patience and humility which you have learned from me also. “For I have given you an example, my soldiers, that just as I have done, you also should do likewise.” Charity, monk, compelled me to be born as a man of a virgin in the world; let that same charity keep you always lowly and humble in the cloister for love of me. The work of human salvation which the Father commanded me, I strove for your sake to accomplish always with unceasing meditation; and if you love me, take care to preserve inviolate, even to death, the holy order of monastic life which you have undertaken for your sins. I was always subject to my heavenly Father in all things according to his will, obedient even to the most bitter death of the cross; you also, if you wish to come to me, obey your abbot, appointed in my place, humbly in all things and always according to the Rule.

I never transgressed in anything the command that my Father gave me; and unless you keep inviolate, even to death, the three principal vows of the monastic institution, you are by no means to be counted among my ministers and servants. Though I was rich, for your salvation I became poor and needy; but unless you contemplate my loves and give up all things, you cannot be my disciple. For your sake I endured want, hunger, thirst, hardship, cold, and continual labors in the world; unless you follow me in these, according to your Rule, as far as you are able, patiently and voluntarily, you will certainly not be my minister, my soldier, or my monk. In your patience you shall possess your soul.

I desired nothing in this world, had nothing, loved nothing earthly, but as poor, humble, and wretched I always subjected myself in all things to the perfect will of my Father, receptive of injuries and blows. But you, cloistered monk, who profess the monastic Rule under an abbot or prior, if henceforth you follow the desires of your flesh, if you possess gold or silver of your own in the monastery, if you love anything besides me, if you despise the commands of your superior, if you do not serve me perseveringly in humility, know for certain that I shall not regard you as a soldier, nor count you a monk, a minister, or a disciple, but I shall judge you a fugitive, an apostate, faithless, lying, derisive, and wickedly unfaithful; and when, with my betrayer Judas and the angels sitting in judgment, I am the judge forever, I shall condemn you without mercy.

What your lips vowed to me is no longer yours but mine. If, however, you usurp for yourself what is mine, you do not wish to be my monk and faithful servant, but a thief and a wicked robber, impious and unjust; and do not doubt that when I come I shall judge you as such. But if you faithfully persevere in the holy purpose even to death; if, under the authority of my vicar whom I have appointed for you as father, you preserve inviolate the solemn will by which you consecrated yourself to me; if you observe obedience, poverty, and chastity with humility for love of my name until the end; if you always strive to please me alone in all your actions; if you take care to follow me without interruption along the arduous road of the virtues: then know, and know with undoubting certainty, that I shall have you as a most beloved friend made from a servant, as a soldier to be crowned with me in my kingdom. I shall love you; I shall cherish you; I shall set you forever glorious in the midst of my saints.

Now hear me, my servant, who out of reverence and honor for my name have enrolled yourself in the military service of monks: with a great and perfect heart, follow the footprints of my feet, just as that teacher of yours, once blessed and your friend, taught you and strove with all his efforts to imitate. Let not the enticements of the flesh deceive you, brave soldier; let not the vain and pernicious dealings of this world seduce your soul, which is dedicated to me. Never yield to the temptations of the unclean spirit, which no mortal has ever been able to escape. Let those last things which you must necessarily undergo always remain in your memory; and from their swift passage, and from the pleasure of the flesh which passes away, take up for yourself the necessary dread and contempt of the future things of this sojourn.

Just as a brief labor undertaken for me in the world with patience and charity prepares eternal happiness for my soldiers, so the desires and pleasures of the flesh, which pass away suddenly, plunge the soul of the sinful man into hell, to be tormented without end. Therefore let all your desires be directed to me, monk, you who minister to me and who undertake to follow me along the arduous road of regular observance. Give me your heart if you desire happiness; seek me alone if you love your own life and salvation. When you love God, you are tranquil and holy; when you have followed him, you are blessed and glorious without end.

What would you do, my brothers, if it happened that you heard these words which we have spoken from the mouth of our Savior and king as he spoke? What, I ask, would you do? I certainly believe there would be great compunction in your hearts if you heard Christ speaking personally in this way.

But we, bearing Christ’s place in the monastery, speak the words which he has given, and you do not attend. We testify to you about the necessary arena of salvation, and you show no signs of compunction. If you do not know why these things have been said, I now beg you to understand. I see that some of you, given over to vanities, shrink from sacred readings and care for nothing less than the perfection of spiritual warfare; their god is their belly, and their glory is in their shame. Do you think, most worthless knaves, who have no taste for anything spiritual, that Christ will come again from heaven to speak to you those things which he committed to his preachers to say? Or have you not heard Paul saying: “Christ, rising from the dead, now dies no more; death shall no longer have dominion over him. For in that he died, he died to sin once; but in that he lives, he lives to God.”

Once Christ was seen on earth; once he appeared visibly among mortals; once he preached the way of eternal salvation; he will not add a second time. He established pastors and vicars over his household to teach the ignorant. Wishing to make their authority known, he said in the Gospel: “He who hears you hears me, and he who despises you despises me.” Therefore hear those whom Christ has set over you as rulers, to whom he has committed the distribution of spiritual wheat, as he said in the holy Gospel: “Who, do you think, is the faithful and prudent steward, whom the lord has appointed over his household, to give them wheat in due season?” Therefore, brothers, bear fruit worthy of repentance while it is granted to you at the opportune time, lest the time be cut off if you neglect to repent. For you know what divine Scripture says to us: “The day of the Lord will come like a thief in the night.” I beg you, beware of putting off the good, because none of you knows when he is going to die. Rather, attend to that true saying of the wanton poet Naso, when he gives this warning: “But hasten, and do not put yourself off to the hours that are to come; he who is not ready today will be less ready tomorrow.” No one [unclear].

[segment 10]
if you have kept chastity with humility for love of my name until the end; if you have always striven to please me alone in all your actions; if you have taken care to follow me, without interruption, through the difficult path amid the virtues, then know, and know most certainly and without doubt, that as one made very dear to my soul and service I shall hold you as my soldier, to be crowned in my kingdom. I shall love you; I shall cherish you; in the midst of my saints I shall establish you glorious forever.

Hear me now, my servant, who for reverence and honor of my name have bound yourself in the strictest warfare of monks. With a great and perfect heart follow the footprints of my steps, as that blessed preceptor of yours, once my friend and yours, taught you, and strive with all your efforts to endure injuries. Let the enticements of the flesh not deceive you, O valiant soldier. Let the vain and destructive commerce of this world not seduce your mind, dedicated to me. Never yield to the exceedingly deceitful suggestions of unclean spirits, who rage with all their power for your ruin, but remember that your life in this world is most wretched and brief, and that death stands before the doors, which no mortal has ever been able to escape or outlive. Always keep in memory those last things which you must necessarily undergo; and because the pleasure of the flesh passes by in the swiftest flight, take up dread and contempt for this sojourn which must soon be ended.

Just as brief labor for me in the world, borne with patience and charity, prepares eternal happiness for my soldiers, so the desires and pleasures of the flesh, which suddenly pass away, sink the soul of the sinful master down to hell, to be tormented without end. Therefore, O monk who minister to me and follow me along the difficult path of regular observance, let all your desires be directed toward me. Give me your whole heart if you desire happiness. Seek me alone if you love your own life and salvation. When you have loved me, you are tranquil and holy; when you have followed me, you are blessed and glorious without end.

What, my brothers, would you do if it happened to you to hear these words which we have spoken with your own ears, as uttered by our king and savior? What, I ask, would you do? I certainly believe that great compunction would arise in your hearts if you heard Christ speaking in person. But we, who bear Christ’s place in the monastery, speak the words which he has given, and you do not attend. We proclaim to you the contest necessary for salvation, and you show no signs of compunction.

If you do not know what the cause is, I shall say something useful; now, I pray, understand. I see some of you shudder at sacred readings, devoted to vanities, and care for nothing less than the perfection of spiritual warfare. Their god is their belly, and their glory is in confusion. Do you think, vilest idlers, who savor nothing spiritual, that Christ will come again from heaven in order to speak to you the things which he entrusted to his preachers to say? Have you not heard Paul saying: Christ rising again from the dead now dies no more; death shall no longer have dominion over him. For in that he died, he died to sin once; but in that he lives, he lives to God.

Once Christ lived on earth; once he appeared visibly among mortals; once he preached the way of eternal salvation. He will not add a second time what he entrusted to faithful pastors and vicars over his household, who are to teach the ignorant. Wishing to make their authority clear, he said in the Gospel: He who hears you hears me, and he who despises you despises me. Therefore hear those whom Christ has appointed over you as rulers, to whom he has entrusted the administration of spiritual wheat. In the holy Gospel he said: Who, do you think, is the faithful and wise steward, whom the lord has set over his household, to give them their measure of wheat in due season? Blessed is that servant whom his lord, when he comes, shall find so doing.

Therefore we exhort you, brothers, in the Lord Jesus, and with whatever diligence we can we beg you: do not consume your days in vanity, but consider the extreme brevity of this life, and bear fruits worthy of penance while the opportunity is granted to you, lest, if you neglect to repent, the time be cut off. For you know what divine Scripture says to us: The day of the Lord will come like a thief in the night. Do not, I pray, put off the good, because none of you knows when he is to die. Rather, attend to that true saying which has lately been put in verse: For this reason, do not postpone yourself to future hours; he who is not fit today will be less fit tomorrow.

Let no brother promise himself a long life, but rather let each one think that death is near at hand, and therefore let him live so prepared as if he were to depart from the body tomorrow. For the hope of a longer life has deceived many, and, holding them back from the fervor of their holy purpose, has suddenly sunk them down to hell. Therefore perfect your warfare, which after not much time must necessarily be ended, in such a way that you may not fear to appear before your king when he comes. Always be fearful, because you do not have here an abiding city, nor can you remain long in this mortal life; death will soon come and compel you to depart.

Whoever is a true monk and desires to be a faithful soldier of Christ despises all the delights of this world. He always thinks of his Lord as present to him, and, having cast aside all impediments and every little burden, he takes care above all how he may please him. He should not be anxious in seeking the necessities of life, since the providence of his pastor arranges all these things for those subject to him. Let the monk’s whole care be to keep himself clean from this world, so that, free and released from all things, he may seek nothing except God and think of nothing except Jesus crucified for us.

O happy soldier of Christ, to whom the kingdoms of heaven are promised, if he remains faithful, prudent, and watchful in the love of God to the end. Certainly nothing is happier than the good monk, to whom the Son of God promises himself as reward; yet nothing is more laborious than he who, placed in the contest of battle, daily risks his salvation. Nothing is stronger than the monk who conquers the world together with the devil; and nothing weaker than the one who is overcome by his own flesh.

Therefore, O monk, you must always keep watch against the hidden enemy of your salvation. He is never more easily conquered than when he is kept far from the entrance of the mind. Therefore, if you desire to escape victorious against him, do not allow him to move the devices of his deceit and fraud nearer to you. Guard your senses first of all, lest death enter through the windows; and beware especially the ambushes of your enemy when he has allowed you for a time to be secure in your devotion. Never forget the warning of the wise man, who says: Unless you hold yourself constantly in the fear of the Lord, your house, that is, your conscience, will quickly be overthrown. Therefore Solomon said in Proverbs: Blessed is the man who is always fearful; but he who is hard of mind will fall into evil.

For while the monk lives presumptuously in security, he is without doubt heading toward destruction. The soldier of Christ must therefore always be prepared against his adversaries, so that as long as he lives in this mortal body he may not trust that he has perfectly overcome them. For the flesh always desires against the spirit; and lest the soul ever be overcome, it is necessary to fight as long as life continues. Whenever you have conquered your invisible enemy, he rises up again and gathers greater forces against you, so that, if he cannot obtain victory by strength, he may at least bring on despair by persistence.

But you, most valiant soldier of Christ, take care not to fail in the contest of this very brief struggle. Take care not to lay down the arms of necessary defense before the battle is completed at the end. For the Savior says: He who perseveres to the end will be saved. Do not give rest to your soul as long as it has been granted to you to remain in this miserable pilgrimage, but always do some good, lest the devil ever find you idle. Always remember the warning of Scripture, which says: Whatever your hand can do, do it earnestly, because neither work nor reason nor wisdom nor knowledge will exist in hell, where you are hastening.

There is no one who will live forever, but we all die, and like waters we sink into the earth, which will not return again to their former state. Then there will no longer be time for doing good toward the merit of eternal happiness; but whatever a person has sown here and now in due season, that he will then receive as reward. Therefore holy Scripture says again: In the morning sow your seed, and in the evening do not let your hand cease. And Saint Paul, admonishing Timothy, says: Labor as a good soldier of Christ Jesus. No one serving God as a soldier entangles himself in secular affairs, so that he may please him to whom he has proved himself. For also, of those who compete in the contest, no one will be crowned unless he has competed lawfully. Everyone who contends in the contest abstains from all things. In another place the same lover of Christ says: I want you to be without anxiety.

You therefore, soldier of Christ, who contend in the contest of daily battle so that at last you may appear glorious forever before your God, take care to imitate the corruptible athlete who, competing in the contest for a corruptible crown,

[segment 11]
*On the Progress of Spiritual Warfare.*

Let no one, brothers, promise himself a long life; rather, let each one think that death is impending, and let him live so prepared, as if he were to depart tomorrow. For hope of a longer life has deceived many, and by holding them back from the fervor of their holy purpose has suddenly plunged them down to hell. Therefore pass through this life of yours, which must necessarily end before long, in such a way that you do not fear to appear before your king when he comes. Always be mindful that you do not have here a lasting city, and that you cannot remain long in this mortal life; but soon the day will come which will compel you to emigrate.

Whoever is a true monk and desires to be a faithful soldier of Christ despises all the delights of this world, always thinks of the Lord as present to him, and, casting away from himself every impediment and burden, takes care above all how he may be able to please him. He ought not to be anxious in seeking the necessities of life, since the providence of the shepherd disposes all these things for those subject to him. Let the whole care of the monk be to keep himself clean from this age, so that, free and released from everything, he seeks nothing except God, thinks of nothing except Jesus crucified for us.

O happy soldier of Christ, to whom the kingdoms of heaven are promised, if, faithful, watchful, and vigilant in the love of God, he remains to the end. Certainly nothing is happier than the good monk, to whom the Son of God promises himself as a reward; nothing also more laborious, for, set in the arena of battle, he is in danger each day concerning his salvation; and nothing is stronger than the monk who conquers the world and the devil together; nothing also weaker than he who is overcome by his own flesh.

Therefore, O monk, you must always keep watch against the hidden enemy of your salvation, who is more easily overcome if he is kept far from the entrance of the mind. Hence, if you desire to come away victorious against him, you must learn beforehand the tricks of his deceit and fraud. Guard your senses at the first entrance, lest presently he enter through the windows; and beware especially the ambushes of your enemy, when he has allowed you for a time to be secure in your devotion. Never forget the admonition of the wise man, who says: Unless you hold yourself steadily in the fear of the Lord, your house will quickly be overthrown. Therefore Solomon said in Proverbs: Blessed is the man who is always fearful; but he who is hard of mind will fall into evil. For as soon as a monk has presumed upon security, without doubt he tends toward destruction.

Therefore the soldier of Christ must always be prepared against his adversaries, so that as long as he lives in this mortal body he does not trust that he has perfectly overcome them. For the flesh always lusts against the spirit; and lest the soul ever be overcome, so long as one happens to live, it is necessary to fight. As often as you overcome the invisible enemy, he rises again and contends with you with greater savagery, so that, if he cannot obtain victory by strength, he may at least bring on despair by persistence. But you, most valiant soldier of Christ, beware lest you fail in the arena of this very fierce contest; beware lest, before the battle is completed at the end, you lay down the arms of your necessary struggle. For the Savior says: He who perseveres to the end will be saved.

Do not give rest to your soul as long as it has been granted you to remain in this miserable pilgrimage, but always do some good thing, lest the devil ever find you idle. Always remember the admonition of Scripture, which says: Whatever your hand is able to do, do it earnestly, because there will be no work, nor reason, nor necessity, nor knowledge among the dead, where you are hastening. There is no one who lives forever, but we all die, and like water we shall flow down into the earth, and shall not return again to our former state. Then there will be no more time for working good toward the merit of eternal happiness; but whatever you, O man, have sown here now in time, that you will receive as your reward. Therefore holy Scripture again says: In the morning sow your seed, and in the evening let not your hand cease. And Saint Paul, admonishing Timothy, says this: Labor like a good soldier of Christ Jesus. No one serving as a soldier for God entangles himself in secular business, so that he may please the one to whom he has approved himself. For also among those who compete in the arena, no one will be crowned unless he has competed lawfully. But everyone who contends in the arena abstains from all things. In another place, too, the same lover of Christ says: I want you to be without anxiety.

You therefore, soldier of Christ, who contend in the arena of daily battle so that at last you may appear glorious forever before your God, imitate the perishable gymnosophist, who, competing in the arena for a perishable crown, abstains from all things so that he may not be defeated and may run more swiftly, naked and free. And so, if you desire to walk the way of God in true purity, if you wish to conquer the enemy in the arena of battle, cast away from yourself all garments of carnal pleasures. For whoever, with hair and clothing cast off from himself, has learned to run naked, carries around nothing besides his unencumbered body by which he can be held by the adversary. For he who runs naked has stripped himself of every anxiety over the things clamoring around him, and thinks of nothing other than victory; nor meanwhile does he care what is being done at home with wife or children. Nothing present recalls him from the course; the agility of his companions does not deter him; among many he contends as if alone, hastening breathless to the prize as if certain.

Imitate him, soldier of Christ, in the spiritual contest; contemplate the naked Savior hanging naked on the cross, so that you may be able to lay low your adversary all the more easily the less he sees that you possess anything of the vanities of the world. Naked, therefore, light, and unencumbered, fight on every side; naked and released from all things, fly into heaven. Naked you came into this world; naked you will return to the earth. Thinking diligently on these things, my brothers, run in such a way that you may obtain eternal life with all the saints. Amen.

The homily ends.

What Is the Armor of Spiritual Warfare. Homily III.

Most dear brothers, today we have heard the holy Apostle writing in his epistle to the Ephesians: Put on the armor of God, that you may be able to stand against the snares of the devil; for our wrestling is not against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers, against the rulers of the world of these darknesses, against the spiritual forces of wickedness in the heavens. Therefore take up the armor of God, that you may be able to resist on the evil day, and in all things stand perfect. He then adds what the armor of God is: Stand, therefore, having girded your loins in truth, and having put on the breastplate of justice, and having shod your feet in preparation for the Gospel of peace; in all things taking up the shield of faith, with which you can extinguish all the fiery darts of the wicked one; and take up the helmet of salvation, and the sword of the Spirit, which is the word of God; praying at every time in the Spirit through every prayer and supplication, and in the same keeping watch with all perseverance and supplication for all the saints.

To the Thessalonians likewise he says: But we who are of the day, let us be sober, putting on the breastplate of faith and charity, and as a helmet the hope of salvation. These are the arms, brothers, with which the soldiers of Christ, clothed, wage war against the savage adversary; these are the insignia of the virtues of monks, without which no one obtains victory over vices. The instruments of human warfare, which most mortals pursue with excessive zeal, are helmet, shield, breastplate, sword, dagger, cuirass, belt, lance, and many other things of this kind, with which men preparing to go to war are most carefully armed; and unless they have been most diligently equipped in their whole body, they are certainly judged less fit for fighting.

But the arms of our spiritual warfare are faith, hope, charity, patience, humility, obedience, chastity, together with the holy virtues of the soul enumerated one by one by our blessed teacher in the fourth chapter, where he makes beautiful mention of the instruments of good works. And would that, my brothers, we were as careful and diligent about these spiritual arms of our warfare as we see worldly men to be anxious about their corruptible armor. But now, what I say with too much grief, since we shamefully cast aside the arms of salvation with which we ought to fight down all our adversaries, what else remains except that we fall before our enemy in the contest?

But perhaps you will say: If Christian faith, as the Apostle testifies, belongs to the arms of spiritual warfare, we certainly intend to remain in it. But suppose this is so. What, however, does Saint James say in his epistle? What will it profit, my brothers, if someone says that he has faith, but does not have works? Will faith be able to save him? For just as the body without the spirit is dead, so faith without works is dead. Therefore, whenever Scripture speaks of faith, it must always be understood not as ordinary and dead faith, but as faith formed by good works and illumined by the sweetness of divine love. For no one will be saved by faith so

[segment 12]
--- Page 021 ---

Homily III.

...for a crown, lest he be overcome, he abstains from everything, so that naked and free he may run the more readily. Therefore, if you desire to walk the way of God in true purity, if you wish to conquer the enemy in the arena of combat, cast away from yourself all the garments of cares; lay aside earthly hair and all that belongs to carnal pleasure. Whoever, with hair and clothing cast away from him, has learned to run naked brings nothing with him except his bare body by which he can be held by the adversary. For he who runs naked is stripped of every solicitude for things surrounding him, and thinks of nothing but victory; nor meanwhile does he care what is happening among wife or children. Nothing recalls him from his course; the swiftness of his companion does not frighten him away from winning the prize. If he contends among many, he hastens to the prize as though alone, panting as though he were certain.

Imitate him, soldier of Christ, in spiritual combat. Contemplate your Savior hanging naked on the cross, so that you may be able to overthrow your adversaries all the more easily, the less they see you possessing anything of worldly vanity. Therefore, naked, light, and unencumbered, fight on every side; naked and freed from all things, fly to heaven. Naked you came into this world; naked you will return to the earth. Thinking carefully on these things, my brothers, run in such a way that you may so obtain eternal things with all the saints. Amen. End of the homily.

What Is the Armor of Spiritual Warfare. Homily IV.

Most beloved brothers, today we have heard the holy Apostle writing in the epistle to the Ephesians: Put on the armor of God, that you may be able to stand against the wiles of the devil, because our wrestling is not against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers, against the rulers of the world of this darkness, against spiritual wickedness in the heavenly places. Therefore take up the armor of God, that you may be able to resist on the evil day, and, having been made perfect in all things, to stand.

He then adds what the armor of God is: Stand, therefore, with your loins girded in truth, and clothed in the breastplate of justice, and your feet shod in preparation for the gospel of peace. In all things taking up the shield of faith, with which you can extinguish all the fiery darts of the most wicked one. Take the helmet of salvation, and the sword of the Spirit, which is the word of God, praying at every time in the Spirit with all prayer and supplication, and in this very thing watching with all persistence and supplication for all the saints.

Likewise he says to the Thessalonians: But we who are of the day, let us be sober, clothed with the breastplate of faith and charity, and with the helmet, the hope of salvation. These are the arms, brothers, with which the soldiers of Christ, when clothed, defeat the fierce adversary. These are the badges of monastic virtue, without which no one attains victory over vices.

The equipment of human warfare, which most mortals pursue with excessive zeal, is the helmet, shield, breastplate, sword, dagger, cuirass, belt, lance, and many other things of this kind, with which men about to go forth to war are most carefully armed; and unless they have been most diligently protected over the whole body, they are certainly judged too unfit for battle. The arms of our spiritual warfare are faith, hope, charity, patience, humility, obedience, chastity, together with the holy virtues of the soul enumerated by our blessed father Benedict in the fourth chapter of the Rule, where he makes beautiful mention of the instruments of good works.

And would that, my brothers, we were as careful and diligent for these spiritual arms of our warfare as worldly men are seen to be solicitous for their corruptible armor. But this is what I say with too much sorrow: since we shamefully cast away the arms of salvation with which we ought to defeat all our adversaries, what remains except that we should fall before our enemy in the contest?

But perhaps you say: If Christian faith, as the Apostle testifies, belongs to the armor of spiritual warfare, we have certainly never cast it away. For we firmly believe whatever holy mother Church has set before us to be believed, and in that profession of faith we steadfastly intend to remain even to death. So be it. But what does Saint James say in his epistle? What will it profit, my brothers, if someone says that he has faith but does not have works? Surely faith cannot save him? For just as the body without the spirit is dead, so faith without works is dead. Therefore, whenever Scripture speaks of faith, it must not be understood as common and dead faith, but as faith formed by good works and illumined by the sweetness of divine love. For no one will be saved through faith alone.

[segment 13]
[Page 022]

as James says in his epistle; but one must join to faith the works of Christian piety. You believe in one almighty God, creator of heaven and earth, Father and Son and Holy Spirit. You do well. The demons also believe, and tremble. What then? Where are the works by which your faith is shown to have been formed in Christ? Faith is idle and dead when it is not commended by holy and necessary works. We believe in Christ: that is good, if we follow Christ’s examples. For if we do not follow Christ in works of piety, we flatter ourselves in vain about faith alone. You, I say, believe; the demons believe. Your faith, most Christian beloved, is a faith of love, not the faith of the devil. You believe rightly, if you love God with your whole heart and with all your strength. But if you do not truly love God, you have an idle and dead faith. The proof of love, as Gregory bears witness, is the showing of work in deed. For thus the Lord speaks in the Gospel: If anyone loves me, he will keep my word.

This, then, is true faith: ordered according to Christ’s teaching by good and holy works. If, by faith and at the same time by work, you believe in the Lord Jesus, surely you hold the shield of salvation in your hands; with it, by the Lord’s own gift, you will easily overcome all adversaries. Of this armor it is written: The saints through faith conquered kingdoms, worked justice, and obtained the promise. John likewise says: Everything that is born of God conquers the world; and this is the victory that conquers the world: our faith. And Peter, the prince of the apostles, admonishes us thus, saying: Brothers, be sober and keep watch, because your adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour; resist him, strong in faith.

True faith and confidence in our Lord Jesus Christ is an invincible and most powerful armor, which none of the adversaries has ever been able to resist, as it is written in the book of Paralipomenon. King Jehoshaphat said to his army: Believe in the Lord your God, and you will be secure. In Ecclesiasticus also we read thus: You who fear the Lord, believe in him, and your reward will not be made void. And again: In your work believe from the faith of your soul, for this is the keeping of the commandments. Whoever believes God attends to the commandments; and whoever trusts in him will not be diminished.

Now therefore take care, dearest brothers, and with the highest zeal always strive that you may be able to hold in your hands this shield of faith, formed by holy works, against all assaults of your enemies, because one who is fortified by this impregnable shield is never conquered by the adversary. Confirming all this, Augustine too says from the words of the Lord: There are no greater riches, no treasures, no honors; no substance of this world is greater than the catholic faith. It saves sinful men, illumines the blind, heals the sick, baptizes catechumens, justifies the faithful, restores the penitent, increases the righteous, crowns martyrs, preserves virgins and widows and the married in chaste modesty, ordains clerics, consecrates priests, and places believers in the eternal inheritance with the holy angels.

Just as no appearance of beauty at all is visible in the root of a tree, and yet whatever beauty or grace there is in the tree proceeds from it, so too from the humility of faith whatever merit, whatever blessedness the soul is going to receive proceeds wholly from the foundation of faith. For, as the apostle says, whatever is not from faith is sin, because without faith it is impossible to please God. For the one who comes to God must believe that he exists, and that he is the rewarder of those who seek him. The more anyone loves, the more abundantly he advances in faith. This faith without charity is like a useless missile and a shield without a strap. O treasure more opulent than all things: faith, which, once formed in the mind of a human being, compares mortal things with immortal, conquers demons and all kingdoms, and bravely overcomes hard adversities.

Faith is the foundation of all virtues; without it no spiritual building stands. Faith gives light to the mind, and is the chief instrument of spiritual warfare. For without the shield of faith the devil is not conquered, nor is the impulse of wanton flesh overcome. Therefore let us consider, brothers, how necessary faith formed by charity is for our salvation, since in this warfare neither contest nor victory is granted to us, nor is confidence preserved, without it. For faith is the shield by which we most easily avert the hostile attacks on our salvation, standing beneath the yoke of divine love. For blessed Pope Gregory says: Then we are truly faithful if we fulfill in deeds what we promise in words. In this armor, then, faith is the shield of all good works, joining our acts to us.

[segment 14]
--- Page 023 ---

and holiness to himself; patience to an enemy; mercy to the poor; brotherly charity in the Lord to all. Therefore holy Christophilus says: “Brothers, we are debtors, not to the flesh so as to live according to the flesh. For if you live according to the flesh, you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the deeds of the flesh, you will live.” Therefore he fights rightly for the Lord who gives to each what is his.

Next, we are commanded to have our feet shod in preparation for the gospel of peace, so that the good which we ourselves have come to know by the Lord’s generosity we may also gladly share with others. For our law, brothers, according to which we are commanded to serve the Lord as soldiers, is the gospel of Jesus Christ; to it all the rules and constitutions of cloistered persons are ordered. For our monastic constitutions are not the gospel, but the statutes of monks were made on account of the gospel. A Christian can indeed be saved without being a monk, but a monk cannot be saved unless he has been a Christian.

Let certain excessively superstitious and foolish cloistered persons be ashamed, who value their rules and statutes more than the gospel of Christ; who observe the traditions of men even to prisons and chains, but do not heed the constitutions of God and of the holy universal Church. To these the words of our Savior, spoken to the Jews according to Matthew, properly apply: “Why do you also transgress the commandment of God because of your tradition?” The evangelical teaching must be preferred by far to all the constitutions of the whole world. After the gospel, however, the statutes of the universal Church hold the first place of authority; without them the constitutions or rules of monks are not compared in dignity. Let us take care to live according to evangelical purity, and we shall be perfect in the monastic way of life of the fathers.

Let our feet always be shod in preparation for the gospel of peace, so that we may mortify in ourselves the desires of the flesh and, through love of God and neighbor, have peace with all. For without peace and the concord of brotherhood, mortification of the flesh is of no benefit, as Saint Jerome says: Whoever afflicts the body but abandons concord does indeed praise God on the drum, but does not praise him in the choir. Hence Solomon says: Better is a dry morsel with joy than a house full of sacrifices with strife. And again he says: It is an honor for a man to separate himself from contentions, but all fools are mingled with insults.

Saint Christophilus also, lover of peace, admonishes us, saying: “Pursue peace with all, and holiness, without which no one will see God.” Hence also the prophet Zechariah says: “Love peace and truth, says the Lord almighty.” And our Savior says in the holy gospel according to Mark: “Have salt in yourselves, and have peace among yourselves.” “There is no peace for the wicked,” says the Lord.

But he is truly a soldier of Christ who has peace with God, with his neighbor, and in himself. A man is said to have peace with God when, moved by good will and turned toward him through the continual desire of love, despising transitory things, he directs the gaze of his mind to things above and most diligently keeps everything that the Lord has commanded. For when the Son of God was born in the flesh, the holy angels sang glory to the Lord, who sits above all things in the highest, and announced peace to people of good will. But wicked and unjust people cannot have this peace with God, because they have turned their wills away from the Lord.

He has peace with his neighbor who does injury to no one, who when injured does not return vengeance, but, overcoming evil with good, never abandons brotherly charity. If someone among his neighbors murmurs unjustly against such a person, the soldier of Christ has not for that reason abandoned peace, because perhaps another, without just cause, has begun unjust murmuring. A just man, who has peace with God and desires to harm none of his neighbors, will rightly be called peaceable, even if someone disturbed rises up unjustly against him. For the just man says: “With those who hated peace, I was peaceable; when I spoke to them, they attacked me without cause.”

Hence Saint Augustine says: Among true worshipers of God, even wars themselves are peaceful, because they are waged not from greed or cruelty but from zeal for peace, so that the evil may be restrained and the good relieved. He has peace in himself who, illumined by purity and holiness, lives secure in his own conscience without error, whom anxious conscience does not gnaw, nor does unjust or wicked thought disturb. Hence Augustine again says: Peace is serenity of mind, tranquillity of soul, simplicity of heart, the bond of love, the fellowship of charity. For it removes enmities and rivalries; it checks wars.

[segment 15]
What constitutes the armor of spiritual soldiery.

To oneself he should render holiness; to his fellow monks, love; to the enemy, patience; to the poor, mercy; to all, brotherly charity in the Lord. Therefore the most chaste one says: “We are debtors, brothers, not to the flesh, that we should live according to the flesh. For if you live according to the flesh, you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the deeds of the flesh, you will live.” Rightly, therefore, the learned soldier distributes the one thing that is his own.

After this we are commanded to have our feet shod in preparation for the gospel of peace, so that the good which we ourselves have come to know by the Lord’s gift we may also gladly communicate to others. For this warfare of ours, brothers, according to which we are commanded to serve the Lord as soldiers, is evangelical; under Christ all are enrolled.

[segment 16]
--- Page 024 ---

Homily III. Fol. IX.

It restrains anger; it treads down the proud; it loves the humble; it calms those at odds; it reconciles enemies. It is gentle to all, burdensome to none, troublesome to none, oppressive to none. It does not know how to be exalted; it does not know how to be puffed up. Whoever has received it, let him hold it; whoever has lost it, let him seek it again; whoever has let it slip away, let him search for it, because whoever is not found in it is disowned by the Father, disinherited by the Son, and no less made alien from the Holy Spirit. He can never come to the inheritance of God who has refused to observe the testament of peace.

For the place of God is in peace, but the dwelling of the devil is in anger and disturbance. Therefore every person who does not have this threefold peace in his heart, who does not offer it in his speech, and who does not show it in his works, is not to be called either a monk or a Christian. Hence the Lord says in the Gospel: “Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God.” If those who make peace are called sons of God, beyond doubt those who throw peace into confusion are sons of Satan.

Therefore, my dearest brothers, have among yourselves that peace which the world cannot give, which is the peace of God and of our Lord Jesus Christ, of which the prophet Isaiah said: “And the work of justice shall be peace; and the service of justice, silence and security forever.” For God is the Lord of peace and love, and so whoever violates peace and brotherly love shows that he is not a true lover of God. Let the true peace of Christ be in your hearts, and you will fear none of this world’s adversities. Let there also be true peace among you, not painted peace, because Christ desires peace not from the mouth but from the heart, and wants one’s neighbor to be greeted with affection rather than with lips. Therefore if any one of you has saddened his brother in anything, let him make satisfaction to him as soon as possible before sunset; and take care, all of you, that the sun not set upon your anger. For anger grown old begets hatred and strips the soul of every adornment of virtue. Therefore, monk, if you have sinned against your brother, do penance at once before him and, falling at his knees, beg forgiveness.

“And the sword,” he says, “of the Spirit, which is the word of God,” through every prayer and entreaty, we soldiers of Christ must always hold in our hands. The word of God, which is contained in the holy Scriptures, is a victorious defense against all attacks of demons, as we are warned by the example of our Savior, who, when tempted by the devil, with calm gentleness hurled in his face the testimonies of the divine Scriptures, saying: “It is written: Man lives not by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God.”

And you, monk, serving as a soldier for Christ, whenever you are tempted by the devil, immediately imprint on your forehead the sign of the holy cross, and say with Saint John Chrysostom in a certain homily: “I renounce you, Satan, with all your iniquities, and I join myself to you, Jesus Christ, Son of the living God, because it is written: The soul that has sinned, that one shall die.” This is the sword of the Spirit, sharpest on both sides; if you brandish it in your hands against the enemy from the very beginning of temptation, with God’s protection you will easily overcome all his devices and ambushes. For Scripture says: “Resist the devil, and he will flee from you.”

And so, rising early in the morning, at once make that protestation which I have described against the adversary of your salvation; and neither the devil nor any perverse person will be able to harm you by incantation or by malefice, whether that be spaticum [unclear] or something else. Do this same thing when you are about to sleep at night, and as often as you perceive any temptation of the malignant spirit rising up in your thought. Thus you will be safe in all your ways, and the devil will flee far from you and will not dare come nearer to you, unless your own sloth first overthrows you, so that either you cast away the sword or at once commit some mortal sin.

For Scripture says: “Those who do iniquity and sin are enemies of their own soul.” Solomon also says in Proverbs: “His own iniquities seize the impious man, and he is bound by the cords of his sins. He shall die because he did not have discipline, and in the multitude of his folly he shall be deceived.” Therefore, dearest brothers, if you wish to dwell securely under the protection of almighty God, flee sin, because sin separates us from the Lord’s help, just as purity and cleanness of conscience join us to God, as the prophet Isaiah says: “Behold, the hand of the Lord is not shortened, so that it cannot save; nor is his ear made heavy, so that it cannot hear. But your iniquities have divided between you and your God, and your sins have hidden his face from you, so that he would not hear.” Jeremiah also says: “Our iniquities have turned these things away, and our sins have kept good

--- Page 025 ---

from us.” For everyone who sins mortally opens the door of his heart to the devil and closes it to God; nor can he trust in the help of the Most High so long as he lies in crime without repentance.

Here ends Homily III.

On the reading and study of the divine Scriptures: Homily IV.

Since all Scripture, divinely inspired, as the holy Christ-lover says, is useful for teaching, for arguing, for correcting, and for instructing in justice, so that the man of God may be perfect, furnished for every good work, we ought gladly to turn our effort to the reading of the divine Scriptures, and to direct whatever study of secular literature we once sweated over to the sharp edge of this contemplation. For in the divine Scriptures whatever saving knowledge requires is found expressed most abundantly, so that what you do not find there is by no means necessary for salvation.

Indeed, the majesty of the divine Scriptures far surpasses all the knowledge and teaching of the whole world. The Holy Spirit infused it into men of God so that it might be written, and, so that it may be understood, he grants the grace of his illumination to devoted readers. My brothers, turn this Scripture over diligently with purity of mind and the fervor of twofold charity, because whoever truly loves God spends every time that he can find free for leisure in sacred reading.

Let the frenzied acts, so to speak, of fleshly lovers be incentives for you, I pray, toward the wholesome study of the Scriptures: when they receive letters and tokens from their mistresses, they do not cease to read them, and they read them again and again and kiss them; and, as a greater sign of love, they either hang them about their neck or place them on their breast, so that the cherished memory of the one they desire may never depart from their mind. Consider, brothers, the madness of fleshly people in love, and blush at your own lukewarmness concerning the love of God.

Which of you is affected toward the holy Scriptures of God with as much love of heart as a blind lover, mad with desire for the filthy and utterly vain love letters of one little prostitute, is inflamed with affection? He, because of love for the absent woman whom he desires, protects her letters and little gifts with frequent kisses; he examines each individual stroke again and again, scrutinizing it with excessive love; in everything he does he always thinks of the absent woman; anxiously he hastens to come into her sight.

O if we who are called Christian monks, who serve as soldiers for the supreme and immortal King, Christ Jesus, who are commanded to love our God above all things and are instructed to love nothing in this world besides him, if, I say, we pursued our Creator with as much love as fleshly-minded people honor and love their corruptible and mortal girlfriends. Holy Scripture, when read daily, makes God known to us; it reveals his will to us; it instructs us what we ought to do and discloses what we ought to avoid. Through it the Almighty speaks with us, and, forming us by an invisible teaching, consoles us, as the holy Apostle says: “Whatever things have been written were written for our instruction, so that through patience and the consolation of the Scriptures we might have hope.”

Therefore the true soldier of Christ, and especially the cloistered man, who loves God with his whole heart and desires to please him alone, embraces the divine Scriptures, and especially the holy Gospel, with affection, and is constantly occupied in meditating on its reading. But whoever neglects the reading of the divine Scriptures plainly shows that he is not a true lover of God. For what does Scripture itself say? “Whoever turns his ear away so that he does not hear the laws, his prayer will be execrable.” But “blessed are those who search the testimonies of the Lord; with their whole heart they seek him.”

And so, my brothers, if you truly love the Lord Jesus with your whole heart, if as true soldiers you desire above all things to please him, if finally you wish to advance toward the perfection of monastic life, you ought always to occupy yourselves with fervent affection in the reading of the divine Scriptures. How useful the reading of the Scriptures is, blessed Jerome indicates when he says: “Love the knowledge of the Scriptures, and you will not love the vices of the flesh.” For after God nothing in this life is taken in more pleasantly, nothing is felt more sweetly, nothing separates the mind more powerfully from love of this world, nothing strengthens the soldier of Christ so much against all temptations of adversaries, nothing disposes and confirms the monk so greatly for every good work, as frequent reading and ardent study of the Scriptures.

Indeed, if the benefits of the Almighty which
