On the reading and study of the divine scriptures: Homily Four.

Since all scripture, divinely inspired, as Saint Chrysostom says, is useful for teaching or for arguing, for correction, and for instruction in righteousness, so that the man of God may be perfect, instructed for every good work, we ought freely to devote ourselves to the reading of the divine scriptures, and to direct every pursuit of secular literature in which we have sweated at all toward the sharp point of this contemplation. For in the scriptures everything that saving knowledge requires is found set out in the greatest abundance, so that whatever you do not find there is in no way necessary for salvation. Indeed the majesty of the divine scriptures far surpasses all the knowledge and teaching of the whole world, which the Holy Spirit poured into the men of God so that it might be written, and which, so that it may be understood, he imparts by his most glorious grace to diligent readers.

Turn over this scripture, my brothers, with purity of mind and fervor of twin charity, for whoever truly loves God spends all the time he can find in sacred readings before his eyes. Let the frenzied conduct, so to speak, of carnal lovers move you to the wholesome study of the scriptures: when they receive letters and tokens from their mistresses, they do not cease to read them; they read them again and again, kiss them, and, as a greater sign of love, hang them either from the neck or over the breast, so that the dear memory of the one they desire may never depart from their mind. Consider, brothers, the madness of carnal lovers, and blush at your own sluggishness in the love of God.

Which of you is affected toward the holy scriptures of God with as great a love of heart as a blind lover is toward the love letters, foul and most empty, of one little prostitute? Mad with love for his infamous beloved, he follows with repeated kisses the letters and little gifts sent because of the absence of the one he desires. Out of excessive love he examines every single stroke again and again; in everything he does he always thinks of the absent woman; in his love he hastens to come into her sight. O if we, who are called Christian monks, who serve as soldiers for the supreme and immortal king, Christ Jesus, whom we are commanded to love above all things and are ordered to love nothing in this world except him, if, I say, we pursued our Creator with as great a love as carnally minded men cherish and love their corruptible and mortal mistresses!

Holy scripture, when read, makes known to us what God says, reveals his will to us, instructs us what we ought to do, and lays open what we must avoid. Through these writings the Almighty speaks with us, and, invisible, consoles us by informing us with his teaching, as the holy apostle says: Whatever things were written were written for our instruction, so that through patience and the consolation of the scriptures we might have hope. Hence the true soldier of Christ, and especially the cloistered monk, who loves God with his whole heart and strives to please him alone, embraces the divine scriptures, and above all the holy Gospel, with affection, and is continually occupied in meditating on its reading. But whoever neglects the readings of the divine scriptures plainly shows that he is not a true lover of God. For scripture itself says: He who turns away his ear so that he does not hear the laws, even his prayer will be detestable. Blessed, however, are those who keep the testimonies of the Lord; with their whole heart they seek him.

Therefore, my brothers, if you truly love the Lord Jesus with your whole heart, if as true soldiers you desire to please him above all things, if finally you desire to advance toward the perfection of the monastic life, you ought always to occupy yourselves with fervent affection in the reading of the divine scriptures. How useful the reading of the scriptures is, blessed Jerome shows when he says: Love knowledge of the scriptures, and you will not love the vices of the flesh. For after God nothing in this life is taken up more sweetly, nothing is felt more pleasantly, nothing separates the mind more powerfully from love of this world, nothing so strengthens the soldier of Christ against all the temptations of adversaries, nothing so disposes and confirms a monk for every good work, as frequent reading and ardent study of the scriptures.

Indeed, if through the whole breadth of scripture we read and with studious devotion recall to memory the benefits that the Almighty has bestowed on the human race, they will provide us the greatest matter and occasion by which, burning ever more in the love of God, we may make progress. For what else does the whole sequence of the divine scriptures contain than a certain right order of the good things that God either has already bestowed upon human beings or has promised that he will yet give? Or what is there that can so strengthen us for enduring evils and render us voluntarily patient as if we read in the Gospel that the Son of God suffered innocently for us, and after that read of the victories of the holy martyrs?

For sacred scripture contains the things necessary for us to saving knowledge, and it admonishes us what, as soldiers in this pilgrimage, we ought usefully to seek, and also what we ought to avoid. Hence Saint Jerome again says: Our riches are to meditate on the law of God day and night. Let us therefore prepare strength for ourselves; let us take up perfect vigor, so that amid the darkness of errors and the confusion of night the light of the knowledge of Christ may appear, and days and nights may have a protecting light, so that, with scripture as guide, we may be able to come to the holy land.

For what is better than this learning, what sweeter than this discipline? What is sweeter than our Lord Jesus Christ, who is the true salvation and sweetness of faithful souls? Let Christ be our banquet; let sacred reading be our food and drink. For what can be the life of a monk or priest without knowledge of the scriptures? We know Christ not by sight, not by hearing, not by touch, but by reading the scriptures; and the more we advance by reading toward true knowledge, the more we delight in the holy scriptures. For what is sweeter than to know God’s will and not to be ignorant of the rewards of his promises? For, as Jerome says, the field of the divine word is most rich: it has every delight within itself, and whatever you wish is born from it, just as the Jews hand down that when the manna was eaten in the desert, it tasted in the eater’s mouth according to his will and desire.

Therefore, my brothers, read the divine scriptures more often. Through them you can plainly know the will of God and arm yourselves most strongly against the snares of the devil. For if, according to the apostle Paul, Christ is the power of God and the wisdom of God, then whoever does not know the scriptures does not know the power and wisdom of God; and how will he know Christ? Ignorance of the scriptures is ignorance of Christ; and whoever does not know Christ will not belong to his fellowship, as Saint Chrysostom says: For if anyone is ignorant, surely he too will be ignored. Moreover, the more constant a person has been in the study of the divine scriptures, the greater understanding and sweetness he draws from reading them.

But it especially befits you, brothers, to turn over the divine scriptures daily by reading and learning, since abundant time, free from occupations, is granted to you, and a place quite suitable by the rarity of those who come upon you is offered. Therefore I exhort and admonish you, if you love your salvation, always to have divine readings in your hands, so that all the time that, after the worship of prayer, is allowed you without labor may be spent on the study of sacred scripture. Let no day pass, my brothers, in which a monk serving Christ as a soldier does not read something from the sacred volumes that may make him better in his holy purpose. Rather, let him follow the counsel of Saint Jerome, who says: Let the divine scriptures always be in your hands, and let them be constantly turned over in your mind; nor should you think it enough to store God’s commandments in memory unless you also strive to fulfill them in works.

For God requires two things from us, my brothers. First, he wants us to know his most holy will; second, that once known we should do it. But we cannot know his commands if we are ignorant of the knowledge of the divine scriptures. Nor does a person do the will of God if he is ignorant of it; nor has he known it if he has not learned it. Learn, therefore, what the good and pleasing will of God is, so that you may do it; do it once known, so that you may understand it more fully. For no one understands the will of God better than the one who, once he has known it, does it out of love. And so, if you desire to become a most learned interpreter of the divine scriptures, learn to begin the study of knowledge not from words but in holy works. For then you will grasp the rich knowledge of sacred reading, if you keep your heart clean from every sin and, pierced in the love of God, constantly turn over holy scripture with compunction. For the knowledge of the divine scriptures is a high mountain on which Moses speaks with the Lord, to which the filth of vices [continues].

[segment 18]
he has bestowed on the human race throughout the whole sacred Scripture, recalling them to memory by reading with studious devotion, they will furnish us the greatest material and occasion by which, burning more deeply in the love of God, we may make progress. For what else does the whole series of the divine Scriptures contain than a certain orderly text of good things, which God has either already bestowed on human beings or promised that he would give? Or what is there that can strengthen us for the endurance of evils and make us willingly patient, as when in the Gospel we read of the innocent one suffering for us, and afterward the victories of the holy martyrs? For sacred Scripture contains the things that are necessary and saving for our sufficiency, and it warns us what, as soldiers in this pilgrimage, we ought usefully to seek and also what to avoid.

Hence blessed Jerome again says: Our riches are to meditate on the law of God day and night. Therefore let us prepare solitude for ourselves; let us take up perfect strength, so that amid the darkness of errors and the confusions of night and day the light of Christ may appear, and so that nights may have light like a powerful lamp, that by Scripture as our guide we may be able to reach the holy land. What is better than this instruction? What is sweeter than this discipline? What is more pleasant than the dominion of our Lord Jesus Christ, who is the true salvation and sweetness of faithful souls? Let Christ be our food; let sacred reading be our nourishment and drink.

For what can the life of a monk or priest be without knowledge of the Scriptures? And we come to know this not by hearing idle tales, but by reading the Scriptures; and the more we advance by reading toward true knowledge, the more we delight in the holy Scriptures. For what is sweeter than to know the will of God and not to be ignorant of the value of his promises? For, as Jerome says, the field of the divine word is most fertile: it has every delight within itself, and whatever you wish is born from it, just as the Jews hand down that when manna was eaten in the desert, it tasted in the mouth of the eater according to his will and desire.

Therefore, my brothers, read the Scriptures often; through them you can plainly know the will of God and arm yourselves most strongly against the snares of the devil. For if, according to the apostle Paul, Christ is the power of God and the wisdom of God, whoever does not know the Scriptures does not know the power and wisdom of God; and how will he know Christ? For ignorance of the Scriptures is ignorance of Christ, and whoever does not know Christ will not belong to his fellowship, as the holy Christ-lover says, because if anyone is ignorant, he will surely be ignored.

Moreover, the more constant anyone has been in the study of the divine Scriptures, the greater understanding and sweetness he receives from reading them. But it is especially fitting for you, brothers, to turn over the divine Scriptures daily by reading and meditating upon them, since abundant time is granted you free from occupations, and a place sufficiently suitable is offered, with the rarity of those who come by. Therefore I exhort and admonish you, if you love your salvation, always to have divine readings in your hands, so that all the time permitted to you without labor after the service of prayer may be spent on the study of sacred Scripture. For this reason I think no brother of mine a monk, as a soldier of Christ, unless he reads something in the sacred volumes that may make him better in his holy purpose.

Rather, let him follow the counsel of blessed Jerome, who says: Let the divine Scriptures always be in your hands; let them be continually turned over in your mind. Nor should you think it sufficient to store the commandments of God in memory, unless you strive also to fulfill them in works. For God requires two things of us, my brothers. First, he wishes us to know his most holy will; second, that when we know it we should do it. But we cannot know his commandments if we are ignorant of the knowledge of the divine Scriptures. Nor does anyone do the will of God who is ignorant of it; nor will he know it who has not learned it. Learn, therefore, what the pleasing will of God is, so that you may do it; do what you have learned, so that you may understand more fully. For no one understands the will of God better than he who, knowing it, also does it out of love.

And so, if you desire to become most learned interpreters of the divine Scriptures, show the zeal for knowledge not in words but in holy works. For then you will grasp the fruitful knowledge of sacred reading, if you have kept the world of your heart from every sin and, groaning in the love of God, pricked by holy compunction, have continually turned over Scripture. For knowledge of the divine Scriptures is a high mountain on which Moses speaks with the Lord, and a person polluted by the filth of vices is forbidden to approach it.

[segment 19]
--- Page 028 ---

On the reading and study of the divine Scriptures.

A man polluted by the filth of vices is forbidden to it. Thus, indeed, it is read in Exodus: “Let no one go up onto the mountain, nor let anyone touch its borders. Everyone who touches the mountain shall surely die. If a hand touches it, he shall be overwhelmed with stones. Whether it is beast or man, he shall not live.” A man touches the mountain and dies whenever someone, swollen with secular learning, takes offense at the simplicity of the divine Scriptures. A beast touches the mountain and is buried with stones whenever a carnal man, sunk in the frenzy of the passions and not purified in soul, advances to the study of sacred Scripture.

Therefore, if you wish to cultivate the readings of the divine Scriptures to your salvation, if by reading you desire to come to true knowledge, take care first to put away every swelling of pride and rashness of presumption; and then cleanse the chamber of your heart from every uncleanness of carnal pleasures. For God, as canonical Scripture says, resists the proud but gives grace to the humble. No one proud, puffed up, arrogant, and rash, no one carnal in soul, lustful or restless, is considered fit for the study of sacred Scripture, but only one humble in divine matters, devout in mind, meek, modest, chaste, and tranquil in body and mind.

Hence Prosper says in an epigram:

The commands of God cannot be kept unless the mind is at rest.
For religious study to be carried on, the quarrels of the malicious must be cut away.
Those who are at peace can write and read the law,
if their hearts, free from the tumult of the world, are at leisure.
Thus the mind, intent on pious studies, finds its battles;
divine love conquers carnal love.
By sacred knowledge this temple of the Lord grows,
and silently a house is built that will not fall.

Therefore proceed to the study of the Scriptures with devoted minds and with a heart pierced in the love of God and humbled in true contrition, because the Lord will not enlighten you unless he has first made you clean from every iniquity. For, as Scripture itself says, perverse thoughts separate from God, and tested virtue corrects the foolish, since wisdom will not enter a malicious soul, nor dwell in a body subject to sins; for the Holy Spirit of discipline will flee deceit and will withdraw from thoughts that are without understanding. And so we must often pray to the Lord with holy David: “Create a clean heart in me, O God, and renew a right spirit within my inward parts.” For no one reads and understands the sacred Scriptures of God who does not turn to the Lord with his whole heart through repentance. And so, if you wish both to retain the divine utterances and to understand them to your salvation, take the greatest care first to keep yourselves, as far as you can, clean from every pollution of sin.

Do not let the mad folly of certain monks seduce you, who try to excuse their ignorance with a fine proverb, saying: “Why do we need knowledge of the Scriptures, since we have no office of preaching? Let us rather embrace the counsel of Wisdom, which says in the book of Proverbs: ‘He who walks simply walks confidently.’ It is enough for us to live simply, since the servant who knew the will of his lord and did not do it will be beaten with many blows, but he who did not know and did things worthy of blows will be beaten with few.”

O foolish and utterly ruined men, who despise saving knowledge and love ignorance of the divine Scriptures more than understanding. Will chosen ignorance make you liable to fewer blows? Or will you, when you sin before God, be innocent for this reason, that you are willingly ignorant of the commandments of God? You will have to be tormented with a double punishment: one, because you despise learning the commandments of God; the other, because you do not keep his word. What you plead is not simplicity, but impiety hidden under that name. And since, according to the prophet, “blessed are those who keep the testimonies of the Lord,” who can doubt that those are accursed who neither seek God nor attend to his Scriptures, but rather, with hardened mind, despise them?

Hear what Solomon says to you in Proverbs: “Where the soul is without knowledge, it is not good,” and “the foolishness of a man trips up his steps.” Of you also, O most foolish dwellers in darkness, that saying of Saint Jerome is to be understood: ignorance is no longer excusable where one knows what it is that one does not know. But you must take greater care lest you at last be counted with those who said to God: “Depart from us; we do not want the knowledge of your ways.”

You understand Solomon’s saying badly if you think that “simply” is said of ignorance. He who walks simply, without any deception of his neighbor, walks confidently in keeping the commandments of God; but he who corrupts his ways will not be able to lie hidden, but will be made manifest. Hence again he says: “The lips of the just instruct many.”

[segment 20]
--- Page 029 ---

Homily III. Fol. XI.

...words; but those who are unlearned will die in poverty of heart. What is more pitiable than an unlearned priest, who, even if he does not have the office of preaching, is nevertheless bound by reason of the order he has received to knowledge of the Scriptures?

For God wills every person to fulfill his holy will; no one who is ignorant of it can do this. First, therefore, the Lord commands that you learn what is pleasing to him; second, that you do it. How, then, will you think ignorance of the law excusable, through which you have first become transgressors of the will of God?

Why do you think almighty God handed over to human beings the volumes of the Scriptures? Was it so that they might be read and understood by them, or so that people might despise reading them? Surely the Scriptures of God’s commandments were given to mortals first to be read, and afterward to be fulfilled in deed. But you, who with deliberate mind wish to be ignorant of the Scriptures: how can you do his will, which they disclose? Or how will you be able to be doers of the law, when you have not first been hearers? For it can happen that someone who knows the will of God either observes it or neglects it; but it cannot happen that someone who is ignorant of it fulfills it. Indeed, someone will more easily fail to do the good he knows than carry out in deed what he does not know. No one does the good he does not know.

Saint Augustine says of the ignorant in a certain place: Not everyone who is ignorant is free from fault. That ignorant person can be excused from punishment who did not find what he might learn. But those cannot be forgiven who, having persons from whom they might learn, did not apply themselves to instruction.

Saint Leo the pope also says this: If ignorance seems intolerable in laymen, how much more in those who are placed over others; nor is it worthy of excuse or pardon.

Therefore, brothers, ignorance of the divine Scriptures does not make you excused before God, but doubly guilty, since you wish to be wholly ignorant of the will of God so that it need not be done by you. For a person ought not be ignorant of the divine will, since he is placed in the world for this: that through illumination of understanding and purity of affection he may merit the eternal enjoyment of God’s vision. And so knowledge of God, and also of oneself, is necessary for every mortal unto salvation; no one will find it without knowledge of the Scriptures. For just as from knowledge of oneself there comes to human beings fear of God, and from knowledge of him love is born, so from ignorance of oneself comes pride, and from neglected knowledge of God come contempt and despair of salvation.

Nothing is more unhappy than an unlearned monk who either neglects or despises the study of the holy Scriptures, because he can never stand pure within himself in true tranquility of heart, but is compelled by his own restlessness, contrary to the integrity of monastic life, to occupy his mind with outward and worldly things. We see among you some who are ignorant of the Scriptures, unstable in mind, undisciplined and wandering; their ignorance is the more impure, the less they are inclined toward discipline. It is shameful indeed not to know what one is ordered to know; more shameful not to learn; but most shameful of all to despise it.

And there are among you some who do not know saving knowledge; there are also some who neglect to learn. What if I add that there are still others who utterly despise the knowledge of the divine Scriptures? And yet I am not lying: you know that what I say is true. For some of you have bitterly opposed my studies, even to the point of envy, quibbling that they would rather have a plowman than an orator as their superior. Have they not also tried to draw the visitators over to their opinion, wishing to lead me [unclear] into renunciation of the Scriptures, so that they might make me a little rustic and a keeper of pigs rather than of souls? But those who hinder the divine [unclear] strive in vain; at God’s urging I have resolved, as long as I live, to set all the honors and riches of the world after the study of the Scriptures.

I am your abbot for as long as he who called me wills it. If one of the two must be done, I would rather give up the abbacy than renounce the holy study of the Scriptures. For I know truly that nothing more wholesome can happen in this life to a cloistered man than if he begins truly to love the divine Scriptures. Whoever among you wishes to follow this instruction of mine will have me alike as guide and companion on the way. But whoever despises following me as I learn will nevertheless be compelled, even unwillingly, to hear me as I teach.

I have said these things against those who, savoring earthly and perishable things, cast away from themselves the fear of the Lord; who, ignorant of almost everything, despise learning. I know the face of my flock, and I believe I am not wholly ignorant of the several inclinations of your hearts. Therefore I will not cease to exhort you to the holy studies of the Scriptures, knowing that a lover of sacred reading cannot be a worker of carnal vices.

[segment 21]
On the daily renewal of holy purpose.

...the more studious a monk has been in learning the Scriptures, the less he is inflamed by the ardors of carnal pleasures. For although, according to the true opinion of Saint Jerome, a monk seems to have the office not of teaching but rather of lamenting, nevertheless, since he professes the military service of Christ, it is fitting, indeed necessary, that he know the well-pleasing will of almighty God, in whose words and arms he has sworn allegiance. But just as a soldier cannot know the will of his lord, who is far absent, except through messengers and letters, so a monk will not understand the commands of the eternal King except through sacred readings and sermons, the evangelical Scriptures.

The reading of the Scriptures opens the gate of salvation to those who do not know it; it leads the diligent reader by the straight path to the knowledge of God; it makes the lover of itself despise all worldly things and love nothing other than true wisdom. Nothing, however, is more shameful than for a monk in spiritual life to be unwilling to advance, and knowingly to wish to be ignorant of the wills of God. Let no one flatter himself on account of his weakness or ignorance, since he who is ignorant will be ignored, and he who hates saving knowledge is deservedly condemned.

When the Lord was about to send out his preachers into all lands, he admonished them beforehand, saying: “Be prudent as serpents and simple as doves,” so that through ignorance you should neither be craftily deceived by anyone nor wrongfully lay a snare for anyone by fraud. For there is little difference in fault between being able to deceive and being deceived. Therefore I beg you, dearest brothers, to attend to the Rule of our holy father Benedict, which admonishes you in very many places to sweat more frequently over the readings of the divine Scriptures. For in the Scriptures we are taught and instructed for all piety, how in this most wretched pilgrimage of our sojourning we ought to live, so that we may be able to please the Lord our God above all things.

For it belongs to monks to despise the world and the filthy pleasures of the present life for the love of God; usefully to restrain the unlawful habits of the flesh; to devote themselves also to prayer, reading, and meditation on the Scriptures; and continually to lift the mind from earthly things to heavenly things. Blessed is that monk who has not gone away in the counsel of the wicked, and has not stood in the way of sinners, and has not sat in the chair of pestilential men, but meditates on the law of the Lord day and night. He is shown wisdom in the Scriptures of God, the fruit of which, after the end of this pilgrimage, he will receive as eternal life.

On the Daily Renewal of Holy Purpose. Homily Five.

We have heard, dearest brothers, the reading just recited to us from the book of holy Job, who laments the misery of the human race and the calamity of this life, and at the same time had said: “Man born of woman, living for a short time, is filled with many miseries; he comes forth like a flower and is crushed, and flees like a shadow, and never remains in the same state.” “Behold,” blessed Job says, “my years pass by, and I walk a path by which I shall not return.” “All flesh will fail together, and man will return to dust.”

What do you observe among these things, brothers, or what do you think? Is there any man among us who does not know that he is mortal, who can truthfully say that he will remain forever? God, my brothers, how blind we are, while holding our eyes open; how forgetful and sluggish travelers we are, who certainly discern and know that we are going to die, and yet we trust in the vanity of the present life as though we were not going to become mortal. But who is the man who will live and not see death, and who will rescue his soul from the hand of hell? Behold, Scripture says: “We are all dying, and like water we flow away upon the earth.”

Therefore remember, my brothers, that death does not delay, and all that passes by is short when reckoned; for, as the holy lover of Christ Paul says, “It is appointed for human beings to die once,” and no one will be able to flee or escape beneath this common law of necessity. O if only we set daily before the gaze of the mind that hour of coming death, which each one of us must necessarily undergo shortly, and arranged our life and conduct in such a way that we might always be found ready, how happy and secure our departure could become. The judge of the living and the dead, Christ himself our Lord, admonished us in the holy Gospel always to be ready for death, saying: “Therefore you also must be ready, because at the hour when you do not think it, the Son of Man will come.”

How much frequent meditation on death avails for purity of mind, the wise man in Ecclesiasticus admonished beforehand, saying: “In all your works remember your last things, and you will not sin forever.” The thought of future death induces fears; fear in turn necessarily subdues the mind, and like a nail driven by a hammer fastens the proud movements of the flesh to the wood of the cross. In consideration of this view many of the pagan philosophers used to say that the whole life of a wise man is a certain continual meditation on death.

Hence Saint Jerome says: “Nothing will benefit you as much for temperance in all things as frequent meditation on this brief and uncertain life, which fails like a leaf carried off by the wind, and when it is not feared falls into death.” Therefore keep in mind the remembrance of your death, which by no counsel can you avoid, and you will easily be able to overcome all the movements of the flesh. For the one who sometimes thinks that he is going to die despises present things and, unless he is wholly foolish, will prepare himself for future things.

For I know, and I confess that I have partly experienced it, that the will of the human mind cannot long remain uniform in the holy fervor of spiritual life. Therefore it has pleased me to recall the memory of death before your eyes more often, so that the mind, shaken by fear of future things, may daily be renewed in a holier purpose. The eternal human spirit, bound to the body by the exercise of itself, is slight, fluctuating, and unstable: now it is snatched toward this thing, now moved toward another.

For this reason the Wisdom of God says through Solomon in Proverbs: “My son, give me your heart, and let your eyes keep my ways.” It is as though he said: Unless you have devoted the whole love of your heart to divine Wisdom, you will never be stable and confirmed in the keeping of the Lord’s commandments. For just as the heart is the first principle of movement in a human being, the greater is its guarding for the preservation of life, since if it is struck by an assault of diseases, the whole life of the body is already in danger. Hence it is written: “With all watchfulness keep your heart, because life proceeds from it.”

By the name of the heart, however, we understand the affection, whenever it inclines toward love of some thing and cleaves to it so strongly that it involves the whole will. Hence the psalmist says: “If riches abound, do not set your heart on them,” that is, do not love them with the desire of your will. What is more changeable than the human heart, which is altered by almost every single thought and can scarcely remain in the same purpose unless it restrains itself by a certain forceful intention? Saint David, not ignorant of this, said to the Lord: “But I said in my abundance: I shall not be moved forever. Lord, in your will you gave strength to my beauty. You turned your face away from me, and I was troubled.”

Therefore, my dearest brothers, if there is anyone among you who desires to serve our Lord and Savior in true purity of heart, if any man wishes to be a monk in deed as well as in name, let him learn first of all to trust not in himself but in God, and let him take care to renew the good purpose of holy life always by a new beginning, not only daily, but, as far as he can, at every hour and at every moment. For the fervor of spiritual life, to whose practical operation the institution of cloistered life is known to be wholly ordered, cannot long remain at the same point; because if it does not always advance toward what lies ahead, it necessarily runs back toward lower things and, what is worse, toward weaker ones.

In the wrestling-ground of spiritual exercise there is no stationary and lingering place of standing still, since it is the property of the human heart by its own natural motion always either to advance toward better things or to fall back again into what is evil. Hence if you do not advance in spiritual life, you necessarily fail; if you do not go forward toward holier things, you certainly go backward. Since experience has made this clear to us, I do not cease to admonish your charities to stretch out your desire in good life always toward what lies further ahead, lest you dangerously incur the peril of falling away there, where you did not know that the opportunity of becoming perfect lay open.

The fervor of spiritual purpose must be directed toward the love of God, and it cannot otherwise be preserved except in him. For the love of God is not absurdly compared to fire: just as fire, when the fuel of wood is withdrawn, is necessarily extinguished, so the love of God, if continual exercises of good works are lacking, is endangered as it dies away. Hence there is that saying of the Savior in the Gospel: “I came to cast fire upon the earth, and what do I wish except that it burn?” O monk, happy is that conscience in whose breast nothing burns except the love of Christ, which loves him above all things and despises all the studies of vanity. Now consider, brothers, your consciences with the greatest care, and examine exactly whether you love God above all things.

*Homily V.*

*Fol. XII.*

...for purity of mind, Wisdom admonishes us in Ecclesiasticus by frequent meditation on death, saying: “In all your works remember your last things, and you will never sin.” The thought of future death brings on fear; fear necessarily shakes the mind and, like a nail driven by a hammer, fastens all the motions of the flesh to the wood of the cross. In view of this consideration, many of the pagan philosophers used to say that the whole life of a wise man is a kind of continual meditation on death. Hence Saint Jerome also says: “Nothing profits you so much for temperance in all things as frequent meditation on this brief and uncertain life,” which, like a leaf snatched away by the wind, falls into death when it is not feared.

Therefore remember your death, which you cannot escape by any counsel, and you will easily be able to overcome all the motions of the flesh. For whoever thinks every day that he is going to die despises present things and wholly prepares himself in mind for future things. For I know, and in part confess that I have learned by experience, that the human mind and will cannot long remain in one state within the holy fervor of the spiritual life; and therefore I have resolved often to renew the memory of death before your eyes, so that, while the mind is struck by fear of future things, it may daily be renewed in the holy purpose.

For the human soul, bound to the body by its own exercises, is exceedingly fluid and unstable: now it is carried off to this thing, now it is moved to another. Therefore the Wisdom of God says through Solomon in Proverbs: “My son, give me your heart, and let your eyes keep my ways.” It is as though he were saying: If you have handed over the whole love of your heart to divine wisdom, you will never be unstable or weakened in keeping the commandments of the Lord. For just as the heart is the first principle of motion in man, so the guarding of it is most important for the preservation of life; for if it is struck by an attack of diseases, the whole life of the body is already endangered. Hence it is written: “With all watchfulness keep your heart, because life proceeds from it.”

By the name of the heart, however, we understand the affection by which anyone inclines toward love of some thing, clinging to it so much that it entangles the whole will. Hence the Psalmist says: “If riches abound, do not set your heart upon them,” that is, do not love them by the desire of the will. Nothing is more changeable than the human heart: it is altered by almost every single thought, and it can scarcely remain in the same purpose unless it restrains itself by a certain forceful intention. Saint David, not ignorant of this, said to the Lord: “But I said in my abundance: I shall not be moved forever. Lord, in your will you gave strength to my beauty. You turned your face away from me, and I was troubled.”

Therefore, my dearest brothers, whoever among you desires to serve our Lord and Savior in true purity of heart, whoever wishes to be a monk both in deed and in name, let him learn first to take thought not for himself but for God; and let him take care always, by a fresh beginning, to renew the good purpose of holy conduct, not only daily, but as far as he can even at every hour and every moment. For the fervor of spiritual conduct, to the practical exercise of which the whole institution of the cloistered life is known to be ordered, cannot long remain at the same point; for if it does not always advance toward what lies ahead, it necessarily falls back to what is lower and, what is worse, weaker.

In the arena of spiritual exercise there is no fixed standing still without motion, because it is the property of the human heart, by its natural movement, always either to advance successfully toward better things or to fall back toward what is evil. Therefore, if you do not make progress in spiritual conduct, you necessarily fail; if you do not advance toward holier things, you certainly go backward. Since experience has made this clear to us, I do not cease to admonish your charity that in good conduct your desire should always be stretched out toward what is further, lest you fall into the destructive danger of failing precisely where you have not renewed your longing to advance.

The fervor of the spiritual purpose is directed toward love, and it is preserved only in love. For the love of God is not absurdly compared to fire: just as fire, when the fuel of wood is taken away, is necessarily extinguished, so the love of God is endangered if it lacks the continual nourishment of good works and the memory of eternal things. Hence that saying of the Savior in the Gospel: “I have come to send fire upon the earth, and what do I wish except that it burn?” O inexpressibly happy is that conscience in whose breast nothing burns except the love of God, which loves God above all things and despises all pursuits of vanity. And consider, my brothers, most diligently, your consciences, and [unclear] whether we love God above all things.

[b iiij]

[segment 23]
--- Page 032 ---

On the daily renewal of holy purpose.

If the fire of divine love is now burning in you, apply the necessary fuel, lest through your lukewarmness the little sparks of holy fervor should ever be felt to have been wholly extinguished. Believe me, brothers, for experience has taught me this: you will more easily preserve in yourselves the burning fire of divine fervor by exercise than kindle it again after it has been extinguished.

Therefore whoever has now received love into himself, whoever has received heavenly charity in his heart, should take the greatest care not to fall back into the negligences of his former life. Rather, he should apply the necessary fuel of spiritual exercises, so that, as he makes progress, he may preserve the fervor of God within himself. Let him beware not only of great sins but also of small negligences, and, as far as he can, of the smallest ones; for whoever despises small things falls little by little, and whoever does not always fear danger will soon suffer loss in spiritual fervor and purpose. Scripture says: “Unless you hold yourself steadily in the fear of the Lord, your house will quickly be overthrown.” Blessed, then, is the man who is always fearful, who does not presume upon the virtues he has received, who judges for certain that he is failing whenever he has not understood himself to have advanced at every moment toward higher things.

But truly, brothers, I also greatly fear, unless the appearance of certain works deceives me, that the fire of holy divine fervor is not present in you, since you seem to care little for the true pursuits of monks. Your conduct confirms this, as none of you can fail to know. Yet it remains for me to admonish those who seem to have made too little progress thus far, since the hand of the Lord has not been shortened so that it cannot save penitents. I trust in the mercy of the living God that, because of my charity toward you and the exercises of piety, he will at some time grant those among you who do not walk according to the purity of the rule to come to their senses and to love more ardently the pursuits of holy fervor.

What is this, brothers, that you do not live monastically in the monastery, and, though you have professed a spiritual manner of life, you walk according to the flesh? Do you not know how dangerous it is for a monk to live in a worldly way in a cenobium, and to violate by lukewarm conduct the vow of holy purity which we have all made? Know, I beg you, that the last things of all cloistered persons lie near the extremes: the good and zealous lovers of their own salvation receive in heaven the rewards not of ordinary men but of saints; and those who conduct themselves badly or lukewarmly in the monastic religious life are punished not with a moderate or common torment, but with the most grievous torment of demons. The higher the state in which someone sins, the heavier and more serious will be the punishment of the offender in it.

Therefore, if anyone among you has already learned to walk the way of divine love with fervor of spirit, he must go forward, so that he may make greater progress each day. But whoever still lies in the disgrace of sheer sloth should strive to rise again, since just as punishment is promised to those who lie fallen, so the crown of victory is denied to those who do not persevere. Beware, then, lest destructive lukewarmness overthrow the mind. It is exceedingly dangerous for cloistered persons, because it is most troublesome and full of sorrows, and plainly very near to hell. From its abyss a monk who has once fallen into it rarely rises again.

And alas, how many fainthearted cloistered persons are seen today, slack and idle, who frequently collapse under the burden of spiritual warfare and rarely rise again to the true fervor of spirit. Their condition is truly pitiable: they have the name of being alive, and yet they are dead. These are the ones whom neither the memory of death could bring to true compunction of heart, nor the recollection of Gehenna set before them could soften. As for monks of this kind, afterward, when they have remained thus sluggish in acedia, they are either overcome by bad habit and collapse, or they are in a certain sense in hell, so that they never fully come back to the light of the Lord’s mercies, nor reach the freedom of spirit which alone is nearest to the kingdom of heaven and makes the Lord’s yoke sweet and his burden light.

These evils befall them because their will has not yet been purified from carnal passions, nor do they will the good as they know it, being enticed and drawn away by their own desire. For they love the corrupt consolations of the flesh; and even if they sometimes interrupt them, they never entirely break them off. Hence it is that they very rarely direct their affections toward God; and if at times they are moved to compunction for an hour, they do not persevere long. Instead, wearied in spirit by laziness and inertia, they suddenly abandon, out of sadness and melancholy, the good they had begun. A mind tossed by these storms cannot be filled with the fervor of divine love.

[segment 24]
--- Page 033 ---

On the Daily Renewal of the Holy Purpose.

As rust consumes iron, and a garment is gnawed by worms, so idleness weakens the mind; and virtue of the soul, long interrupted, gradually flows away and is utterly uprooted. Therefore flee idleness, and overcome sloth.

But since we cannot remain firmly and without change of mind in one state of divine love, it is necessary for us, lest we entirely fall away, to renew the holy purpose every day. Thus, as often as through the negligence of human weakness we begin, however slightly, to grow torpid from spiritual fervor, so often, examining ourselves by the memory of future things and by contemplation of the Lord’s Passion, we should immediately recall our conscience to its former purpose of advancing.

But we must take the greatest care not to defer this holy effort of advancement until tomorrow, because it has often been harmful to put off a good that could have been done. Hence the wise man says in Ecclesiasticus: “Do not delay to be converted to the Lord, and do not put it off from day to day. For his wrath will come suddenly, and in the time of vengeance he will destroy you.” Augustine also says: “Why do you defer the good until tomorrow, saying, ‘Life will still be long’? For who knows whether it will be long? And if it is not good, what profit is there that it will be long?”

Without a good life, the conduct of a monk should be called death rather than life. Therefore the remedies of a more corrected life must not be deferred by any delays, lest the time of conversion perish through tardiness, when no delay for repentance is any longer granted. For he who promised pardon to the penitent did not promise tomorrow. No one who fears God delays being converted to repentance, nor extends today’s salvation into tomorrow.

But every person who knows that he is established in lukewarmness of mind, and who defers amendment of life from day to day, is deceived by hope of a longer life; for if he knew he would die tomorrow, he would without doubt change his purpose today. Hope of a longer life has miserably deceived many who, while they daily put off the amendment of their way of life, suddenly fall into the depth of death.

Who among you, brothers, does not resolve in his mind to assume the purpose of a more amended life before he dies? For I believe that each one of you intends to exercise still greater devotion toward God before he falls into the danger of death. And why do you wait so long for that day? Why do you defer from day to day? Behold, you who now live in health and do not give yourselves to the spiritual exercises of divine love when you are able, while the Lord patiently waits: the last day will come for each one of you, and it is already hastening, on which, when the angel of death presses hard, the power whose effect you have delayed so long is taken away from you.

Therefore again and again I ask you, brothers, and with all the diligence I can I admonish you: turn to the Lord our God with your whole heart while you live, sound and unharmed, and do not further defer your will, lest you lose even the power granted to you. Think and often think again that we are not citizens of this world, but strangers and pilgrims; nor do we have here an abiding city, but we seek the one to come, and we hasten daily toward death, even without knowing it.

On the Love of Quiet and Solitude.

Homily VI.

Holy Scripture does not plainly reveal how many intellects were created from the beginning. But it is known from the Fathers that the work of the angels is the work of God, and it is counted among the works of the sixth day. There are, however, some who write that the angels were created long before this visible world; and just as we cannot prove this by the testimony of the divine Scriptures, so neither do we rashly attack it. For there are many weighty and holy men who have placed the creation of the angels long before the beginning of the world.

But the angelic intellect was made from the very beginning in the act of all knowable things, and it knew all that it could know; afterward it admitted nothing toward its perfection. For, created by the Lord in its perfection, it required no increase by which it might understand more. Since, without any intermediary, it is joined to God, it was made a pure intellect by nature, nor does it need exercise for understanding after our manner, since its being admits no corruption. And so, adhering to its object through the affection of love, it immediately and inwardly loves God, whom it understands to be the center of its blessedness.

[segment 25]
with full movement he contemplates and loves it, and he is blessed and fears no alteration of himself. But the human intellect, which our teachers have called the soul, was indeed created at the beginning in complete ignorance, and knows none of those things which the angelic intellect understands by nature.

These two intellects differ as act and potency, light and darkness, knowledge and ignorance, habit and privation, beginning and end, simple and composite, substance and accident, nature and art, truth and likeness. For all things, as divine Scripture testifies, which belong to the angelic intellect by nature and from the beginning, the human intellect will attain in the end through arts, as the Lord Jesus says in the holy Gospel: “In the resurrection of the dead they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but will be like the angels of God in heaven.”

For the mind has been placed for a short time in the workshop of the body, so that it may be trained toward understanding through many labors, as though through arts. If there, turned toward God, it has duly completed its craft through love, then at last, when it departs from here, it becomes equal to an angel. But if, consenting to the motions of the flesh, it has not kept its proper order, then, disordered and wretched, like the fallen apostate spirits, it will leave the body to be tormented forever.

For since our mind or soul was created in ignorance and privation of all things, it must come to knowledge of things by continual action, movement, and art. The end, indeed, makes the human intellect equal to the angelic by changing its potency into act; it will make from privation a habit, from emptiness fullness, from imperfection perfection, from one deprived one possessing, from darkness light, from night day, from lunar solar, and from earthly heavenly.

This end, my dearest brothers, is the beginning of eternal salvation. No one can happily come to it who does not strive with all his powers toward the summit of perfection. But since it is difficult, amid crowds where a thousand occasions for sin are offered, always to keep unbroken this necessary striving for progress, the holy fathers, founders of the monastic order, taught us by word and example to flee the world and to avoid its crowd not only in mind but also in body.

Of those who were stronger in spirit, many hastened into the desert, so that they might become nearer to the Lord in mind the farther they withdrew in body from the world. Of them we read the words of the prophet Isaiah: “The wilderness will rejoice and blossom like the lily; it will bud forth abundantly, and will rejoice with gladness and praise.” Others, wishing to benefit many, established cenobia, in which they placed a multitude of monks, whose way of life would be separated from the peoples and nevertheless would be shared socially with one another.

For it is not fitting for everyone to sit in the desert, when for some it is more suitable to serve the Lord in the midst of brothers. For even our monasteries have their own solitude, so that whoever wishes to be solitary can do so in the midst of brothers, provided he keeps the privacy of the cell in which he dwells. For a monk, according to the interpretation of his name, is called solitary, because in mind and body he is separated from the many things of this world.

Therefore, as I am about to deliver today’s sermon to your charities, I wish to admonish you, since I desire your progress in spiritual life. I ask you, then, and exhort you in the bowels of brotherly charity, to love the quiet of the cell as the workshop of your salvation, in which there is great peace for those who love God, and great advantage for perfecting the intellect toward knowledge of the highest truth. For the prophet Isaiah says: “In silence and hope will be your strength.”

Whoever does not love the solitude of the cell and the quiet of silence by no means loves purity of mind; rather, wandering in body, he always drinks in new vanities in his soul. Hence blessed Jerome says: “Why do we desire the crowdedness of cities, we who are reckoned by our singularity? For to me a town is a prison; solitude is paradise.”

If the desert and solitude delighted us, brothers, this place would offer great convenience for our purpose, where, apart from poor peasants, a guest rarely appears. No one disturbs our quiet, no one troubles us, no one introduces spectacles or rumors of momentary novelty. But I see few among you who are lovers of inward quiet, and I grieve deeply, because I know very well that a monk cannot make progress in spiritual life if he is known not to love the quiet of the cell.

But one who knows how much good the privacy of solitude brings to the soul loves his cell and does not readily go outside, always fearing lest in tumult

[segment 26]
immutably contemplates and loves him in whom it is blessed, and undergoes no alteration of its own. But the human intellect, which our preacher has called the hand of our soul, was indeed created in all ignorance from the beginning: it knows nothing coevally which the angelic intellect understands by nature. Those two intellects differ as act and potency, light and darkness, knowledge and ignorance, habit and privation, beginning and end, simple and composite, substance and accident, nature and art, truth and likeness. For, as all divine Scripture bears witness, all the things that are present in the angelic intellect by nature from the beginning, the human intellect will attain through art at the end, as the Lord Jesus says in the holy Gospel: “In the resurrection of the dead they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but they will be like the angels of God in heaven.”

For as the mind is exercised toward understanding through many labors, as though through art, it has been placed for this time in the workshop of the body. If, turned toward God, it has duly completed its craft through love, then at last, departing from here, it becomes equal to an angel. But if, by the habits of the flesh and conscience, it has not kept its own order, it will depart from the body, like the troubled and wretched spirits, to be tormented forever. For since our mind, or soul, was created in ignorance and privation of all things, it must come to the art and knowledge of things by the movement of continual action. The end, indeed, makes the human intellect equal to the angelic, changing its potency into act; and from privation it makes habit, from empty full, from imperfect perfect, from deprived possessing, from dark luminous, from nocturnal diurnal, from lunar solar, from earthly heavenly.

This end, my dearly beloved brothers, is eternal salvation from its beginnings; no one can happily come to it who does not strive with all his strength toward the summit of perfection. But since in this troubled middle state it is difficult always to keep unbroken this necessary effort of making progress, where a thousand occasions for sinning are offered, the holy fathers, founders of the monastic order, taught us by word and example to flee the world, and often to withdraw from it not only in mind but also in body. And to those who were stronger in spirit they entrusted many things for the desert, so that they might become nearer to the Lord in mind the farther they withdrew from the world in body. Of these we read in the prophecy of Isaiah: “The wilderness will rejoice and blossom like the lily; budding, it will bud forth and exult, rejoicing and praising.” Others, wishing to be of use to many weaker men, founded cenobia, in which they placed a multitude of monks, so that their way of life would be separated from the people, yet they would nevertheless have fellowship with one another.

For it is not fitting for everyone to sit in the desert, for those for whom it is more suitable to serve the Lord in the midst of the brothers. For our monasteries have their own solitudes; and whoever wishes to be solitary can be so in the midst of the brothers, provided he has remained continually in the cell in which he dwells. Indeed a monk, according to the interpretation of his name, is called solitary because he is separated in mind and body from the tumult of this world. Therefore, since today’s sermon is addressed to your charity, I admonish you with fatherly concern, and I ask that I not have spoken these things in vain, since I seek nothing other than your good, and desire nothing more than your progress in spiritual conduct. I ask you, I say, and exhort you in the bowels of brotherly charity, to love the quiet of the cell as the workshop of your salvation. In it much is ready for those who love God, and there is great advantage for bringing the intellect to perfection in the knowledge of the highest truth. For the prophet Isaiah says: “In silence and in hope will be your strength.”

Whoever does not love the solitude of the cell and the quiet of silence in no way loves purity of mind; but, wandering in body, he always draws in new vanities in his soul. Hence Jerome says: “Why do we desire the bustle of the city, we who are counted by our singularity? For to me the town is a prison; solitude is paradise.” If, brothers, the desert and solitude were pleasing to us, that place would offer great advantage to our purpose, where, apart from poor peasants, a guest rarely appears; no one disturbs our quiet, no one troubles us, no one brings in spectacles or empty rumors to disturb monastic renewal. But among you I see few lovers of inward quiet, and I grieve deeply, because I know very well that a monk cannot persevere in equal conduct if it is clear that he does not love the quiet of the cell. But one who knows how much good the deeds of solitude confer upon the soul loves his cell and does not readily go outside, always fearing amid the tumult to lose what he has gathered with great labor in secret.

[segment 27]
--- Page 036 ---

...the more one is accustomed to vary one’s study and to use alternate exercises against weariness. Hence Jerome again says: “Let prayer frequently interrupt reading, and let a pleasing alternation of holy action come to the soul that is continually intent.” A monk who does not love the privacy of the cell is not a true soldier of Christ, because he bears the name and habit of a monk without the works; and he who ought to have been solitary for the love of God has turned back in mind to the world and loves the pursuit of vanity. Hence the divine Pope Gregory says: “He who lives bodily removed from human beings, but plunges himself in thought into the tumults of human dealings through desire for earthly things, is not solitary, although he dwells in solitude. But if someone is bodily pressed by crowds of people, and yet suffers no tumults of worldly cares in his heart, he is not in the city, even if he lives in the midst of human beings.”

And this is what holy Jerome says against Jovinian: “The wise man,” he says, “can never be alone. For he has with him all who are and have been good; and with a free mind he brings forth whatever he wishes and transports himself wherever he wishes. What he cannot do in body, he embraces in mind; and if there is a lack of human beings, he speaks with God. Therefore a good man will never be alone.” And so, while remaining in your cell, always beware idleness, because nothing so enervates all the powers of the mind as idleness, after it has produced acedia from itself. If you love God ardently, you will never be idle, because true love of Christ does not allow sluggishness to arise in the soul, but always impels the lover toward the pleasure of the beloved friend. And just as a certain carnally minded person truly said, “Let him who does not wish to become idle love,” so experience has taught us in spiritual love that it does not permit the mind which it has begun to inhabit to grow sluggish with idleness and laziness. For divine love always impels one toward better things; and the more it advances by burning, the more it takes on increase of itself.

The workshop of this love is a clean conscience. If a monk desires to have it and to keep it clean, it is necessary first of all that he despise the vanities of the world and the business of temporal things. For it is not possible for twin loves to reside in one breast, nor can a person prevail in loving Christ who is unwilling to despise the world. For God, who in the Trinity of persons is one in the essence of substance, is either loved by us alone, or is not loved with a partner. Therefore, so that the conscience may remain clean in all purity, and so that the mind may be able to love God above all things, let the monk love the privacy of the cell and never withdraw from the exercise of inner sweetness. For in the cell of a good and wise monk there is the greatest tranquility; and if there is any earthly paradise in a monastery, it belongs to those who live according to the purity of the cenobitic institution. There are peace, piety, goodness, light, virtue, and honor. There are joy, purity, sweetness, and great tranquility of life, where the devout monk at prayer speaks with the Lord and the sweetness of the future heavenly kingdom is foretasted by the contemplative soldier.

O cell of monks truly serving God, paradise: you are surely the little garden of most honorable pleasure, in which the mind is joined to God through love and sweetly lifted up through desire. The monk who loves, venerates, and cultivates you is a true soldier of Christ and serves the Lord in the fullness of virtues. But he who abhors and deserts the privacy of the cell despises the workshop of his own salvation. Therefore, my dearest brothers, I beg you to begin to inhabit the privacy of the cell with desire of mind; without love for it no one will be able to attain the perfection of virtues in the cenobium.

On the Manual Labor of Monks. Homily VII.

“Because you will eat the labors of your hands,” says the prophet of the Lord, “blessed are you, and it will be well with you.” If all who live from the labor of their own hands are counted blessed, surely no one eats bread idly without guilt and crime. For almighty God imposed this penalty on Adam, the first-formed, for the transgression of the original commandment, declaring to him that in the sweat of his face he would obtain food from the earth by working with his hands. And since in Adam we have all sinned, we are necessarily subject to the same penalties to which he is known to have been subjected. He labored with his hands for the food by which he might live, and he did not eat bread idly. Hence our divine father [unclear]

[segment 28]
Homily VI. Fol. XV.

...the more he is accustomed to vary his pursuits and to use alternation against weariness. Hence blessed Jerome also says: let action often be interrupted by prayer; then, in turn, after prayer has been made, let a pleasing alternation come to the soul. A monk who does not love the secrecy of the cell is not a true soldier of Christ, since he bears the name and habit of a monk without the works; and he who ought to have been solitary for the love of God, having in a sense returned to the world, loves the pursuit of vanity. Hence blessed Pope Gregory says: He who lives bodily removed from human beings, but through the disturbances of human intercourse plunges himself in thought into earthly desire, is not solitary, although he dwells in solitude. But if someone is bodily pressed by crowds of people, and yet suffers no disturbances of secular cares in his heart, he is not in the city, even if he lives in the midst of human beings.

And this is what Jerome says against the dreams of those who sleep: The wise man, he says, can never be alone. For he has with him all who are and who have been good; he has a free mind, and whatever he wishes he follows, and he carries himself wherever he wishes. For what he cannot do with the body he embraces with the mind; and if he lacks human company, he speaks with God. How, then, will a good man ever be alone?

And so, remain constant in your cell; let song always be in your mouth, since nothing so weakens all the powers of the mind as idleness, after it has produced acedia. If you love God ardently, you will never be idle, because the true love of Christ does not allow sluggishness to arise in the soul, but always drives the lover toward the will of the beloved. And just as a certain carnal-minded man truly said, “Let him love who does not wish to become idle,” so experience has taught us in spiritual love that, once it has begun to dwell in a mind, it does not permit that mind to grow torpid through idleness and sloth. For divine love always impels one toward better things, and the more it advances by burning, the greater increase it receives.

The companion of this love is a clean conscience; and if a monk desires both to have and to preserve it clean, it is necessary first of all that he cast off the vanity of the world and the business of temporal things. For it is not possible that twin loves should reside in one breast; nor can he prevail in loving Christ well who does not wish to despise the world. For God, though in the Trinity of persons he is one in the essence of substance, is either loved by us alone, or he is not loved with a partner. Therefore, so that conscience may remain clean in all purity, and so that the mind may be able to love God above all things, let the monk love the secrecy of the cell and never withdraw from the exercise of inward sweetness.

For in the cell of a good and wise monk there is the highest tranquility; there is, as it were, an earthly paradise in the monastery for those who are converted to the purity of the cenobitic institution. There are peace, piety, goodness, justice, virtue, and honor. There are joy, purity, sweetness, and great tranquility of life, where the devout monk speaks with the Lord in prayer, and the future sweetness of the heavenly kingdom is tasted beforehand by the contemplative soldier. O cell of monks truly serving God, paradise, indeed a little garden of most honorable delight, in which the mind is joined to God through love and sweetly raised up through desire. The monk who loves, venerates, and cherishes it is truly a soldier of Christ and serves the Lord in the fullness of virtues. But he who abhors and deserts the secrecy of the cell scorns the workshop of his own salvation. Therefore I beg you, my dearest brothers, begin to dwell in the secrecy of the cell with desire of mind; without love of it no one will be able to reach perfection of virtues in the cenobium.

On the Manual Labor of Monks. Homily VII.

“You shall eat the labor of your hands,” says the prophet of the Lord; “blessed are you, and it shall be well with you.” If all who live from the labor of their own hands are judged blessed, surely no one idle eats bread without guilt and fault. For almighty God imposed this upon Adam the first-formed as a penalty for the transgression of the original commandment, declaring to him that in the sweat of his face he would work food from the earth with his hands. Since, moreover, we all sinned in Adam, we are necessarily subjected to the same penalties to which it is clear that he was subjected. He labored for the food by which he might live, and he did not eat bread in idleness.

Hence our blessed father Benedict, detesting the idleness of monks, says in the Rule which he handed down to us: Idleness is the enemy of the soul, and therefore at certain times monks ought to be occupied in manual labor, and again at fixed hours in divine reading. For then they are truly monks, if they live by the labor of their hands, as our fathers and the apostles did. Hence also the holy apostle Paul says in the epistle: “He who does not labor shall not eat.”

In the beginning of the monastic state this was the custom of monks: that they admitted no one into their fellowship without labor, not so much for the necessities of food as for the salvation of the soul, lest through idleness an opportunity be given to destructive thoughts. At that time monks had no rents, no tithes, no revenues, no annual proceeds of any kind; rather, all living together in common procured food and clothing for themselves by their hands, burdening no one by demanding what was owed. The holy apostles of our Savior too, although they had the power to live from the preaching of the Gospel, preferred to labor with their hands, lest they seem to have burdened any of the faithful, and so that they might have not only the means by which they themselves lived, but also something with which they supplied necessities to the needy.

But after monks began to have rents and revenues, the former pursuits of simplicity soon failed, and little by little, along with wealth in temporal things, pride increased, to such a degree that in time monks refused all labors of the hands. As long as monks were poor and humble in birth, they worshiped God in truth; but after they began to abound in wealth, duplicity drove away simplicity, and the pride of nobles extinguished the humility of monks. Once the integrity of regular observance had been taken away, the wealth of temporal things soon also failed in many cenobia by the just judgment of God.

From their number was the present monastery of Sponheim, my brothers, neither the last nor the first, which for many years remained miserably desolate in both states, to such an extent that the five monks who alone lived there, leading a dissolute life, did not have the means from which they could obtain the necessities of life for themselves. Hence, at last, by the provision of God’s mercy, Frederick, duke of Transylvania, having called upon the counsel of the fathers of the reformed order, in the year of the Christians one thousand four hundred and sixty-nine, expelled the monks deformed in life, deposed the abbot, Otto by name, from Cologne, and placed in this monastery four others of the observance of Bursfeld, called from the cenobium of Schoenberg. Over them he set as abbot Johannes, from the noble family of Budisbach, which they commonly call “of the house of Carbonaria,” a man such as you have seen and heard, worthy of eternal memory. He found this place in extreme poverty, and, presiding over it for almost fourteen years in great patience, recalled it to the state in which we now live.

Therefore, since lack of temporal goods still presses us heavily, there would be need for continual labor, so that we might sustain ourselves with necessary food and free the house of God in which we dwell from debt. But what are your labors, or how much can they contribute to such great needs? We are few in number and unsuited to rustic labors; nor does the institution of the priestly order permit us to spend the whole day in the field turning stones with our hands and to interrupt divine service. We are wretched and weighed down by excessive poverty and lack of all necessities; and if we labor much, we gain little from it, yet if we work little, we certainly fail still more in spirit. For, as Scripture bears witness, man is born to labor as a bird is to flying.

Therefore, lest you eat your bread in idleness, you must always be bound to some honorable and useful labor, by which the mind may both be relieved from weariness with regular observance and our grievous poverty may at least in some small way be repaired. In the time of harvest, for hay, and in the days of autumn, for the vintage, we have been accustomed to employ you outside the monastery only when labor is clearly very necessary and the scarcity of hired workers increases the price and wage of servants. At other times we have decided to keep you in the cenobium, because your labors, compared with the expenses of the monastery, contribute little to its needs, and because, as it seems to us, very few of you willingly present yourselves voluntarily for rustic pursuits. There is also another consideration that stirs my mind, lest you be drawn away by external...

[segment 29]
Blessed Father Benedict, detesting the idleness of monks, says in the Rule which he handed down to us: “Idleness is the enemy of the soul; therefore at fixed times the brothers ought to be occupied in manual labor, and again at fixed times in divine reading.” For then they are truly monks, if they live by the labor of their hands, as our fathers and the apostles did. Hence also Saint Paul says in the Epistle: “Whoever does not work should not eat.” In the first day of the monastic state this was the custom of monks: they admitted no one into their fellowship without labor, not so much because of the necessities of food as because of the soul’s salvation, lest through idleness an occasion be given to destructive thoughts.

At that time monks had no revenues, no tithes, no rents, no annual income from anyone; but all who lived together in common procured food and clothing for themselves by their hands, burdening no one by the exaction of what was owed. The holy apostles of our Savior too, although they had power to live from the preaching of the Gospel, labored with their hands, preferring not to seem to burden any of the faithful, so that they might have not only what they themselves could live on, but also what they might provide as necessities for the needy.

Again, afterward, when monks began to have estates and revenues, the first love of simplicity and study failed, and pride gradually increased with the wealth of temporal things, to such a degree that all withdrew from the labor of their hands, with time, after monks had become rich. As long as monks were poor and humble in birth, they worshiped God in truth; but afterward, when they began to abound in riches, duplicity drove out simplicity, and the arrogance of nobles uprooted the humility of monks. For the integrity of regular observance gave birth to an abundance of temporal things, but little by little the riches of monks uprooted the purity of the order from its foundations. But once the integrity of regular observance had been removed, in many cenobia even the riches of temporal things failed by the just judgment of God.

And among their number was this present monastery of Sponheim, the last among my brothers, and not the first, which for many years remained wretchedly desolate in each condition, to such a degree that five monks lived in it, leading a dissolute life, and did not have the necessities of life to support themselves. Therefore, by the mercy of God at last providing, Frederick, count palatine across the Rhine, having called upon the counsel of fathers of a reformed order, in the Christian year one thousand four hundred sixty-nine, expelled the monks deformed in life, deposed the abbot named Otto of Cologne, and placed four others in this monastery from the observance of Bursfeld and from the cenobium called Mount Speciosus. Over them he set as abbot Johannes, from the noble family of Buchenbach, which they commonly call “of the Carbonaria house,” a man such as you have seen and heard, worthy of eternal memory. He found this place in extreme poverty, and, presiding for almost fourteen years in great patience, restored it to the condition in which we now live.

Therefore, since lack of temporal goods still presses us heavily, there was need of continual labor, both that we might support ourselves with necessary food and that we might free the house of God in which we live from another’s debt. But what are your labors, or how much can they contribute for us toward such great needs? We are few in number, and our priestly order’s institution does not often permit us to spend the whole day in the field turning stones with our hands and to interrupt divine service. We are few, and extreme poverty necessarily weighs us down with lack of all things; and even if we have labored much, we accomplish little afterward; and even if we have been able to work only a little, surely we fail all the more in spirit. For man, as Scripture bears witness, is born for labor just as a bird is born for flying.

Therefore, lest you eat your bread idly, you will always have to be assigned to some honest and useful labor, by which both the weariness of regular observance may be relieved and grave poverty may at least in part be repaired. At certain times of the year, in old age, and in the days of autumn for the vintage, we have been accustomed to occupy you outside the monastery, that is, when labor is both very necessary and the cost and wage of hired workers increases the price of servants. At the remaining time we have decreed to keep you in the cenobium, partly because your labors contribute little to the monastery’s needs in comparison with its expenses, and because, as it seems to us, very few of you come gladly as volunteers to rustic tasks.

There is also another consideration which moves my mind not to occupy you easily, without reasonable cause and manifest necessity, in external works of the hands. For I have learned by experience that labors of this kind do not suit you in regular observance, but rather destroy the integrity of monastic discipline among you than advance it. I am ashamed to say it, and nevertheless I am compelled to utter it: your labors outside the monastery and the cell bring more destruction to the purity of the soul than salvation. For what do you labor at, and what are your pursuits in your labors? Is it not to wander about, to run here and there, to indulge in conversation and cups, and to spend the rest of the time in vain and useless talk?

You do not have in yourselves that fervor of the Holy Spirit of God by which, as we read, the ancient monks of our institution were once inflamed. For the common necessity they all and singly labored with their hands with such desire, such zeal, and such exertion that you would have thought the business of the whole monastery was placed in each one’s prudence. For each labored at that time with his hands as if he alone ought to nourish the whole congregation. Amid these things they paid the services of divine worship with such cheerfulness by day and night as if they had in no way been occupied in penitence by the labors of their hands.
