Who among you, brother, is an imitator of such men, so that he reckons the whole of our common want as his own, or strives according to his strength to relieve it by laboring in the sweat of his brow? They, laboring with their hands, prayed to God with continual hearts; but you, intent on stories, satisfy neither the duty of the hands nor the minds of monks. Likewise, in the time of the regular fast, they either diligently kept the measure of abstinence appointed for them, or, if they could not because of the magnitude of their labors, they ate for this reason: that they might be made fit and able for labor. But you do not eat in order to labor; rather, you labor in order to eat, though by the Rule you ought to be fasting. You take occasion from labor to break your fasts.

As for the clothing which some of you treat foully and most disgracefully, what zealous lover of regular discipline could bear it with equanimity? These are, my brothers, things which are ruinously tended. Everything that belongs to the monasteries of the servants of God is the patrimony of Christ; the whole is dedicated to God, and no one may misuse the things of Christ, or turn them to some other use. Do you not know what our holy lawgiver Benedict says to you in the Rule: “If anyone treats the things of the monastery filthily or negligently, let him be corrected. But if he does not amend, let him be subject to regular discipline.” And in the chapter on the cellarer of the monastery: “Let him regard all the substance of the monastery as if it were the sacred vessels of the altar.”

Finally, I cannot and ought not be silent about how filthily and negligently many of you treat everything, as if either you were not seen by God, or no loss came to us from negligence in these matters. Know this, brothers, that I announce it to you beforehand: for these things, as also for your other sins, you will have to render account to the Lord God. For this reason I have reduced your labors outside the monastery, lest by acting badly you sin more gravely; and manual work is to be enjoined upon you: writing books, binding them, and things like these, which you can accomplish within the enclosures of the monastery with tranquillity of mind and body.

If only you would do at least these works of the hands, or of the pen, diligently for the love of God, so that you might not eat bread altogether idle. There is no manual work which, in my judgment, befits a monk more than writing books of sacred reading and preparing the necessary materials for others who write. It will very often be permitted to interrupt this holy labor with prayer, and, as food for the mind, it is no less watchful for the body. Necessity also compels us to apply ourselves diligently to writing books, if we wish to have at hand the means by which we can usefully occupy ourselves with one another in spiritual study. For you see that the whole library of this cenobium, which was once distinguished and large, was scattered, sold, and alienated by the deformed monks before us, and that no more than fourteen volumes were found here by us.

Indeed, the industry of the printing art, recently invented in our days at Mainz, daily brings many volumes into the light; but it is by no means possible for us to buy all new books, since this house is still oppressed by the greatest poverty. Therefore I admonish and exhort you: since you do not come gladly enough to external manual labors, at least labor diligently in writing volumes for the honor of God. For just as idleness is the enemy of the soul, so moderate labor is the spiritual preserver of life. Since a monk is a soldier of the eternal king, he must never be idle. For idleness produces acedia, and acedia introduces the soul into the want of destructive death. But the labors of monks are to be moderated so that [unclear].

[segment 30]
**Homily VII.**

**Fo. XVI.**

works with your hands, you may easily occupy yourselves without reasonable cause and manifest necessity, having found by experience that labor in such work does not accord with regular observance among you, but destroys rather than promotes the integrity of monastic discipline among you. I blush to say it, and yet I am compelled to declare it: your labors outside the monastery and the cell bring more destruction to the purity of the soul than salvation. For what is your labor, and what are your pursuits in your labors? To wander about, run here and there, indulge in feasts and cups, and occupy the remaining time idly with vain and useless things.

You do not have in yourselves that fervor of the Holy Spirit with which we read that the monks of our former institution were once inflamed: that, from desire for the necessity of the community, with such zeal and such effort, all and each labored with their own hands, so that almost the whole business of the monastery was placed in the prudence of each one. For in this way each and all ought to have labored with their own hands for the support of the congregation. Amid these things, too, they performed the service of divine worship with such eagerness day and night that they seemed in no way at all to have been occupied in the labors of their hands.

Who among you, brothers, is an imitator of such men, so that he regards the needs of our whole community as his own, or wishes and strives by his own labor, according to his strength, to relieve them? They, while laboring with their hands, continually implored God with their hearts; but you, intent on subtleties, neither satisfy the office of the hands nor remember the monk. In the time of regular fasting, they either diligently kept the measure of abstinence appointed for them, or, if they could not because of the greatness of their labors, they ate for this reason: so that they might be rendered fit and suitable for labor. But you do not eat so that you may labor; rather you labor so that you may eat, as though according to the Rule one had to fast. You take occasion from labor to relax the fast.

Indeed, how foully and uselessly some of you handle your garments: what zealous lover of regular discipline could pass it over? For what is there, my brothers, that we consume wastefully? Everything that first comes to the monasteries as God’s servant is Christ’s patrimony, and the whole of it is dedicated to God; nor is it lawful for anyone to misuse the things of Christ or to turn them to another use. Do you not know what our legislator Benedict says plainly enough to you in the Rule? “If anyone treats the things of the monastery filthily or negligently, let him be corrected. If he does not amend, let him be subjected to regular discipline.” And in the chapter on the cellarer of the monastery he says: “Let him regard all the vessels of the monastery and all its substance as if they were the sacred vessels of the altar.”

Finally, I cannot and certainly ought not to keep silent about how filthily and negligently many of you handle everything, as if they were either not seen by God, or no one were causing loss to the goods of the monastery by equal sloth. Know, brothers, that I announce to you in warning that for these things also, just as for your other sins, you will have to render account to the Lord God. For this reason we have reduced your labors outside the monastery, lest by working badly you sin more gravely; and we have enjoined upon you manual work: to write books, bind them, and things similar to these, which you can carry out within the enclosures of the monastery with tranquility of mind and body. But in this too your hands are slow, though you would have enough leisure in the whole week to do it diligently for the love of God.

There is no manual work that, in my judgment, befits a monk more than writing books of sacred reading and preparing what is necessary for others who write. For this holy labor, although prayer often interrupts it, requires vigilance for the eye of the mind no less than for that of the body. Necessity also urges us to apply ourselves diligently to writing books, if we desire to have at hand the things from which we may be usefully occupied with one another in spiritual study. For although the library of this monastery, and all that belonged to it, was once distinguished and great, it was so scattered, sold, and alienated by the deformed monks before us that no more than fourteen volumes were found here by us.

Indeed, by the industry of the art of printing, recently invented in our days at Mainz, many volumes are brought to light every day; but it is by no means possible for us to buy them all, since we are still oppressed by the greatest poverty. Therefore I admonish and teach you that, since you do not come readily enough to external labors of the hands, you should labor as diligently as possible in writing volumes for the honor of God, because just as idleness is the enemy of the soul, so moderate labor is the preserver of the spiritual life. For the monk, because he is a soldier of the eternal king, must never be idle. Idleness begets acedia, and that brings upon the soul the ruinous poverty of death. But the labors of monks are to be moderated in such a way that never

[segment 31]
[Page 040]

or at midnight, or at cockcrow, or in the morning, lest, when he comes suddenly, he find you sleeping. Therefore watch at all times, praying that you may be accounted worthy to escape all the evils that are to come, and to stand with confidence before the sight of the Son of God, who judges all things. For, as the divinely inspired Christophilus bears witness, we shall all stand before the tribunal of Christ, so that each may receive what belongs to his own body, according to what he has done, whether good or evil.

Blessed is that monk whom the Lord finds watching in good works: who has prepared himself for the marriage-feast of the heavenly Lamb in true purity of heart; who will not fear to go forth to meet the Judge. Let that memorable saying of Saint Jerome on the Gospel according to Matthew always be in your hearts: “Whether I eat or drink, or do anything else, that voice always seems to sound in my ears: Rise, you dead, and come to judgment.” Whenever I think of that day of future examination, I tremble in my whole heart and body. Therefore, brothers, if there is any joy in the present life, it must be so practiced that the bitterness of that dreadful judgment never recedes from memory.

O that day, great, terrible, exceedingly dreadful, and very bitter, on which the sun and moon will be darkened, not by a lessening of their light but by comparison with his majesty; where every argument of excuse will cease; where all things will be manifest, and nothing will be able to lie hidden from the Judge. For he who knows the hidden thoughts of minds will then judge openly; he is not anticipated by favor, nor bent by mercy; he is not corrupted by offerings and gifts; he will not be softened by prayers, nor thereafter appeased by any satisfaction.

Placed in this life, therefore, brothers, let each one of you do as much as he can for his soul, because repentance for sins made here alone is useful and fruitful, so long as there is a place for pardon and the sentence of justice is still deferred. He who postpones doing good and practicing repentance until death is very near to damnation, because the good that a man neglects while healthy and sound he rarely restores when sick and already about to die. For Saint Pope Gregory says: Whoever does not fear to put off the fruit of repentance until the hour of death is himself a witness against himself that true repentance was never in his mind. Now, therefore, turn yourselves to our Lord with your whole heart, doing penance before him; and so live, I beg you, that when you must depart from here, you may not fear to appear before his face.

Fol. XVII.

That One Must Pray Without Intermission. Homily IX.

Our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, brothers, wishing in the Gospel according to Luke that we be constant in prayers, admonished us in these words: “It is necessary always to pray and never to fail.” This counsel of the Lord befits monks above all mortals, and all who are cloistered in any religious order whatsoever, whose every action and whole life, indeed whatever they do, ought either to be prayer or at least conformed to prayer.

For all who profess the monastic soldiery are called spiritual, not without reason, so that by the very name they may be reminded to despise the pleasures of the flesh for love of God and to walk in the ardor of the spirit through true charity. For the holy apostle of Christ says: “Those who are in the flesh cannot please God,” and, “If you live according to the flesh, you will die; but if by the spirit you put to death the deeds of the flesh, you will live.” Knowing, however, that without continual prayer the fervor of the spirit cannot long subsist in us, he again says to the Thessalonians: “Rejoice always; pray without intermission; in all things give thanks, for this is the will of God in Christ Jesus for all of you.”

The Savior taught us in the Gospel what the manner of praying is, saying: “But you, when you pray, enter into your chamber, and, having shut the door, pray to your Father in secret; and your Father, who sees in secret, will reward you.” And in another place he said to the Samaritan woman: “Woman, believe me, that the hour is coming. The Father seeks such people to worship him. God is spirit, and those who worship him must worship in spirit and truth.”

It is clear and manifest from Christ’s teaching that prayer is of the heart rather than a lengthy speech of the mouth, because, since God is spirit, he must be worshiped in no other way than with the inward fervor of the spirit. For prayer of the heart in the fervor of the spirit has great power; but the multiplication of words without attention of the mind [unclear].

[segment 32]
That one must pray without interruption.

...has no usefulness at all. Hence Saint Jerome says: God is to be prayed to not only in words, but with the heart. Therefore the chanting of five psalms with purity of heart, serenity, and spiritual gladness is better than the chanting of the whole Psalter with anxiety and sadness of heart. And the divine Pope Gregory says: True petition is not in the sounds of the mouth, but in the voices of the heart, that is, in holy thoughts. For words do not make stronger sounds in the ears of God, but desires do.

Therefore, apart from those prayers which we are bound to say either by decree of the Church or by the institution of the monastic order, complexity in the rest and multiplication of words contribute nothing, or little, to true inward devotion of mind; for where there is no fervent desire in the love of God, a superfluous extension of words is introduced. For holy Augustine says: Let much talking be absent from prayer, but let fervent intention persevere; for to speak much in prayer is to carry on a necessary matter with superfluous words. For prayer comprehends many things in few words, so that the simplicity of faith may quickly learn the sufficiency of its salvation, and the prudence of the more ingenious may be amazed at the depth of the mysteries.

But those who, because of hardness of heart, lack the compunction of inward fervor, when they prostrate themselves for prayer need longer speech, crying, and tears, so that by prolonged exercise of the mind they may at last strike out within themselves the burning fire of divine love, until they rise into the affection of holy prayer in the Spirit of God. But after the fervor of divine love and compunction has penetrated the mind, there is no longer need of the exercise of words; rather, the continuation of spiritual nourishment is required, lest the fervor of the spiritual fire be extinguished. To this end our most holy father wished prayer in community to be shortened, so that, if anyone wished to pray in the spirit, he could do so apart.

There are, however, some who are so hard of heart that in praying they find little or nothing of spiritual compunction; for them liberty brings ruin, and solitude often begets despair. Attend, I beg you, dearest brothers, to what I say to you, and consider how much you are bound to pray without interruption. It is not enough for a monk, striving toward perfection in the school of Christ the eternal king, to discharge the canonical prayers; nor is it sufficient for a man solemnly dedicated to God by vow to fulfill the common prayers of the order in community. For he who is commanded always to pray without interruption is bound beyond verbal prayers also to spiritual prayers. For prayers instituted by the Church or the order fall under the merit of obedience and under the law of necessity by reason of precept; but that manner of praying which is called spiritual is strengthened at all times for the monk as useful and necessary for perfection in the liberty of the spirit.

Hence it must be noted that for prayers which are of the precept of the Church or the order, the utterance of words is necessarily required, with the intention of satisfying the statute by which anyone is bound to pray. Let those who do not express the seven canonical prayers by utterance of words, but with closed mouth run through the sequence of prayers by thought alone, with the eyes assisting, see how they satisfy the command of the Church. But in those prayers which we undertake to say not by command but by our own will and from devotion alone, the fervor of the mind toward God suffices without the utterance of words; for God, as we have said, requires such worshipers as worship in spirit and truth. But we are bound to say the canonical prayers which they call the Hours, and not only to think them.

In both cases devotion of mind toward God is useful; without it no prayers are believed to be effective. And so, my brothers, when we stand for prayer, we ought with the greatest zeal to keep watch and attend to the prayers which we pour out, lest we pray without the fruit of inward compunction. Therefore let every thought of the flesh withdraw far away; let no occupation with household affairs impede the mind; and let the soul at that time think of nothing else whatever except this alone, which is being prayed. For this reason also the priest, after the customary preface has been given before the sacrifice, prepares the minds of the brothers, saying, “Lift up your hearts,” so that when the people answer, “We have lifted them to the Lord,” they may be warned that they ought to think of nothing other than the very God whom they invoke.

Let the breast, then, be closed against the adversary and lie open to the Lord alone; and in the time of holy prayer let it admit absolutely no concern of worldly occupation. Great indeed is the usefulness of pure prayer, which, like a faithful messenger, carries out the command of a fervent mind and, flying, ascends into heaven where the flesh does not reach. For prayer is sustained by two wings, that it may fly up into heaven: one of them is called fasting, and the other is named almsgiving. Hence Saint John Chrysostom says: Nothing is more powerful than a person praying, who prays with fasting...

[segment 33]
*That one must pray without ceasing.*

There is no usefulness at all. Hence Saint Jerome says: God is to be prayed to not in words alone, but with the heart. Therefore the recitation of five psalms with purity of heart, serenity, and spiritual cheerfulness is better than the chanting of the whole Psalter with anxiety of heart and sadness.

And blessed Pope Gregory says: True petition is not in the sounds of the mouth, but in the voices of the heart, that is, in holy thoughts. For it is not words that make the stronger voices in the ears of God, but desires.

Therefore, except for those prayers which we are bound to say either by decree of the Church or by the institution of the monastic order, in the rest, prolixity and the multiplication of words contribute nothing, or very little, to true and inward devotion of mind. For where there is not fervent desire in the love of God, a suspicious lengthening of speech is introduced. For Saint Augustine says: Let much speaking be absent from prayer, but let fervent attention persevere; for to speak much in praying is to conduct a necessary matter with superfluous words.

Prayer, indeed, comprehends manifold matters in few words, so that it may quickly satisfy the simplicity of faith and what is sufficient for salvation, and by the depth of its mysteries may suffice for prudent and discerning minds. But for those who, because of the hardness of the inner heart, lack fervent compunction when they have given themselves to prayer, words are needed for gain, and cries with tears, so that by daily exercise of the mind they may at last kindle within themselves the burning fire of divine love, until in the affection of holy prayer their spirit rises together to God. But afterwards, when the fervor of divine love and compunction penetrates the mind, there is then no further need for the exercise of words; rather, the continuation of spiritual fuel is required, lest the fervor of the spiritual fire be extinguished.

For this purpose our most holy Father wished our prayers to be shortened in content, if anyone should wish to pray in the spirit. But there are some who are so hard of heart that they find little or no compunction while praying, and for them liberty often breeds ruin, and solitude often breeds despair.

Attend carefully, beloved brothers, to these things that I say to you, and consider how greatly you are bound to pray without ceasing. It is not enough for a monk, striving toward perfection in the school of Christ the eternal king, to gloss over the canonical prayers; nor is it sufficient for a man solemnly dedicated to God by vow to fulfill the common prayers of the order in community. For he who is commanded always to pray without ceasing is bound, beyond verbal prayers, also to spiritual prayers. For the prayers instituted by the Church or the order are under the merit of obedience and under the law of necessity by reason of precept; but that manner of praying which is called spiritual, useful and necessary at all times for a monk’s perfection, is strengthened in freedom of spirit.

Hence it must be noted that for prayers which are by precept of the Church or of the order, the verbal utterance of petitions is required by necessity, with the intention of satisfying the statute by which one is bound to pray. Let those see how they satisfy the Church who in certain churches do not express the seven canonical prayers by the utterance of words, but, with closed mouth, perform the solemn precept by thought alone, their eyes serving as ministers. But in those prayers which we undertake to say not by command, but by our own will and by devotion alone, the fervor of the mind in devotion is sufficient without the petition of words, because God seeks such worshipers, as we have said, who adore in spirit and truth. Yet the canonical prayers which we undertake to say at the hours we are bound to speak, and not merely to think. In both cases devotion of mind toward God is useful; without it no prayers are believed to be effective.

And so, my brothers, when we stand for prayer, we ought to watch with the utmost care and attend to the prayers that we say, lest we pray without the fruit of inward compunction. Therefore let every thought of the flesh withdraw far away; let no occupation with household affairs hinder the mind; nor let the soul think anything else within itself except this alone, what it prays. For this reason the priest too, in the prayer before the sacrifice, prepares the minds of the brothers by saying, Lift up your hearts, so that when the people answer, We have them lifted to the Lord, he admonishes them that they ought to think of nothing else than the very God whom they invoke.

Therefore let the breast be closed against the adversary and open to the Lord alone; nor in the time of holy prayer let it admit any worldly occupation at all. Great is the usefulness of pure prayer, which, like a faithful messenger, carries out the command of a fervent mind and, flying upward, ascends into heaven, where the flesh does not reach. For by what wings is prayer sustained, so that it may fly up into heaven? One of them is called fasting, and the other is named almsgiving. Hence Saint John Chrysostom says: Nothing is more powerful than a person who prays; he who prays with [unclear].

[segment 34]
--- Page 042 ---

...with fasting, because it has two wings and neither strays nor grows sluggish. Let us therefore pray with the highest fervor of spirit, and let us live with as much purity of conscience as we can, since that prayer is perfect and pleasing to God in which both the cause and the tongue cry out, whose deed and speech agree with the petition, whose life and thought bring support to the prayers.

For without the justice of truth every prayer will be fruitless, since we know that God does not hear sinners, and that salvation is far from sinners, as Scripture says. Whoever turns his ears away so as not to hear the laws, his prayer will be abominable. The holy prophet also says in the psalm: If I have regarded iniquity in my heart, the Lord will not hear me. For just as no medicine is effective in a wound unless the iron is first drawn out, so the prayer of one in whose mind deceit still remains, or hatred in his breast, is of no effect.

But in order that God may hear your prayer, first of all conscience must be cleansed from every stain of mortal sin, and the mind must undertake a true purpose of amendment. To pray much and at the same time offend in many things: what is this other than to mock God? For prayer is hindered in two ways so that it is not heard by the Lord: one, when the person praying still commits evil by sinning; the other, if for God’s sake he does not forgive his debtor the debt.

You therefore, who desire to have the fruit of your prayers, who wish to come with the penitents to God’s mercy, take care that you do nothing evil against God’s will, and diligently see to it that you truly forgive from the heart everything committed against you by human beings. For you know that the Lord said: Forgive, and you shall be forgiven. But after you have cleansed your conscience from evil and forgiven every offense to your neighbor, you will then be fit to pray, if in the fervor of a right purpose you have been ordered toward God.

Now then approach the Lord confidently for prayer; prostrate yourself humbly in his sight, and pray with purity of heart and fervor of spirit. Do not cease from prayer as long as you live in the flesh. Let your prayer be pure, simple, clear, and plain; let it be full of seriousness, weight, and dignity, adorned with true humility. Do not ask for anything in prayer that is contrary to the salvation of the soul, nor should you beg too importunately for a temporal thing, since it is shameless to ask God for riches, which furnish the greatest occasion for pride and often defile Christian purity.

It is not shameless if you ask for daily bread, since it is one thing to ask for matter from which you may become proud, and another to ask for the necessary food by which you may live. Do not justify yourself while praying, lest, like the Pharisee, you go out condemned; nor ask anything from the Lord as though owed to your merits. Rather humble yourself in all things, so that you may deserve to receive grace. Remember the humility of the publican, and pray with such great humility that with him you may find forgiveness of sins and justification of the soul. For God’s grace is richer than the prayer of the suppliant, and he grants us more when we pray than we ask.

You will not pray with a loud cry of the voice to him who knows the hidden things of the heart, but in fervor of spirit and with the inward cry of the mind you will strike the ears of the divine majesty. Nor should you prolong the length of words before him who is Spirit, because God will be appeased not by much speaking but by the pure mind of the one praying. At the time of prayer cast away from yourself all malice and disturbance of heart, and, as we often say, forgive if you have anything against your neighbor. For unless for love of the Savior you forgive your neighbor every debt, you will obtain nothing from the Lord by your prayers.

There is said to be a certain kind of serpent which, when it wishes to go into the water to drink, first vomits out all its venom. You too, monk, imitate this serpent in this respect: vomit out all the venom of wrath and bitterness, forgiving your brother the hundred denarii, so that the Lord may forgive you the ten thousand talents. As you desire the Lord to be toward you, so you also should show yourself toward your fellow servant.

Call upon the Lord with your whole heart and you will find him. Let your prayer be burning and most pure, so that your mind may be joined to him in his most holy love. Strive for this throughout your whole life, that at all times you may offer pure prayer to the Lord God. Never cease from prayer, nor despair of God’s mercy. Even what he is going to grant, he sometimes delays; he does not take it away. He does not act with fickleness, nor is he easily moved in his sentence; what he has promised is fixed. If you pray insistently, you will see a miracle. Remember that you stand under the gaze of God, who understands all the secrets of hearts and knows the hidden things of the mind. Therefore stand watchfully in his sight, who has known all things, and pray with as much fervor of spirit as you can,

--- Page 043 ---

That One Must Pray Without Interruption.

so that you may deserve to obtain mercy. For by holy prayer the conscience of the penitent is cleansed; and whoever raises his heart to the Lord in true purity, his prayer does not return empty. O, dearest brothers, if you knew how pleasing to God are the prayers of a pure mind, and how much usefulness they confer upon the soul, you would allow no time to pass without prayers.

For if our prayer is pure and burning in fervor of spirit, it is a most faithful messenger to God; it speaks to God on our behalf, and while it prays it obtains everything necessary for our salvation. Therefore I admonish you and exhort you in true charity to pray without interruption and never cease from the fervor of the Holy Spirit. Let there be no time empty for you, or idle in sluggishness of mind. In the fear of the Lord, I beg you, always be watchful and solicitous, so that you may be able bravely to conquer and overcome the temptations that threaten.

As soon as foul thoughts begin to strike the mind, run back to the practice of prayer, and they will be wholly unable to prevail against you. For prayer is a great fortification of the soul; in its presence all the schemes of the opposing side fail. Through the purest prayers all useful things are granted to us by God, and all adversities are driven far away. By pure prayers the mind is powerfully kindled in the love of God, and whatever slight stain comes upon it is cleansed by praying.

By holy and continual prayers the world is conquered; the plagues of the soul are healed by devout petitions; by tears and weeping the devil is overcome; and he for whom a thousand cohorts are not enough for battle is weakened by the prayer of a single poor man. Just as wax melts and dissolves before the face of fire, so at the fervor of pure prayer all the power of demons, crushed, is enfeebled.

If a foul thought has been sent into your mind by demons, immediately, with the sign of the cross impressed upon your forehead, say: Save me, Jesus Christ, mediator of God and human beings, and drive away from me all the wickedness of the ancient enemy through your most innocent passion. What can be shorter than this prayer, or what more powerful? For as soon as it has been uttered with fervor of mind against a demon, whether present or by suggestion, every scheme of iniquity withdraws.

For the power of pure prayer is very great, since it both penetrates heaven and by its steadfastness defeats the powers of the air. Prayer illumines the mind, purifies the affection, obtains forgiveness of sins, and strengthens the spirit in the love of God against all adversaries. Nothing so raises the mind from earthly things to the love of heavenly things; nothing so quickly and powerfully destroys the pleasures of the flesh; nothing so illumines the understanding; nothing, too, so greatly confounds the adversary of truth as pure prayer, which is poured out to God with the fervor of divine love and adorned by the testimony of a good conscience.

If you desire to be terrible to demons, if you wish to be a friend of God, keep your conscience pure and never interrupt spiritual prayer. For devout prayer can do all things if it is accompanied by uprightness of faith and purity of life. Prayer puts demons to flight, enfeebles the strength of malignant spirits, heals the soul languishing in tepidity, and confirms the weak and infirm spirit in the fear of God.

Pure prayer makes one near to God, and the one whom it had received as a servant it makes to be God’s friend. Pure prayer, fervent in the love of God, penetrates heaven and wonderfully illumines the understanding of the one praying; it closes hell, opens paradise, and leads in the souls of the faithful. For Truth does not lie, who in the holy Gospel promises, saying: Ask and it will be given to you; seek and you will find; knock and it will be opened to you. For everyone who asks receives, and whoever seeks finds, and to the one who knocks it will be opened.

Who among you has ever turned to the Lord with his whole heart and prayed, and has not been heard? Or who has called upon the Lord, who cast down Amalek trusting in his own strength, not by fighting with iron but by praying with holy prayers? For the continual petition of the just person has great power, and the prayer of one who humbles himself penetrates the clouds. Elijah was a man subject to suffering like us, and he prayed in prayer that it might not rain upon the earth, and it did not rain for three years and six months. And again he prayed, and heaven gave rain, and the earth gave its fruit.

By prayer and tears King Hezekiah obtained fifteen years of longer life, and by his prayers changed the prophet’s sentence. By prayers holy men became renowned for miracles, and by supplicating the Lord they recalled the dead to life. All things also are subject to the purity of prayer, and there is nothing in us so difficult that devout and fervent prayer cannot equally

[segment 35]
That one must pray without interruption.

...to deserve to obtain mercy. For by holy prayer the conscience is cleansed through penitence; and whoever raises his heart to the Lord in true purity, his prayer does not return empty. O dearest brothers, if you knew in secret how pleasing to God are the prayers of a pure mind, and how much benefit they confer upon the soul, you would let no time pass without prayer. For if our prayer is pure and burning in the fervor of the Spirit, it is a most faithful messenger to God; it speaks to God on our behalf and obtains all things that are necessary for our salvation.

Therefore I admonish you and exhort you in true charity to pray without interruption, and not to cease even for a moment from the fervor of the Holy Spirit. Let no time be wholly empty for you of mental prayers. In the fear of the Lord, I beg you, always be watchful and solicitous, so that you may be able bravely to conquer and overcome the temptations that threaten. But when foul thoughts begin to knock at the mind, run back to the practice of prayer, and they will have no power to prevail against you. For prayer is a great fortification of the soul; at its presence all the contrivances of the opposing side fail. Through very pure prayers all useful things are granted us by God, and all adversities are driven far away. Good men are kindled by pure prayers in the love of God, and whatever lighter stains meet them, they cleanse by praying.

By holy and continual prayers the world is conquered; deadly sins are healed by devout supplications; the devil is overcome by tears and weeping; and he whom a thousand cohorts are not enough to fight is overcome by the prayer of one poor man. As wax melts and is dissolved before the face of fire, so at the fervor of pure prayer every power of demons is broken and weakened. If a foul thought, sent by a demon, enters your mind, drive it away at once, making the sign of the cross on your forehead and saying: “Save me, Jesus Christ, mediator of God and men, and drive far from me all the wickedness of the ancient enemy by your most innocent passion.” What can be shorter than this prayer, or what more powerful? For as soon as it has been poured forth with fervor of mind against the presence or suggestion of demons, every contrivance of iniquity withdraws.

The power of pure prayer is very great, since it penetrates heaven and by its constancy overthrows the powers of the air. Prayer illuminates the mind, purifies the affection, obtains delightful pardons, and strengthens the spirit in the love of God against all adversaries. Nothing so strongly draws the mind away from earthly things and raises it to heavenly love; nothing so powerfully exterminates the pleasures of the flesh; nothing so illuminates the understanding; nothing likewise confounds the adversary by the insistence of truth, as pure prayer poured out to God with the fervor of divine love and adorned with the good testimony of conscience.

If you desire to be terrible to demons, if you desire to be a friend of God, keep your conscience pure and never interrupt spiritual prayers. For devout prayer can do very much, if it is offered with uprightness of faith and purity of life. Prayer puts demons to flight, weakens the power of evil spirits, heals souls languishing in tepidity, and strengthens the weak and infirm in the fear of the Spirit. Pure prayer brings a person near to God, and makes the servant whom God accepts to be his friend. Pure prayer, fervent in the love of God, penetrates heaven, wonderfully illuminates the understanding of the one praying, closes hell, opens paradise, and brings in the souls of the faithful.

Its truth is not measured out, for in the holy Gospel he promises, saying: “Ask, and it shall be given you; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you.” For everyone who asks receives, and he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it will be opened. Which of you has ever prayed, converted to the Lord with his whole heart, and has not been heard? Or who has called upon the Lord, and the Lord has withdrawn himself from him? For Scripture says: “Know that the Lord will hear your prayers, if you remain and persevere in fasting and prayers in the sight of the Lord.”

Be mindful of Moses, the servant of the Lord, who brought down Amalek, proud in his strength, not by fighting with iron but by praying with holy supplications. For the constant prayer of a just man is very powerful, and the prayer of humility penetrates the clouds. Elijah was a man subject to suffering like us, and he prayed with prayer that it might not rain upon the earth, and it did not rain for three years and six months. And when he prayed again, heaven gave rain and the earth gave its fruit. By prayer and tears King Hezekiah obtained fifteen additional years of life, because the Lord changed the sentence by prayers. By prayers holy men shone with miracles, and by supplicating the Lord they called the dead back to life. All things are subject even to the purity of prayer, and there is nothing among us so difficult that devout prayer is not able equally to obtain it.

[segment 36]
--- Page 045 ---
Homily VIII. Fol. XIX.

cannot obtain. Then, when we pray, we receive from the Lord confidence that we shall obtain what we ask, when no perversity of life contradicts our prayers. For the Lord is the hearer of a righteous prayer when integrity of life commends the prayer, and no guilt of conscience makes the one praying hesitate. Friendship will not stand between dissimilar persons, and there can be no communion between God and a sinner. If you wish to be heard when you pray, take care first that you do not disagree with the will of God. Live in spirit very like to God, and you will suffer no rejection in prayer.

Indeed, purity of conscience in the fervor of divine love makes a human being very like to God in understanding. Likeness causes love; love introduces familiarity; familiarity brings confidence; and confidence in petition readily obtains its request. For just as there is no union between things unlike, so prayer profits nothing between God and an evil man. Therefore whoever among you desires to gain the effect of his prayers should first cleanse the dwelling of his heart from every defilement through true repentance; then he should strive to inflame himself by the exercise of holy compunction; afterward he should continually feed his mind in the practice of the love of God, so that he may never thereafter interrupt the good that he has begun.

But after, with the desire of divine love always increasing in him, he has by long practice brought the exercise of holy purity into habit, and the mind has acquired the necessary firmness in a good purpose, then at last the likeness of divine communion is gradually introduced, and prayer is shaped into its proper state. For the more the mind, stirred in the love of God, advances, the sooner prayer will obtain from the Lord what it asks. For only the love of God makes prayer powerful; and the more fervently the mind burns in it, the more easily it will gain the effect of its petitions. For if you love much, you can do much. God is charity, and he who remains in charity remains in God, and God in him. But he in whom God remains has nothing stronger than himself, except God alone.

There are among you some weak and feeble in spirit who already consider themselves strong and perfect in the way of the Lord. Their devotion is rare and bound to the hour; they boast that they have approached the kingdom of heaven without the wedding garment, although they still lie far below. To these I say again what I have often said: no one suddenly becomes the highest; and unless a monk has subdued all his animal passions by mortifications of the flesh, he does not reach the lofty mountain of divine contemplation. For whoever, placed in a monastery under authority and obedience, follows his own will contrary to monastic institutions is not worthy to be heard by the Lord in his prayers.

Those of whom we speak are great and wise in their own eyes. Loving their own will, they murmur at everything, and whatever they themselves have not arranged they think badly done. They examine the actions of their brothers with curious inquiry, but neglect and despise the discernment of their own negligences. In their conduct they are loose and disorderly, in speech frivolous and noisy, unwilling for obedience, and always slow and full of sloth in good works. They neglect the common exercises of the order and give themselves to private ones according to their own will. And as long as they remain in their own rashness, they do not rise up to the true love of God.

Let monks who love their own will hear what the Lord says: ‘I have not come to do my own will, but the will of the Father who sent me.’ Brothers, attend to this teacher; unless you live in imitation of him, you will pray without fruit. For the Lord gladly hears the prayer of the one who keeps his commandments in truth. But the one who transgresses God’s commandment, and follows with desire the pleasures of the flesh and the comforts of the world, his prayer is not heard. For God, just and upright, is believed to hear your prayer gladly when mind and good life pray together. But if someone’s way of life displeases God, how will his prayer please him? For God is spirit, and the man who walks not in the spirit but in the flesh certainly does not draw near to God.

Therefore you, monk, who do not walk according to the purity of the Gospel and the Rule, who love your own will and not God’s, know that you will not feel the efficacy of your prayers unless you first amend your life. For unless the conduct of your life agrees with your prayer, you will not obtain what you ask. Thus you cry to the Lord with a loud voice when, even with the tongue silent, you cry out by good works. For good prayers and an evil way of life merit nothing good. Therefore do what a certain wise man advised: if you have sinned, do not add to it again, but pray also concerning your former sins, that they may be forgiven you. Prepare your soul before prayer, and do not be like a man who tempts God.

Let the mind be pure and burning in the love of the Lord; let the conscience be clean and strengthened in the purpose of holy conduct, and prayer will be pleasing to divine ears. If you pray otherwise, you will obtain nothing. He prays well who lives well, and he who prays with holy works obtains easily. Finally, prayer is made very efficacious and pleasing in the sight of God if at all times it is consonant with the Passion of our Lord Jesus Christ. For Christ is God and man, the mediator between God and us, through whom we have secure access to the Father, as he himself bears witness in the holy Gospel: ‘No one comes to the Father except through me.’

Therefore let no day pass on which, in prayer, you do not give thanks to Christ at least once: he healed the illnesses of your soul by the pain of his wounds; he poured out his blood for you; and by his most innocent death he redeemed you, wretched as you were, from the destruction of eternal death. A man is certainly most ungrateful and unworthy of the name Christian if he is so forgetful of such great benefits of Christ that he does not give him thanks daily, at least once. But the monk, who is commanded to be dead to the world and to live for God alone, should continually carry the memory of the Lord’s Passion in his breast, always giving thanks to his Redeemer, always blessing, always adoring, and daily, indeed as often as possible at every moment, turning over with tears and compassion the bitterness of Christ’s wounds.

Every day, after each of the regular prayers which the Greeks call canonical, with knees bent and mind lifted to heaven, let him say: I give you thanks, and blessing you I adore you, O mediator of God and men, Christ Jesus, sweetest God and man: for your most holy incarnation, for your birth from the Virgin, for your circumcision, for the flight into Egypt and the seven years in exile that you endured for us. I adore you, Lord Savior Jesus Christ, for your labors, for hunger and thirst, cold and nakedness; for your journeys, for your teaching and preaching, for your miracles, for the calling of the nations, and for the justification of sinners. I give you thanks, Lord, Son of the living God, for the pain of your wounds; I bless you for the outpouring of your most holy blood; I adore you for the death that you endured most innocently; and I beg you, through all your Passion, mercy, and clemency, have mercy on me now and in the hour of my death.

A devout monk should recite this very brief prayer at least seven times a day, and whenever he passes before an image of the Crucified, he should adore Christ in the same way, with fervor of mind. Whoever is a true lover of Christ always thinks of Christ, speaks of Christ, and venerates Christ; he does not doubt that Christ is everywhere present to him. The Passion of Christ is never absent from his memory, and because of the greatness of love he forgets himself and is wholly occupied in Christ in mind and desire. Whoever burns in spirit with the love of Christ despises earthly things, conquers the flesh, and desires only heavenly things. Awake, he always thinks of Christ with desire; asleep, he dreams of Christ; and sighing continually, he cries out with Saint Ignatius: ‘My love has been crucified.’ He joins sigh to sigh, and Jesus, whom he loves with his whole heart, he sees present not with the eyes but with the spirit.

When the desire of love presses upon him, as often as he looks upon the Crucified, he is at once dissolved into tears, and, burning with the fire of charity, cries aloud: O love stronger than all strengths, how sweetly he burns who is inflamed with desire for Christ. He spoke rightly who said, ‘Love is strong as death.’ For what can be stronger than the love of Christ, by whose ardor the holy martyrs, despising so many thousands of kinds of death, overcame them? O my crucified love, have mercy on me; grant me to feel that sweetness of your love of which I speak, so that I may always carry your most bitter Passion in my heart.

I know, my brothers, I know that there is no more efficacious manner of praying, nor one more useful for us who are still weak, than in the memory and power of the Passion of our Lord Jesus Christ. Therefore, since it is clear that without a mediator there is no ascent of the mind to God, every good work must be begun in the name of Christ, because then we pour out prayers to God profitably, when with a purified conscience we pray through his only-begotten Son.

On the guarding and origin of thoughts: Homily Nine.

That One Must Pray Without Interruption.

...that they may be forgiven you. Before prayer, prepare your soul, and do not be like a man who tempts God. Let your mind be pure and burn in the love of the Lord; let your conscience be clean and strengthened in the purpose of holy conduct, and your prayer will be pleasing to divine ears. If you pray otherwise, you will obtain nothing. He who lives well in his works prays well, and he who prays with holy works obtains easily. Finally, prayer is made very effective and pleasing in the sight of God if it is at all times in harmony with the passion of our Lord Jesus Christ. For he is God and man, the mediator between God and us, through whom we have a safe access to the Father, as he himself bears witness in the holy Gospel: “No one,” he says, “comes to the Father except through me.”

Therefore let no day pass in which you do not, at least once, give thanks in prayer to Christ, who by the pain of his wounds willed the sicknesses of your soul to be healed, who poured out his blood for you, and by his most innocent death redeemed you, wretched as you were, from the destruction of eternal death. A man is certainly most ungrateful and unworthy of the Christian name if he has been so forgetful of Christ’s many benefits that he does not every day, at least once, return acts of thanksgiving to him. But the monk, who has died to the world and is commanded to live for God alone, should continually carry in his breast the memory of the Lord’s passion: always giving thanks to his Redeemer, always blessing him, always adoring him, and daily, indeed as far as possible at every moment, turning over in his mind with tears and compassion the bitterness of Christ’s wounds.

Every day, after each of the regular prayers which the Greeks call canonical, with knees bent and mind lifted up to heaven, let him say:

I give thanks to you, and blessing you I adore you, O mediator of God and men, most sweet Christ Jesus, God and man: for your most holy incarnation; for your birth from the Virgin; for your circumcision; for your flight into Egypt and the seven-year exile which you endured for us. I adore you, Lord Savior Jesus Christ, for your labors, for hunger and thirst, cold and nakedness; for your journeys; for your teaching and preaching; for your miracles; for the calling of the nations; and for the justification of sinners. I give thanks to you, Lord, Son of the living God, for the pain of your wounds. I bless you for the shedding of your most holy blood. I adore you for the death which you most innocently endured, and I ask you, through all your passion, pity, and mercy: have mercy on me now and in the hour of my death.

With this very brief prayer the devout monk should pray at least seven times in the day, and whenever he passes before an image of the Crucified, he should in the same way adore Christ with fervor of mind. Whoever is a true lover of Christ always thinks of Christ, speaks of Christ, and venerates Christ; he does not allow Christ to be absent from himself, but senses him present everywhere. The passion of Christ is never absent from his memory, and from the greatness of love he forgets himself, wholly occupied in Christ in mind and desire. Whoever burns in spirit with the love of Christ contemns earthly things, conquers the flesh, and desires only heavenly things. Such a man, waking, always thinks of God with desire; sleeping, he dreams of Christ; and, continually sighing, he cries out with blessed Ignatius: “My love has been crucified.” With continual sighs he sighs, and Jesus, whom he loves with his whole heart, he sees not with the eyes but with the spirit present to him.

When the desire of love presses upon him, as often as he gazes on the Crucified, he is at once dissolved in tears and, burning with the fire of charity, cries out: O love, stronger than all strengths, how sweetly he burns who is kindled with desire for Christ. Rightly did he speak who says: “Love is strong as death.” For what can be stronger than the love of Christ, in whose ardor so many thousands of martyrs overcame, despising every kind of death? O love of the crucified Jesus, have mercy on me. Grant me to feel that sweetness of your love of which I speak, so that I may always carry in my heart your most bitter passion. I know, my brothers, I know that there is no more effective manner of praying, nor any more useful for us who are still weak, than in the memory and power of the passion of our Lord Jesus Christ. Therefore, since it is clear that without meditation there is no ascent of the mind to God, every good work must be begun in the name of Christ, because then we pour out prayers to God profitably when, with purified conscience, we pray through his only-begotten Son.

On the Guarding and Origin of Thoughts. Homily Nine.

Solomon, that once most wise king, in the book which he entitled Proverbs, exhorting his son to true wisdom, said among other things: “My son, attend to my wisdom, and incline your ear to my prudence, that you may guard your thoughts, and that your lips may preserve discipline.” For it is no small part of wisdom, dearly beloved brothers, if a monk knows how to discern his thoughts prudently and to guard their movements carefully at all times. It is a great thing to think nothing evil; but it belongs to no less prudence to be able to repel evil thoughts from their beginning. For the deep inward state of the human heart is always mobile, changeable, manifold, and unstable; to observe its beginnings and restrain its movements is the highest prudence and a great sign of wisdom.

If I have called the heart of man a mill driven by perpetual motion, I have not strayed from the truth, especially if someone wishes to examine its restlessness very closely. For what else is the human heart than a kind of mill, always grinding something in motion? The human heart is never idle; it never rests without motion, but is always doing something. And just as an empty mill consumes itself by its own motion, so the heart of man shamefully runs down from within itself when it is not held fast by thought of the good. It is always moved toward some object with the desire of its love; it is always grinding, always acting, always occupied about something delightful, whether it is good or evil. As the body is not moved without the soul, so the mind of man does not remain fixed in the good unless it is continually restrained by holy exercises.

For this reason the wise man said in Ecclesiasticus: “Gladness of heart is the life of man, and a treasure without defect is holiness, and the rejoicing of a man is length of days. Have mercy on your soul, pleasing God, and contain yourself; gather your heart in his holiness, and drive sadness far away from you.” What is the gathering of the heart, if not the diligent guarding of thought? For he gathers his heart in the holiness of the Lord who continually observes the movements of his thoughts in the fear of God. And therefore Scripture says: “With all custody guard your heart, for from it life proceeds.”

Thoughts take the beginnings of their movements from those things which are presented by the senses, and they wander the more dangerously the less they are considered from the beginning by the judgment of reason. Therefore whoever desires to be watchful over his thoughts, whoever longs to gather his heart in the holiness of the Lord, should first guard his exterior senses most diligently and place the bridle of prudence on each of them. Hence, concerning hearing and sight, Scripture commands thus: “Hedge your ears with thorns, and do not listen to a wicked tongue; make doors for your mouth, and bars for your ears.” “Blessed is the man who stops his ears lest he hear bloodshed, and closes his eyes lest he see evil.” Likewise in holy Job we read: “I made a covenant with my eyes, that I would not even think about a virgin.”

But against those who have fallen through the service of their senses into the pit of sins, the Lord bears witness through the prophet Jeremiah: “Death has climbed in through your windows; it has entered your houses to destroy the little children outside and the young men from the streets.” Death enters our houses through the windows whenever an iniquitous thought is admitted into the mind through the service of sight or hearing. For the house is the conscience, of which Scripture says: “Unless you hold yourself constantly in the fear of the Lord, your house will quickly be overturned.” Therefore the house of conscience must be fortified by the fear of the Lord, and the senses also must be restrained most diligently, because he does not sufficiently guard his thoughts who does not turn his ears and eyes away from the occasion of evil.

And so, if you wish to be a prudent guardian of your thoughts and a true gatherer of the heart in the holiness of the Lord, hedge your ears with the thorns of compunction and the fear of God, lest they hear evil, and turn your eyes away, lest through sight they introduce sin. What you are not permitted to desire, do not look upon with desire; and what it is not expedient to have, do not think upon. Many have perished through the service of the senses, and because they go on carelessly looking at outward things, they have very often shamefully lost their inward goods. He who does not fear danger is certainly already near destruction, and he who presumes on his own strength runs hastily toward the ruin of sin. He who darts his eye into the flash of lightning will be blinded for a time, and he who gazes at the beauty of women will not be free from danger.

I praise the prudence of that monk who is alone in the midst of crowds and sets a censor over his senses, lest they admit anything evil into the house. He stations guards beside the gate, who with sword and club drive back those who rush in disorderly. Fear...

[segment 38]
...encouraging him to true wisdom, says among other things: “My son, attend to my wisdom, and incline your ear to my prudence, that you may guard your thoughts, and your lips may preserve discipline.” For it is no small part of wisdom, dearly beloved brothers, if a monk knows how to discern his thoughts prudently and to guard their movements carefully at all times. It is a great thing to think nothing evil; but it belongs to no lesser prudence to be able to repel evil thoughts from their very beginning. For, according to the profound judgment of the human heart, it is mobile, changeable, manifold, and always unstable; and to observe its beginnings and restrain its movements is the height of prudence and a great sign of wisdom. If I say that the heart of man is driven by perpetual motion, I shall not stray from the truth, especially if anyone wishes to examine its restlessness more particularly. For what else is the human heart than a kind of mill, always in motion, grinding something? The human heart is never idle; it never rests without motion, but is always doing something. And just as a mill, set in motion while empty, consumes itself, so the heart of man shamefully runs down from itself when thought of the good does not constrain it. It is always moved toward some object with the desire of its own love; it is always grinding, always acting, always occupied with something delightful, whether to its good or to its harm. Just as the body is not moved without the soul, so the mind of man does not remain in the good unless it has been continually restrained by holy exercises. Therefore the wise man said in Ecclesiasticus: “The joyfulness of the heart is the life of a man and a treasure without loss of holiness, and a man’s exultation is length of life. Have compassion on your soul, pleasing God, and restrain yourself; gather your heart in his holiness, and drive sadness far from you.” What is the gathering of the heart, if not the careful custody of thought? For he gathers his heart in the holiness of the Lord who continually observes the movements of his thoughts in the fear of God. And therefore Scripture says: “With all custody keep your heart, for from it life proceeds.”

Now thoughts take the beginnings of their movements from those things which are offered by the senses; and the more dangerously thought strays, the less carefully they are considered from the beginning by the judgment of reason. Therefore whoever desires to be watchful over his thoughts, whoever wishes to gather his heart in the holiness of the Lord, must first of all guard his outward senses most diligently and place the bridle of prudence upon each of them. Hence Scripture says about hearing and sight: “Hedge your ears with thorns, and do not listen to a wicked tongue; make doors and bars for your mouth, and bolts for your ears.” “Blessed is the man who stops his ears lest he hear bloodshed, and shuts his eyes lest he see evil.” In the book of Job, too, we read: “I made a covenant with my eyes, that I would not so much as think about a virgin.” But against those who have fallen into the pit of sinners by the ministry of the senses, the Lord protests thus through the prophet Jeremiah: “For death has climbed in through your windows; it has entered your houses, to destroy the little children outside and the young men from the streets.” Death enters our houses through the windows whenever an evil thought is admitted into the mind through the ministry of sight or hearing. For the house is the conscience, of which Scripture says: “If you do not constantly hold yourself in the fear of the Lord, your house will quickly be overthrown,” that is, your conscience. This house is very fragile and quickly collapses unless it is upheld by the continual exercise of the fear of God. Unless the Lord guards the house, in vain does the man keep watch who guards it.

Therefore the house of conscience must be upheld by the fear of the Lord, and the senses must also be most diligently restrained, because he is not sufficient to guard his thoughts who does not turn his ears and eyes away from the occasion of evil. And so, if you wish to be a prudent guardian of your thoughts and truly to gather your heart in the holiness of the Lord, hedge your ears with the thorns of compunction and the fear of God, so that they do not hear evil, and turn away your eyes, lest through sight they introduce destruction. What it is not lawful for you to covet, do not look upon with desire; what it is not expedient to have, do not think about. Many have perished through the ministry of the senses, and because they rashly go on looking at outward things, they have often shamefully lost their inward goods. He who does not fear danger is already certainly near destruction; and he who presumes upon his own strength runs hastily toward the ruin of sin. He who fixes his eye on the radiance of lightning will be blinded for the moment; and he who considers the beauty of a woman will not be free from danger. For this reason I praise the prudence of the monk who, in the midst of crowds, is alone, and sets a doorkeeper over his senses, lest he admit any evil thing to the mistress within. Guards are stationed by the gate, who with sword and shield repel assailants rushing in. For the holy fear of the Lord never deserts the mind of a wise man, but, like a strong champion always keeping watch, stands at the praise of his prince, and so guards the mistress of the heart. He turns his ears away from all scurrility of words and shuts his eyes so that they may not see evil. He always walks according to the twelfth degree of humility, with his gaze cast down to the ground, praying to the Lord with the prophet: “Turn my eyes away, lest they see vanity; give me life in your way, O Lord.”

Likewise I admonish you, brothers: direct your senses in the fear of the Lord, so that you may always be able to keep the dwelling of the heart clean and inviolate. Remember that Scripture says: “Be clean, you who carry the vessels of the Lord.” Consider, I ask, with how great a cleanness and purity of mind you ought to shine, who not only carry the vessels of the Lord by reason of the priestly order, but, what is more, you yourselves are the vessels of the Lord. For the holy lover of Christ, Paul, says: “Do you not know that your bodies are members of Christ?” And again he says: “Do you not know that your members are the temple of the Holy Spirit, who is in you, whom you have from God, and that you are not your own?” And elsewhere: “For the temple of God is holy, which you are.” And concerning Paul himself, in the Acts of the Apostles, the Lord said to Ananias: “Go, for this man is a vessel of election for me.”

The monk who performs priestly office in the Church of God is bound by a twofold reason to the observance of most holy purity. For Christ’s soldier, because of the vow he made to God, must be pure, holy, and continually tireless in good works; and by reason of the priestly ministry he must be circumspect and most diligently watchful. For it is written: “Cursed is the man who does the work of God negligently.” You are ministers of almighty God, who daily offer his Son at the altar; his work is committed into your hands, and therefore great purity of mind and body is required in your way of life. Therefore, so that chaste purity of mind may remain, the instability of thought must be restrained. The senses must be guarded at all times, for through them the integrity of the mind is corrupted. For you are dead to the world and have already begun to live for Christ. It is not lawful for you to think except holy things, nor is it fitting for you, ministers of God, to love anything earthly. For he whom you serve, O monks, is God; he whom you minister to, my brothers, is almighty. Live pure, holy, and innocent, because it is not fitting for you to be occupied with worldly business, you who have been called to God’s service. Let the love of God and purity of heart grow in you with time, so that the longer you live in this world, the holier you may become. Always turn yourselves away from every evil, so that you may be able to remain without failing in good thoughts. Always lift up the eyes of your mind to heaven, and do not occupy the sight of the flesh with perishable things. Close your hearing also, and yours, to every useless, vain, and foolish speech; always think with fear about the future judgment of God. Do not pollute your hands with unlawful and shameful contact with any things whatsoever, so that you may be able to offer the Son of God with a clean body and a pure conscience.

In food and drink it is not for monks to distinguish flavors, nor is it fitting for soldiers of Christ to love bodily delights. Nature requires little, and rustic foods are enough to nourish it; but appetite, unless it is restrained by the command of reason, is carried off toward unlawful things, and through it the simplicity of nature is violated. Therefore flee excess in all things, and in your manner of life observe regular temperance. Let none of you patiently listen to one who disparages his brothers or speaks ill of his neighbor. Let each one of you restrain his tongue from useless speech, and let a good monk blush to speak ill of any Christian. Let that threat of the Lord in the holy Gospel never depart from your memory, where he says: “Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks. A good man from a good treasure brings forth good things, and an evil man from an evil treasure brings forth evil things. But I say to you that every idle word which men have spoken, they shall render an account of it on the day of judgment.” O sentence greatly to be feared! If an account is demanded for idle speech, what do detraction, scurrility, and foul speech deserve? Or if a useless word is judged liable to the reason of strict examination, what answer will he give who so readily speaks what is evil and destructive? An idle word is every utterance of human beings that is brought forth without just usefulness and lacks the reason of legitimate necessity. Therefore, brothers, consider your words, and let no one speak a word not pertaining to the matter either of just usefulness or of necessary reason.

[segment 39]
