*On the Custody and Origin of Thoughts*

The holy fear of the Lord never leaves the mind of a wise man; rather, like a strong and watchful fighter who always stands at the side of his prince, so it guards the mistress of the heart. It turns its ears away from every scurrility of words, and closes its eyes lest they see evil. It always walks according to the twelfth degree of humility, with its gaze fixed on the ground, praying to the Lord with the prophet: “Turn my eyes away, lest they see vanity; in your way give me life, O Lord.”

Thus I admonish you, brothers: direct your senses in the fear of the Lord, so that you may always be able to keep the dwelling of the heart clean and inviolate. Remember the saying of Scripture: “Be clean, you who bear the vessels of the Lord.” Consider, I pray, with what great cleanness and purity of mind you ought to shine, you who not only by reason of the priestly order bear the vessels of the Lord, but, what is more, are yourselves also vessels of the Lord. For the holy Christ-loving Paul says: “Do you not know that your bodies are members of Christ?” And again he says: “Or do you not know that your members are the temple of the Holy Spirit, who is in you, whom you have from God, and that you are not your own?” And elsewhere: “For the temple of God is holy, which you are.” And concerning this same Paul, in the Acts of the Apostles the Lord said to Ananias: “Go, for this man is a vessel of election for me.”

The monk who exercises the priestly order in the Church of God is bound by a twofold reason to the observance of most holy purity. For Christ’s soldier must, because of the vow he has made to God, be pure, holy, and continually tireless in good works; and by reason of the priestly ministry, circumspect and most diligently watchful. For it is written: “Cursed is the man who does the work of God negligently.” You are ministers of almighty God, who daily sacrifice his Son at the altar; his work is entrusted to your hands, and therefore great purity of mind and body is required in your way of life.

Therefore, so that chaste purity of mind may remain, the instability of thought must be restrained. The senses, through which the integrity of the mind is corrupted, must be guarded at all times. For you are dead to the world and have now begun to live for Christ. It is not lawful for you to think anything except what is holy, nor is it fitting for you, as ministers of God, to love anything earthly. For he whom you serve, O monks, is God; he to whom you minister, my brothers, is almighty. Live pure, holy, and innocent lives, because it is not fitting for you to be occupied with the business of the world, you who have been called to the warfare of God. Let the love of God and purity of heart grow in you with time, so that the longer you live in this world, the holier you may become.

Always turn your sight away from every evil, so that you may be able to remain without failure in good thoughts. Always raise the eyes of your mind to heaven, and do not occupy the sight of the flesh with perishable things. Always close your hearing also against every useless, vain, and foolish word, and always think with dread upon the future judgment of God. Do not pollute your hands by unlawful and shameful contact with any things, so that you may be able to sacrifice the Son of God with a clean body and a pure conscience.

In food and drink it is not for monks to distinguish flavors, nor is it fitting for Christ’s soldiers to love bodily delights. Nature requires few things, and rustic foods by which it may be nourished; but appetite, unless it is restrained by the command of reason, is carried off toward unlawful things, by which the simplicity of nature is violated. Flee excess in all things, and preserve regular temperance in your manner of living.

Let none of you patiently listen to someone disparaging his brother or speaking ill of his neighbor. Let each one of you restrain his tongue from useless speech, and let a good monk blush to speak ill of Christian people. Let that threat of the Lord in the holy Gospel never depart from your memory, where he says: “Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks. A good man brings forth good things from a good treasure, and an evil man brings forth evil things from an evil treasure. But I say to you that every idle word that men have spoken, they will render an account of it on the day of judgment.”

O sentence greatly to be feared! If an account is demanded for idle speech, what do detraction, scurrility, and foul speech deserve? Or if a useless word is judged liable to the reckoning of a strict examination, what will he answer who so readily speaks what is evil and destructive? An idle word is every utterance of human beings that has been brought forth without just usefulness and lacks the reason of lawful necessity. Therefore, brothers, consider your words, and let no one speak a word that does not pertain to the matter, or that lacks just usefulness or necessary reason.

[segment 40]
Homily IX. Fol. XXI.

...of necessity. You have heard in these words the dread sentence of the Lord, and you are not ignorant of the command of our holy lawgiver in the Rule: “As for scurrilities and idle words, or words that provoke laughter, we condemn them to perpetual exclusion in every place, and we do not permit the disciple to open his mouth for such speech.”

Here the holy father Benedict forbids monks three kinds of words, and shuts them out everywhere by perpetual exclusion: namely scurrilous words, idle words, and words that provoke laughter. What “scurrility” is, however, is made clear to us by grammatical interpretation. For scurrility is the opposite of urbanity and modesty; it is a kind of wanton jesting that provokes laughter, an immoderate amusement in words and gestures without reverence or propriety, not in accord with reason, nor befitting a virtuous and good man, nor becoming to the wise. It is called scurrility from the scurra, the buffoon, who frequents the houses of the wealthy and, by wanton and dishonorable words and gestures, provokes his hearers to laughter for a reward or small coin.

This kind of speech the holy father Benedict forbids to monks and has utterly condemned, because those who serve God ought in his sight always to abstain from shameful and dishonorable words. Between scurrility, idle speech, and much speaking there seems to be this distinction: the first sins by dishonorable jesting, the second by lack of usefulness, the third by excess. The first is shameful and dishonorable; the second is superfluous and without use. Foolish speech is rightly joined to these, since it is forbidden not only to monks but also to all Christians. Hence the holy apostle of Christ says: “But fornication and all uncleanness or covetousness, let it not even be named among you, as befits saints; nor filthiness, nor foolish talk, nor scurrility, which has no purpose, but rather thanksgiving.”

Likewise every idle word is forbidden to all monks, because, as you have heard, it is prohibited by the Lord in the holy Gospel. Words that provoke laughter are also not becoming to Christian men, and much less do they befit cloistered monks. For the monk, who is dead to the world, is commanded always to think upon his last things; and for the love of God he is ordered everywhere to despise laughter and every insolence of the present life. Laughter is the outward loosening of the mouth that reveals an immoderate inward gladness of the mind. But nothing that exceeds modesty and temperance is permitted to monks, whose life and conversation does not follow flesh and blood, but is compared to angelic purity.

Untimely laughter, poured forth with immoderate levity, is a sign of an undisciplined mind and shows a man forgetful of moderate propriety and modesty. But the monk, who is commanded to imitate Christ and to renounce the world on the condition that he may live in greater purity, must beware of all things that overthrow the manly steadiness of the mind. He must despise all the vain joys of this world for desire of the heavenly homeland, and keep himself always unconquered in every maturity of mind and body. And just as immoderate laughter is forbidden to monks in every circumstance and place, so too they are forbidden to speak words that provoke laughter in their hearers.

Therefore now, dearest brothers, attend to yourselves, and carefully consider how you ought to restrain your tongues. Recall often to memory what the prophet says in the psalm: “I said, I will guard my ways, so that I may not sin with my tongue. I set a guard upon my mouth; I was mute and humbled, and kept silence even from good things.” Recognize that in every place you stand before the Lord God, who is present everywhere, who sees all things, hears all things, knows all things, understands all things, and from whose knowledge nothing is hidden; to whose exact examination all things are reserved. Live in his sight in such a way that you may be able to come securely to his judgment.

Whenever, with our permission or that of the prior, you come together for conversation, take the greatest care, according to the command of our lawgiver, to avoid scurrilous words, idle words, and words that stir up laughter. Let your speech be full of modesty: not loud, not frivolous, not rash, not contentious, lest exact accounts be demanded for them. Likewise flee much speaking, because it is not without sin and makes a monk wearisome to all people. Let the seniors speak, not just anything whatever, but words of saving instruction, by which they may instruct the minds of hearers for the seed of righteousness and kindle them more fully to the love of God. For our holy lawgiver says: “It befits the master to speak and teach, and the disciple to be silent and listen.” And therefore let the seniors speak useful things and teach; let the juniors, however, listen in silence and learn.

And if anything must be asked of the prior, let it be asked with all humility, reverence, and subjection, lest anyone seem to speak more than is fitting. A man is known from his words, and he who multiplies speeches in his mouth is foolish in heart. A man’s speech is the witness of his conscience; and whoever loves modesty and innocence of heart ought to have modest words, free from vanity. For you are ministers of the living God, my brothers. Let no vain, useless, or idle speech go out of your mouth, but only what is good for the edification of hearers, so that it may increase charity in their minds and enlighten the heart in the knowledge and love of the highest truth.

Precious is the tongue of a monk that says nothing at all except what is holy; and worthy of blessing is the mouth from which nothing proceeds except what is divine. Let each person keep his heart clean from every wicked thought, because one who is good in heart does not speak an evil word; and one who truly loves God does not utter idle speech. Apply all care and consideration to your thoughts, because evil must chiefly be watched there where it takes the beginning of its progress, so that as soon as it has begun to arise it may be cut off at the root. For there is no thought of evil so weak that it will not grow with time, if it is nourished by the consent of the thinker.

Therefore evil must be conquered immediately at the beginning, while it is still weak, because when delay intervenes, evil, however small it seemed in the beginning, gains strength. A great fire often arises from a small spark, and from a little thought a great sin sometimes rises up. When thought gives birth to delight, it soon tends to break out into consent, and afterward, if it can, to cast itself headlong even into deed. But when the will has given consent through delight to an evil thought for action, even if what the soul desires is not accomplished, the sin is already consummated before God. But what does Scripture say? “When sin has been consummated, it brings forth death.”

For almighty God looks upon the will of a human being, whether he wills good or evil. Therefore in the holy Gospel he says: “Whoever looks at a woman to desire her has already committed adultery with her in his heart.” God counts the will as the deed whenever a person cannot bring into effect what he wished. Therefore again and again I admonish you, brothers, and with all the diligence I can I bear witness, that according to the holy apostle you should walk carefully, not as fools, redeeming the time, because the days are evil. Watch most carefully for the snares of the evil demon, and resist evil and unclean thoughts immediately from the beginning, because remedy is considered much too late when the disease has grown strong through long duration.

Accustom yourselves at all times to dwell upon good and holy thoughts, and you will easily be able to overcome the insolence of evil ones. For just as vessels filled with good liquids admit no external corruption, so minds filled with good thoughts do not admit the intemperance of evil ones. For the eye of the heart is that by which God is seen, and through purity of conscience one comes to the glory of eternal blessedness. For the Lord says in the holy Gospel: “Blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God.” A clean heart is the dwelling place of the Holy Spirit; and where an evil thought is not admitted, God without doubt abides. A clean heart is the seat of wisdom; and where purity of mind is guarded, no dangers of adversity will do harm.

Be mindful of the Scripture which says: “Perverse thoughts separate from God, but proven virtue corrects fools. For wisdom will not enter a malicious soul, nor dwell in a body subject to sins. For the Holy Spirit of discipline will flee deceit, and will withdraw from thoughts that are without understanding.” A perverse thought is any thought admitted contrary to evangelical purity; by contact with it, the mind of the one thinking is defiled whenever it delights in thinking over and turning over those things that are opposed to God. These perverse thoughts separate the thinker’s mind from God and little by little cast it down into the depth of evils. For an evil and perverse thought, once it has penetrated, first poisons by the venom of delight, then clouds reason, afterward draws the will into consenting to the evil suggestion, and finally consummates sin in deed. From then on, an evil once presumed is done more freely, and all shame or fear is removed by habit. Sin is joined to sin, and little by little the habit of doing evil is strengthened.

[segment 41]
--- Page 050 ---

All things. IX. Fol. XXII.

...generates the necessity of sinning. Here is the hardest dominion of death; here is the utmost depth of evils. Whoever has once entered this abyss of sins rarely, indeed not easily, rises again to purity of heart without the special grace of God and the very great labor of repentance. By his own will the sinner has fallen into this pit of death; but without God’s mercy he will not be able to rise again. Most harsh is the yoke of evil habit: if anyone has once submitted his neck to it, he rarely recovers his former liberty of spirit.

All these things are worked by perverse thought when it is admitted into the mind, and through delight is changed into consent and action. For no thought is so small that it does not gradually grow if it is not driven from the mind. Blessed, therefore, is that monk who casts all his thought upon the Lord; who always and everywhere denies a place to depraved thoughts; who despises all the perishing riches of the world; who scorns all bodily comforts. He prepares the purity of his heart for his Lord; he loves nothing in this world except the Lord; he directs all his thoughts to the glory of the Savior, and observes his commandments most diligently.

What monk is happier than he who shows himself to the world not as a citizen, but as a pilgrim? He lives in the world as though he were not in the world; indeed, he is greater than the world, whose whole conversation is in heaven.

Consider now, therefore, dearest brothers, the brevity of this time, and always remember that you are mortal and will soon pass away. For here we have no abiding city, but we seek the one to come; and since we know that so many thousands of men have gone before us through death, let us consider how soon we too must necessarily die. Now indeed a short time, but a very acceptable one, is granted to us, in which we may work the fruits of eternal life. For now is the time for labor; now is the hour for sowing good, because after death each person will receive the reward of his labors, when no further opportunity for labor is granted.

Therefore, my brothers, anticipate this hour of death with holy works, and do not be willing now to fail in the sowing of labor, so that in eternal recompense you may be able to rejoice without end. Let no day pass in which each one of you has not become better, and has not added something new to the good works he has already set before him. Always think on the value of the future harvest, and no greatness of labor will terrify you.

On the Daily Struggle of Temptation: All things, tenth.

In yesterday’s sermon, most beloved brothers, I admonished your charities to resist the beginnings of evil thoughts, and to prepare yourselves with all your strength, by good and holy works, for the prize of the future reward. But since, as Scripture bears witness, the life of man upon earth is a temptation, I have thought it necessary to discuss this very matter in your hearing, as much as the Lord grants, so that, having been forewarned, you may be able to fight more strongly against the invisible adversary.

You are not ignorant of the words of the wise man, who says in Ecclesiasticus: “Son, when you come to the service of God, stand in justice, and prepare your soul for temptation.” Let no one think himself free from temptation, brothers, as long as he walks in this mortal way of life; but the more anyone advances in the fear of God, the more cautiously he should know that he must live. For holy Pope Gregory says: “The more our enemy sees that rebellion is being made against him, the more fully he strives to attack. For he neglects to strike those whom he feels he possesses by undisturbed right.” The devil pursues the one fleeing from him, and tries to overthrow the one who rebels against his rule.

You, men, the more you advance in spiritual way of life, the more urgently you must keep watch against the adversary of your salvation. For, as Saint John Chrysostom says, our adversary never sleeps, but is always watchful for the destruction of the human race; and none of us can resist him except one who has learned to keep watch at all times in the fear of the Lord.

And Saint Leo, pope of the city of Rome, says in a certain sermon: “The ancient enemy does not cease to spread the snares of temptations everywhere, so that in whatever way he can, by corrupting the faith of believers, he may darken it. He knows to whom he should bring the enticements of the flesh; into whom he should pour the poison of envy; before whom he should set the incitements of lust. He knows whom he should disturb with grief, whom he should deceive with joy, whom he should oppress with fear, whom he should seduce with admiration.”

[unclear]

[segment 43]
--- Page 052 ---

...let us recall all the words of him who says: “You who have been weaned from milk, you who have been drawn away from the breast, expect tribulation upon tribulation, hope upon hope, yet a little while, because of the malice of lips, because of the evil tongue.” Those weaned and drawn away from the breast are men advanced in age, whose part it is to take up tribulation upon tribulation for God, so that they may deserve to gain hope upon hope, as sacred Scripture says: “But we glory also in our tribulations, knowing that tribulation works patience, and patience testing, and testing hope. And hope does not confound, because the love of God has been poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us.” For through many tribulations we must enter the kingdom of God.

For by temptations we become known to ourselves, and we learn whether we truly fear God. Hence the Lord speaks through Moses to the people, saying: “The Lord your God tests you, that he may know whether you love him with all your heart and with all your soul.” This world, dearest brothers, must be compared to a furnace, in which the fire of temptation and adversity continually burns, and through which we must necessarily pass. In this furnace of tribulation, straw is burned up, but gold and silver are cleansed from every stain. Straw is turned to ash; gold is reserved for the adornment and beauty of the royal palace.

The furnace, I say, is the world; the fire is temptation or tribulation; the gold is righteous people; the straw is the unrighteous and sinners; the craftsman who purifies the gold is almighty God himself, who favors our progress and cooperates by grace toward the merit of virtue. Therefore let us do what God wills; let us patiently bear for love of him what he lays upon us, because, as the holy lover of Christ says, “the sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come that shall be revealed in us.”

Hence Saint Jerome says: “Who would not wish to be troubled? Who would not desire to be cursed, so that he might deserve to be praised by Christ’s voice and rewarded with a heavenly and abundant recompense? Would that, for the name and righteousness of my Lord, the whole crowd of unbelievers might persecute and trouble me. Would that this whole world might rise up to my reproach, provided only that I may deserve to be praised by Christ and to hasten toward the reward of his promise.”

And the wise man says in Ecclesiasticus: “The furnace tests the potter’s vessels, and the temptation of tribulation tests righteous men.” Therefore, whichever of you, my brothers, desires to reach the kingdom of heaven, whoever takes care above all things to please God, let him willingly learn to take up, for love of him, the tribulations of temptations, and joyfully endure whatever adversity may befall. For there is none of us who does not need daily purification, none who does not fail almost at every moment.

Therefore, if we love our own salvation, if we demand that our offenses be forgiven us and that we be cleansed from every stain of heart, let us patiently bear whatever adversity may happen to us, because the evils of tribulation, which the soul patiently tolerates for love of God, purify it more than the good works it now performs of its own accord in this world.

In this place a great matter for suffering has been left to us, whom want presses heavily, poverty weighs down, and the debt of borrowed money also constrains. What adversity do we not experience in this monastery? What squalls and storms do we not suffer, brothers? By what hardships are we not shaken? None of you can be ignorant how desolate all the goods of this monastery were when, fifteen years ago, regular observance was introduced. But the tribulation of common want rages more severely against me, since I must provide you with the necessities of life, and very often I do not sufficiently know from where I may repay.

Yet your patience consoles my spirit, and the hope that you have conceived in the Lord, the savior of the humble, so that I do not doubt that his mercy will come to our aid in time. And I hear him saying to me through Isaiah the prophet: “Behold, I have refined you, but not as silver; I have chosen you in the furnace of poverty.” I will gladly have patience in the poverty that, with God so ordering it, we now suffer, so that after death we may deserve to come to the riches of eternity. For the more, in this pilgrimage of ours, we have been afflicted by persecutions, poverty, want, labors, and tribulations, the greater rewards we shall obtain in the future recompense, with the rewarder of the righteous granting them.

Therefore, my brothers, endure patiently with me whatever almighty God has preordained that we suffer, because, as Saint Pope Gregory says, confidence in almighty God is then certain...
