Homily 10. Fol. xxiii.

let us diligently recall, saying: You who have been weaned from milk, who have been drawn away from the breast, expect tribulation upon tribulation, hope upon hope, yet a little while because of the malice of lips, because of the malicious tongue. Those weaned and drawn away from the breasts are men advanced in age, whose part it is to undergo tribulation upon tribulation for God, so that they may deserve to receive hope upon hope, as sacred Scripture says: But we glory also in our tribulations, knowing that tribulation works patience, and patience trial, and trial hope. And hope does not confound, because the charity of God has been poured out in our hearts through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us. For through many tribulations we must enter into the kingdom of God. Through temptations we become known to ourselves and discover whether we truly fear God. Hence the Lord speaks through Moses to the people, saying: The Lord your God tests you, so that it may be known whether you love him with all your heart and with all your soul.

This world, most beloved brothers, must be compared to a furnace, in which the fire of temptation and adversity continually burns, and through which we must necessarily pass. In this furnace of tribulation the chaff is burned, but gold and silver are cleansed from every stain; the chaff is turned to ashes, while the gold is reserved for the adornment and splendor of the royal palace. The world, I say, is the furnace; the fire is temptation or tribulation; the gold is the righteous man; the chaff is the useless and sinful man; and the artisan who purifies the gold is almighty God himself, who favors our progress and, through grace, cooperates toward the merit of virtue.

Therefore let us do what God wills; what he imposes, let us bear patiently for love of him, since the true lover of Christ says: The sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come that will be revealed in us. Hence Saint Jerome says: Who would not wish to be troubled? Who would not desire to be cursed, and to deserve to be praised by the voice of Christ and rewarded with an abundant heavenly recompense? Would that, for the Lord’s name and for justice, the whole crowd of unbelievers might persecute and trouble me. Would that this whole world might rise up to my reproach, provided only that I might deserve to be praised by Christ and hasten toward the reward he has promised. And the wise man says in Ecclesiasticus: The furnace tests the potter’s vessels, and the temptation of tribulation tests righteous men.

Therefore, whoever of you, O my brothers, desires to reach the kingdom of heaven, whoever above all things is concerned to please God, let him learn willingly to undergo the tribulations of temptations for love of him, and gladly to endure whatever adverse thing occurs. For there is none of us who does not need daily purification, none who does not often fail at every moment. Therefore, if we love our own salvation, if we ask that our offenses be forgiven us and beg to be cleansed from every stain of heart, let us patiently endure whatever adversity befalls us, since the evil of tribulation, when patiently endured for the love of God, purifies souls more than the good works that one now performs in this world by his own will.

Great material for suffering has been left to us in this place: lack presses us heavily, poverty weighs us down, and debt owed to others constrains us. What adversity do we not experience in this monastery? What storms and tempests do we not suffer, brothers? By what inconveniences are we not worn down? None of you can be ignorant how utterly desolate all the goods of this cenobium were when regular observance was introduced fifteen years ago. But the tribulation of want as a companion rages more severely against me, since I am obliged to provide you with the necessities of life, and often I do not sufficiently know from where I shall give them. Yet your patience consoles my spirit, and the hope that you have humbly conceived in the Lord Savior, so that we do not doubt that mercy will come in due time; and I hear him saying to me through Isaiah the prophet: Behold, I have refined you, but not as silver; I have chosen you in the furnace of poverty.

Let us therefore gladly have patience in the poverty that we now suffer according to God’s ordinance, so that after death we may deserve to come to the riches of eternity. For the more we have been afflicted in this our pilgrimage with imperfections, poverty, want, labors, and tribulations, the greater rewards we shall obtain in the future recompense, when the just Lord bestows them. Learn, my brothers, to endure patiently whatever almighty God has foreordained for us, since Saint Pope Gregory says: Faith in almighty God is then certain when adversity is endured for a good work, and in this world the scourge of God is received with joy, so that a more beautiful reward may be preserved in the future recompense of the saints.

Again he also says: Those whom the adversity of temporal life humbles take joy from the hope of an everlasting inheritance; for divine dispensation would not restrain them, for their instruction, under the rule of discipline, if it did not perceive that they were to be saved forever. Afflicted, we lose earthly things; but by humbly sustaining affliction we multiply heavenly goods for ourselves. For the present life is a road by which we hasten toward our homeland, and therefore we are often touched in it by disturbances, lest we suppose our homeland to be here.

Therefore let the monk rejoice in his adversity; let the tempted man, to whom the world has been crucified, be glad. Let him trust that he belongs to the fellowship of God, who with a pure conscience always does good and receives the opposite in tribulation. For it is a very true sign of divine mercy if a Christian man is struck by various adversities in this world, while he serves God with a pure conscience. Some whom God sees to be able to sin greatly, he forestalls with bodily weakness so that they may not sin, and mercifully scourges them for their salvation, seeing that it is more useful for their souls to be broken by illnesses unto salvation than to live healthy unto damnation. He wills others to be poor, whom he has foreknown to be unable to use riches for salvation.

For many people have been cast down by the wealth and delights of this world; and that unexpected enemy has struck them more readily because, from long habit of ease, he found them negligent. Hence holy men, when they trust that they are making great progress by prosperity in virtues, rejoice also to be exercised by temptations through a certain moderation of heavenly dispensation, because they guard the glory of the virtues they have received all the more strongly when, shaken by the assault of temptation, they recognize their own weakness more humbly. The way of tribulation is the shortest road to Christ, and a person is found on it more quickly than in tranquility. For the holy bishop Ambrose says: Many seek Christ in leisure and do not find him; and there are those who seek him amid persecutions and quickly find him. In the dangers of his faithful ones he is present, as he speaks of the just man in the psalm, saying: I am with him in tribulation; I will rescue him and glorify him.

Let purity of conscience guard us in adversity; let the integrity of life always be preserved inviolate; and whatever adversity comes will undoubtedly be for us toward the merit of salvation. The ancient enemy, by fighting, can overthrow no one except the one whom he has first found to have fallen away from himself. He seeks those made sluggish by idleness; he undermines the unwary soldier; and he most easily casts down the one whom he finds stripped of arms.

Therefore, my brothers, always keep watch in holy prayers; always be intent on good works, so that whenever the tempter approaches, he may never find you idle. Remember the words of our Savior Jesus Christ, saying in the holy Gospel: Let your loins be girded and lamps burning in your hands, and you yourselves like men waiting for their lord to return from the wedding, so that when he comes and knocks, they may open to him at once. He keeps watch who, standing firm in the fear of the Lord, never dozes in mental sluggishness. He keeps watch who preserves his soul, by the tireless constancy of holy purpose, against any adversities of this world. He keeps watch who never ceases to advance daily in the love of the Lord.

He carries a lamp in his hands who adorns his holy purpose with fitting works. He also holds a lamp in his hands who, cleansed in mind and body, shines for his neighbor through works of holy devotion. Hence the holy lover of Christ, Paul, says in the epistle: I beseech you, dearly beloved, by the mercy of God, that you present your bodies as a living sacrifice, holy and pleasing to God. He who chastens his body through the pursuits of holy abstinence and brings it into servitude to the spirit has consecrated a living sacrifice to the Lord. But whoever has already learned to keep his heart clean and pure from every pollution of sin has undoubtedly offered a holy sacrifice to the Lord. And whoever perseveres in the purpose of holiness to the end has without doubt offered a pleasing sacrifice.

[segment 45]
[Page 054]

for when adversity is added to a good work, and in this world the scourge of God is received with joy, beautiful rewards of the saints are being stored up for the future recompense. Again he also says: “God’s dispensation often gives joy from temptation, when it humbles by the adversity of temporal life those whom it sees are to be saved forever, if it did not preserve them by correction under the rule of discipline.” When we are afflicted, we lose earthly goods; but by humbly enduring affliction we multiply heavenly goods for ourselves. For the present life is a road by which we are journeying toward our fatherland; and for this reason we are often touched in it by disturbances, lest we suppose that our fatherland is here.

Therefore let the monk rejoice in his adversity, if, as a tempered servant, to whom the world has been crucified, he trusts that he will attain the fellowship of God: he who, with a pure conscience, always does good, and receives its opposite in tribulation. For it is an evident sign of divine mercy if a Christian man passes through various adversities in this world, while serving God with a pure conscience and living in true humility without offense. For the merciful God frequently strikes in various ways in this world those whom he loves, for whom he has prepared eternal rewards in heaven. But those whom he has foreknown cannot be saved without sin, nor be overtaken by sin through weakness of the body, he mercifully scourges for their salvation, seeing that it is more useful for their souls to be broken by bonds unto salvation than to live unharmed unto condemnation. He wills others to be poor, whom he has foreknown could not come to salvation with riches. For the delights and wealth of this world would have furnished many with the cause of eternal damnation.

Hence Saint Pope Gregory again says: “Many have been laid low by their own previous good fortune; prolonged peace has rendered many conquered, and the unexpected enemy struck them down more grievously because he found them negligent through long use of quiet.” Therefore holy men, when they consider that they are making much progress through the prosperity of virtues, rejoice that, by a certain moderation of supernal dispensation, they are also exercised by temptations; for they guard the accepted glory of virtues the more robustly, the more, shaken by the impulse of temptation, they more humbly acknowledge their own weakness.

Thus the road of tribulation is a shorter path to Christ, and he is found in the heart more quickly than in tranquillity. Saint Bishop Ambrose says: “There are many who seek Christ in leisure and do not find him; and there are some who seek him amid persecutions and quickly find him.” In the dangers of his faithful he is present, just as he speaks of the righteous man in the Psalm, saying: “I am with him in tribulation; I will rescue him and glorify him.” Therefore let purity of conscience be guarded in adversities; let the inviolate integrity of life always be preserved, and whatever adversity comes will undoubtedly be for us unto the merit of salvation.

The ancient enemy cannot overcome anyone by fighting unless he finds that the man has first failed from within himself. He openly seeks to attack; he trips the soldier in the plain, and he casts down most easily the one whom he finds stripped of arms. Therefore, my brothers, always keep watch in holy prayers; always persist in good works, so that when the tempter approaches he may never find you idle. Remember the words of our Savior, who says in the holy Gospel: “Let your loins be girded and lamps burning in your hands, and be like men waiting for their lord when he returns from the wedding, so that when he comes and knocks, they may open to him immediately.”

He keeps watch who, solidly established in the fear of the Lord, never sleeps in the torpor of the mind. He keeps watch who preserves his soul against every adversity of this world through the indefatigable constancy of a holy purpose. He keeps watch who does not cease to advance daily in love of the Lord. He carries a lamp in his hands who adorns his purpose of holiness with worthy works. Likewise he holds a lamp in his hands who, purified in mind and body, shines before his neighbor by works of holy devotion.

Hence Saint Paul, the lover of Christ, says in the epistle: “I beseech you, dearly beloved, by the mercy of God, that you present your bodies as a living sacrifice, holy and pleasing to God.” Whoever, chastising his body through the practices of holy abstinence, brings it into the service of the spirit, consecrates a living sacrifice to the Lord. Whoever preserves the heart clean and pure from every pollution of sin, has already without doubt offered a holy sacrifice to the Lord. And whoever perseveres in the purpose of holiness even to the end has without doubt offered a pleasing sacrifice. Lum/

[segment 46]
Homily 10. Fol. XXIII.

We gird these, however, when we keep our bodies pure and unstained in mind from every form of lust. The virtue of holy chastity is of great merit before God, and, because of the greatness of the struggle, is to be endowed with a special reward in the appointed recompense, as the Lord says through Isaiah the prophet: Thus says the Lord to the eunuchs: Those who keep my sabbaths, and choose the things that I have willed, and hold fast my covenant, I will give them in my house and within my walls a place and a name better than sons and daughters. I will give them an everlasting name, which shall not perish.

And concerning these the Lord says in the holy Gospel: For there are eunuchs who were so born from their mother's womb; and there are eunuchs who were made so by men; and there are eunuchs who have made themselves eunuchs for the sake of the kingdom of heaven. He who can receive this, let him receive it.

But why did he say, He who can receive this, let him receive it? I think that this statement about eunuchs, who castrate themselves for love of the kingdom of heaven, should not be referred to the cutting away of the genital members, as many, in their exultation, have strayed from the truth of the Gospel, but rather to the mastery of the mind, by which chastity is preserved whole and conceived in chaste love. For they castrate themselves for the kingdom of God, who, for love of God, keep true chastity of mind and body inviolate. And just as no one is easily castrated bodily without the greatest torment of pain, so no one can attain the perfection of chastity without the greatest labor of zeal. And certainly no one is chaste by his own strength, no one modest by his own merits. It is God who gives the good will, and he also joins strength to the will so that it can prevail.

But because it does not belong to many to remain chaste, he leaves each person to his own judgment, saying: He who can receive this, let him receive it. Cutting off the members does not make the mind chaste, since the root of lust is within, and requires something more than the amputation of the shameful parts. But if the mind is chaste and whole, it will easily restrain the insolence of the members. For nothing is stronger than a mind ruling with a pure heart and sweetly loving above all things.

Think now, most beloved brothers, on the future beauty of the breast, and the labor of chastity will not burden you. For to the great height of the breast, to the surpassing joys, the infancy of labor leads. Therefore whoever flees the labor of the contest will never attain the height of honor. Now is the time in which, laboring, we resist the devil; but the eternal age will come very soon, in which, reigning without end, we shall rejoice with Christ.

Let the body be bound fast by discreet abstinence; let it submit to the command of the mind; and let the concupiscence of the flesh be broken by holy exercises, so that it may always obey the counsel of reason. If at any time it begins to swell with movements against salvation, let it also be restrained with blows, because it is better humbly to endure the bruises of wounds than, through the indulgence of the flesh, to cast the soul down into hell.

For it is said in Scripture: Whoever delicately nourishes his servant from childhood will afterward feel him stubborn. The flesh is the servant of the mind, and ought always to obey the command of reason. But if the flesh has been nourished too licentiously, already accustomed to pleasures, it will refuse to bear the rule of the spirit. For a servant is made insolent toward his master whenever he is delicately nourished by him through habit. And it is difficult to bring a rebellious and insolent servant back to humble obedience after he has long been accustomed to kick back.

For it is written: A servant cannot be instructed by words, because he understands what is said and scorns to answer. Therefore the wise man says in Ecclesiasticus: Fodder and rod and burden for the ass; bread and discipline and work for the servant. Put him to work under discipline and he seeks rest; slacken your hand with him, and he seeks freedom. Yoke and strap bend a stiff neck, and constant tasks bow a servant down.

If we begin to transfer these testimonies of the Scriptures from the literal to the moral sense, everything that is said about the servant and the ass we may rightly understand as applying to the sluggish and delicate flesh. For truly the flesh is the servant of the soul, and it always rises up against its master unless it is wearied every day by discipline and work. Whoever keeps it delicate at all times in idleness and pleasures will necessarily find it insolent against spiritual promptings, because, while the flesh is lifted up in pride, the spirit, languishing in idleness and grown lukewarm, is weakened. Therefore, unless you continually humble this servant of yours, namely the flesh, O brother monk, by work and abstinence; unless you place upon its unlawful habits the bridle of continence and sobriety, you will go wholly after it as it runs ahead into the abyss.

[segment 47]
--- Page 056 ---

On the Daily Struggle with Temptation.

Unless you impose upon its unlawful movements the yoke of continence and subjection, you will go wholly with it, as it leads the way, into the abyss. For we know, as the holy Apostle says, that the flesh lusts against the spirit; and unless by the spirit we mortify the works of the flesh, we shall die in its corruption. Nothing casts the soul more headlong into the pit of sin than the insolence of the flesh, which, when it is permitted to live by its own will, struggles back like a contumacious servant against every command. Unhappy flesh, which through the concupiscence of vanity casts the soul down, it will not pass unpunished for you if it has undertaken to suggest any evil to you, for God will judge both. You too will be punished when you have sinned with it, because when Adam, having transgressed the commandment, is driven from paradise, Eve, the instigator of evil, is necessarily joined with him in misery.

Be subject, O flesh, to the spirit, for the Apostle says: 'Those who are Christ's have crucified their flesh with its vices and concupiscences.' And you, O men and brothers, who profess the service of Christ, who have willingly sworn yourselves to the law of monastic life, who have promised to imitate the footsteps of the Savior: attend to your calling, that you may walk carefully, not as the unwise, but as true lovers of Jesus Christ, who by the spirit mortify the deeds of the flesh. You, I say, my most beloved fellow soldiers, always keep watch in good works, mortifying for Christ's love our bodies that are upon the earth, constant in fasts and prayers, and continually occupied in the meditation of the divine Scriptures. Always stretch yourselves toward what lies ahead; each day renew with fresh zeal the fervor of your holy purpose. Flee idleness and every sloth, and diligently consider how brief is the time of this life. Let the brief and moderate labor of a hard struggle not frighten you; let the enemy's importunity not make you fainthearted, since every labor is quickly overcome by willing persistence, and the devil, however fiercely he rages, is conquered as soon as you invoke Christ.

Remember the words of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, who says in the holy Gospel: 'Whoever wishes to come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross daily and follow me.' But what is our cross, my brothers, except a life full of temptations and adversities, and the burden of regular discipline under which we are wearied day and night? But let us gather our hearts, most dear brothers, and for the love of God gladly take up whatever he wishes us to endure, so that in his judgment we may deserve to say: 'We have passed through fire and water, and you have brought us into refreshment.' O how glorious a reward is laid up in the kingdom of heaven for the servants of God, who now struggle in this brief course and are wearied for his love. Eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it ascended into the heart of man, the immensity of that good which almighty God has prepared for those who love him, to remain without end in eternal felicity.

The highest happiness of this good is acquired by humility and possessed by purity of heart and the fervor of divine love. Let us hasten there, most dear brothers; let us run there with all the devotion of mind that we can, where the spirit is joined to its Creator through the enjoyment of sweetness, where the intellect is perfected in the knowledge of the highest good, which is God. There is the joy of everlasting happiness, which does not diminish; there is gladness which does not end; there, after every labor, is perpetual rest; there is the glorious reward for tribulations and injuries borne for Christ. Whatever good we now do, there without doubt we shall find it stored up; whatever adversity we patiently endure for the love of Christ, there we shall receive it back, rewarded with a most abundant prize.

O monk, monk, who spend fruitlessly the time mercifully granted to you by the Lord God for doing good, what do you deserve, or what reward will you receive in that terrible hour, you who are now so sluggish and unwilling to sow? Have you not read in Scripture concerning the labors of the saints: 'Going, they went and wept, casting their seeds; but coming, they shall come with exultation, carrying their sheaves'? Now is the time, O monk, to work the field of your heart; now is the hour to sow good seed with the blessing of cheerfulness, because he who now sows sparingly will also reap sparingly in that future recompense of reward. We must labor now and never cease from good work, since whatever we have now done here, there without doubt we shall receive. Therefore, if there is among you any faithful monk, if anyone is a zealous lover of his own salvation, let him always think on the hour of future reward, which no one can escape; let him always prepare himself to render the account of his stewardship. Let no single day perish in which he has not done some particular good work to send ahead for future recompense.

Omnia XI. Fo. xxv.

Let him consider that he has spent uselessly any day on which he has not sent ahead some good work for which he expects to be rewarded. A good monk should strive at all times to cultivate the field of his heart, and, so far as he can, utterly root out all the thorns and briars of evil affections. Let him learn to love the Lord Jesus above all things with a pure mind, and to keep most diligently whatever he has commanded. Let him sow in the tears of devotion and constancy what he may reap with all the saints in exultation. Let the continual difficulty of laboring not deter the mind; let the hard struggle with the flesh not break the spirit, since he who labors little gathers little fruit at the harvest, and he who now sows much in tears for the love of Christ will bring back most abundant fruit on that day. For this, brothers, is the destination of our journey which we make in the present pilgrimage: that, purified by the continual exercise of labors and adversities, we may depart from here clean and pure for our fatherland.

On Fleeing the Company of the Wicked: Omnia, the Eleventh.

The company of the wicked has drawn many back from the way of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ. Therefore, my most beloved brothers, we do not cease to admonish you to walk in the way of the Lord with the good and holy, so that by their instruction and example you may become better every day. For as the blessed Apostle of Christ says, imitating a verse of a certain Greek, Menander: 'Evil conversations corrupt good morals.' He does not truly desire to be good who delights in the way of life of the wicked. No one speaks wicked things unless he is first found corrupt and depraved in his heart against the purity of the evangelical tradition. As each person is, and as he goes over things by rumination and thought in the secret of his heart, so speech more often proceeds from his mouth. For the speech of the one speaking is the truest witness of the heart, as our Lord and Savior says in the holy Gospel: 'Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks.' For a good man brings forth good things from the treasure of his heart, and an evil man likewise brings forth evil things.

Since, then, he who speaks evil is not good, we must beware of all who use wicked, scurrilous, and pernicious speech, in whose talk certain shameful things frequently resound, while words of holiness are heard either never or very rarely. Shameful speech is the sign of an immodest heart, even if deeds do not follow. Therefore, my dearest brothers, you who have promised, by profession both implicit and explicit, according to the rule and the ecclesiastical statutes of the holy fathers, not only to preserve chastity and every form of modesty in deed until death, but who also ought to be far removed from every unclean thought: flee the company of wicked men and the assemblies of the malicious, and at all times avoid those who speak unjust, shameful, or idle things.

Remember the Scripture saying: 'He who touches pitch will be defiled by it; and he who associates with the proud will put on pride.' Hence holy Pope Gregory says: 'The weak ought to avoid the society of the wicked, lest they delight in imitating the evils which they frequently see and are unable to correct.' And the blessed bishop John says: 'If a good man is joined to an evil one, either they are made equal, or they are quickly separated from each other. For friendships either seek equals or make them.' For the nature of things is such that, whenever a good man is joined to an evil one, the evil man is not made better by the good, but the good man is contaminated by the evil. For diversity of things can never have concord, and wicked society tempts many.

There are among you certain brothers, I say with grief, professed men very forgetful of regular discipline, for whom the cell is a prison, the choir a siege, prayer a nausea, silence a punishment, and the reading of Scripture a weariness. The love of God either never or only rarely lifts their hearts upward, for their whole way of life rests in lower things. Whenever I happen to be absent, they immediately gather among themselves, not to speak of Christ, nor of the sweetness of the heavenly kingdom, nor of the integrity of regular observance, but they produce a long discourse about the vain and useless affairs of this world; and by conversing they busy themselves with tales of kings and wars, matters about which a monk is not permitted to think. But whatever I say to these men, whatever I speak, whatever penance I impose, [unclear].

[segment 48]
Homily XI. Fol. XXV.

Let him judge that he has spent uselessly any day on which he has not sent ahead some good work for which he may expect reward. A good monk should strive at all times to cultivate the field of his heart, and to root out completely, so far as he can, all the thorns and briars of evil affections. Let him learn to love the Lord Jesus above all things with a pure mind, and to keep with the greatest diligence whatever he has commanded. Let him sow in the tears of devotion and constancy what he may reap with all the saints in exultation. Let not the continual difficulty of labor frighten his mind; let not the harsh mortification of the flesh break his spirit, since he who labors little gathers little fruit at harvest, and he who now, for the love of Christ, sows much in tears will reap most abundant fruit on that day. Nor is it part of our delight, brothers, what we do in this present pilgrimage, but rather that, by the continual exercise of labors and adversities, we may be purified here and come clean and pure to our homeland.

On Fleeing the Company of the Wicked: Homily Eleven.

The company of the wicked has drawn many away from the way of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ. Therefore, my most beloved brothers, we do not cease to admonish you to walk in the way of the Lord with the good and holy, so that by their instruction and example you may daily become better. For as the divine apostle of Christ says, imitating a certain Menander, a Greek writer: “Bad conversations corrupt good morals.” He does not truly desire to be good whom the companionship of the wicked delights. For no one speaks wicked things unless he is first found inwardly corrupted and depraved in his heart against the purity of evangelical tradition. As each person is, and as he turns over and thinks upon things in the secrets of his heart, so speech more often proceeds from his mouth. For speech is the most truthful witness of the heart, as our Lord and Savior says in the holy Gospel: “Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks.” For a good man brings forth good things from the treasure of his heart, and likewise an evil man brings forth evil things.

Since, therefore, he is not good who speaks evil, we must beware of all who use depraved, scurrilous, and destructive speech, in whose conversation certain shameful things frequently resound, while words of holiness are either never heard or very rarely. Shameful speech is the sign of an impure heart, even if deeds do not follow. Therefore you, my dearest brothers, who have promised according to the rules and statutes of the divine fathers of the Church not only to preserve chastity and every kind of modesty in deed, even to death, by an implicit as well as explicit profession, but also to be far removed from every unclean thought, must flee the company of wicked men; avoid at all times the assemblies of the malicious and those who speak wicked, shameful, or idle things.

Remember the Scripture saying: “He who touches pitch will be defiled by it, and he who associates with the proud will put on pride.” Hence holy Pope Gregory says: “The weak must avoid the fellowship of the wicked, lest they take delight in imitating the evils they often see and cannot correct.” And the divine bishop [unclear] says: “If a good man joins himself to an evil one, either both are quickly made equal, or they are quickly separated from each other. For friendships either seek equals or make them.” For the nature of things is such that whenever a good person is joined to an evil one, it is not that the evil is made better by the good, but that the good is corrupted by the evil. For the diversity of things can never have concord, and wicked fellowship troubles many.

There are among you certain brothers, I say with grief, professed men very forgetful of regular discipline, for whom the cell is a prison, the choir a siege, prayer nausea, silence a punishment, and the reading of Scripture disgust. The love of God never, or only rarely, lifts their hearts upward, since their whole way of life is fixed on lower things. Whenever I happen to be absent, they soon contend among themselves; not about Christ, nor about the sweetness of the heavenly kingdom, nor about the integrity of regular observance, but they draw out long speech about the vain and useless affairs of this world, and by talking together they involve themselves in tales and wars of kings, things about which it is not lawful for a monk even to think. But whatever I speak to these men, whatever I say, whatever I impose as penance, where

[segment 49]
*On fleeing the company of the wicked: Homily eleven.*

...makes progress; those whom evil habit corrupts from the beginning of their training. Nothing, however, is worse than evil habit. He is the monk who neglects to find anything new in all the days of his life, and passes his time in sloth. For Scripture says: A young man walking according to his own way, even when he is old, will not depart from it. Therefore I say to you, venerable elders, I beseech you, I warn you, and with all the urgency I can, by the mercy of God, I ask you: from now on live in such a way, conduct yourselves in such a way, walk in such a way, that your manners may befit the years of old age, and that you, who have already advanced far into age, may offer an example of holiness for the training of the younger men. Without doubt whatever fault is found in the morals of the younger men is charged to you. For the younger men look to you; therefore you ought to go before them in word and deed; you ought to show yourselves to them as an example of complete perfection, so that, when the fullness of every perfect virtue is seen in you, they may be compelled, as though by a certain impulse of reverence, to imitate your holiness. For the conduct of elders gives the younger men an example of living, just as the proverb says: What the elders have sung, the young men have learned.

But you younger men, show reverence with humility to your elders, considering their gray hair; and those whom you know to be more observant in keeping regular discipline, rejoice to have them as teachers by keeping their company. Carefully avoid those who either walk restlessly or observe the purity of regular discipline too negligently. Not men who are called elders are more worthy of imitation by the young; years do not make a monk venerable, but good morals, together with prudence in the love of God, establish him as worthy of reverence. Therefore, whoever among you, dearest brothers, desires to be a true lover of God, whoever longs to make progress in holy monastic conduct, let him always flee the company of undisciplined brothers, as he has professed; and let him seek the companionship of those who support his purpose in the good. For this reason it says in the Psalm: With the holy you will be holy, and with the innocent man you will be innocent, and with the chosen you will be chosen, and with the perverse you will be perverted.

For evil company has harmed many, and one who delights to dwell with the reprobate will not be free from the contagion of sin. Indeed, everyone who lives among the wicked and reprobate must necessarily have the integrity of his life damaged, at least somewhat, by their perversity. For either he does not correct them and becomes like them and evil, or he will hate them and be an enemy to many, and for that reason iniquity necessarily follows. But if, overcome by the multitude of the wicked, he has once begun to delight in their company, he is already corrupted in three ways, and little by little he will be corrupted further. If, however, he detests the works of the wicked, he will suffer wearisome inconvenience, and his desire for progress toward the better will be slower in proportion as he is more troubled by the company of the perverse.

Yet I give thanks to almighty God for you, because even if not all of you are as fervent in the love of God as I would wish, nevertheless, as I trust, you all live far removed from the stain of sins and from the rashness of pride. And my exhortations and fatherly admonitions, which I make to you daily as I am bound to do, you receive willingly, although not all of you yet show them perfectly in deed. But I hope in the Lord Jesus that he will complete in you the good which he has begun, so that my joy may be fulfilled, and I may see you all burning with one purpose of zeal and one fire of holy fervor, delighting and being exercised in the workshop of divine love.

But since some have not yet taken up the fullness of the amendment I desire, I am compelled by just necessity to admonish those who are making progress, that, rising up with a manly spirit, they should flee the wicked societies of the sluggish. For as yet the fire of divine love is small in their hearts; as yet the fire of holy fervor burns new and weak, and unless it is nourished by daily kindling, it will easily suffer loss. Therefore again and again I warn you who have begun to receive the fervor of inward love: continue steadfastly with the spirit by which, inspired by the Lord, you are directed; and those whom you find opposed to you in this holy journey, whether by deed or speech, avoid as deadly poison. For just as water extinguishes the fire onto which it is poured, so the beginning of a holy purpose in novices is harmed by the examples of the lukewarm.

Beware of conversation with those men who, as I see, are negligent in observing the commandments of God and monastic purity, because, since they are stirred by carnal passions, out of the abundance of the heart they speak nothing but vain and carnal things. For wicked conversations corrupt the good morals of the young. All their pursuits are empty, and their words are useless, indeed pestiferous; being carnally minded, they do not know the exercises of divine love. For just as one diseased sheep gradually corrupts the whole flock, so one slothful and perverse monk in a congregation often becomes the cause of many transgressions. As, according to holy Christopher, a little leaven that remains corrupts the whole mass of flour with which it is mixed by its smell, so young and new monks who associate with lukewarm and slothful elders are imbued with their depraved morals and never thereafter are restored to the integrity of a holier way of life.

Therefore I say to you elders: be a pattern of living for the young by good works and chaste and always modest speech, so that whenever they are corrected for their faults, they may not defend themselves by the examples of their elders. For it is utterly shameful for elders to be culpable in those things for which the younger men are deservedly rebuked. I beseech you, elders, live in such a way that before you the younger men may presume to say or do nothing that seems reprehensible. Let the wicked fear you as judges, not as men acting in the place of patrons, because as many as sin by following your examples are reckoned to have perished through your instruction. Let there be among you no one lukewarm or slothful, because desires kill the lazy man, and vanity destroys every idle monk. Let there be among you no verbose man, no wanderer, no gadabout, no murmurer or restless man, because whoever delights in such things and pursuits destroys himself by evil action and kills his brother by his examples.

Let there be among you no mocker of the simple, no despiser of the humble, no rash and censorious man, because one who is ready to mock will be consumed in his own folly and will not be glorious. He opens his mouth against the humble, always seeking quarrels, and rejoices that he is talkative about the deeds of others. He will not prosper in his counsel; and if he is raised to some honor, he will quickly fall into the depths. His pride is hateful to God, because, though he is foolish and senseless, he boasts that he is learned and wise. Soon his foolishness will be laid bare, and grave punishment will seize him inwardly.

[segment 50]
His mouth will meditate folly, and there is no equal around him. He will be hateful to those near him and to his neighbors, and no faithful man will pursue his friendship. Even if he takes on the hypocritical pretense of simulation, his foolishness will draw others into aversion. He hates peace, loves discord, and will be caught in the sloth of his mind; and there will be no one to grieve with him. He will be crushed by a double affliction, because he did not permit peace to those who had it. The devil has made a nest in his tongue, and Satan has had his dwelling in his mind. Therefore there is no love of the brothers in his heart, because he sows discord between the shepherd and the sheep. He boasts that he is a friend of God, and accepts no one as his equal in regular observance, but he does not understand the very great humility of the Canaanite monk. He does not open his mouth to words of spiritual edification, and from his heart nothing proceeds except vanity. All day long, dissolved in mind and wandering in body, he runs about in the monastery; he gives himself to useless and wholly idle talk; he thinks nothing of the salvation of his soul; he cares for his belly as his god. Guard your soul from his speech, and do not set your foot in the path of his words.

Whoever you are who desire to show yourself a monk and minister of the cross, whoever you are who truly love the Lord Jesus with your whole heart: be cautious and watchful in all your works, and always flee the company of those in whose life nothing is taught and nothing heavenly is seen. Be a companion and associate of humble monks who follow perfection; and gladly admit as preceptor and master the one whom you have known to be grounded in the fear of God. Walk gladly with those who love discipline and purity of mind, and do not be like a man who yields when shaken by every wind; but strengthen your soul in a good purpose, lest you be taken by the counsel of the wicked. For it is best to establish the heart by grace, and neither to be able to deceive nor to wish to deceive. Have as your friend a brother of discipline; and the one by whose word you can be corrected from evil and instructed toward good by example, love and venerate as a father. Have no commerce of familiarity with a restless brother, lest at some time, encouraged by his evil example, you fall into the rebellion of the proud.

Let not a wrathful monk, full of murmuring, be at your side when he stubbornly kicks back against his superiors, nor ever choose him as a friend. Whoever you see violating the canonical fast without the elder’s blessing, flee him as an enemy of salvation; and flee the one who, overcome by talkativeness, without any cause disturbs peace and stirs up quarrels. Flee a rebellious monk, disobedient to elders, as from the face of a snake, because he seethes full of the poison of malice and in arrogance of mind has lost his soul. He who does not obey the command of an elder is perverse; and he who, when corrected, is not humbled is an apostate. Let the one who detracts from and speaks against his brother not be in conversation with you, because he is far worse than a thief.

Whoever you see wholly solicitous in regular observance, love him as a father and venerate him as a most loving preceptor. Keep confidently to the familiarity of the one whose friendship always makes you better in the love of God. With all zeal imitate good men, and follow the manners of those who live in conformity with monastic perfection. Have as your friend a brother of good will, but turn your ears away from the one who is hard-hearted. Let the one who is light in mind and dissolved in conduct be to you like a man who scatters a hedge in a field. Do not be the companion of one who despises the rule and silence, who does not distinguish places or times, who without the prior’s blessing multiplies words everywhere.

Avoid the monk who is given to wine and drink, who too often exceeds the monastery’s canonical measure, as an encouragement of lust and a spring of all vices. The monk who, by the counsel of his own head, strives against the elder’s command is near perdition and hastens breathlessly into the danger of his soul. Do not share in the works of a stubborn monk, and do not have the friendship of one who, laughing at his own rashness, speaks against his superiors in deeds. Be continually in the midst of those who are disciplined, and let your soul gladly walk with the humble. For whoever joins himself to proud men will never be exalted.

He who raises his voice when speaking and cries aloud with a straying voice cannot hide the folly of his heart, and he will boast in his vanity. The friendship of good men is the firm salvation of the soul, and in the company of the wicked the innocence of the simple is corrupted. The company of those whose fear of God has confirmed the examples of good works is best. Nothing is more precious than a friend who fears God, and nothing more dangerous than a companion who has an evil will. A brother who fears God with his whole heart will be a wall of strength for his friend; and whoever is joined with such a man in soundness of mind is sanctified at all times by the blessing of the Spirit of God. He who walks among those who fear God is upheld, lest he fail, as though by a very strong wall around him.

From his words the servant of the Most High will be known, and works of holiness mark out the good monk. No shameful, scurrilous, or idle word comes from the mouth of one who fears the Lord; and he who is a true lover of Jesus does nothing that offends goodness. In the mouth of the monk who loves God Christ always resounds, and he who is a true worshiper of God demonstrates the word of justice both in his mouth and in his work. He who fears God is the best counselor, and he who walks according to the canon is a prudent judge of thoughts. An elder who fears God, and has been proved and tested for a long time in regular observance, is a director of young men; and one who has no stain on his garment is a good instructor of novices. Whoever is an understanding teacher and interpreter of the divine Scriptures, guarding his soul in humility, receive all the words of his mouth gladly as if they were golden, because they have the marrow and solidity of future eternal happiness. He who is truly learned has compassion on the weak; and he who loves Jesus exhorts the wavering soul to strength.

He who walks with a talkative man will not escape contention, and he who delights in lying will never be a friend of wisdom. He who is constantly occupied with external things will not make progress, and boasts without cause that he is a monk. The purpose of holiness is quickly corrupted when a monk more frequently wanders outside his cell with desire. He who leaves the secrecy of his cell without the elder’s command does not strive toward the perfection of a holy purpose. Beautiful is the company of good men and of those who fear God; and one who has grown accustomed to live with them will not feel confusion.

The heart of a young monk is a clean tablet, on which the conduct of elders is inscribed, never to be erased. The worst writer on a beautiful heart is an elder who either does or says shameful things before a young man. He who infects the minds of young men with rash speech is like the impious Herod who

[segment 51]
*On Avoiding the Company of the Depraved.*

... he who by rash speech infects the minds of youths is like the impious Herod, who killed the boys. The evil that a young man receives from a foolish and negligent old man, even when he has become a priest, he will not correct. Every vessel that is filled with vinegar is spoiled; and a monk who is neglected in his novitiate is not corrected when he has grown old. Cursed is the old man by whose evil way of life youths perish, because he who has left evil tracks for those who follow him is like one who with his own hand has hurled a blind man into a pit. Before the inner Judge each person is worthy of as many deaths as the innocent people who have perished by his depraved example.

Young man, seek an old man as the moderator of your life: not one who has passed many years in a dissolute and lukewarm way of life, but one whom the fear of the Lord and knowledge of God’s Scriptures commend. Better is the company of a young monk who fears the Lord than the fellowship of an old teacher whose life lacks the light of divine love and the integrity of regular observance. An old man disordered in morals, lacking cleanness of mind and without discipline, is like one injured in the leg, led lame to the grave. An old and dissolute monk is like a foolish man who, not knowing the end, is taken among robbers at a drinking feast. An elder without maturity is like a little elder-pipe of sambucus, crushed under the feet of players.

He boasts vainly that he has been a monk for many days who has not perceived the beginning of the interior life. He who loves the cleanness of his heart guards himself at all times from the company of undisciplined brothers. Just as a spot of pitch clinging to the garments of dancers grieves virgins, so a good young man blushes at the shameful words of elders. Lascivious speech is not the speech of a man who fears God, because the mouth speaks from the abundance of the heart; and he who brings forth shameful words is not clean before the Most High. If sinners begin to hate you because of the fear of God, do not be saddened, because unless you displease the wicked you will not attain the perfection of the good. For to displease the wicked on account of righteousness is praiseworthy, and is a clear sign of divine love. If you love your soul, flee the counsels of the undisciplined, and do not associate with mockers. Walk with the good, and let him who fears the Lord be your counselor.

*On Monks Sent Out on the Road. Homily XII. Fo. xxvii.*

It belongs to monks to flee the world, and, for love of solitude, to avoid the crowds of men as much as possible at all times. A monk is so called from solitude, just as one is named from unity. He cannot be a monk who follows after the frequenting of peoples; and he who delights in cities has not yet tasted monastic sweetness. If you are a monk of a cenobium, you ought not abandon its solitude without reasonable cause; and if you are bound to silence, you ought not be pleased by the conversations of worldly people. Whoever loves wandering excursions in the city has abandoned his conscience; and he who is often outside his monastery does not preserve the purity of inner progress. He who goes around the houses of relatives and delights in the feasts of neighbors does not consider himself a monk; and he who goes to the banquets of drinkers kills his own soul. He who sleeps in the house of his kinsman seeks comfort, and he who avoids the assemblies of the religious follows destructive liberty.

A monk in the house of his father and mother is like a lamb in the midst of wolves, and like a hare surrounded by hunters’ dogs. If you are a monk, if a servant and soldier of God, what are you doing in the house of your parents? Or what are you seeking in the midst of peoples? A monk remaining for a long time outside the cenobium among secular people is like a man who has buried his only-begotten son beneath the depths of the waters. For just as fish have no life on dry land, so monks have no salvation of soul in the world. Outside the river the fish dies, and the purity of a monk’s conscience is quickly violated outside the monastery. The life of monks is in the cenobium, and the death of souls is amid secular business.

It is a great virtue of the soul to flee the crowds of men, or, when it is altogether necessary to be among them, nevertheless to avoid them in mind. At times we are compelled by necessity to make a journey through the world; but the purity of conscience is polluted all the less by encounters with men, the less the desire of the mind is drawn away from the secrecy of the cell. Therefore every good monk must be warned that whenever, by the command of the elder, he is sent out on the road for the necessity of some matter, he should never wander outwardly in his mind. He who does not guard his senses is quickly corrupted when evil is set before him; and he who casts off the fear of the Lord necessarily returns impure to the monastery.

[segment 52]
--- Page 061 ---
On monks sent on the road.

...will return to the monastery of necessity, and impure. When a stream flows out, by its muddiness it soils the beauty of the open meadows around it; and when a monk attends to the tales of secular people, he stains the purity of his conscience. Therefore, before you set your foot outside the monastery, arm yourself with holy prayers; and before you go out, do not fail to commend your road to the Lord with prayers. Then carry out what the lawgiver commanded, and entrust yourself for protection to the prayers both of the abbot and of all the brothers.

Prescribe for yourself the canon of strict regular observance, so that, holding it firmly in memory, you may guard your soul from every drift toward the vanities that meet you. Go nowhere out of the monastery without the abbot’s blessing, because, since you ought to be dead and buried to this world, nothing further will be lawful for you without the command of your superior. Recognize yourself as a soldier of King Jesus Christ: let your whole life be governed by command. For a monk who goes out of the monastery anywhere without the abbot’s permission is like a little sheep in the darkness, burned up in the fire of a house.

But a true monk of Christ, even when he is outside the monastery in body, remains in the cloister of the mind; and he is no less alone when with many than he is accustomed to be solitary in his cell. The lover of Christ thinks of Jesus even in the midst of a multitude; and he who is a true worker of virtues keeps his heart firm in the fear of God in every place. For even if he is not in the monastery in body, nevertheless by the strength of his spirit he always stands as firmly as if alone in the cloister. The spirit is not defiled by the body’s movement, when the solitude of the mind is guarded.

O monk, you must live with the same holiness on the road with which you are commanded to conduct yourself in the monastery, and you must walk with a certain greater caution, lest you scandalize anyone by a more lax example, where there seem to be as many judges of monks as there are onlookers present. The eyes of all secular people look toward monks, and in their works they seek the holiness they hear them profess. A monk gives more offense in one rash word than a man given to the world does in ten thousand. For however frivolous and scurrilous a worldly man may seem, he has nevertheless not learned to tolerate calmly the insolence of monks, because in rustic simplicity everyone believes cloistered men to be not as they are, but as they ought to be. Hence a bad word or deed among them is charged to a monk’s damnation, while it is imputed to pardon in a worldly man.

And not without reason. For a monk who has professed a holy and approved rule is bound to direct his whole effort toward perfection, and in every purity of mind and body to surpass by far the conduct of secular people in word and deed. For a light or idle word, which is granted to secular people for pardon, is reckoned to cloistered men for destruction. Gold is more precious than all metals and is more greatly desired by mortals; and in none is a stain or corruption of falsity more quickly charged. And the defect that is discerned in gold with judicial severity is overlooked in other metals because of the cheapness of their nature. Gold gladdens the eyes by its beauty, and by its virtue strengthens the heart of life. Gold, when fused in fire, is not diminished; and the true monk of God is not changed from the purpose of holiness in the tribulation of persecution.

A small stain on a golden vessel greatly displeases in the sight of a powerful man; if a clay vessel is crooked, no one detests it. When a monk offends in small matters, a great outcry of many arises against him, because his conduct is compared to gold. The canonical life of monks is a golden mirror. Gold in the midst of mud receives no stain upon its nature, but preserves all its beauty inviolate. So too the true monk and lover of Christ remains, when sent on the road among people, the same as he has learned to conduct himself in the privacy of his little cell, because he walks before the Lord pure in mind and body alike. For if he is a true monk, he is not stained by the dust of another’s conduct, nor is he changed from the rectitude of his holy purpose by anyone’s presence or conversation. Among the tumultuous conversations of worldly people he thinks of something else, and the lax conduct of others does not delight his mind. He who is firm in heart is not divided even in the midst of disturbances.

Therefore, monk, you who desire to be good, gather your mind back to yourself; and before you leave your cell for the journey, set your heart in order. Let the fear of the Lord always be the companion of your journey.
