[unclear] will return. When it is green, by its muddiness it stains the beauty of the surrounding meadows; so, when a monk gives attention to the tales of secular people, he defiles the purity of his conscience. Therefore, before you set your foot out of the monastery, fortify yourself with holy prayers; and before you go out, do not neglect especially to commend the road to the Lord. Then carry out what the lawgiver commanded, and entrust yourself to be protected by the prayers both of the abbot and of all the brothers.

Prescribe for yourself the canon of the strictness of regular observance, under the steady remembrance of which you may guard your mind from every distraction of the vanities that meet you. Never go out of the monastery without the abbot’s blessing, because, since you ought to be dead and buried to this world in your conduct, it will not be lawful for you thereafter to do anything without the command of your superior. Recognize that you are a soldier of King Jesus Christ; you must follow the whole emperor, without whom there is no salvation. He, fastened to the cross, was taken down not by his own hands but by those of others; if you desire salvation, your will must necessarily be governed by the abbot’s command. For the monk who goes anywhere out of the monastery without the abbot’s permission is like a little sheep in darkness, which trembles when the house burns with fire.

But the true monk of Christ, even when he is outside the monastery in body, remains in the cloister of the mind; and he is no less alone when with many than he has been accustomed to be solitary in his cell. The lover of Christ thinks of Jesus even in the midst of a crowd; and he who is a true worker of virtue keeps his heart firm in the fear of God in every place. For even if in body he is not in the monastery, nevertheless, through the vigor of his mind, he always stands fixed and steadfast, as if alone in the cloister. The spirit is not polluted by the body’s motion, when the solitude of the mind is kept secure.

You must live on the road, O monk, with the same holiness with which you are commanded to conduct yourself in the monastery, and you must walk with a certain greater caution, lest you scandalize anyone by a more lax example, where there seem to be as many judges of monks as there are observers present. The eyes of all secular people look upon monks, and in their works they look for the holiness which they hear them profess. A monk gives more offense by one reckless word than a man given to the world does by ten thousand. For however frivolous and buffoonish a worldly man may seem, nevertheless he has not learned to tolerate calmly the insolence of monks, since with rustic simplicity he believes all cloistered men to be not as they are, but as they ought to be. Hence among them an evil word or deed is charged to a monk for condemnation, whereas it is imputed to a worldly man for pardon.

This is not undeserved. For a monk who has professed the holy and approved canon is bound to direct his whole effort toward perfection, and in word and deed to surpass by far the conduct of secular men in all purity of mind and body. For a light or idle word, which is granted to secular people for pardon, is judged for cloistered men to be destruction.

Gold is more precious than all metals and is more greatly desired by mortals; and in nothing is a stain or corruption of falsity more quickly detected. And a defect which is discerned in gold with judicial severity is overlooked in the other metals because of the baseness of their nature. Gold delights the eyes with its beauty, and by its virtue strengthens the innermost parts of life. Gold, tested in the fire, is not diminished; and a true monk of God is not changed from the purpose of holiness in the tribulation of persecution. A small stain on a golden vessel greatly displeases in the sight of a powerful man; if an earthen vessel is crooked, scarcely anyone detests it. When a monk offends in small matters, a great outcry of many is raised against him, because his way of life is compared to gold. The canonical life of monks is a golden mirror. Gold in the midst of mud receives no stain upon its nature, but preserves all its sweetness inviolate.

So too the true lover of Christ, such as he has learned to conduct himself in the secrecy of his little cell, such also he stands among men when sent onto the road, because he walks pure in mind as well as body before the Lord. For if he is a true monk, he is not polluted by the dust of another’s way of life, nor is he changed from the rectitude of his holy purpose by anyone’s presence or conversation. Amid the tumultuous talk of worldly people he thinks of other things, and the dissolute conduct of others does not delight his mind. He who is single in heart is not divided even in the midst of those making an uproar. Therefore, monk, you who desire to be good, gather your mind back to yourself, and before you leave your cell for the journey, dispose your heart. Let the fear of the Lord always be the companion of your road.

[segment 54]
*Homily XII.*

and let the keeping of monastic discipline never depart from your side. Fence your ears with the memory of the last things, lest hearing vain speech pollute the integrity of your mind. Let your eyes, humbly cast down to the earth, take care not to look at what it is unfitting for a servant of God to desire. Do not take part in the conversations of carnal men; if you cannot avoid their company, take care not to give them your mind. Even in the midst of ribald men, do not transgress your rule, and do not abandon your accustomed gravity. Do not fear the insults and reproaches of mockers, and let your heart not be troubled by the words of men of darkness. The preservation of monastic discipline is justice, and those who despise the simple persecute the innocent with laughter.

Keep your holy purpose in all things, and the Most High will avenge the injury. Do not say in your heart, “I suffer violence,” nor be ashamed at the words of those who deride you. The glory of a monk is to endure insult, and the crown of beauty is to rejoice in reproaches. He who is patient under injuries is stronger than Atlas, and he who is not saddened when mocked is greater than all. If you fix your desire in heaven, nothing on earth will trouble you; if you are a true monk, your heart will exult at injuries. There is a judge in heaven who knows all things, and he is the Most High God, who cares for all things. He is the just repayer of merits, and he will remember his poor. The more, now in this life, you cast your soul down into the depth of humility for love of him, the more in future glory you will be exalted above the rest. For by patience heaven is climbed, and by humility the kingdom is reached. For Christ himself also had to suffer, and so to enter into his glory.

A self-indulgent soldier is one who seeks delights while his emperor endures insults and mockeries, and while he hangs on the gibbet of the cross for his army, the soldier himself follows pleasures. A soldier who goes out naked to war without weapons, and a monk who disdains to embrace patience and humility amid insults: the destruction of both is swift. The monk who is a sincere lover of Christ is not angered by injuries, does not threaten those who bring insult, stands fixed in his holy purpose, and, however much the wicked provoke him, is not moved.

Just as timely rain increases hay in the meadows, so merit grows for the monk from the humble endurance of injuries. Humility is the sinew of all virtues, and constant patience under injuries for the love of God is the mine of good works. Therefore, when you are about to go out on the road from the monastery, prepare your heart for the endurance of injuries, and strengthen your mind against the insolences of mockers. When worthless men behind your back shout out, repeating the name “monk,” remember Christ mocked and spat upon before the governor, who did not answer a word, but in silence preserved patience. Imitate him as your leader, O monk; always listen to him as your teacher, who was meek and humble of heart. Do not answer a word to those who mock you; do not turn your face backward, but say with the prophet: “Those who sought evils for me spoke vanities, and all day long they meditated deceits. But I, like a deaf man, did not hear; and like a mute man not opening his mouth. And I became like a man who does not hear, and who has no reproofs in his mouth.”

Christ did this; Jesus taught this; the king of all commanded this. If you wish to be his disciple, you must always be ready for every injury. When set upon a journey, do not neglect to say your canonical prayers with what devotion you can, and always turn over something good with yourself in your mind, either by praying or by meditating. The Lord goes with you, and his holy angels accompany you as you walk. And therefore preserve a clean heart in all things, lest you offend the majesty of such great companions. The Lord is wholly eye and ear; from his knowledge nothing is hidden. Apply constant guard to your words, and let deliberation of the heart precede every word of the mouth, so that you speak nothing at all for which you could repent. The mouth of a monk will speak both few and reasonable things, and his lips never go beyond measure in speech. His whole conduct is set toward the edification of his neighbor, and he who teaches very many by his morals, even when silent, speaks useful things. He who is a true lover of God walks most humbly on the road, and he who fears the Most High restrains his mouth from loud laughter. In your whole life, think of yourself as mortal, and live as one dying, as one who is already breathing out his last breath. Happy is the kingdom, and rich before God the province, in which monks rise with the dawn, since where there is no fear of God the cloistered theaters are filled. The monk on the road

[segment 55]
*Concerning monks sent on the road.*

As the man to whom the Lord speaks, when he wakes from sleep, remembers nothing of what he had dreamed, so too the monastic canon commands. Therefore no one should presume to report any other things he has seen or heard outside the monastery, for this is a very great distraction. If anyone presumes to do so, let him undergo regular punishment. The same applies to anyone who presumes to go out beyond the cloisters of the monastery, or to go anywhere, or to do anything, however small, without the abbot's command.

Since we have voluntarily undertaken this rule of life and have sworn by his words, it is not lawful for any of us to turn back or to attempt anything to the contrary. What he has seen or heard outside, let him leave outside when he returns from the road, and not bring novelty in among the brothers, lest he produce various distractions of mind. He who keeps the commands of the Lord's rule is a lover of discipline. The man who always multiplies words and walks on the road speaking vocabulary after vocabulary is [unclear]. But the humble brother, speaking with God in the hidden silence of the heart, likewise walks rejoicing in his simplicity. Whatever the father's ordinance has commanded, he considers most diligently; and wherever he is, he carefully preserves everything.

The monk who is sent on the road, if he is preserved pure and keeps himself from every stain of fault, returns joyful to the monastery, and, as though he had escaped the danger of the sea, hastens with longing to the harbor of rest. But he who has lost shame, having become insolent, returns sad and deserving punishment, as though to a prison.

The true monk of Christ the Lord neither seeks nor loves the friendship of secular people, but despises it; but those who multiply familiar relations with people unlike themselves gradually fall away from their purpose of holiness. Cursed is the cloistered man who puts his confidence in human beings and withdraws his heart from the Lord God. Do not seek friendships with men, but take care to obtain the fellowship of the invisible angels. Frequent friendships with mortals do not suit a monk; through them the vigor of the mind is loosened and empty occupations are introduced. For just as dripping water over a long time hollows out stone, however hard and solid, so too excessive familiarity with secular people gradually softens monastic rigor. Have charity toward all, but familiarity with few.

He who has God as his friend easily disdains human familiarity; and he who speaks with angels takes very little delight in the conversation of mortals. Whenever on the road you see a sign of the Lord's Passion or of the Crucified One, do not pass by unless you say: I give you thanks, Lord Jesus Christ, for the pain of your wounds, for the shedding of your most holy blood, and for your most innocent death, which you endured for us on the altar of the cross. I beseech you, by your bitter Passion, have mercy on me now and in the dreadful hour of my death. Repeat this most blessed prayer often as you go, and never pass an image of the Crucified One unless you have given thanks to the Savior.

Do not quarrel with the one who walks with you on the road, lest, when he has returned to the monastery, he remember some evil thing against you. He who provokes his companion on a journey to impatience is like one who drives a rabid dog to bark by striking it. The wicked man is overcome by gentleness, and the humble of heart calms one stirred up in mind. Among secular people do not pour out speech, lest perhaps they take occasion for mockery against you.

We permit good things to be spoken of [unclear] among young men; but let the one who is lower in rank listen to him. The weary ploughman hastens to rest; and the monk who is sound in mind returns with longing to his cell, hastening by a direct way. Let the word be weighed in the mouth until reason brings forth a judgment with equity; and speech that can instruct its hearers should not be hidden.

Do not trust the songs of flatterers, for [unclear] turns the innocent man away, and they soothe his ears with disordered speeches. A monk who is fed on contentions is like [unclear], and like a spasmodic man who stirs up quarrels under the name of religion. Do not presume to speak before the learned, lest perhaps, when you do not understand the parable, you be laughed at by those standing around. The monk who recognizes himself as such does not use profound speech; and the one who teaches little ones makes questions plain.

When you go to people unlike your profession, do not boast in anything; but if you are questioned, answer humbly in few words. When set on a journey, always think of God, and regard reverence for your senior as your companion. Do absolutely nothing that you would wish not to be known to him, for, as the holy [unclear] says, in whatever...

[segment 56]
*Homily XII.*

The servant of Christ is believed to act in Christ’s place in the monastery; therefore he is rightly honored by all. Live, then, as though you always had him present before your eyes, and whatever you would not presume to do or say while he was looking on, do not attempt in any way when he is absent. For it is foolish consolation, when conscience is gnawed by guilt, for sin to lie hidden from men, and for one who knows himself sick unto death to flee the judgment of his shepherd. A monk who does evil and wants it hidden from his prelate has violated the integrity of the monastic rule and has gravely sinned against the fifth degree of humility. For it commands thus: The fifth degree of humility is this: if, by humble confession, he has not concealed from his abbot all evil thoughts coming into his heart and evil deeds done by him in secret.

These are the admonitions of father Benedict; these are the holy teachings of monastic instruction. If you, monk, despise or neglect them, you are proved forgetful of your own salvation. It is a sure sign of humility if a monk has not refused to open his heart to his senior, and wants none of the evils he has committed to be hidden from his shepherd. He who confesses his offense with humility will be strengthened against the adversary of his salvation; and he who hides his thoughts will advance into evil. For there is no evil thought so small that it will not gradually grow if neglected, and no vice so slight that it will not gain strength if it is dissembled.

Therefore evil thoughts must be driven away at once, from the beginning, because when a depraved thought is not immediately extinguished, after delight it begets delight; delight, in turn, begets consent; consent plunges one into action. Then the action of evil, more often repeated, produces habit; habit, necessity; necessity brings on despair; despair, contempt; contempt, obstinacy; and that soon begets the eternal death of the soul. For Scripture says: When the wicked man has come into the depth of evils, he despises. Is not your abbot, O monk, the shepherd and physician of your soul? Am I not, my dearest brothers, after God, the one who feeds your mortal bodies with necessary nourishment? Am I not compelled to minister to you the word of salvation? If you, the sheep, flee the shepherd, what pastures of the spirit do you expect from us? The sick man who flees the physician despises his own salvation and willingly kills his own soul.

Fo. XXIX.

A monk, therefore, walking in the way of malice and turning aside from the paths of justice, is near to death; and he falls so much the more into the depth of vices as he is less brought back by the judgment of the one correcting him. But he is truly a monk who guards his soul from every iniquity and does nothing at all that he would wish his shepherd not to know. He who conceals his way is not pure, and he who flees the presence of judges increases suspicion. Like a sheep you walk in the desert, to be seized by the wolf, negligent and impure monk, who withdraws yourself from the knowledge of your shepherd and are devoured by demons. A diseased sheep running to destruction, and a monk hiding every sin: there is no salvation for them unless they return to the physician.

Monks returning from a journey should immediately, on that very day, according to the monastic rule, prostrate themselves before the brothers and in the sight of all, so that whatever has been committed on the way through negligence may be expiated by the common prayers. For the common prayer of many avails greatly for washing away sins, if it has been poured out to the Lord with the fervor of devotion.

Here ends Homily XII, On monks sent on a journey.

*On the daily expiation of sins.*

*Homily XIII.*

Since we have been placed in this corruptible life, dearest brothers, we offend daily in many things; and however holily we may seem to conduct ourselves, we are not free from sin. For the body, which is corrupted, weighs down the soul, and the earthly dwelling depresses the mind that thinks many thoughts. For Scripture says: There is no just man on earth who does good and does not sin. Likewise the evangelist John says: If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us. Indeed, the soul sins through commerce with the flesh, when it is too much delighted, and the strength of the spirit is lessened by despair. For the mind is deceived by the presentation of sensible things, and readiness to offend is strengthened by long habit. The weakness of the flesh weakens the fervor of the spirit, and the frailty of the will resists the holy purpose of the mind.

Hence Solomon, the wisest king, says in the book of Proverbs: Seven times in a day the just man will fall and rise again, but the wicked will fall into evil. By the witness of this sentence we are admonished not to grow lukewarm in the purpose of holiness, even if it should happen that we have fallen seven times in a day. As often, O monk, as you fall from the purpose of holiness through sin, so often rise again to penance, intending thereafter never to return. For however often men have fallen, they can always rise again to repentance, and by tears and prayers obtain the grace of God which they had lost by sinning. For the heart of man is flexible and can be moved in either direction, because no man in the flesh can become so evil, if he makes use of the grace of the Lord.

Since these things are so, my brothers, you must be admonished by today’s exhortation to walk carefully, so that anyone who, with conscience as witness, already walks in the paths of justice may persevere daily by making progress in that path; and anyone who understands himself to be guilty before the Lord may amend his life as quickly as possible. If human frailty daily drives you, or, to speak more properly, casts you down and makes you offend, let the love of God and of your own salvation also stir you daily to expiate your fault humbly through true contrition. For thus the just man falls seven times in a day and rises again, since even if he slips through human frailty, nevertheless, always persevering in the purpose of holiness, he is strengthened more and more in soul in the love of God.

For while he keeps his conscience immune from grave sins, he is unwillingly defiled by daily negligences; and when he sees that he cannot live without offenses, he groans the more deeply, humbly acknowledging his own weakness. But if he has also been overtaken by a graver offense, he soon rises again to amendment through penance. Therefore, considering the condition of human frailty, the ancient governors of monastic discipline established various kinds of expiation, by which monks of good will might daily make progress toward the better, and the wicked might be compelled to the observance of regular life.

For in the Rule the holy father Benedict commands thus, saying: With all solicitude the abbot should take care concerning delinquent brothers, because the physician is needed not by the healthy but by those who are ill. For this reason the first leaders in the observance of the rule established, with provident consideration, that every day after Prime an assembly of faults should be held in the appointed place; in it, with the abbot or prior presiding, the deeds of all the brothers should be brought into the open with equal examination.

This place, my brothers, bears the figure of the purgatory of souls, because here not only are past sins expiated, but we are also forearmed against future ones. For in this place, which we call the chapter house, whatever you have done wrong openly is corrected and expiated through salutary penance. Here every day, with the reader reciting it, you come to know your monastic law, according to whose integrity you have promised to live. Here on each day you hear the exhortatory word of salvation. Here you listen to brief recitations of the victories of Christ’s martyrs and their manifold sufferings for the orthodox faith. This is the school of monks, necessary for the consideration of regular discipline, in which everything commanded to cloistered men for observance is learned.

There are two things, my dearest brothers, which this place requires of you: one, that in correction you always be humble

[segment 57]
--- Page 066 ---

Homily XIII. Folio XXX.

that you always maintain humble patience in correction; the other, that you willingly receive our exhortation, such as it is. Desiring you to be prepared for the first of these, our holy lawgivers ordered the brave deeds of the martyrs, whose imitators you ought to become, to be read; and then, so that you might be teachable in spirit, they commanded you to submit your necks to the yoke of the Rules. And so, that you may be strong in imitation of the holy martyrs and patient under necessary rebukes, the account of their passion is read here in your ears, so that, with your minds prepared for salutary doctrine, the reading from the Rule which you profess may be recited to you at the same time. The holy martyrs of Christ strengthen you by their example toward patience; and the blessed Benedict admonishes you by his teaching that you too should be prepared for injury.

Therefore let none of you, most beloved brothers, refuse in this place humbly to receive penance each day for his negligences; let no one disdain it. This place is the public expiation of sin, in which the severity of judgment is so tempered that the mercy of discretion is not violated. For I wish to be the physician of your souls, not their destroyer. Therefore approach the examination of our judgment without fear, so that, when you are charged with negligences committed openly, you may humbly proclaim them. I do not wish to burden you beyond the Rule, so as to transfer the most secret offenses of conscience from sacramental confession to the public examination of faults, which we know some have done to the grave harm of the souls of their subjects. For our lawgiver Benedict speaks thus in the Rule: “But if the cause of the sin is hidden in the soul, let him disclose it only to the abbot or to spiritual elders, who know how to heal their own wounds and those of others, and not to reveal or make them public.”

There are two places in the monastery for the expiation of sin: one for those sins that are manifest, and the other for those that are hidden. For manifest sins are to be expiated publicly before all in the daily proclamation of faults; hidden sins, however, and those whose disclosure would scandalize the weak, are to be referred to confession. For when anyone’s sin is manifest, it must also be punished publicly; and one who has offended in secret must also be corrected in secret. There are certain rulers of cloistered communities who bring the hidden sins of their subjects to public examination
