Homily XIII. Fol. XXX.

...that you should preserve patience; the other, that you should willingly receive our exhortation, such as it is. For the first point, our holy lawgivers, wishing you to be prepared, prescribe that the brave deeds of the martyrs, whose imitators you ought to become, be read; and then, so that you may be teachable in mind, they commanded that you be subjected to the discipline of the Rule. Thus, so that in imitation of the holy martyrs you may be brave and patient under necessary corrections, the account of their passion is read here in your ears, in order that, your minds prepared for saving instruction, a reading from the Rule which you profess may be recited to you. The holy martyrs of Christ encourage you by their example to patience; blessed Benedict also admonishes you by his teaching that you be ready even for injury.

Therefore let none of you, most beloved brothers, refuse in this place humbly to receive penance each day for his negligences; let no one disdain it. This is the public place for expiating sin, in which the severity of judgment is so tempered that the kindness of discretion is not violated. For I wish to be the physician of your souls, not their destroyer. Therefore come securely to the examination of our judgment, so that, concerning negligences committed openly, you may humbly proclaim them in simplicity. I do not wish to burden you beyond the Rule by transferring the most secret offenses of conscience from sacramental confession to the public examination of faults, which we know certain persons have done, to the grave detriment of the souls of their subjects. For thus our lawgiver Benedict says in the Rule: “But if the cause of the sin has lain hidden in the soul, let him disclose it only to the abbot or to spiritual elders, who know how to heal their own wounds and those of others, and not to uncover or publish them.”

There are two places in the monastery for the expiation of sin: one for those sins that are manifest, and another for those that are hidden. Manifest sins are to be expiated publicly before all in the daily proclamation of faults; but hidden sins, and those whose manifestation would scandalize the weak, are to be referred to confession. For a sin that is manifest must also be punished publicly; and one who has offended in secret must also be corrected in secret. There are certain rulers of cloistered persons who do not fear to bring the hidden sins of their subjects to public examination; in my judgment they do not carry out the rights of their office with sufficient prudence, since they close off the way of salvation to their subjects and bind the free act of confession under the bond of ill will. How dangerous this is, no one with even moderate wisdom fails to understand. For the holy founder rebukes this rashness in dealing with the passions when he says to monks: “The abbot must exercise very great solicitude, and with all sagacity and industry take care not to lose any of the sheep entrusted to him. Let him know that he has undertaken the care of weak souls, not tyranny over healthy ones.”

We have appointed for you four spiritual counselors from among the elders, so that if any of you has been overtaken by some hidden temptation and has conceived shame before us, he may be able to have secure recourse to them for his consolation. Yet so far we have found no one in the whole congregation whom we have observed to have withdrawn trust from us, whether in confession or outside it. For all of you know and plainly see that I seek nothing more in you, desire nothing more, than your salvation and the daily increase of spiritual progress. You are my crown, you are my good, you are my joy, because I count myself happy when I know that you have made progress in the love of God.

You have obtained a worthy opportunity for progress, dearest brothers, since the place in which we serve the Lord is remote from every disturbance, and we do not cease to advance you toward better things by daily admonition. Whenever one of you falls into some sin through human frailty, he is not permitted to remain long in dejection of spirit, since one is proclaimed by another in true fraternal charity each day for his negligences. This is the workshop of salvation, in which the mind is fed by the food of heavenly teaching, and the soul, by public sanctification, is daily cleansed from the stains of offenses. This is a place of purgation on earth, in which the monk is discreetly corrected for his negligences; and by humble patience, for the love of Christ, he is daily offered in a long martyrdom. It is my part, brothers, in this arrangement of faults, to correct discreetly those who are proclaimed; it is yours to suffer all things most patiently.

[segment 59]
--- Page 068 ---

*On the daily expiation of sins*

to make satisfaction. For we must make satisfaction for our sins; and if we neglect to do this in this life, in the life to come we shall necessarily do it under compulsion. There are some among you who take correction badly. To them we declare what we have often said: that those who refuse to endure small and light things here must necessarily endure very grievous things in the future. But I, considering their weakness and ignorance, am grievously tormented within myself, because I see their minds wounded and yet am afraid to apply the necessary medicines. For if I apply remedies suited to their diseases, I fear that, made fainthearted, they may fall into despair, or certainly, given over to a reprobate sense, may withdraw altogether from their holy purpose. Therefore I use as much discretion as I can, as all of you will bear me witness, preferring to send the weak, through patience with charity, to purgatory, rather than, through just severity, to have them descend in despair to hell.

For who in this mortal life has ever been able to make satisfaction for sins who has not applied, or does not now apply, the good will of penance? For good will alone commends the works of penitents. But since many are punished unwillingly, they are still far removed from perfect satisfaction. Believe me, my dearest brothers, that satisfaction for sins is commended more by suffering than by acting. For even our Lord himself and Savior of us all, Jesus Christ, expiated the sin of the whole world by suffering on the altar of the cross. Would that you knew what an effective medicine it is for a soul stained by sins to receive proclamations and corrections with humble patience, and also, for the increase of perfect satisfaction, to bear injuries and insults with equanimity. I am certain that humble patience in adversity far surpasses every kind of satisfaction; and the readier the mind has been to bear insults and injuries for the love of Christ, the more easily and quickly it obtains from the Lord pardon for all its sins and a fuller indulgence.

For this place in which we now speak is the workshop of purgation and expiation; in it zeal for regular discipline holds first place, discretion presides over the examination, and charity directs and completes all things. Therefore in this very place patience is very necessary for you, for without its presence no satisfaction will be completed. Whatever monk is a zealous lover of his salvation and comes to this place as to a workshop of expiation diligently never goes away from it without having been made better than he was. For this place is to be feared and dreaded by demons, but dear and greatly venerable to the holy angels of God; in it the fault of humble monks is washed away, and whatever is committed openly is also expiated publicly before all. But if some hidden fault has been committed by any of you, the secret confession of the penitent will likewise purge it away. In both cases pure confession with humility is required, and both here and there patience is declared necessary for you.

Therefore, whatever monk desires to be good, prepare your soul in all patience, so that both in adversity and in prosperity you may keep the tranquility of your mind most constant. When you have been proclaimed by some fellow-brother for your negligences, bear it patiently, brother, with all humility, faithfully bringing to mind how innocently the Son of God was accused for you. For if that just and innocent one, who committed no sin, suffered insults, why should you not patiently bear reproof from a senior, you who offend daily in many things? He most patiently endured innumerable injuries for your salvation; and why should you not, for your sins, humbly and patiently endure brief and few admonitions? For if, when you are justly proclaimed in judgment, you are blamed for fault, you must necessarily be patient, since you yourself have deserved what you suffer; and humble toleration in correction will be imputed to you as satisfaction for sins. But if you did not deserve the proclamation in judgment, nevertheless bear it patiently for the love of Christ; then it will be reckoned to you not only as satisfaction for preceding sins, but much more as justification. Therefore whether a monk is proclaimed justly or unjustly, he has no just cause for defense, because if he has sinned, he suffers deservedly; but if he is innocent, he is the more bound to patience. For no one who is not patient and humble will be able to imitate Christ. But since a monk of Christ is especially bound to be his disciple and imitator, it is especially fitting for him to be patient. Without

[segment 60]
--- Page 069 ---

to make satisfaction for his sins, and to expiate all his iniquities by the daily chastisement of his body in humility.

Know, my brothers, that there is no sin so small that the justice of almighty God lets it go unpunished. Therefore, since there is none of us who has not offended in many things, arm yourselves, I beg you, with patience and humility, so that as often as you are corrected for your sins, you may bear it with all gentleness. For in this time of free choice you can make satisfaction for many debts with comparatively slight expiation, whereas after death in purgatory the soul will be tormented for a long time and most severely even for small faults. Indeed, voluntary satisfaction has great value; what is done from necessity wins less favor. Altogether, brothers, it is fitting and necessary for souls to be cleansed of sin, if they are to reign in heaven and rejoice with Christ. But the soul that has neglected the purging of its sins in the present life will be punished more heavily in the life to come.

Let none of you say, my dearest brothers, what many ignorant people are accustomed to say: “It is enough for me if my soul undergoes the pains of purgatory.” Believe me, brothers: whoever has said this word from the heart does not consider what purgatory will be. For he does not know what he is asking who desires purgatory. Just as a man is foolish who, when he could pay his debt as a free man, scorns to do so and willingly goes to prison, so he deserves to be judged the most foolish of all men who, when he can make satisfaction to God for his sins in this life, puts it off to the future. The penance of this life is fruitful, and voluntary satisfaction is also greatly meritorious; after death, however, there will be no place for repentance, but each one will then receive according to what he has done in his body, whether good or evil.

Think on these things, brothers, while you live in health, lest, overtaken by the command of death, you finally grieve too late. Let not doubtful tomorrow await today’s salvation; and he who can pay his debt with grace while at liberty should not allow himself to be dragged to prison with torture. As much as each person can throughout his whole life, let him take care to make satisfaction for his sins, because the spirit returns secure to the Lord if, while still existing in the body, it is daily purified by the fire of divine love. You have here a place in which the daily expiation of public offenses is carried out; if you kept true and humble patience in your soul, you would undoubtedly obtain the certain remission of them all. As for secret and hidden sins, not only does the Rule admonish you what you ought to do, but daily sacramental confession also instructs you in all things.

For we are accustomed daily, each one devoutly, to offer the Son of God at the altar; and none of us approaches that most holy mystery unless he has first, according to custom, made confession, because the greatest purity is required where, through the Mass, the salvation of the whole world is renewed. In the midst of these things, most beloved brothers, who among us would not make daily progress in the love of God, unless he shamefully and entirely laid aside every desire for progress? For I dare to say that a monk who does not make progress in this holy congregation is plainly proved to have utterly forgotten his own salvation.

Believe me, brothers: God will save no one against his will. If you neglect your own progress, it will not be imputed to him, but to you. For God is the intellectual light that enlightens every man coming into this world, as the high-flying John says; but those who do not receive the light necessarily remain in darkness. He who turns his mind away from the faith that is in the Son of God remains in darkness; and if he does not do the works of light, faith will profit him nothing for salvation. Outside the faith of our Lord Jesus Christ there is no salvation, and whoever thinks otherwise is not a Christian. O man, do not let a foolish notion of piety seduce you into believing that anyone outside the Christian faith has salvation unto eternal life. For Christ, the only Son of God, is the sole Savior of the world; whoever does not believe in him, the wrath of God remains upon him, and he will be condemned with the devil.

But you, my brothers, who already believe in our Lord Jesus Christ, I beg you henceforth to live in such a way that, Christian in name and deed, you may be able to come to his fellowship, who lives and reigns with God the Father, in the unity of the Holy Spirit, God, throughout all ages.

On True Penitence

[segment 61]
*On the daily expiation of sins.*

[unclear], and humbly to expiate all their other iniquities by the daily chastisement of the body. Know, my brothers, that there is no sin so small that the justice of almighty God leaves it unpunished. Therefore, since there is none of us who does not offend in many things, arm yourselves, I beg you, with patience and humility, so that whenever you are corrected for sins in the body, you may endure it with all gentleness. For in this time of free choice you can make satisfaction for many debts with a small expiation, whereas after death in purgatory the soul will be tormented long and most grievously even for small sins.

Indeed, voluntary satisfaction is of great value; but what is done from necessity merits less toward grace. Altogether, brothers, it is fitting and necessary that the soul be purged of fault, if it is to reign in heaven and rejoice with Christ. But the soul that has neglected the purgation of its faults in the present life will be punished more heavily in the life to come. Let none of you say, my dearest brothers, what many ignorant people are accustomed to say: “It will be enough for me if my soul has reached the punishments of purgatory.” Believe me, brothers: whoever has said this word with his heart and mind does not weigh what purgatory will be. For he does not know what he asks who desires purgatory.

Just as he is foolish who, when he could pay his debt as a free man, scorns to do so and willingly goes to prison, so he must deservedly be judged the most foolish of all men who, when he can make satisfaction to God for his sins in this life, postpones it to the future. The repentance of this life is fruitful, and voluntary satisfaction is also very meritorious; but after death there will be no place for repentance. Then each one will receive according as he has done in his body, whether good or evil. Think on these things, brothers, while you live in health, lest, forestalled by the command of death, you at last grieve too late. Let today’s salvation not wait for a doubtful tomorrow; and he who can pay his debt with grace and in freedom should not prepare himself to be dragged to prison with torment.

As much as each person can throughout his whole life, so much let him take care to make satisfaction for sins, since the spirit returns securely to the Lord when, while still existing in the body, it is daily purged by the fire of divine love. Here you have a place in which the daily expiation of public offenses is carried out; if you hold true and humble patience in your mind, without doubt you will obtain certain remission of them all. As for secret and hidden faults, not only does the Rule admonish you what you ought to do, but daily sacramental confession also instructs you in all things. For at the altar we are each accustomed daily to offer devoutly the Son of God; and none of us approaches that most holy mystery unless he has first confessed according to custom, since the highest purity is required where the salvation of the whole world is renewed through the Mass.

Amid these things, most beloved brothers, who among us would not daily make progress in the love of God, unless he shamefully and utterly laid aside every desire for progress? For I dare say that the monk who does not make progress in this holy congregation is proved to have utterly forgotten his own salvation. Believe me, brothers: God will save no one unwillingly. If you neglect your own progress, it will be imputed not to him but to you. For God is the intellectual light that enlightens every man coming into this world, as John, soaring aloft, says; but those who do not receive the light necessarily remain in darkness. He who turns his mind away from the faith that is in the Son of God remains in darkness; and for him who does not do the works of light, faith will profit nothing toward salvation.

Outside the faith of our Lord Jesus Christ there is no salvation, and he who thinks otherwise is not a Christian. Do not let the consideration of foolish piety seduce you, O man, so that you believe that someone outside the faith of Christ, in any religion whatever, is to be saved, however innocently he may live or according to the law of nature; for just as outside Noah’s ark there was no life for men, so outside the Christian faith there is no salvation for anyone unto eternal life. For Christ, the only Son of God, is the sole Savior of the world; whoever has not believed in him, the wrath of God remains upon him, and he will be condemned with the devil. But you, my brothers, who now believe in our Lord Jesus Christ, I beg you, live hereafter in such a way that, Christians in name and deed, you may be able to come to his fellowship: he who lives and reigns with God the Father, God in the unity of the Holy Spirit, forever and ever.

*On true repentance.*

[segment 62]
all the joys of this world always and everywhere to despise for love of God, and to refuse to undergo no adversity or hardship for the honor of your Savior. For thus the canon admonishes you, thus the monastic institution commands, thus reason necessary for salvation dictates. You are commanded to be content with every vileness and extremity, and always to be fearful in all things.

But I see that many of you overturn the rule of salvation and live in the monastery otherwise than you ought. Monks now are not afraid to seek delights in monasteries, though they came from the world to conversion poor and beggarly. For the things they could not have outside, if they are now lacking in the monastery, they rebel against humility as if they had suffered the gravest insult. Woe to the monks whom not love of God but want has made cloistered men, whose god is the belly, and who think of nothing less than poverty.

It is not yours, O monks, henceforth to seek delights in the monastery, which the rule commands you wholly to renounce. It is fitting for you to despise all pleasures of the present life, and to love absolutely nothing in this world except God. For your whole way of life ought always to be bound to divine services, since you promised to live according to the spirit and not according to the flesh. He who is a true monk never lives without penance; rather, whoever truly longs for the feasts of eternal happiness daily mortifies his body through abstinence. For it cannot be that a monk should enjoy the delights of the present life and the eternal ones; but if he longs for those, he must despise these.

For the monk who professes the cenobitic canon is commanded to be a disciple of Christ the Savior. If he desires to please him in truth, he must become his imitator. Thus he says to him in the holy Gospel: Whoever wishes to be my disciple, let him follow me. And what it means to follow him the knowing apostle John teaches in a certain epistle, saying: He who says that he remains in Christ ought himself also to walk just as he walked. And the Lord himself again says in the Gospel: Whoever wishes to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross daily, and follow me. Whoever loves father or mother more than me is not worthy of me. And whoever loves son or daughter above me is not worthy of me.

Your master has not spoken obscurely, O monk, nor has he wrapped his meaning in riddles; rather, he has uttered a plain discourse, in which you can offer no excuse. For Christ himself is the pattern and rule of good and perfect conduct for every Christian, and especially for the monk. The cloistered man who does not follow him with his whole heart in these things boasts in vain of his profession of faith. For just as a Christian is called from Christ, so a disciple is named from imitation of the master. Therefore imitate the master in conduct, so that you may prove yourself a true disciple of Christ.

For the Lord Jesus Christ, whose disciple you profess yourself to be, O monk, though he was rich, became poor for us; but you, though vile and needy, seek abundance of all things in the school of poverty. And though the preceptor commands you to despise all things for love of him, you again desire to possess all things in the monastery. Your Lord and master was obedient to the Father for us, even to death on a cross; but you, full of pride and impatience, either refuse to obey your superiors for love of him, or obey unwillingly and with indignation.

For your salvation he endured not only the words and reproaches of wicked men with the greatest patience throughout his whole life, but also lashes, livid blows, and horrible wounds for love of you. He also did not refuse to undergo a most shameful death, so that he might recall your soul from the error of death to the life of eternal light. From the day of his birth he lived in the world poor and humble, filled with many tribulations and reproaches, so that by teaching and benefits he might restore you, a most ruined man, to recognition and love. For what would we be, if the Son of God had not appeared as man, except most utterly lost? And what are we, if we do not imitate the master according to our strength, except ungrateful?

Woe to us ungrateful mortal monks, who disdain to follow humbly our Lord and Savior. Let us examine our way of life, brothers, and compare the disciples with the master whose name has been invoked over us; and we shall plainly understand how far we have withdrawn from the paths of our most kindly preceptor. But let us return again with our whole heart, I pray, and do penance, because we have withdrawn far from the way of salvation, serving the flesh rather than the spirit. Let no one deceive himself with the vanity of hope; let no one wait until the last hour of this life, when he can no longer sin, for penance, because neither the length of life has certainty.

[segment 63]
--- Page 072 ---

[segment 64]
--- Page 073 ---

Homily XIII. Folio XXXIII.

Nor does delaying repentance bring security. He who does not repent while he is healthy is foolish; and he who does not amend his faults when he can, by the just judgment of God loses the power he has deferred. Repentance is fruitful when it has been voluntary by freedom of choice; but when the danger of death is already pressing, it seems rather to be necessary than to have been taken up by repentance's own choice.

Let no one say in his heart, my brothers, let no one think that the labor of repenting is hard, and for that reason must be deferred until the last little portion of this life. That thought is exceedingly dangerous, since the fixed term of life, which divine providence has appointed for each person, just as he has willed it to be hidden from all, so he will not prolong it for anyone who deliberately defers repentance.

If we wish to be safe and secure in conscience, let us do penance while we are healthy, while we are able to sin, while no necessity of approaching death yet presses upon us. For it is a very doubtful thing to do penance when necessity already compels, and the nearness of death makes you no longer able to sin: when, that is, you have not dismissed your sins, but they, finding you powerless, have dismissed you.

O how many, deceived by the hope of a longer life, descend into the abyss of eternal damnation, who when healthy scorned to do penance. For the longer you defer repentance, the less disposed to repent you will always be found. Wickedness gains strength with the passage of time, and there is no sin so small that it does not grow by neglect. Therefore embrace repentance while you are healthy, and do not put it off from day to day, since you know what you can do today, but you do not know what tomorrow will be. For each day brings its own new troubles, and he who today is not disposed toward repentance will perhaps be less fit tomorrow.

But let none of you say, “What sin have I committed that repentance should seem necessary to me, when my whole life is nothing other than a certain hard and long necessity of repenting?” You have heard Scripture testify that there is no man on earth who does good and does not sin, since no one is clean before God. For even if he does not commit the great crimes of human wickedness, such as homicide, theft, adultery, fornications, and things like these, nevertheless, because you have been set in that calling in which you ought with all your strength to raise every effort toward the state of perfection, sins that are small for mortals living in the world are certainly imputed to you as more serious.

For all sins, so far as possible, must be avoided by cloistered men, not only those that impede salvation, but also those that stain the perfection enjoined upon them. And which of you lives with as much purity as monastic perfection requires? It is a great sin for a monk if he has set aside his effort toward perfection, if he has lived negligently in the monastery, if he has transgressed even one commandment of the canon and the Gospel. And who among you, brothers, loves the Lord God with such sincerity, such charity, and such fervor of love as a man is bound to love his maker and redeemer? Or whom will you give me from among all of you who does not at some time exceed, either in taking food or in drink, more than befits the integrity of monks? Which of you does not offend in word, or who is not sometimes disturbed by evil thoughts? Whom among you has acedia not struck, and whom has sad sloth not troubled? Who is there in this small congregation of yours whom elation does not touch, and whom impatience has not quite often made murmuring?

Let each one of you consider the paths of his way of life, and he will find that he fails in many things, even at any moment. For what if a monk has committed no evil? What if he has kept himself clean from this wicked age? He will not be able to be perfect unless he has both turned away from evil and done the good that his perfection requires. For one without the other does not suffice, but from both perfection rises up in a man.

Therefore, brothers, we must always keep watch with the highest zeal, so that we may satisfy the institution of the holy fathers, according to which by public profession we have solemnly bound ourselves to live, in order that we may direct our effort without interruption toward the perfection set before us. Let not the consideration of our own weakness draw us back from holy effort, because we are helped no less by the grace of the creator than we run by our own desire. Just as every day, by living rather negligently, we fail on many occasions in many things, so also at every moment, rising from the torpor of mind, let us renew holy effort in a good purpose. Let us join tears to holy prayers, and with devout supplications always call upon God's help.

Let us keep charity inviolable in all things, since it is charity that covers the multitude of sins. Each day the good soldier of Christ, who desires to be faithful to his Lord, should examine himself by discussion at least once, and should consider more diligently how much he has advanced or fallen back in holy desire on that day. Whenever he finds that he has failed in anything, let him immediately do penance, and with tears ask mercy from the Lord. Let him not wait doubtfully for tomorrow; salvation is today, and he who can quickly find pardon should in no way insert a delay. Let him at once grieve in heart that he has sinned against the Lord; let him also renew his holy purpose with new fervor, and let him judge that he has utterly lost that day on which he did not become better by advancing.

Prayer should always be on the monk's lips; constant compunction should be in his heart; tears of devotion should continually flow from his eyes; and the exercises of inward love should never recede from his memory. The most bitter remembrance of the Lord's passion should often be in the mind of the cloistered man; and all the prayers he makes to the Father should be offered to God in the power of the blood and wounds of our Lord, whether prayer is being extended to God or meditation on his words is being turned over in the mind. Let the devout monk turn away from evil and do the good he can, with cheerfulness and discretion of the Spirit of God, since, as has been said, for doing penance it is not enough to change one's morals for the better and withdraw from evil deeds, unless one also strives to adorn one's conscience with good works.

For true repentance, therefore, as you know, my brothers, four things are required. The first is true contrition for sins, which is a voluntary sorrow of the heart, undertaken in detestation of the evils one has committed. Of this Scripture says: “A contrite and humbled heart, O God, you will not despise.” And again: “Those who sow in tears” [unclear]. Sorrow of contrition is greatly useful; the greater it is, the more fruitful repentance will be. For true contrition is that which both sorrow of mind commends and the continual cleansing of tears attests. Great is the usefulness of tears, because there is no sin so grave that contrition, accompanied by tears, does not destroy it.

Hence the holy teacher Augustine says: “O humble tear, how powerful and virtuous you are, for yours is the kingdom, yours is the power. You do not fear the gaze of the judge; you impose silence on accusing enemies; you alone enter to the king, but you do not return alone; you conquer the unconquerable and bind the omnipotent.” The same man also says: “The holier anyone is and the fuller of holy desire, the more abundant is his weeping in prayer.” True compunction has sorrow; sorrow, moreover, is shown by tears, and by these repentance is adorned as if with certain ornaments. True compunction makes the soul shudder at sin, and when it has begun from fear, increased by its own growth, it gradually rises up into love. Its virtue is great and its power truly wonderful, before which every enormity of sins soon gives way. For whomever true compunction of heart has made suppliant, the merciful Lord soon hears him.

O happy tears, O saving affliction and truly sweet bitterness, by which [unclear], through which the compunct soul rises up to the mercy of God. For, as the holy [unclear] says, [unclear] is renewed. The second thing that pertains to the truth of repentance is a firm purpose of amendment, since whoever has the word of confession on his lips and does not have the purpose of amendment in his heart is either deceitful or empty. But whoever has it in his heart and not on his lips is either proud or timid, and by no means fit for true repentance. For Scripture says: “With the heart one believes unto righteousness, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation.” This sentence, lovers of Christ, is especially true in the time when the statute of the Church has decreed confession necessary for salvation. The third, therefore, is confession of the mouth. For the truth of repentance, contrition of heart alone is not sufficient, especially from this point, nor is the purpose of future amendment alone sufficient. But what this saving confession ought to be, I know that none of you is unaware.

[segment 65]
*On the True Penitence of Monks.*

... let us invoke him with supplications. Let us preserve charity inviolate in all things, because it is charity that covers a multitude of sins. Each day the good soldier of Christ, who desires to be faithful to his Lord, should examine his conscience by discussion at least once, and consider more carefully how much on that day he has advanced or fallen back in holy desire. Whenever he finds that he has sinned in anything, let him at once do penance and with tears ask mercy from the Lord. Let him not wait for the doubtful morrow: salvation is for today. He who can immediately find pardon should by no means introduce delay. Let him at once grieve in his heart that he has sinned against the Lord; let him also renew his holy purpose with new fervor; and let him judge that he has utterly lost that day on which he has not become better by making progress.

Prayer should always be on the monk's lips; continual compunction should be in his heart; tears of devotion should continually flow from his eyes; and he should never withdraw from the inward exercises of love or from their remembrance. Let the very bitter remembrance of the Lord's Passion often be in the mind of the cloistered man; and let all the prayers that he makes to the Father be brought forward in the power of the blood and wounds of our Lord Jesus Christ. Let there always be on his lips words that show the sign of a compunct mind: either let prayer be offered to God, or let meditation on his words be renewed. Let the devout monk turn away from evil and do the good that he can, with cheerfulness and with the discretion of the Spirit of God. For, as has been said, to do penance it is not enough to change one's conduct for the better and withdraw from evil action, unless one also strives to adorn his conscience with good works.

Therefore, my brothers, as you know, four things are required for true penitence. The first is true contrition for sins, which is sorrow of heart voluntarily assumed in detestation of the evils that one has committed. Of this Scripture says: “A contrite and humbled heart, O God, thou wilt not despise.” And again: “Those who sow in tears shall reap in exultation.” For every sorrow of penitence is sorrow of contrition; the greater it is, the more fruitful penitence will be. True contrition is that which both sorrow of mind commends and continual cleansing by tears attests. Great is the usefulness of tears, because there is no sin so grave that contrition, joined with tears, does not wipe it away.

Hence the holy doctor Augustine says: “O humble tear, how powerful and virtuous you are, because yours is the kingdom, yours is the power. You do not fear the gaze of the judge; you impose silence on accusing enemies. Alone you enter before the king, but alone you do not depart; you conquer the unconquerable; you bind the omnipotent.” He likewise says: “The holier anyone is, and the fuller of holy desire, the more abundant is his weeping in prayer.” True compunction has sorrow; sorrow is shown by tears; and penitence is adorned by these as by certain ornaments. True compunction makes the soul shudder at sins; and when it has begun from fear, increased by its own advances, it gradually rises into love. Great is its virtue and truly admirable its power; before it every enormity of sins soon yields. Whoever true compunction of heart has made to supplicate, the merciful Lord soon hears him.

O happy tears, saving affliction and truly sweet bitterness, which the devil fears, through which the compunct soul rises to the mercy of God. For, as the holy pope Gregory says, the more richly guilt falls away, the higher the knowledge of truth is attained, because, for seeing the inward light, the conscience stained by former fault is renewed when baptized with tears. The second thing that pertains to the truth of penitence is a firm purpose of amendment, because whoever has the word of confession in his mouth and does not have the purpose of amendment in his heart is either deceitful or vain. But whoever has it in his heart and not in his mouth is either proud or timid, and in no way fit for true penitence. For Scripture says: “With the heart one believes unto righteousness, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation.” This judgment, lovers of Christ, must be understood principally of faith in our Lord Jesus Christ; nevertheless it seems applicable to true penitence. For without confession of the mouth, contrition of heart alone is not enough for penitence, especially from that time when the statutes of the Church decreed confession necessary for salvation. The third, therefore, is confession of the mouth, necessary for the truth of penitence; without it neither contrition nor the purpose of future amendment is enough. But what this saving confession ought to be, I know that none of you can be ignorant.

[segment 66]
[Page 075]

He disgracefully expelled our first parents from the kingdom of paradise and innocence, who did not devour a bird, a fish, a calf, or a pig, so that you might judge food to be the fault, but presumed against God’s commandment to take a very lowly apple, so that you may know that it is not the food but the appetite that is liable to the vice of sin. For it can often happen that a person takes more splendid foods without fault while he regulates his appetite, and that another takes meaner foods not without guilt of conscience, when he exceeds the measure of temperance through immoderation. Therefore let none of you consider himself safe from the vice of gluttony on the grounds that he eats neither lavish nor delicate foods, since not the food but the appetite for food is held at fault. For it is quite possible for one who eats beans and vegetables to contract the guilt of sin, and for another who takes the most delicate birds as food not to sin. Hence one of the holy fathers says: “A belly filled with any foods produces seedbeds of lust; nor can a mind, choked by the weight of foods, avail to govern the rudders of discretion.” For not only overindulgence in wine customarily makes the mind drunk, but also excess of every food makes it unsteady.

[segment 67]
--- Page 076 ---

*All things, chapter 15. Folio 35.*

...and unsteady, and it wholly strips it of every contemplation of integrity and purity. Therefore great discretion is needed, so that only the food necessary for sustaining life is supplied to the body, and no excess of indulgence occurs.

Hence the holy pope Gregory says: “It is a great labor of discretion for the exactor of foods both to supply something to the body and to deny it something; by not giving, to restrain gluttony, and by giving, to nourish nature.” For through abstinence vices are to be extinguished, not the flesh; and through food the body is to be nourished, not sins.

Thus it is necessary for a monk to preserve the art of continence in his diet, so that he kills not the flesh but the vices. For often, while we pursue the enemy in the flesh, by immoderate abstinence we severely slay the spirit of devotion which we love. Very often also, while we spare the flesh, pitying it like a fellow citizen, we nourish an enemy for battle against ourselves. But when the vice of gluttony rules, men lose everything they have bravely accomplished, because when the belly is not restrained, all virtues are overthrown together. For one does not rise to the conflict of spiritual combat unless the enemy placed within us, namely the appetite of gluttony, is first subdued by us with God’s help; for unless we overcome the things that are nearer to us, we hasten in vain to fight against those set farther off. Indeed, war is waged in vain in the field against foreign enemies if a treacherous citizen is held within the city walls. We never overcome the assaults of demons unless we first overcome gluttony in ourselves.

Brothers, let no one seduce himself with empty persuasion, so as to believe that there is no sin among beans and vegetables, if the mind relaxes the appetite for receiving them with excessive pleasure. For in consumption appetite can be excessive anywhere, when it is extended beyond the norm of nature. See, we never eat meat, whether in the monastery or outside it; here in this country district we very rarely see fish; no one among us has ever seen more sumptuous foods; we neither can nor indeed ought to have delicate dishes. Our foods are barley, oats, beans, peas and vegetables, milk, cheese, and eggs. From these we receive the daily sustenance of our life, and hunger stirs appetite so that disgust at them may not arise. Outside the season of the Lenten fast, fish are so rare for us that scarcely once in a month a few, and very tiny ones, are brought from the river Nab. In the whole Bursfelde reform there is no monastery less provided for in the necessity of fish.

But surely for that reason we can still fall into the vice of gluttony, since we cannot find more luxurious foods? Indeed, since the fault is not in food, as has been said, but in appetite, the eater of peas or beans can sin no less in eating than one who is accustomed to eat the most sumptuous and exquisite foods. Poverty does not make us utterly free from fault, but marks out for us a simple and rustic diet, by which nature may somehow be sustained. This, my brothers, I beg us to take care over: that in the very thing which produces disgust at delicacies, our conscience may not be polluted by excess. Let us receive with patience and thanksgiving from what the Lord has given as much as necessity itself requires, and let us not think that the snares of the devil cannot harm us, for he overthrew the first men in paradise not with more luxurious food, but with a small and cheap apple. For the vice is not in the food but in the appetite, when we either desire things forbidden to us or, eagerly indulging in things permitted, use them otherwise than we ought.

Nor does abstinence befit anyone more than a monk, who, since he has been specially called to Christ’s military service, ought not by his profession to have leisure for any delicacies. Our poverty imposes on us a certain necessity of abstinence, because we can restrain ourselves to temperance so much the more easily when, the less we abound in foods, the more purely we fast in holiness of soul. For temperance is more easily preserved in poverty, while among riches it is certainly more quickly endangered.

What the food of monks ought to be, blessed Jerome makes clear, saying in a certain letter to Paulinus: “Let your food be cheap and taken in the evening: vegetables, namely, and legumes; and count it among the highest delights sometimes to eat a few little fish. One who longs for Christ and is fed by that heavenly bread does not greatly ask what dung he may make from costly foods. The lover of Christ loves abstinence; he cultivates fasting as the one remedy of soul and body; with the utmost zeal he takes care that his heart not be weighed down by drunkenness, and he takes no more food than nature, content with little, demands.”

But the monk who casts off fasting, who spurns abstinence, the nurse of virtues...

[segment 68]
Homily XV. Folio XXXV.

and renders it wavering, and strips it entirely of every contemplation of integrity and purity. Therefore great discretion is needed, so that only the food necessary for the support of life is supplied to the body, and no excess of indulgence occurs. Hence the holy Pope Gregory says: “It is a great labor of discretion to give something to the body asking for food, and to deny it something: by not giving, to restrain gluttony, and by giving, to nourish nature.” For through abstinence vices are to be extinguished, not the flesh; and through food the body is to be nourished, not sins.

Thus, then, it is necessary that the monk maintain continence in his diet, so that he kills not the flesh, but vices. For often, while we pursue the enemy in the flesh, we cruelly slaughter, through immoderate abstinence, the spirit of devotion which we love. More often also, while we are too sympathetic to the flesh and spare it as though it were a fellow-citizen, we nourish a very evil enemy against ourselves. But when the vice of gluttony has mastery, men lose all the brave deeds they have done; for when the belly is not restrained, all virtues are overthrown together.

For a person does not rise up to the conflict of spiritual combat if the enemy placed very near within us, namely the appetite of gluttony, is not first subdued by us, with God’s help; for unless we overcome the things that are nearer to us, we hasten in vain to fight against those placed farther away. For war is waged in vain in the field against foreign enemies if within the city walls there is a citizen lying in ambush. We never overcome the assaults of demons unless we first overcome gluttony in ourselves.

Let no one, brothers, deceive himself by empty persuasion, so as to believe that there is no sin among beans and vegetables, if the mind seeks to take them with too much eager pleasure. For in taking food there can be excess everywhere, when appetite is extended beyond the rule of nature. See, we eat meat neither in the monastery nor outside it; here in this open countryside we see fish very rarely; no one among us has ever seen richer foods; delicacies we neither can nor ought to have. Our foods are barley, oats, beans, peas, and vegetables, also milk, cheese, and eggs. From these we receive the daily supports of our life, and hunger stirs appetite so that disgust for them may not arise. Outside the time of the Lenten fast, fish are so rare for us that scarcely once a month a few are brought from the river Nahe, and those are very small. In the whole Bursfelde reform there is no monastery less provided with the necessity of fish.

But can we therefore not fall into the vice of gluttony, because we cannot find rich foods? Indeed, since the vice is not in the food, as has already been said, but in the appetite, the eater of peas or beans can sin no less in eating than he who is accustomed to chew the richest and choicest foods. Poverty does not make us wholly safe from gluttony, although it diminishes the occasions for sinning for those who sin. We have, indeed, simple and rustic foods by which nature may somehow be sustained. This, my brothers, I pray that we take care of: that in that which disgust creates instead of delicacy, our conscience may not be polluted by excess. Let us take with patience and thanksgiving from what the Lord has given as much as necessity itself requires; and let us not think that the snares of the devil cannot harm us, since he overthrew the first human beings in paradise not by rich food, but by a small and common apple. For the vice is not in the food, but in the appetite, when either we desire things forbidden to us, or, eagerly gaping after permitted things, we use them otherwise than we ought.

Nor does abstinence befit anyone more than a monk, who, since he has been specially called to the warfare of Christ, ought by the content of his profession not to have leisure for any delicacies. Our poverty imposes upon us a certain necessity of abstinence; for we can restrain ourselves to temperance all the more easily, the less we find that by which we might overflow with delights. Let us therefore turn this most welcome necessity, I pray, into weapons of salvation, so that the less we are refreshed by rich foods, the more purely we may fast in holiness of mind. For temperance is more easily preserved in poverty, though in riches it is indeed quickly endangered.

What the food of monks ought to be, blessed Jerome makes clear, saying in a certain letter to Paulinus: “Let your food be cheap and taken in the evening: namely vegetables and legumes; and sometimes consider it the height of delicacy to eat a few little fish. He who desires Christ and feeds on that heavenly bread does not greatly seek what dung he may make from costly foods. The lover of Christ loves abstinence; he cultivates fasting as the one remedy of soul and body; he takes the greatest care that his heart not be weighed down by surfeiting, nor does he take more food than nature, content with little, demands.”

But the monk who rejects fasting, who hates abstinence, the nurse of virtues; who stuffs foods down with eagerness; who drinks wine or strong drink and whatever can intoxicate, to satiety: he is unworthy of the very name of monk, and cannot be a disciple of Christ.

Woe to our times, in which already very many clerics and monks have shamefully cast aside the virtue of abstinence. They ought to have shown the people of God the way of salvation no less by example than by word; but, given over to feasting and continual drinking and pleasures, and immersed contrary to all clerical honor, they hasten to precede them into the perdition of eternal death.

Scripture says: “The beginning of human life is bread and water.” But now our monks and cloistered men buy for themselves whatever is sold more dearly, whatever they find more lavish, at a great price; by swallowing it down they bury it in their bellies, and through intemperance they corrupt soul and body together. Amid banquets they debate fasting, and when they are drunk they praise temperance. There each one boasts that he is victor over vice, when he has already been overcome by gluttony and drunkenness.

Our Lord Jesus Christ says in the holy Gospel according to Luke: “Take heed that your hearts not be weighed down in surfeiting.” It does not befit a person dedicated to God to eat or drink to satiety, nor is it lawful for a monk to eat more than nature requires. Eating should settle hunger, not induce fullness. Let belches, which satiety is accustomed to produce in feasters, be far away; let vomiting and indigestion never be heard of among monks. All cloistered men must use great moderation of discretion, so that they nourish the body with foods and drinks in such a way that, serving the spirit, it can live; and not so that, fattened, it begins to grow wanton against the command of the mind. Let satiety always be far away, because nothing so inflames the body and excites the genital members as undigested food and a belly hot with wine or beer.

Hence the holy Jerome, admonishing Eustochium about temperance, the preserver of all virtues, says in a certain letter: “Let the bride of Christ flee wine as poison; for wine and youth itself are weapons against adolescence, which is more prone to gluttony. Avarice does not so shake adolescence, nor does pride puff it up, nor ambition delight it, as do those enticements of pleasure which seem innate in us.”

[segment 69]
On abstinence and the fasting of monks.

[unclear]: he who gulps down foods greedily; who drinks wine, or beer, or whatever can intoxicate, to the point of satiety, is unworthy of the very name of monk and cannot be a disciple of Christ. Woe to our times, in which many clerics and monks have already shamefully fallen away from the virtue of abstinence, and those who ought to have shown the people of God the way of salvation no less by example than by word, given over to gluttonies and immersed in continual drinking and pleasures, contrary to every propriety of clerics, hasten to go before them into the perdition of eternal death.

Scripture says: The beginning of human life is bread and water. And now our monks and cloistered men buy for themselves, at the greatest expense, whatever is sold most dearly, whatever is found most luxurious; by gorging their bellies they bury it, and through their intemperance corrupt soul and body alike. Amid banquets they argue about fasting, and when they have become drunk they praise temperance. There each man boasts that he is the conqueror of vices, although he has already been overcome by gluttony and drunkenness.

Our Lord Jesus Christ says in the holy Gospel according to Luke: See that your hearts are not weighed down with excess. It does not befit a man dedicated to God to eat or drink to satiety, nor is it lawful for a monk to eat more than nature requires. Let eating satisfy hunger; let it not bring on repletion. Let belching be far away, which satiety is accustomed to produce at feasts; let vomiting and indigestion never be heard of among monks. It is necessary that all of us cloistered men use a great moderation of discretion, so that one may nourish the body with foods and drinks in such a way that, serving the spirit, it may be able to live, and not, grown fat, begin to grow wanton against the command of the mind.

Let satiety always be absent from drinking, for nothing so inflames the body and excites the genital members as undigested food and a belly heated by wine or beer. Hence Jerome, admonishing Eustochium concerning temperance, the preserver of all virtues, says in a certain letter: Let the bride of Christ flee wine as poison; for wine and youth itself are weapons against adolescence, already inclined to gluttony. Avarice does not shake adolescence so much, nor does pride puff it up, nor ambition delight it, as those vices of the flesh which seem to us to be bound up with pleasure. We shall easily lack the other vices if we keep watch, even moderately, in the fear of the Lord. But this enemy of ours is always shut up within us; wherever we go, we carry this adversary with us. Wine and youth: a double blaze of pleasure. Why do we add oil to the flame, or why do we administer fiery fuel to a little body already burning?

Excessive eating has laid low many who were walking chastely in the middle of the road, and those whom the devil could not conquer in their poverty he casts into the depths of luxury by excessive drinking of wine. For this reason the most holy founder of our way of life, Benedict, when enumerating the instruments of good works, placed among the chief ones: to chastise the body, to embrace hardships, to love fasting. For no one truly follows Christ who, nourishing his flesh delicately, lives in the monastery without daily abstinence.

Moderate abstinence, pleasing to God, works many good things in us if it is continued with religious intention to the end of life. For first of all it increases merit, and nourishes in us every kind of virtue. For what was the reason that almighty God forbade our first parents to eat the fruit under threat of death, except that he might offer them a cause of merit through the good of obedience? For, as Scripture bears witness, he created two intellects from the beginning, namely the angelic and the human, to which he would communicate himself by grace for the enjoyment of eternal happiness. Yet although he created each by the gift of his grace, he most rightly ordained that they should also be perpetually happy by their own merit, so that in them both mercy might have a place and conversion might obtain the merit necessary for salvation.

But the angelic intellect was at once divided in two: when some, falling of their own accord through pride, and others, humble and subject through love, clung to their beginning. Those, indeed, apostates from their beginning, withdrew in misery; these, however, were granted everlasting blessedness for the merit of their adhesion. The human intellect, however, was sent down into flesh; and, when the devil persuaded him, he transgressed the commandment of God and feasted on the food forbidden to him. From this there followed the destruction of the whole human race, which no one could remove except the Son of God, made man from a virgin for us.

[segment 70]
*Homily XV.*
*Fol. XXXVI.*

[unclear] For what condemned our first parents except the transgression of the commandment? For God’s concern was not with food, but with obedience. For if man had abstained, as almighty God had commanded him, then surely through this obedience he would have merited, in his innocence, the enjoyment of eternal happiness. For it is established by the tradition of the Scriptures that man cannot be saved without the merit of free will, since, although the grace of God works all things in all, nevertheless it will save no one without the cooperation of his own will, except infants baptized in the name of the divine Trinity, and those who cannot have the free use of reason.

For just as free will accomplishes nothing toward salvation without the grace of the Savior, so the grace of God will not save an adult without the merit of a good will. But the first man, placed in paradise, and having transgressed God’s commandment through intemperance, did not have the merit of obedience in a good will; therefore, wretched and unhappy, he was cast out. But the compassion of almighty God determined from eternity to restore him who had fallen through weakness, whom he knew to have been deceived by the envious suggestion of the serpent rather than by his own malice. He assigned this guilt to the passion and death of his only-begotten Son, born in time of the Virgin, so that he who had fallen by his own rashness might be restored to salvation through the obedience of the mediator. For it was impossible for the human race, lost through disobedience, to reach the mercy of the Creator except through the obedience of such a mediator, who would be God and man, in whom there would be both the strength of power and the innocence of nature. For we were blind through ignorance and mute through silence. The mediator of God and men, Christ Jesus, came, sent to enlighten every man coming into this world; he delivered the knowledge of God to mortals, and, by dying for the lost, sealed for the redeemed the greatest cause of love. He instructed the intellect through faith, by which we know God; he moved the affection through his passion, by which we rightly love him above all things.

And so, if we love our Lord and Redeemer with the whole integrity of sincere charity, it is plainly fitting that we chastise our body for love of him. For our abstinence will conduce to merit for us in two respects: namely, when by fasting, through obedience to the statutes of the fathers, we make satisfaction, and when by abstaining through the charity of God we show ourselves grateful to our Redeemer, who endured death for our sake. Abstinence increases merit; commends obedience; supplies wisdom and instruction; purges the affection; enlightens the intellect; grants victory against demons; perfects religion; obtains forgiveness of sins; tames the unlawful motions of the flesh; wins efficacy for prayers; and displays to one’s neighbor an example of virtue. Abstinence purifies the mind, adorns the soul with virtues, and heals the weak little body. He who loves abstinence is wise, and he who loves fasting is a strong ruler. A sober mind is the dwelling-place of wisdom; but he who pursues banquets will die in poverty of soul. It is difficult for a man to become wise who lives his life amid pleasures. Holy nature is content with little, and the health of life is preserved by modest things. Greediness long continued has harmed many, and more have perished by gluttony than by the sword. For habit seems to alter nature, and the longer it has prevailed, the more firmly rooted it will be in any matter.

There is among the Helvetians a man named Nicholas, whose abstinence turns the men of all Germany to admiration; and today, as you have heard, he is named in everyone’s mouth with the title of sanctity. Once a farmer in a certain village of the Helvetians, he was accustomed, in the manner of country people, to pronounce judgment among villagers in disputes. It happened at one time that he could not revoke a decision of his companions which he thought unjust; and, fearing danger to the salvation of his soul, he left his house, wife, children, friends, worldly affairs, and possessions, and withdrew alone into a nearby wood for the love of God. There he lives a solitary life. And behold, today is the twentieth year since he entered the solitude, without having taken human food. I speak of things known to all, nor do I think there is any man living in Germany who has not heard the report of this miracle. And lest perhaps in future anyone believe that we have been deceived by pretense, we cite as witnesses the Helvetians, together with the prelate of the church of Constance, who observe his ways and life with the greatest care.

[segment 71]
May they so weaken the body that they do not lift the mind into pride, lest the exercise of humility become material for vices. Fasting detests idleness; it does not love verbosity; it cherishes secrecy; it devotes itself to prayers and tears. Fasting, as Saint Ambrose bears witness, is the death of guilt, the destruction of sins, the remedy of salvation, the root of grace, the foundation of chastity. By this step Moses, the just man, came to God; by this gift Elijah, before he died, ascended in a chariot into heaven.

Those who walk in the Spirit love fasting, and those who profess chastity must necessarily flee an abundance of delicacies. For hunger is a friend to virginity and an enemy to lust. Satiety, however, betrays chastity and nourishes lust. Fasting extinguishes the burning arrows of the devil; persistence in prayer restrains them; and the continuation of vigils in the love of Christ repels all the snares of demons.

Hence Saint Leo the pope says in a certain sermon: What can be more effective than fasting, by whose observance we draw near to God and, resisting the devil, overcome his enticing snares? For fasting has always been food for virtue, and abstinence the nourishment of holy chastity. From abstinence proceed chaste thoughts; it gives birth to reasonable desires and modest counsels; and through voluntary afflictions the flesh dies to its concupiscences, while the spirit is renewed in virtues.

Therefore, my brothers, let us love fasting for the love of virtue, because of him who did not refuse to undergo death for us; and let us abstain not only from unlawful things, but also, as far as can be done, from permitted things, so that we who have offended in many ways against the commandments of salvation may, in recompense of satisfaction, restrain ourselves henceforth from every pleasure. Let us fast from every sin; let us take no food of malice; let us devour no banquets of pleasure; nor let us become drunk with the wine of luxury. Let us fast from all depravity; let us abstain from evil works; let us not speak harmful things; and let us always beware of noxious and perverse thoughts that separate us from God.

Let us always keep watch most earnestly in prayers to God, knowing that he whom we call upon in our prayers is everywhere most present to us. Let his praise always be in our mouth; and when we cannot praise his magnificence in speech, let us adore him in the heart with the desires of a most chaste love. For then our fasts are pleasing to him, when they have been adorned with holy works and continual prayers. For the prayer of the just man, as sacred Scripture bears witness, pierces the heavens, and it becomes the more effective the more it is found adorned with charity and fasting. Let us join to it, as it were, two wings, fasting and charity, and it will obtain for us from the Lord whatever we ask that is conducive to salvation.

On the Lenten Fast of Monks. Homily XVI.

Because, by the bounty of our Lord Jesus Christ, most dear brothers, we have begun the Lenten fast, reason requires that we say something briefly about its observance. For although you have many writings of the holy fathers concerning this most sacred exercise of fasts, nevertheless, because I have found by experience that my sermons among you are not wholly fruitless, I gladly share with your edification what the Lord has granted.

For, as blessed Jerome bears witness, the action of the living voice has within it a certain hidden operation, which in the Greek tongue is more elegantly called energy; and instruction spoken from the teacher’s mouth is stronger for persuasion. But I, set apart among you in the name of pastor alone, confess that I am young, unlearned, and lacking the experience and discretion necessary for the governance of souls. Hence it happens that I speak with a certain blush and shame: a young man among elders, ignorant among the learned, a disciple among masters.

Therefore I beg that none of you despise the simplicity of my speech; let no one take it badly that I speak, since, even if I am not fit for speaking, nevertheless by reason of the pastoral care committed to me I confess before God that I am bound by the duty of admonishing you. Your charity gives me boldness to speak, and the more ready you show yourselves to listen, the more you provoke me to speak. Nor will the labor be heavy for me, however great it may seem, by which the salvation of your souls is advanced.

Assist me with your prayers, so that the Lord Jesus may speak in my mouth, and may grant strength to my little talent for bringing forth into the open whatever can produce in you an increase of holy fervor. For as much as I desire to profit your charity by my sermons, I cannot, because I am too greatly compelled to be occupied with the care of secular business. Hence [unclear]

Hence Saint Leo the pope says in a certain sermon: What can be more effective than fasting, by whose observance we draw near to God and, resisting the devil, overcome the flattering vices? For fasting has always been food for virtue, and abstinence the nourishment of holy chastity. From abstinence, indeed, proceed chaste thoughts; it begets reasonable desires and modest counsels; and through voluntary afflictions the flesh dies to concupiscences, while the spirit is renewed in virtues. Let us therefore love fasting, my brothers, for love of virtue, on account of him who did not refuse to undergo death for us; and let us abstain not only from unlawful things but also, as far as can be done, from permitted ones, so that we who in many ways have offended against the commandments of salvation may, as recompense of satisfaction, henceforth restrain ourselves from every pleasure. Let us fast from every sin; let us take no food of malice; let us devour no banquets of pleasure, nor be made drunk with the wine of luxury. Let us fast from all perversities; let us abstain from evil works; let us not speak useless, vain, idle, buffoonish, and harmful words; and let us always beware of hurtful and perverse thoughts, which separate from God. Let us always keep most zealous watch in prayers to God, knowing that he whom we invoke with prayers is everywhere most present to us. Let his praise always be in our mouth; and since we cannot praise his magnificence in speech, let us adore him in the heart with the desires of a most chaste love. For then our fasts are pleasing to him, when they have been adorned with holy works and continual prayers. For the prayer of the righteous man, as sacred Scripture bears witness, penetrates the heavens, and it is the more effective, the more it is found adorned with charity and fasting. Let us join to it, as it were, two wings, fasting and charity, and it will obtain for us from the Lord whatever conducive to salvation we have asked.

*On the Lenten Fast of Monks: Homily XVI.*

Because, by the bounty of our Lord Jesus Christ, dearly beloved brothers, we have begun the Lenten fast, reason demands that we say something briefly about its observance. For although you have many writings of the holy fathers on this most sacred exercise of fasting, nevertheless, because I know by experience that my sermons among you are not wholly fruitless, I gladly impart to your edification what the Lord has granted. For, as blessed Jerome bears witness, the act of the living voice has within it a certain hidden operation, which in Greek he called more elegantly *energia*; and the discourse of a teacher, uttered by the mouth, is stronger for persuasion. But I, set apart in your midst only by the name of pastor, confess that I am a young man, unlearned and lacking both the experience and the discretion necessary for the governance of souls; whence it happens that I speak with a certain blush and shame, a young man among elders, an ignorant man among the learned, a disciple among masters. Therefore I beg that none of you despise the simplicity of my speech; let no one take it ill that I speak, because even if I am not fit for speaking, nevertheless by reason of the office of pastoral care entrusted to me I confess that before God I am bound to admonish you. Your charity gives me boldness to speak, and the readier you show yourselves to hear, the more you move me to speak. Nor will the labor be burdensome to me, however great it may seem, by which the salvation of your souls is perfected. Help me with your prayers, that the Lord Jesus may speak in my mouth, and may grant strength to my poor little talent to bring forth in your midst whatever, when preached among you, can produce an increase of holy fervor. For although I desire to benefit your charity by my sermons, I am unable to do so, because I am compelled to be too much occupied by the care of secular business. Whence

[segment 73]
For laziness is the root of despair and the mother and nurse of all vices. For just as a garment both produces and nourishes the moth, so the most destructive plague of sloth both begets and fosters despair. This beast is accustomed to vex women more than monks, and especially those who lead a life withdrawn in solitude.

Therefore you must take the greatest care, O monks, against this enemy of the salvation of souls, and must fight not by fleeing but by resisting bravely. You must carefully consider that no one has attained the honor of holiness through sluggishness and laziness, nor has anyone earned the kingdom of eternal happiness without great and continuous labor. Therefore the mind must always be renewed by daily and fresh increases of the virtues; and this dangerous journey of life must be measured not by what has been traversed, but rather by what remains. Let no one promise himself security in this life, which is wholly called temptation, full of as many dangers as there are affections in the soul. Rather, let each person take care that he who could become better from worse does not become worse from better.

And especially in these holy days let us all take care, as much as we can, to become better, adding for ourselves, according to the counsel of our divine teacher, something to the usual measure of our service: namely special prayers, abstinence from food and drink, so that each one, beyond the measure appointed to him, may offer something to God of his own will with the joy of the Holy Spirit; that is, let him subtract from his body something of food, of drink, of sleep, of talkativeness, of ease, and with the joy of spiritual desire await holy Easter. For the monk who in these holy days is not found more religious, more fervent in the love of God, and more devout is shown to be only slightly religious at other times.

Hence the divine Pope Gregory says in a certain homily: “Lent must be observed with the greatest observance, so that the fast in it, because of the Lord’s days which are subtracted from abstinence, is by no means relaxed, unless infirmity prevents someone; for these days are themselves the tithe of the year which we are commanded to offer to the Lord.” Saint Ambrose also says: “He keeps Lent who by fasting and watching ascends to Easter. For just as fasting at the remaining time of the year is a reward, so in Lent not to fast is a sin. Those fasts are voluntary, but these are necessary; those come from choice, but these from law. To those we are invited; to these we are compelled.” And therefore he who passes over the appointed number by eating on one day is accused not as a violator of one day, but is charged as a transgressor of the whole of Lent.

In these sacred days all desires of the flesh must be restrained; the body must be chastised through abstinence, so that if perhaps at other times we have lived less temperately, we may in some way make compensation by this Lenten fast. Let us therefore follow the admonitions of our most holy father Benedict, dearest brothers, and with as much diligence as we can let us exercise ourselves in holy works, subtracting something each day from our body for the love of God in food and drink, so that we may prove ourselves true servants of God.

Next, our holy founder adds, saying: “Yet this very thing which each one offers, let him suggest to his abbot, and let it be done with his prayer and will; because what is done without the permission of the spiritual father will be reckoned as presumption and vainglory, not as reward. Therefore all things are to be done with the will of the abbot.”

The words of our holy lawgiver are clear, brothers, so that no one can have recourse to the covering of ignorance. For if all things are to be done with the will of the abbot, then without his permission absolutely nothing is to be done. Indeed, the more some good work is adorned by many virtues, the more there is no doubt that it will be of greater merit before God. Therefore when a monk, by the license and blessing of his abbot, subtracts something of food or drink from his body for the love of God, he will receive from the Lord not only the promised reward of eternal satisfaction for the abstinence itself, but also the kingdom for humility, and, for holy obedience, [unclear]
