On the Lenten fast of monks.

...to act against his command; let him omit or neglect nothing that has been enjoined upon him by him. Let him take the utmost care not to sadden or disturb his pastor by restless conduct, nor murmur against him, nor disparage him, nor say any evil whatever against him. Rather, let him revere him with the sincere affection of charity as Christ’s vicar, and let him not sadden his spirit by importunate or disorderly words or deeds. As far as he can, let him assist him in his labors, and intercede for him before God with continual prayers.

Monks sin greatly against God when they are not afraid to sadden their abbot with their importunate detractions and corrupt ways. Therefore whatever monk desires to be good, whoever truly wishes to be devout in the Lord, should always and everywhere venerate his abbot with pure love as the vicar of Jesus Christ in the monastery; he should humbly obey his commands in all things, and in no way depart from his direction.

The abbot, for his part, should strive to show himself in all things a worthy pastor to his monks, one who feeds the sheep of Christ entrusted to him by word and example alike, in all gentleness of charity. Let him not trouble the brothers without just and reasonable necessity; let him not be contemptuous of the holy community; let him recognize himself as the steward of the monastery’s goods, not their lord. Let him not be a mocker of his subjects, not turbulent, not importunate, not a tyrant, but their sincere lover: calm in speech, gentle and upright in conduct, holy in his way of life, clean in conscience, and always devout in mind toward God.

Let him at all times maintain solicitous care for the souls entrusted to him, and neglect nothing that seems able to contribute to their salvation. Let him not exalt himself on high because he has been placed as ruler of others, but let him remember that he must fear all the more, since he is certainly bound to render an account for all in the dread judgment of God. Let him correct the proud, rebellious, and obstinate as a lord; let him kindly cherish the humble, obedient, and governable as a most gentle father; and let him wisely distribute to each one his due without respect of persons.

Let him always consider himself the mother and nurse of the monks; and therefore those whom he can lead back to better things by gentleness he should not embitter with importunate words and harsh invectives. Let him always maintain solicitous care for the sick, and provide for all in such a way that they have no just cause for murmuring. In his own infirmities let him learn how he ought to have compassion on his subjects. For he cannot be a good pastor of souls who disdains to suffer with exact attention the needs of his infirm subjects.

But he can become a good abbot among monks who knows how to conform himself to the persons of the infirm in such a way that, whenever a brother asks something of him, he takes upon himself in his heart the condition of the one asking and says: “What would I wish to be done for me in this matter, according to the right judgment of reason, if I were in the person of the one asking?” Indeed, if the abbot imagines himself to be a subject, he will let no one go away from him saddened.

We have now said, brothers, what sort of men you ought to be toward us for love of God, so that, existing as good monks, you may please the Lord Jesus in truth. You have heard, nevertheless, what sort of pastor I ought to show myself to you; whether I do this or not, you yourselves will judge better than I. For we shall fast in vain and find no fruit of Lenten observance unless we all stand before God in true charity and in the concord of ecclesiastical unity. Or what good will it do to abstain from richer foods, and to defile the conscience with evil works or thoughts?

For the fasts we have begun to be pleasing to God, it is necessary that they be not only alien and remote from vices and sins, but also adorned with the full abundance of all virtues. For holy Ambrose says: “What good is it to fast in the belly and indulge in hunting, to abstain from foods and go astray in sins? Do you think, brothers, that the man is fasting who at first daybreak does not keep vigil at church, does not seek out the holy places of the blessed martyrs, but rising in the morning gathers his servants, sets out nets, brings forth dogs, and roams through groves and woods; who, I say, drags servants with him, perhaps more eager to hasten to church? For his own pleasures he piles up the sins of others, not knowing that he will be guilty both for his own fault and for the perdition of his servants.”

And Saint Pope Leo says: “The fast of our solemnity does not consist in abstinence from food alone...”

[segment 75]
--- Page 084 ---

Book XVI.

For food is not fruitfully withdrawn from the body unless the mind is called back from every iniquity. The divine Jerome likewise says: What profit is it for the body to be made thin by abstinence, if the soul swells up with pride? What virtue is there in not drinking wine, and yet becoming drunk with anger, hatred, and the other poisonous cups of the soul?

Therefore let none of you promise himself holiness from abstinence from foods alone, since whatever is required for true penitence is also judged necessary for fasting. For fasts profit toward salvation for those who are contrite for their sins in a true purpose of amendment, and who fervently devote themselves to good and holy works. Hence all who fast from food and drink must also abstain from sins, so that the mind, adorned with the beauty of virtues, may sweetly grow warm in the love of Christ and not grow lukewarm in the progress of inner conversion. For the virtue of abstinence is nothing if it has not been adorned by works of charity.

Therefore hear my counsel, monk, if you wish to make your fast fruitful before God. First cleanse your conscience from every stain of sin, so that no desire for evil may intervene, so that the mind may become fervent in the exercise of divine love and be praiseworthily adorned with the other virtues. Through the remedy of contrition and confession wash away past sins, and by prayers, while standing with tears, strengthen your mind against all temptations with God’s help. Toward God, as much as you can, be fervent in the exercise of love; toward yourself, be at peace in a pure conscience; toward your brother, be rightly ordered in true charity. Always be outstanding in humility, firm in constancy and obedience, pure in chastity of mind and body, gentle in meekness, a monk in action, and continually kindled with desire for perfection. If you commend your abstinence with this kind of cleanness and purity, you will fast rightly.

Diligently exercise your mind in meditation on the passion of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, so that no day passes on which, with thanksgiving, you do not at least once run through his life and death in devout meditation. A Christian is, I will not say ungrateful, but without devotion, who is so immersed either in temporal affairs or occupied with the pleasures of the flesh that he does not, at least once each day, remember with thanksgiving the most bitter passion of his Redeemer. But the monk who forgets the benefits of our Lord Jesus Christ, who does not once each day recall his passion with thanksgiving, who does not every day meditate on the law of the Lord or on the Gospel, is deservedly considered the most ungrateful of all men. Nothing is more powerful than that prayer which is offered to God the Father in the union and power of the Lord’s passion.

Exceedingly justly is that spirit to be condemned which, redeemed by the most precious blood of Christ, is led astray by the pleasures of its flesh, so that it does not remember God’s many benefits toward it. For there was no reason why the Son of God should become man except that he wished to justify us sinners: born in the world for us, suffering in the world for us, and cruelly dead on the wood for our crimes. He, the just one, came into the world to justify us who were sinners; the merciful one came to the ungodly, so that from him we might learn mercy, humility, and patience. The physician came to the sick, life to the dead, compassion to the despairing; it was not our merits that drew him down from heaven into the world, but our sins.

It is fitting, therefore, brothers, that we celebrate with all devotion this Lenten fast, dedicated to his most holy passion, since he first deigned to do such great things for our salvation, and, in order to snatch us from the destruction of death, did not hesitate to give himself over to death for us. Let us do, for the love of Christ, whatever good we can; let us endure, in view of his passion and death, every adversity that happens to us in this world, since the Lord Jesus, who willed from charity to suffer for us, has shown himself truly most worthy that we should love him with our whole heart. For if, brother, you acknowledge yourself a sinner, Christ saves you by his death. Contemplate him hanging on the cross as is fitting, and you will be healed from every bite of sin. For nothing is found in this world more effective for cleansing and healing the wounds of conscience than meditation on the wounds of Christ. For the most precious blood of the Lord, poured out, has become a sufficient medicine for sin.

Hence the holy bishop Augustine says: Brothers, that we may be healed, wounded by sin, let us look upon Christ crucified. For just as those who looked upon the bronze serpent in the desert did not perish from the bites of serpents, so those who...

Homily XVI. Fol. XXXIX.

For food will by no means be fruitfully withheld from the body unless the mind is called back from all iniquity. For Saint Jerome says: What does it profit for the body to be made thin by abstinence, if the soul swells with pride? What virtue is there in not drinking wine, yet being drunk with anger, hatred, and the other poisonous cups of the soul?

Therefore let none of you promise himself holiness from abstinence from food alone, since whatever is required for true penitence is also judged necessary for fasting. For fasts profit unto salvation for those who are contrite for their sins with a true purpose of amendment, and who fervently apply themselves to good and holy works. Hence all who fast from food and drink must also abstain from vice, so that the mind, adorned with the beauty of the virtues, may burn sweetly in the love of Christ and in no way grow sluggish from progress in inward conduct. For the virtue of abstinence is nothing unless it has been adorned with works of charity.

Therefore, monk, hear my counsel: if you wish to make your fast fruitful before God, first cleanse your conscience from every stain of sin, so that no desire for evil may remain in you, and so that the mind may grow fervent in the exercise of divine love and be laudably adorned with the holy virtues. By the remedy of contrition and confession wipe away past sins; and by persisting in prayers with tears, strengthen your mind against all temptations with God’s help. Toward God, as far as you can, be fervent in the exercise of love; toward yourself, maintain good tranquillity in a pure conscience; toward your brother, be rightly ordered in true charity. Always be foremost in humility, firm in constancy and obedience, pure in chastity of mind and body, pious in meekness, a monk in conduct, continually kindled with desire for perfection. If you commend your abstinence by this kind of cleanness and purity, you will fast rightly.

Diligently exercise your mind in meditation on the passion of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, so that no day passes in which you do not at least once ponder his life and death with thanksgiving and tears. For crude and exceedingly hard, not to say ungrateful, is the Christian who is so submerged in temporal affairs or occupied with the pleasures of the flesh that he does not call to mind at least once each day, with thanksgiving, the most bitter passion of his Redeemer.

But the monk who forgets the benefits of our Lord Jesus Christ, who does not recall his passion even once each day with thanksgiving, who does not meditate every day on the Lord’s evangelical law, is rightly the most ungrateful of all men. Nothing is more powerful than that prayer which is offered to God the Father in union with, and in the power of, the Lord’s passion. Justly indeed is that spirit to be condemned which, though redeemed by the most precious blood of Christ, is enticed away by the pleasures of its flesh, so that it does not remember such great benefits of God toward itself.

For there was no reason that the Son of God should become man except that he wished to justify us sinners: born in the world for us, suffering in the world for us, cruelly dead upon the wood for our crimes. The just one came into the world to justify us who were sinners; the pious one came to the impious, so that we might learn piety, humility, and patience from him. The physician came to the sick, life to the dead, consolation to the despairing; it was not our merits that drew him from heaven into the world, but our sins.

It is fitting, therefore, my brothers, that with all devotion we celebrate this Lenten fast dedicated to his most holy passion, since he first deigned to do such great things for our salvation, and, in order to snatch us from the destruction of death, did not hesitate to give himself for us unto death. Let us do for love of Christ whatever good we can; in view of his passion and death, let us endure every adversity that happens to us in this world, since the Lord Jesus, who in charity willed to suffer for us, has truly shown himself most worthy that we love him with all our heart.

For if, brother, you recognize yourself as a sinner, Christ has saved you by his death. Contemplate him as it is fitting, hanging on the cross, and you will be healed from every bite of sin. Nothing is found in this world more effective for curing the wounds of conscience, nothing more powerful for purging the terrified eye of the mind, than constant meditation on the wounds of Christ. For the most precious blood of the Lord Jesus, poured out, has been made a sufficient medicine for sin. Hence the holy bishop Augustine says: Brothers, that we who are wounded by sin may be healed, let us look upon Christ crucified. For just as those who looked upon the bronze serpent in the desert did not perish from the bites of serpents, so those who with faith and devotion look upon the death of Jesus Christ are healed from the diseases of sins.

On the Lenten fast of monks.

But if you trust that you have been justified from sin, so much the more, and more fervently, must you look upon Christ hanging on the cross, without the merit of whose passion no one is found just on earth. For outside faith in Christ there is no righteousness. However religiously a person may live, if he does not believe in our Lord Jesus Christ, he will by no means obtain eternal salvation; rather, the wrath of God remains upon him, and he will necessarily perish forever. The blood of Christ has opened the gate of salvation, through which no one can enter except one who faithfully believes in Christ. For this reason Christ humbly subjected himself to the condemnation of death, so that he might free believers from the yoke of perpetual condemnation.

O infinite charity, O piety without measure! Who, believing in you, eternal Majesty, would not marvel at such astonishing benefits granted to us? For your passion, Lord Jesus, is the price of our salvation, the refuge of sinners, the singular remedy for infirm minds, the fountain, origin, and end of all good things. Therefore now, dearest brothers, let us recollect and diligently call back to memory the bitterness of the Lord’s passion, so that, since we necessarily recognize that we are sinners, we may at last be justified through the merit of our Savior himself.

For meditation on the Lord’s passion surpasses all exercises of this kind, if it is continued for a long time with the fervor of inward devotion. That mind of a monk which, kindled by zeal for divine love, has been accustomed each day to keep the memory of the passion of our Lord Jesus Christ with tears for at least a modest hour, does not easily lie open to the suggestions of demons. For the heart which the love of Jesus possesses always remains inaccessible and closed to all evil thoughts.

Happy indeed is that monk, and never to be named except with honor, who meditates day and night with loving zeal on the teaching and passion of our Lord Jesus Christ; who never forgets his Savior; who, whether he eats or drinks or does anything else, always beholds the Lord Jesus hanging on the cross with the eyes of the mind. No destructive thoughts are entertained in his heart; no evil or idle word proceeds from his mouth; he thinks of nothing except Jesus crucified for us, and whatever

On the Lenten Fast of Monks.

One who beholds the death of Jesus Christ with devotion is healed of the diseases of sins. But if you trust that you have been justified from sin, Christ hanging on the cross must be contemplated by you all the more and more fervently; without the merit of his passion no one lives righteous on earth. For outside faith in Christ there is no righteousness, and however religiously a person may live, if he does not believe in our Lord Jesus Christ, he will in no way attain eternal salvation; rather, the wrath of God remains upon him, and he will necessarily perish forever. The death of Christ opened the gate of salvation, through which no one can enter except one who faithfully believes in Christ. Therefore Christ humbly subjected himself to the condemnation of death, so that he might free believers from the yoke of perpetual damnation.

O infinite charity, O loving-kindness without measure: who among those who believe in you, O eternal majesty, would not marvel at such astonishing benefits granted to us? For your passion, Lord Jesus, is the price of our salvation, the final refuge of sinners, the singular remedy of infirm minds, the fountain, origin, and end of all good things. Therefore now, dearest brothers, let us recall and diligently bring back to memory the bitterness of the Lord’s passion, so that, since we necessarily acknowledge ourselves to be sinners, we may at last be justified through the merit of this same Savior of ours.

For meditation on the Lord’s passion surpasses all exercises of this kind, if it has been continued for a long time with the fervor of inward devotion. That mind of a monk which, kindled by zeal for divine love, has become accustomed every day, at least for a little hour, to keep the memory of the passion of our Lord Jesus Christ with tears, does not easily lie open to the suggestions of demons. For that heart which the love of Jesus possesses always remains inaccessible and closed to all evil thoughts.

Truly blessed is that monk, and never to be named except with honor, who meditates day and night with loving zeal on the teaching and passion of our Lord Jesus Christ; who never forgets his Savior; who, whether he eats or drinks or does anything else, always beholds with the eyes of the mind the Lord Jesus hanging on the cross. No pernicious thoughts move about in his heart; no evil or idle word proceeds from his mouth; he thinks of nothing except Jesus crucified for us, and whatever he says sounds of Jesus or of righteousness.

Moreover, when he sees a painted image of Jesus crucified, he melts into tears, and, as if stunned by the excessive desire of his mind, he is filled with a most sweet bitterness. He can admit no consolations of the present life, because when he thinks of his Lord dwelling in heaven, he laments his own prolonged exile on earth. He loves nothing in this world except Jesus; he seeks nothing except the crucified one. If riches are at hand, he utterly refuses to set his heart on them; if they are not at hand, he by no means seeks to find them. For he is eager to recognize himself as a disciple of the poor Jesus, and he understands very well that the riches of this world are a great impediment to Christian perfection.

Having slender food and poor clothing for the body, he is content with his Lord, who became poor for us. That monk can truly be called blessed who can say with Theophorus: “My love has been crucified; therefore my heart receives no consolation of this world.” And that saying of Paul, the lover of Christ: “The world has been crucified to me, and I to the world.” Therefore, my dearest brothers, hear this counsel of mine, and by continual remembrance of the Lord’s passion sanctify the fast you have begun.

For I tell you that among all the exercises of monks none can be holier, none more acceptable to God, none more salutary for you, than that which is practiced in daily meditation on the passion of our Lord Jesus Christ, with compunction of inward love. This exercise of the saving passion quickly makes a penitent out of a sinner; by wonderful compunction it makes the lukewarm and dry soul devout; it makes the impious person righteous. It overcomes the sweetness of sins, gives strength to the spirit against demons, sets an end to vices, and confers a strong increase on the holy virtues of the soul.

Devout and continued memory of the Lord’s passion purifies the conscience of the one meditating, enlightens the intellect in every knowledge of truth, and sweetly inflames the affection in the love of God. Devout meditation on Christ’s passion causes one to despise all the delights of this world and to fear nothing, however hard or adverse. It drives out lusts, tames luxury of both mind and body, puts envy to flight, restrains gluttony, calms avarice, mitigates sloth and anger, and uproots pride by the roots. Moreover, no passion for any carnal thing wearies the mind of a person whom the passion of Christ truly occupies.

O how sweetly burns the one whom the memory of Jesus’ passion sweetly inflames. O how blessed is the mind that embraces continual meditation on Christ’s death. O good Jesus, my crucified love, who are the salvation and health of faithful souls, grant us to keep continual memory of your passion; grant your servants always to commend the fasts begun with good works, because then our abstinence will be most pleasing to your uncircumscribable majesty, when it has been adorned with meditation on your precious blood; you who live and reign with God the Father in the unity of the Holy Spirit, God, for all ages of ages.

Here ends Homily XVII.

On the Sleep and Wakefulness of Monks. Homily XVII.

The monk who always thinks that he is going to die should allow no time to pass idly. For Scripture says: “Remember your last things, and you will not sin forever.” Against the forgetful and heedless, however, it is written: “They are a nation without counsel and without prudence. Would that they were wise and understood and provided for their last things.” The remembrance of death brings watchfulness into the soul and extinguishes every disordered thought of the mind. He who rightly fears death never grows sluggish in good work. For the wise man says: “Whatever your hand is able to do, do earnestly; for neither work, nor reason, nor wisdom, nor knowledge will be among the dead, to whom you are hastening.”

For the monk who is a true worshiper of God spends no moment of time uselessly, but by nights and days, in the exercise of good work, always performs the watchfulness of the mind with the highest devotion; and while sleeping in body, he steadfastly says in spirit to the messengers of God: “I sleep, and my heart keeps watch.” Sleep is the image of death and the binding of the bodily senses; but the one who is a true lover

Homily XVII. Fol. XL.

[The remembrance of Christ's passion], when restrained, [unclear]; it mitigates filthy avarice and sloth that is about to inflame; it extirpates pride from the root. Moreover, the person whom the passion of Christ truly occupies is wearied by no passion of any carnal thing. How sweetly he burns whom the memory of Jesus' passion sweetly inflames; and how happily the mind glows that embraces continual meditation on the death of Christ. Good Jesus, my beloved crucified one, who are the salvation and savior of faithful souls, grant us to keep continual remembrance of your passion; grant your servants always to commend the fasts they have begun by good works, because then our abstinence will be most pleasing to your uncircumscribed majesty, when it has been adorned by meditation on your precious blood. You who live and reign with God the Father in the unity of the Holy Spirit, God, forever and ever.

Here ends Homily XVI.

On the sleep and wakefulness of monks. Homily XVII.

The monk who always thinks on the death of morals permits no time to pass in idleness. For Scripture says: “Remember your last things, and you will not sin forever.” Therefore concerning the forgetful and foolish it is written: “They are a nation without counsel and without prudence. Would that they were wise and understood and provided for their last things.” The memory of death brings watchfulness into the soul, and also every orderly thought of the mind. He who has rightly deserved death never grows sluggish in good work. For the wise man says: “Whatever your hand is able to do, work earnestly; for neither work, nor reason, nor wisdom, nor knowledge will be in the underworld, to which you are hastening.”

For the monk who is a true worshiper of God spends no moment of time uselessly, but night and day, always watchful in mind in the practice of good work, performs bodily duties with the greatest devotion and constantly says to God in his soul: “I sleep, but my heart keeps watch for the Lord.” Sleep is the image of death and a binding of the bodily senses. But he who is a true lover of Christ, even if he sleeps in body, keeps watch in mind, and praying without ceasing cries out to the Lord with an affection of love: “Before you is every desire of my mind and every thought, O Jesus crucified, my love.”

I blush, my brothers, and am deeply confounded to put forward a sermon on the watchfulness of monks, because when I recall to memory the customs of the preceding holy fathers in keeping vigil, I recognize how far our weakness differs from their perfection. They, for a long time, in successive love and in the praises of God, often spent whole nights without sleep; but to us scarcely the space of eight hours for sleeping seems at times sufficient, of which we do not cease to complain of five before matins and three afterward. For the true abstinence of monks consists not only in sparing use of food and drink, but also in the continuation of vigils for the praise of God, because the maceration of the flesh, by which the old man is restrained by them, is in vain, if the whole body is fattened through drowsiness. Indeed, not only is the body refreshed for its subsistence by food and drink, but it is also restored by the rest of sleep. What sort of monastic fast is that: to spare oneself by abstaining from foods and drinks, and to indulge oneself in sleep to satiety? It makes little difference whether you refresh the body by food or by sleep, since there is gluttony in both.

For this reason the old and first leaders of monks, guarding temperance no less in sleeping than in eating and drinking, endured as men most continent in both. Some of them, standing on their feet, others lying on the bare ground, granted to nature only the very smallest amount of sleep. We know that one of them, not least in the merit of sanctity, said: “One hour is enough for a monk to sleep, provided he has been a warrior and adversary of vices.” For sleep nourishes luxury no less than food and drink. Another of the saints thought that two or three hours for sleeping were enough for a monk, so that he might commend sparingness in food also by temperance in rest. But the recent reformers of our order, who under the name of the Bursfeld Union handed down to us throughout Germany a manner of life according to the purity of the Rule, considering the weakness of the human condition, ordered the arrangement of vigils very moderately.

For indeed they mercifully allowed monks in community a space of five hours before the night praises, and likewise added two, or two and a half, after matins for sleep. He for whom this time, namely seven hours, is not sufficient for rest shows himself a very sluggish and slothful monk. Hence we are admonished to keep watch not only in mind but also in body, since he who disdains to keep watch bodily in the service of God is scarcely able to preserve his heart in true purity.

Let us keep watch, therefore, most beloved brothers, especially in these holy days of Lent, in which we have declared war on vices and are firmly prepared to conquer and overcome the ancient enemy, the devil. For you have heard the holy prince of the apostles admonishing in these words: “Brothers, be sober and keep watch.” Why did he give this warning? Because, he says, “your adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour; resist him, strong in faith.” And so, my brothers, let us keep watch as long as we are set in battle, lest at any time, weighed down by drowsiness or idleness, we be overcome by the adversary of our salvation. For as long as we live in the flesh, it is necessary that by watchfulness and chastisement of our body we fight bravely against the enemy. Let the labor of the contest not frighten you, because although our adversary is troublesome, nevertheless he is so weak and feeble that he can overcome no one except one who is willing, and one who has utterly cast off all guard over himself.

But the Lord Jesus Christ is our strong helper and protector, who grants strength to those who fight eagerly and will bestow on the victors an unfading crown in heaven with all his saints. As dust is scattered by the wind, so our adversary the devil, confounded and terrified by the voice of those singing psalms with humble devotion, is driven away. Therefore let none of you, most beloved brothers, act negligently; let no one in the time of prayer grow dull through sluggishness of mind and drowsiness; let no one fear the contest with the devil with disordered fear. Stand manfully for the perpetual salvation of your souls, fighting against a weak adversary, and hold your own flesh no less suspect than demons. For you know, as not only Scripture testifies but also long experience teaches, that the flesh desires against the spirit; and therefore the monk who is not harmed by the wantonness of his own flesh is rarely overcome by demons in the contest. Walk, therefore, in the Spirit, and you will not fulfill the desires of the flesh.

Let him who is watchful and a strong fighter rouse the weaker brother, who is sluggish in devotion, and restore him as far as he can, both by words and by examples, to the holy fervor of the spirit. Let him who is spiritual among you not despise his disorderly brother, but rather bring him back through humble admonition to the pursuit of piety. For Scripture says: “Bear one another’s burdens, and so you will fulfill the law of Christ.” For indeed, if, consenting together in brotherly love toward one another through faith in Jesus Christ, you have always been of one mind, without doubt you will easily conquer your adversary the devil. For where the true charity of Christ makes monks living in a monastery of one mind, there God is worshiped in all holiness; there the invisible presence of holy angels is believed to be present, through whom the rashness of malignant spirits is restrained. In such a congregation of saints God dwells, whom the army of the heavenly kingdom praises together with the voices of the monks singing psalms. A multitude of monks fervent in the love of God is the paradise of the delights of heavenly graces. A city adorned with a multitude of towers is a congregation of monks thinking the same thing in the Lord.

Keep watch, my brothers, I beseech you, keep watch in holy prayers, and especially at that time shake off drowsiness and dullness of mind when you stand in church before the Lord to sing psalms. For if, standing before God in psalmody, you are fervent in prayer, you will without doubt feel his grace present with you. For he is estranged in mind from the purpose of a monk who while praying thinks of something else, and offers his mind to be stirred not by divine things but by transient matters or occupations. For you know what our holy founder Benedict says in the Rule: “If,” he says, “we wish to suggest anything to powerful men, we do not presume to do so except with humility and reverence; how much more must supplication be made to the Lord God of all with all humility and devotion of purity; and let us know that we are heard not in much speaking, but in purity of heart and compunction of tears.” Since, however, for us singing psalms

[segment 79]
--- Page 088 ---

Homily XVII.
Fol. XLI.

Since, when we sing psalms, we believe God to be present everywhere, great reverence is needed in prayer, so that our mind may agree in every respect with our voice. Let us therefore consider, brothers, before whom we stand, and to whom, in the sight of the angels, we recite the psalms in prayer; for the monk who, at the time of prayer, admits vain or even destructive thoughts into his mind is like one who, when matters very necessary to him are being discussed with the king, is summoned by his own servant, leaves the king, and turns aside to the servant’s vain and useless conversations. Truly, whoever did this would be judged foolish by everyone, and would rightly seem deserving of the gravest punishments.

But what does the monk deserve who, standing before the highest king of all and speaking with him about those things that concern the salvation of his body, admits harmful or even destructive thoughts into his mind? If one who mocks and despises an earthly king is punished by beheading, what does one deserve who despises the heavenly emperor? Therefore I admonish you, brothers, and with all the sincerity I can I ask that, having purified your hearts, especially at the time of prayer, from the stain of wicked thought, you receive in the mind only what you sing aloud with the mouth.

As the angels always stand before God with fear and the greatest reverence, singing an everlasting hymn of praise, so let us also take care, my brothers, to stand before him with a pure heart, a clean conscience, and most fervent devotion, so that we may sing praises to him much more in spirit than in body. Let us therefore separate our minds from all earthly affairs; let us cleanse our consciences of all enticements of the flesh; let us put on the fervor of the Holy Spirit; and let us drive all sluggishness and idleness far from us. For just as a heavy burden weighs down the back of a slothful man, so drowsiness at the time of psalmody is accustomed to oppress the minds of monks.

Nothing harms monks more than sloth, which little by little weakens all the powers of the mind and extinguishes the whole fervor of the spirit. For as soon as a monk has begun to fall away from the fervor of holy devotion, countless evil thoughts enter the mind; and he can repel them so much the less, the farther he is known to have withdrawn from his former fervor. Hence drowsiness arises in the performance of the prescribed services; the mind is burdened by sadness and melancholy, which the monk, growing lukewarm in spirit, strives with all zeal to relieve not by spiritual but by earthly and fleshly little consolations. And when he advances too long in this alienation of mind, he is hardened, now almost insensible, against the exercise of compunction; and the more he pants after the consolations of the flesh, the farther he wanders from the inward fervor of the spirit.

For what makes you drowsy at the time of psalmody except sloth? For the mind that has fallen into torpor soon makes the body doze. But again I admonish you, brothers, as I have often been accustomed to do, that you especially flee idleness and drowsiness at the time of prayer, since, just as drunkenness brings forgetfulness into the soul, so also drowsiness makes the mind unsound and foolish before God. Wash away this vice of drowsiness, O young men, by which we grieve that some of the elders have long been infected through long habit; among them dozing in choir is familiar, and compunction of mind toward God is exceedingly rare.

Beware, I say, this evil habit of sleeping, which, after it has once led a monk away captive, rarely allows him to be free. Therefore, at the beginning of conversion, young men especially must fight against this most pernicious enemy of religion, as someone said in verse: “Resist at the beginnings; medicine is prepared too late when evils have gained strength through long delays. But for that reason do not put yourself off to future hours; he who is not ready today will be less ready tomorrow.” For it is difficult to cure a sickness that has become old through long duration.

Let us see with how much diligence and attentiveness servants stand before earthly kings and princes, so that we may not be ignorant of how it befits us to stand in prayers before God, who alone is king of all and lord of the universe. The eyes of all look to the lord, and all and each hang most attentively upon his nod, lest anything commanded by him escape those standing by through negligence. From this, then, let us consider with how great purity we ought to stand before the Lord God in the assembly of prayer, day and night, from whom we desire to obtain the kingdom of eternal happiness. Not the voice, not the cry

[segment 80]
*On the sleep and wakefulness of monks.*

Not shouting, but the intention of one fervent in the love of God, deserves to be heard. Therefore flee drowsiness and torpor of mind as a deadly poison, because sleep and idleness are the spring of all vices. A watchful eye is the servant of inward devotion, and the monk who truly loves Jesus Christ guards himself from every excess. He who is watchful sharpens the mind with understanding; but the monk who sleeps much shamefully blinds his soul. A monk watchful in prayer and fasting is fearsome to the devil; but one who is sated with food and sleep is not permitted to rest by the spirit of fornication. For wakefulness devoted to prayers breaks the madness of lust and powerfully extinguishes the fire of desire; it purifies the inmost heart, enlightens the mind, and inflames the affection toward the love of heavenly things. Wakefulness is the mother, nurse, and guardian of good thoughts, the sure victory over the temptations of the ancient enemy, the flight of phantasms, the death of vices, and the life and fountain of all virtues. Sleep, however, is for many the companion and fosterer of lust, the robber of human life, the image of death, the procurer of oblivion, the partner of sloth. Immoderate sleep corrupts the stomach, harms the brain, and dissolves both body and soul. A monk frequently given over to sleep despises wisdom, prostitutes chastity, neglects the salvation of the soul, loses precious time, kills the mind, and nourishes vices. Therefore, whichever of you, most beloved brothers, desires to come to the rest of heavenly felicity, let him forcefully repel from himself all torpor and sloth of mind; let him learn first to conquer himself, so that thereafter he may be able to overcome the devil, the enemy of his salvation. For unless you first exterminate the evil that is in you, when will you overcome that which comes from outside? Learn to command your passions, and with the Lord’s help you will easily overcome everything that happens outwardly. Cleanse your mind from every disordered desire, so that you may truly love Jesus alone above all things, with whom you hope to rejoice forever. But no one will reign with Christ in heaven who does not imitate him with holy conduct while living on earth, as sacred Scripture says: He who says that he remains in Christ ought himself also to walk just as he walked. For the Lord Jesus, who by his death freed us from the destruction of eternal damnation, himself voluntarily became mediator of God and men; he himself is the way by which the path of life must be continued for us; he himself is the norm and rule of saving conduct for Christians. No one comes to God except through Christ; no one will see the Father except through the Son. Therefore, if you wish to come to salvation, you must pass through the Savior. For he himself said in the holy Gospel: No one comes to the Father except through me. And that other saying: I am the door; if anyone enters through me, he will find the pasture of eternal life. And again: I am the way, the truth, and the life. Through this way, I pray you, my brothers, always walk by pious and holy conduct; fervently love this truth with a pure heart; hasten ardently toward this life, which alone is lasting. Let the teaching of Christ never depart from your hearts, but remain in memory at all times, since he refuses to become his true imitator who has not been exercised day and night in meditation on his words. Therefore, always watchful in prayers, live with such purity of mind that, when Jesus Christ our Lord calls you in the hour of death, you may deserve to hear him with joy: he who lives and reigns with God the Father in the unity of the Holy Spirit, one almighty God, forever and ever. Amen.

*On patience and meekness. Homily XVIII.*

Among all professors of the Christian faith there is today no class of men for whom the virtue of patience seems more necessary than for the company of monks. For in these our days, as the charity of many grows cold, to use Paul’s expression, we have been made a spectacle to mortals, so that wherever a monk comes among worldly men, he is pointed out by the fingers of almost all who see him, to be mocked and derided. Why I have said this you know, you who are not unaware of what recently happened in the town of Bingen. A drunken crowd of young men and boys received a monk

[segment 81]
--- Page 090 ---

*Homily XVIII.*

*Folio XLII.*

...a certain monk passing along the public road, and, as though he had offered himself as a traveler on account of the rain, threw him into the mud. By treating the man with injury, he made him a pitiable spectacle of his own shamelessness. No one was present to restrain the insolent men. Yet the monk’s patience overcame the raging men, and, full of the love of Christ, while he was being badly treated, he answered nothing but, “Thanks be to God.” Thus the more he showed himself tranquil in mind and spirit, the more the scoundrels dragged the innocent man about, thinking him heated with wine, though the love of Christ had made him joyful and glad.

He left all monks a great lesson in patience: when he could have avenged himself, though wronged, he did not wish to do so. Would that, my brothers, for love of God and for our sins, we were as patient in the smallest things as that good man was in the greatest. There are among you certain men, I say it unwillingly, who are far too insolent and impatient: they are moved to anger by a word, even one not insulting, and when they are justly corrected for their negligences, they murmur unjustly, adding sin to sin through impatience.

But to what end do I set before them the patience of the man just mentioned, when they do not heed Jesus Christ, the most patient of all men? Indeed, whoever does not embrace patience and humility after the example of the King of heaven will be recalled to gentleness by the imitation of no man. For the Son of God is the highest and supreme example of patience and humility; whoever is not bent to endurance by the recollection of his passion is rightly judged hardened and a companion of demons.

No good Christian doubts that the words of our Lord Jesus Christ are true. Christ says in the holy Gospel: Blessed are the meek, for they shall possess the earth. If the meek are to be called blessed, what prevents the resentful, wrathful, and unmeek from being named accursed? Certainly nothing. And if, as the Lord himself and our Savior Jesus Christ bears witness, we possess our souls in patience, there is no doubt that through impatience we lose them.

It is therefore especially fitting that monks be meek, gentle, and patient, since they have solemnly devoted themselves to the imitation of Christ and the apostles. But Christ, the meekest of all, commands his disciples and imitators in the Gospel, saying: Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart, and you will find rest for your souls. Hence the holy bishop Augustine says: If you wish to reign with Christ in heaven, first learn from him to be meek, patient, humble, and gentle, for unless you have lived in this world in imitation of his life, you will by no means reign with him forever.

The divine Ambrose also says: When I have laid aside every sin, stripped off every malice, and, content with simplicity, have become poor in evils, it remains for me to temper my conduct. For what does it profit me to be free of crimes, unless I am meek and gentle? He is meek who yields to wickedness and does not resist in evil, but overcomes evil with good. And, as Chrysostom bears witness, he is gentle who, when provoked by injuries, neither thinks of doing evil nor does it.

Cassiodorus says: We call the gentle “accustomed to the hand,” that is, patient and meek, those who tolerate the iniquities of others and do not presume to burden anyone. Of such persons it is said in the psalm: My soul shall be praised in the Lord; let the gentle hear and rejoice. He did not say, let those learned in the law rejoice, nor those who fast, nor those who sing psalms, but the gentle, who, together with charity, have above all been accustomed to possess moderation in all things.

Those, however, are truly meek, gentle, and patient who harm no one, injure no one, offend absolutely no one, but, standing firm in their simple humility, always bear all injuries, insults, and mockeries with equanimity for the love of Christ, and are moved by no pain of spirit against those who harm them, just as we have already said that monk was whom the raging boys at Bingen treated with exceedingly insulting behavior.

But it is easy to be meek, patient, and gentle so long as nothing is done except according to the will and judgment of the patient person. Yet how far he is from true gentleness will become clear if even a slight occasion arises. It is a great virtue if you do not harm the one by whom you have been harmed; great strength of soul if, for the love of God, you forgive injury to the one who inflicts it; great glory if you observe the commandment of Christ and pour out prayers for those who injure you. No one has known the strength of his own soul whom a notable insult has not yet burned; let no one boast that he is meek or patient who has not been sated with reproaches. There is no patience without adversity, nor gentleness without insult, since if due [unclear].

[segment 82]
*On Patience and Gentleness*

If you wish to keep the proper bounds of endurance, first of all there must be someone present who inflicts an insult. “If I had been,” says the lazy and fleshly monk, “in the days of the martyrs, I would have feared no kinds of punishments for the love of Christ; I would willingly have exposed my body to the torturers, so that I might carry away the crown of martyrdom.” Great is the rashness of folly. You have never been able to suffer a word patiently for the love of God, and do you promise the constancy of the holy martyrs? You fear a light correction, you who promise that you would give your body for Christ.

If martyrdom delights you, hear what the most blessed pope Gregory says. If, with Christ helping us, we strive to preserve the virtue of patience and live in the peace of the Church, we also hold the palm of martyrdom. For we can be martyrs without sword and flame, if we truly keep patience in the soul. To die at the hands of a persecutor is martyrdom in open deed; but to bear insults and love the one who hates you is martyrdom in hidden thought. Patience is to endure another’s evils with equanimity, and not to be bitten by any grief against the one who has brought the evil upon us.

What now will you say, lazy monk, who, unable to suffer the very slight martyrdom of words, boast so insolently of constancy in shedding blood? Learn, wretch, to have patience in light matters, and consider that Scripture says: he who is unfaithful in a little, who would entrust to him what is greater? You murmur at everything that happens in the monastery; when rebuked, however gently, for your grave offenses and negligences, you bear it most impatiently. In your eyes no one walks rightly in the monastery; no one except you, in your estimation, conducts himself religiously. You alone, as it seems to you, are holy in the monastery; you, most learned of all, allow that no one knows what you alone do not know. You think yourself worthy of all honors above all others, because you not only lack patience, but retain neither true humility nor obedience.

This is not the way by which one goes to the kingdom of heaven: when in the school of humility a monk is proud, and by his conduct a Christian lives contrary to Christ. A monk, however, the solemnly sworn servant of Jesus Christ, so that he may imitate his Lord, ought not to be proud but humble, obedient not rebellious, gentle not cruel, nor insolent but mild. But a monk who is proud, wrathful, contentious, and a lover of his own will, such as, alas, those appointed over temporal affairs often are, whom among us according to the Rule we call cellarers: their pride has made for many a road to destruction.

Grief compels me to speak, and shame forbids me, although you all know the fall by which the devil lately brought shame upon us in the case of a brother. Who in our whole congregation was prouder than he? Who went about filled with greater insolence? But because he seemed to have greater experience among us in temporal matters, on that account he considered himself superior to all. The Almighty permitted him to fall, so that he who, reputed wise, refused to recognize his own weakness, might at last, confounded not without the scandal of many, understand God to be the adversary of the proud.

I grieve vehemently, and more than can be said; the fault of the man who fell seems to me heavier, because by his fall we are all confounded. May God grant him to do true penance, so that those who had been scandalized by his fall may be edified by his satisfaction before God and men. Pride has always been destructive to monks; and the mind of a man which it has once invaded it sinks into the pit of all vices. Beware pride, my brothers, because it is the beginning of every sin; it is that which, through the envy of the devil, introduced death into the whole world. Pride is the death of all virtues, and the source and spring of all vices. Humility, however, the nurse of virtues, most befits monks, because it makes them both mild and gentle. For every proud man is impatient, and every arrogant man is an enemy of gentleness. He who is humble of heart has patience in adversities for the love of God; and he who is tranquil in soul in the face of injuries merits a double crown.

A patient man in adversities becomes close to God; but he who is proud and impatient gladdens his enemies. For Scripture says: Better is the patient man than the strong man, and he who rules his own soul than the conqueror of cities. Likewise the holy bishop Augustine says: The just and pious man ought to be ready to endure patiently the malice of those whom he desires to become good, so that the number of the good may rather increase, and not that by like malice anyone should add himself to the number of the evil. For patience is very necessary for us, brothers, so that, doing God’s holy will, we may receive in Christ

[segment 83]
--- Page 092 ---

*All things. XVIII. Fol. XLIII. Promise.*

Adversities are daily at hand for us, by which we may be exercised; therefore, just as the cause of endurance is never lacking, so I pray that necessary patience may always be present. We are oppressed by want and poverty of necessary things; we are burdened by commerce with weak flesh; moreover the world tempts us in order to entice us, and the devil tempts us in order to seduce us. Among so many adversaries of our salvation, brothers, it is necessary that we keep watch in all patience and humility, since in this pilgrimage, as long as we live, we are placed, so to speak, in the midst of evils. Our poverty ought to suffice us, because it does not belong to monastic religion to be exalted in temporal things, but rather to be brought low, so that monks, poor and imitating Christ, who was made poor for us, may deserve to glory with him forever in heaven.

What if we see certain wicked men and sinners in the world abounding in riches? If good and just men are rich in this world, God’s providence is caring not for their strength but for their weakness, so that by benefits they may be called to better things. “I was young,” says the prophet, “and have grown old, and I have not seen the just man forsaken, nor his seed begging.” “Cast,” he says, “your thought upon the Lord, and he himself will nourish you; he will not forever allow the just man to waver.” The Lord Jesus knows what is suitable for the salvation of each one of his servants, and therefore he dispenses the gifts of the present life unequally according to the capacity of individuals. For not all things are suitable for all; therefore among the just in this world, with respect to temporal goods, some are poor, while others are wealthy. Concerning the poor the Lord Jesus says in the holy Gospel: “Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.” And through the prophet he speaks, saying: “Behold, I have refined you, but not as silver; I have chosen you in the furnace of poverty.” For it does not conduce to eternal salvation for all to possess the riches of this world; rather, it is expedient for some to be poor and for others to be wealthy, according as the Lord of all has provided what is more useful for each.

*Concerning just and good rich men.* The blessing of the Lord makes men rich, and affliction will not be joined to it. But if the wicked and reprobate abound in riches in this age, this ought not move the good to indignation; rather, let them fear him who leaves no good unrewarded and no evil unpunished. For the wicked, whom he cannot reward in heaven because of their iniquity, he rewards in this life for their good deeds; and for the good, whom he nourishes here in poverty, he preserves eternal rewards in heaven for the merit of justice. But just as not all the poor in this world are just and good, so not all those rich in temporal goods in this life are wicked. The reward of the wicked on earth should rightly be borne without indignation, since not love of God but justice has willed it to be present. Hence, just as the wicked have nothing in heaven, so you who have professed monastic poverty ought to have nothing on earth; but in hope of that good toward which you strive, whatever adversity befalls you on the way, you ought to bear it most patiently. For, as Paul, the holy lover of Christ, says: “The sufferings of this time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come that will be revealed in us.” Let those who are rich in Christ join mercy to humility; but those who are poor, let them take up patience with innocence for the love of God. For the soldier of Christ is neither cast down by poverty nor lifted up by riches, but, standing firm in himself, serves the King of heaven with tranquil spirit and cares nothing for transitory things. Thus the true monk is neither exalted by praises nor broken by reproach; he is not swollen by an abundance of necessary things, nor contracted by poverty; he despises prosperity and adversity alike, because, as the prophet had said, “the sun shall not burn him by day, nor the moon by night.”

But we, dearest brothers, let us make virtue and good will out of necessity, and out of the disadvantage of poverty, which we must necessarily endure, let us acquire for ourselves a great and useful patrimony of goods. This can at last be done if, for the love of God, we bear this very want manfully and with tranquil spirit. The disordered conduct of our predecessors brought upon this monastery the poverty that we suffer; but the mercy of almighty God, if we keep his commandments with a perfect heart, will without doubt relieve it in time. For almost two hundred years back, as far as we can ascer-

[segment 84]
we are able to infer from conjecture and from the preceding writings: the purity of monastic institution in this monastery of Spanheim, as also in nearly all the others, failed; and the disordering of the monks’ life utterly dissipated the whole substance of earthly goods.

Indeed, when, in the Christian year 1469, Frederick, count palatine and duke of Bavaria, a most Christian man who was residing in Simmern, held the corrupt life of the monks in his principality in very great hatred, he reformed three monasteries after expelling the deformed monks, and introduced men very well trained from other monasteries. And first he brought two monasteries of regular canons of Saint Augustine back to the observance of regular discipline: Ravengiersburg, namely, among the [unclear], and Schwabenheim among the [unclear], in the district of the Nahegau. Lastly, in the aforesaid year of the Lord, he also renewed this present monastery of ours, Spanheim, for the reform of the order; and, after removing the abbot together with five monks, he restored four others in their place by observance, setting over them my predecessor Johannes of Kölnhausen, now serving as abbot in Seligenstadt.

But they found the place oppressed by such poverty and burdened with another’s debt that there was no one who believed it could be restored, since all the annual revenues had either been sold or alienated under title of redemption; nor was anything at all left from which they might live. Our brother Heinrich, one of the first four reformers sent from Mainz, is present as reporter and witness of the things we say; from his mouth you will learn everything more fully. And behold, the seventeenth year is now passing since this place began to be restored under the chapter of the reformed union of Bursfelde, and still we are being tried by no small poverty.

I have said these things for this reason, brothers: that you may know that we do not bear alone the necessity we suffer, since those four men who first reformed the monastery endured heavier things; for beans and peas satisfied their bellies, and a thin drink of pear-cider furnished them drink by measure. The mercy of God called us to this place so that through patience we may overcome the adversary of our salvation; so that, according to the counsel of the divine apostle, having scant food and a garment with which to cover ourselves, content and giving thanks to the Lord, we may serve in cheerfulness of mind.

For we are not called to feasts of the flesh, nor to the delights of this world, not to pleasures nor to the comforts of the present life, but to serve as soldiers for the Lord and our emperor Jesus Christ in abstinence from all the delights of this age, in a pure conscience and humility of heart. Attend now, therefore, to your calling, monks, and do not corrupt the purpose of holiness, if you desire to come to the glory of God.

Woe to you, faithless monk, who, made a despiser of your calling and a transgressor of your vow, live in the monastery not monastically; you who were called to tolerate tribulation patiently for love of God, and inquire about refreshment. For your profession commands you to go humbly content in every vileness and extremity of clothing, and you do not fear to devote effort to fastidiousness about garments. You have been called to thirst, and you, despising drinking water, delight in drinking wine to satiety, against the monastic canon. You were made a monk to fight bravely against the adversary of your salvation, and you, having cast away from yourself the arms of the virtues, proceed lazy, idle, and naked; indeed, what is more serious, accompanied by a troop of vices. You were invited to keep watch in holy prayers, and you are dissolved by sluggish sleep and base idleness. You were called into the subjection of humble obedience, and you, swollen with pride, despise obeying the commands of the elders. You were called into charity, and you do not fear to carry hatred against your brother in your breast. You were called to weeping and tears, and you break out in deep laughter with disordered guffawing. The Lord called you to penitence in the monastery, and you, dissolved into wantonness, join sin to sin. You were called to meditate day and night on the law of our Lord Jesus Christ, and you walk after vanity, applying yourself to tales and perishable things. You were called to heaven, and you, loving the world, pursue earthly things. The Lord called you into the meekness of humility, and you proceed full of rancor and pride. You were called by the Lord to the perfection of the purest monastic way of life, and you, like beasts of burden, rot away in the dung of vanity and negligence. To the observance of man-

[segment 85]
--- Page 094 ---

*All Things. XVIII. Folio XLIII.*

You have been called to the observance of God’s commandments; and you, given over to the pleasures of the flesh, think of nothing less than Christ. You have been invited to love the Lord Jesus above all things, and you walk according to the evil desires of your heart, showing yourself ungrateful to your Redeemer. You, who ought to have lived by the spirit, savor earthly things, and, sunk in pride, despise all the holy exercises of spiritual conduct.

And what will you answer on that day of judgment to the Judge, who will say to you, most ungrateful one: I, who am Lord of all, humbled myself for your sake, taking the form of a servant and being found in appearance as a man; I poured out my blood for you; for you I endured scourges; and for you, by my own willing choice, I underwent a death exceedingly bitter. I, O monk, loved you with my whole heart; I redeemed you by my blood from perpetual death; I called you in love to the heavenly kingdom, if you had kept my commandments. But you lived in opposition to my precepts, and, what is worse, although you had been sworn upon my words, you were not afraid to show yourself an apostate and impious traitor against me, your Lord. Now therefore render an account of your stewardship.

What then will you answer, O lazy and idle monk, who now consume this acceptable time so fruitlessly, and despise living in true monastic purity according to the rule? What, I say, will you answer? Will you deny the works of depravity that you have committed, intending deceitfully to show good works that you now refuse to perform, so that by a lie you may circumvent the Judge and thus escape the punishment due to your demerits? But you must not be ignorant that all things you have done, said, or thought throughout your whole life, good and evil, are manifest in the sight of the Judge, and nothing can be hidden from him, who alone knows all things. For all things are written in the book of God, and your conscience will bear open testimony against you concerning all your evil deeds, so that not even the least thought will be able to remain hidden. For the scripture of the prophets says to God: “Because the thought of man shall confess to you.” You are before the tribunal of the Judge who knows all things, before whose terrible judgment every creature will stand with very great fear and trembling; there no excuse at all is admitted, but each shall receive the sentence due to his merits, with appeal set aside. Thousands of thousands and ten hundred thousand holy spirits will attend him; and do you trust that you can deceive the Judge by a lie?

But hear my counsel, and, rising from your ruinous sloth, at last, while there is time, return to righteousness. Do not put it off from day to day, because it is exceedingly dangerous for one remaining in sins to be confident, and to reject the remedy of salvation that is offered. Remember that you are going to die, and when you do not expect it you will be called to judgment. Consider, I beg you, how uncertain the time of life is, how it slips away, and how quickly all mortal things are corrupted. Nothing among human things is stable, nothing fixed, nothing firm; but all things that we see are corruptible and change with time.

Consider also where now our brothers Valentius, Martin, and John are, who in the present year, while living with us, were healthy, unharmed, and glad; they spoke with us, ate, drank, and labored together with us, thinking of nothing less than that they would die this year. Behold, when they did not expect it, death came and seized them, and set their souls before the tribunal of the fearful Judge, who rendered to each according to the works of his hands. Behold, learn now by the example of your brothers that you too are going to die, and perhaps you will pass from this world much sooner than you can estimate.

Come to your senses, wretched man, while you still can; do penance in the time of your health, when the Judge promises mercy, and do not put off today’s good into the uncertain tomorrow. Rise up, even now, late though it is, at last from the sloth of your too negligent past way of life, in which you have hitherto shamefully rotted; and cleanse your conscience from every stain of sin; wipe away with tears the evil that you have committed; and from now on live innocently, as befits a monk. Take up the perfect resolution of amendment, and understand that the kingdom of heaven is promised not to those who begin the good, but to those who persevere unto the end. Know also that heaven is not reached by sloth and torpor, but by great labor, by the very great effort of a will to make progress, and by unwearied zeal for spiritual exercise.

Therefore, wishing to serve the Lord and to please him in truth, always remain within yourself, fearful in dread, and prepare your soul for temptation. One must strongly resist disordered impulses and desires

[segment 86]
Homily XVIII. Fol. XLIII.

You have been called by God: yet, given over to the pleasures of the flesh, you think of Christ less than anything. You have been invited to love the Lord Jesus above all things: yet you walk according to the evil desires of your heart. You show yourself ungrateful to your redeemer, to whom you were obliged to live; and, submerged in the torpor and all the holy exercises of spiritual discipline, you despise them in pride.

And what will you answer on that day of judgment to the judge, who will say to you, most ungrateful as you are: I, who am Lord of all things, for your sake humbled myself, taking the form of a servant, and was found in appearance as a man. For you I poured out my blood; for you I endured scourges; for you I underwent a death exceedingly bitter by my own free will. I, O monk, loved you with all my heart. I redeemed you from perpetual death by my blood. I called you to the heavenly kingdom and to love, if you had kept my commandments. But you have lived in opposition to my precepts; and what is more grievous, although you had been sworn to my words, you were not afraid to present yourself against me, your Lord, as an apostate and impious traitor. Now therefore render an account of your stewardship.

What then will you answer, O lazy and idle monk, who now spend this acceptable time so fruitlessly, and despise living in true monastic purity according to the rule? What, I ask, will you answer? Will you deny the works of depravity that you have committed, and falsely claim the good works you now scorn to do, so that by a lie you may circumvent the judge and thus escape the punishment due to your deserts? But you must not fail to know that all the things you have done, said, or thought throughout your whole life, good and evil, are manifest in the sight of the judge, and nothing can be hidden from him, who alone knows all things. For all things are written in the book of God, and your conscience, reading against you all the evil you have done, will give open testimony, so that not even the smallest thought will be able to lie hidden. For the Scripture of the prophets says to God: Since even human thought will confess to you.

You are to be judged before the tribunal of the all-knowing one, before whose terrible judgment every creature will stand with very great fear and trembling. There no excuse at all is admitted, but each person will receive, according to his deserts, a sentence without appeal. Tens of thousands and hundreds of thousands of holy spirits will stand by, and do you think you can deceive the judge with a lie?

But hear my counsel: rise from your destructive idleness, wake up at last, and by true penitence return to the mercy of God and, while there is time, to justice. Do not put it off from day to day, because it is exceedingly dangerous to remain in sins and to refuse the remedy of salvation that is offered. Remember that you will die, and that when you do not expect it you will be called to judgment. Consider, I beg you, how uncertain is the length of time for living, how it slips away, and how quickly all mortal things are corrupted. Nothing among human things is stable, nothing secure, nothing firm; but all things that we perceive are corruptible and change with time.

Consider also where our brothers Valentine, Martin, and John now are, who in the present year were living with us, healthy, safe, and joyful; they were speaking with us, eating, drinking, and working, thinking less than anything that they would die this year. Behold, when they did not suppose it, death came and seized them, and brought their souls to the tribunal of the dread judgment, which rendered to each one according to the works of his hands. Behold, now learn by the example of your brothers that you too will die, and perhaps you will pass from this world much sooner than you can estimate.

Look to your own interest while you can. Do penance in the time of your health, when the judge promises mercy, and do not defer today’s good into the uncertainty of tomorrow. Rise up even now, late though it is, from the sloth of your negligent past way of life, in which until now you have shamefully rotted away, and cleanse your conscience from every stain of sin. Wipe away by labors the evil you have committed, and from now on live innocently, as befits a monk. Take up a perfect purpose of amendment, and understand that the kingdom of heaven is laid up not for those who begin the good, but for those who persevere to the end.

I know also that heaven is not reached by idleness and torpor, but by great labor, by the strongest voluntary effort to make progress, and by the tireless practice of spiritual exercise. Therefore take care to serve and please the Lord in truth. Always remain within yourself, fearful in awe, and prepare your soul for restoration. One must resist disordered movements and wicked desires of the flesh.

Homily XVIII.
Fo. XLV.

patience is discerned. For the more learned each person has been in the law of the Lord and in knowledge of the ordinance of rational nature, the more patient he will be in adversities. What if I speak more boldly, as a lover of truth: adversities profit us more than prosperous things, if we are learned and not forgetful examiners of our own condition.

But the third degree of patience, certainly higher and more perfect than the rest, is rightly granted to those who not only, when there is need, endure contrary things with equanimity for the love of God and the benefit of the brothers, but also, more fervent and inflamed by the spirit of charity, thrust themselves forward of their own accord and hasten gladly to receive insults and injuries. And this kind of patience is divided in two ways, just as a twofold account of martyrdom is found. For some, although they could have lain hidden during the persecution of believers, with the charity of Christ burning in them, under no compulsion but by their own free will, presented themselves before the tribunals of the persecutors; publicly confessing him as the redeemer of the world, they received with joy the torments inflicted on them. Others, however, did not thrust themselves forward of their own accord, yet gladly received the martyrdom offered to them, always prepared to endure for the faith of Christ whatever the cruelty of tyrants could inflict.

To this degree hasten the most shining confessors, by merit very close to them; and if the cruelty of the impious, by persecuting them, did not take them away, still they in no way lost the glory of martyrdom. For, crucifying their flesh in this world for the name of Christ, they endured both a voluntary and a lasting martyrdom; through abstinence and humility they were made glorious, always remaining steadfast in spirit and most patient, and most ready to receive martyrdom bodily. Therefore take care, O monks, to be imitators of these according to your powers; and since enemies who kill the body for Christ are now lacking, by struggling against the desires of the flesh and by patiently enduring adversities for the love of God, show yourselves martyrs in mind and spirit. The best kind of martyrdom in the peace of the saints is to afflict the flesh for the name of God through abstinence, to desire nothing in this world, always to make progress in good works, and, if anything evil should befall, to bear it in all things gladly and patiently with cheerfulness of mind. Henceforth, dearest brothers, live according to this rule of patience, if you desire to reign eternally with Christ. Bear one another’s burdens, and so you will fulfill the law of Christ: let each person gladly bestow upon his brother what he desires to be done for himself.

On the True Humility of Monks: Nineteenth Homily.

It is almost the opinion of all the learned, dearest brothers, that the highest and chief virtue of monks is none other than humility, because it is itself the mother and nurse of all virtues. For humility makes the monk; pride destroys and corrupts him. Hence the Lord says through Isaiah: To whom shall I look, if not to the poor little one and the contrite in spirit, and the one who trembles at my words? For God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble, because pride is the beginning of every sin, and without true humility no one can obtain salvation.

Since this is so, I think it necessary for me, brothers, to exhort you without ceasing that, fleeing pride, you always preserve profound humility in all your works. Indeed no good work is believed to please the Lord God if it is presented without the seasoning of humility. For humility is the most healthful medicine of faithful souls, which provides for all, because it presses down the swollen ulcers of the spirit, restores what is wasting away, corrects what is depraved, and removes what is overgrown.

O humility, mother and parent of all virtues, you strengthen patience, stir up compunction, illuminate charity, adorn chastity, make prayer fruitful, and commend fasting and abstinence. O holy humility, truly beloved of God, you justify the impious through penance; you preserve the just man in the purpose of holiness; you powerfully open heaven to your lovers; you close hell to the penitent. O humility to be venerated, exceedingly pleasing to God, you compelled the Son of the eternal Father to descend from heaven into the womb of Mary, the most chaste ark, the perpetual virgin; you made him appear visible to us, and you laid him, wrapped in poor cloths, in the manger, so that he might adorn us with the ornaments of perpetual blessedness in heaven. You subjected the Son of God to circumcision of the flesh
