--- Page 097 ---

Homily XVIII. Folio XLV.

...is known as patience. By this, the more each person has been instructed in the law of the Lord and in the knowledge of the teaching of rational nature, the more patient he will be in adversities. What if I speak more boldly, lover of truth: adversities profit us more than prosperous things, if we are instructed and are not forgetful observers of our own condition.

The third degree of patience, certainly the highest and most perfect of the remaining ones, is deservedly granted to those who not only bear contrary things calmly, when need arises, for the love of God and the benefit of the brethren, but who, inflamed by a more fervent spirit of charity, even of their own accord offer themselves and hasten eagerly to receive insults and injuries. And this kind of patience is divided in two, just as a twofold account of the martyrdom of the saints is found. For some, although they could have remained hidden while persecution of believers was raging, because the charity of Christ burned in them, with no one compelling them but by their own free will, presented themselves before the tribunals of the persecutors, and publicly confessing Jesus Christ, the redeemer of the world, received those torments for themselves with joy. Others, though they did not present themselves of their own accord, nevertheless gladly received the martyrdom offered to them, always ready to endure for the faith of Christ whatever the cruelty of persecutors could have brought against them.

The most radiant confessors are rightly seen to hold this degree; and although the cruelty of the impious did not take them away by persecuting them, they in no way lost the glory of martyrdom. For those who crucified their flesh in this world for the name of Christ endured both a voluntary and a lasting martyrdom; through abstinence and humility they were made glorious, remaining constant in soul and most patient, and always most ready to receive bodily martyrdom.

Therefore, O monks, take care to be imitators of virtues, so that, since executioners who put the body to death for Christ are now lacking, by fighting against the desires of the flesh and by patiently enduring adversities for the love of God, you may show yourselves martyrs in mind and spirit. In time of peace the best kind of martyrdom among the saints is to afflict the flesh by abstinence for the name of God, to desire nothing in this world, always to advance in good works, and, if any evil happens, to bear it in all things gladly and patiently with cheerfulness of mind. Henceforth, dearest brothers, live by this rule of patience, if you desire to reign with Christ forever. Bear one another’s works, and so you will fulfill the law of Christ: whatever each person desires to be done for himself, let him also cheerfully bestow upon his brother.

On the True Humility of Monks. Homily Nineteen.

The judgment of all the teachers, dearest brothers, is that the highest and chief virtue of monks is none other than humility, because it is itself the mother and nurse of all virtues. For humility makes the monk; pride destroys and corrupts him. Hence the Lord says through Isaiah: “To whom shall I look, except to the poor little one, contrite in spirit, who trembles at my words?” For God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble; and the beginning of every sin is pride, and without true humility no one can obtain salvation.

Since this is so, it is necessary that I, a brother crucified to you, exhort you without interruption to flee pride and always preserve deep humility in all your works. For no good work is believed to please the Lord God if it is presented without the seasoning of humility. Humility is the most wholesome medicine of faithful souls, which restores all the swelling wounds of a spirit that fears compunction, corrects what is depraved, and removes what grows up excessively.

O humility, mother and parent of all virtues: you strengthen patience, stir up compunction, illumine charity, adorn chastity, make prayer fruitful, and commend fasting and abstinence. O holy humility, truly beloved of God: through penance you justify the impious; you preserve the one who has been set in the purpose of holiness; you powerfully open heaven to those who love you; you close hell to penitents. O venerable humility, exceedingly pleasing to God: you compelled the Son of the eternal Father to descend from heaven into the womb of Mary, the most chaste and perpetual Virgin; you made him appear visible to us in the world; you placed him, wrapped in poor cloths, in a manger, so that he might adorn us in heaven with the ornaments of perpetual happiness. You also impelled the Son to undergo circumcision of the flesh...

[segment 89]
On the True Humility of Monks.

We were impelled to undertake it so that, circumcised in mind, we might be able to enter into the promised inheritance of the saints. O great power of humility, which made the regent of heaven and earth withdraw from before the face of one most lowly little man, and dwell in exile for seven years in the land of Egypt. You caused Christ Jesus, the only-begotten Son of God, who deigned to take on a human body joined to himself for our salvation, also to undergo the most bitter passion and death by his own willing choice. Great is your dominion, O venerable sanctity of humility, since the kingdom of heaven is promised to you; to your devotees the gift of eternal blessedness is granted; and whoever keeps to your footsteps is raised up to the fellowship of the angels of heaven, as Christ says in the holy Gospel: “Everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and he who humbles himself will be exalted.” He taught what he himself had imitated from heaven, when he says: “Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart, and you will find rest for your souls.”

The most blessed mother too, the intact Virgin Mary, in her sweetest canticle of praise says to God: “He has put down the mighty from their seats and exalted the humble.” And the prophet says in the psalm: “The Lord has regarded the prayers of the humble and has not despised their petitions.” The prayer of one who humbles himself penetrates the clouds; until it draws near it will not be comforted, and it will not depart until the Most High looks upon it. A monk who is pure in his conscience, made humble to God, is most pleasing; and the more he has cast himself down by humbling himself for God’s honor, the more glorious he will appear in the kingdom. The monk who has truly humbled himself is made a participant in the grace of the Most High and will rejoice forever with the king of heaven, Jesus Christ.

He who truly desires to imitate Christ should also assume true humility; clothed and fortified with it as with a breastplate of strength, he may do great things, yet think nothing of himself except what is humble, and not incur the stain of vain glory. For this is the true perfection of monks: always humbly to recognize their own imperfection, and to ponder carefully what good is lacking to them rather than what they possess. For true humility is always stretched forward toward what lies ahead through the desire of charity; and, cautiously mindful of the things of the past, lest it lose through fear what it has already gained, and desiring to acquire through the zeal of devotion what it has not yet gained, it does not cease in the work of holiness. It considers most diligently in what things it is still deficient, and does not weigh in what things it has advanced.

Indeed, whoever thinks that he is already something still stands far away from true humility, since true humility does great things and thinks of itself as very little; and the more it has advanced, the more deeply it recognizes its own weakness. For Scripture commands, saying: “The greater you are, humble yourself in all things, and you will find grace before God.” And again it says: “Humble your spirit greatly, for the vengeance on the flesh of the wicked is fire and worm.”

Therefore, my brothers, assume true humility, not that which is sometimes displayed before men by pretenders in outward gesture and voice, but Christian humility, which they possess by the pure affection of the heart. For it is one thing to have the virtue of humility, and another to have the likeness of its image. True humility is always patient with a tranquil heart toward all injuries and insults; it is obedient without dispute to the commands not only of superiors but even of equals; in adversities it preserves constancy, in bodily necessity moderation, in conduct continence, in conversation honorableness. It is cheerful, not lax; serious, not melancholic; generous in acts of mercy, not wasteful; compassionate toward the weak, not remiss.

Christian humility is ceaselessly pierced with compunction in the love of God; fervent in the spirit of devotion, it is continually moistened with tears; it always desires to see its prince, Jesus Christ, and, following his admonitions in spirit, sighs from sorrow. For it grieves over its sin, and whoever embraces true humility groans that he cannot advance as much as he desires, whoever imitates the Son of God. Nothing is more excellent than Christian humility, nothing more splendid than self-knowledge; it is never lifted up in the height of pastoral office, and, being subject to all, it rules over all. For he who is small in his own eyes is made great before God; and he who considers himself to be something great in this world is judged of no account before God. Each person will be precious before the inner judge to the degree that, doing good and never ceasing, he has been more contemptible in his own estimation.

Humility is the stable foundation of all virtues, because every spiritual building that is raised up to the height of Christian perfection is sustained by the rock of humility alone. Indeed, if true humility is lacking, the whole gathering of the other virtues will be nothing other than a kind of ruin of a house built without a foundation on sand. For in the spiritual building humility lays a stable foundation; charity orders the breadth of that house; the wall is raised on high by the continuous joining of good works in the fervor of the spirit; in the faith of our Lord Jesus Christ and in the firm hope of the promise of eternal protection and blessedness, it is set straight at the summit; and its length is arranged in the most beautiful order in the long-suffering of patience.

Therefore, my brothers, again and again I admonish, adjure, and beseech you to take care to found the building of spiritual exercise in the depth of humility, because no good work is pleasing to God that has been offered outside true humility. Now there are many things that are accustomed to stir the servants of God to true humility. For the consideration of their own worthlessness humbles some within themselves before God, because, when they consider that they have already often experienced themselves to be wretched and weak, they place all their hope and confidence in God, humbled. Others, weighing the multitude of God’s benefits toward them, fear the judgment of ingratitude; and therefore in the sight of God, with great fear and reverence of gratitude, they always walk humbly.

And who among us, brothers, is there to whom the compassion of almighty God has not granted many benefits, even singular ones? If we wish to be grateful for them, above all we must be humble. Others, converted by divine mercy after a multitude of sins to penitence, diligently recall to memory how many evils they have committed against the law of the almighty; and for these, humbled in bitterness of spirit, they detest their own impieties with horror. But we offend in many things; hence it is necessary that we humble ourselves always in mind and heart, and even in body, so that grace may abound where sin previously abounded.

Often, moreover, the providence of divine mercy permits certain persons to sin enormously for a time, so that afterward, converted to penitence, they may learn to remain in true humility. A humble penitent is higher than one who has not sinned but is proud, because humility justifies penitents, while pride condemns the arrogant eternally, even if they have done nothing else evil than this alone, that they are proud.

Others, carrying in their heart by daily meditation the bitter passion of Christ Jesus, humble themselves so much the more, the more they know that they are very insufficient debtors for such great and wondrous benefits of their Redeemer. The Son of God humbled himself, being made man for us, and, filled with many reproaches, voluntarily underwent the most shameful death of the cross, to free us from the destruction of perpetual death. What Christian man, and especially what monk, will not greatly humble his soul before God when he has recalled to memory such great humility of his Creator and Redeemer? Indeed, the man whom Christ’s humility does not stir to meekness: no admonitions of men will profit in him. In vain are we called monks, unless according to our strength we have humbly followed Christ. For this reason he said in the holy Gospel that he is the way, so that we may imitate his humility, and the way of life of so great a master may become the spiritual form of the disciple.

And so, brothers, as much as we can, let us labor with all our strength, since for us he humbled himself into such great humility. Therefore the highest medicine for inducing true humility in the servant of God is this: if daily and very often he recalls to memory the most bitter passion of Christ Jesus. Tribulations, adversities, and illnesses, together with poverty or want, have also made many humble who perhaps in prosperous and favorable circumstances would have perished, puffed up. And we trust that the poverty which we often say conduces to our salvation does so, since we have a great cause of humility, but find no matter at all for elation and pride. Others, by continual exercise of virtues and by the memory of the death which they must necessarily suffer shortly, have come to great humility. Whom, even the proudest, would not the intent meditation of death humble? Or who is so fierce in spirit that he would not little by little lay aside his swelling pride, if he considered more diligently that he will die shortly? Therefore the conscience of a monk must by no remembrance drive from itself the thought of future death, so that he may remain both humble and sorrowful, so that, standing firm in humility, he may not [unclear].

*Homily XIX. Fol. XLVI.*

[unclear] is raised up; it is sustained by the rock of humility alone. Indeed, if true humility is lacking, the gathering together of the other virtues will be nothing other than a kind of ruin: to build a house without a foundation on sand.

For in the spiritual building humility lays the stable foundation; charity arranges the breadth of that house; the wall is raised upward by the continuation of good works in fervor of spirit; by faith in our Lord Jesus Christ and by firm hope in the promised eternal perfection and blessedness, the straightness is set at the top; and the length is arranged in the long-suffering of patience with most beautiful order.

Therefore, my brothers, again and again I confess and admonish you to take care to found the building of your spiritual exercise on the foundation of humility, because no work, however good, pleases God if it has been offered apart from true humility.

There are many things that can provoke the servants of God to acquire true humility. Some, through consideration of their own vileness, humble themselves in the sight of God; while they know by their own experience that they are wretched and weak, they place all their hope and confidence humbly in God. Others, fearing the multitude of benefits shown to them and God’s particular judgment against ingratitude, always walk humbly in the sight of God with great fear and reverence of gratitude. And which of us is there, brothers, to whom the mercy of almighty God has not also granted many benefits individually? If we wish to be grateful for these, we must above all be humble.

Others, after a multitude of sins, converted to penance by divine kindness, diligently call to mind how many evils they have committed against the law of the Almighty, and because of these, in bitterness of spirit, they detest their own sins with horror and humility. But we have offended him in many things; therefore it is necessary that we humble ourselves always in mind and heart, and even in body, so that grace may abound where sin had formerly abounded.

Often, indeed, the providence of divine mercy permits certain people to fall grievously for a time, so that afterward, converted to penance, they may learn to remain in true humility. The humble penitent is better than the proud man who has not sinned, because humility justifies penitents, while pride condemns the arrogant forever, even if they have done nothing else evil except this alone, that they are proud.

Others, carrying the bitter passion of Jesus Christ in their hearts by daily meditation, humble themselves the more, the more they recognize the debt of such wondrous love from their redeemer and the greatness of his benefit, for which they are insufficient. For the sake of humility the Son of God himself, made man for us, mocked and bound, voluntarily underwent the most shameful death of the cross, to free us from the destruction of perpetual death. What Christian man, and especially what monk, would not greatly humble his soul before God when he calls to mind such great humility of his creator and redeemer? Truly, if Christ’s humility does not move a man to meekness, no admonitions of men avail in him. Monks are called so in vain unless, according to their strength, they have humbly followed Christ.

For this reason this way has been set forth for us in the holy Gospel, that we should imitate his humility, as the master’s conduct is the form of the spiritual disciple. Therefore, brothers, let us labor with all the strength we can, so that humility, conceived through meditation on the most bitter passion of Jesus Christ, may beat down, restore, and reform everything in us that swollen pride has made crooked.

How greatly it befits us to humble our souls, beloved brothers of Jesus Christ, when God himself, the creator of all things, made man, humbled himself so greatly for us. Therefore the highest medicine for bringing true humility to a servant of God is that he should daily and often call to mind the most bitter passion of Christ Jesus.

Many tribulations, adversities, and illnesses, together with poverty or want, have also made humble those who perhaps would have persisted proud in prosperous and favorable circumstances. And let us consider what we are often accustomed to say: that poverty conduces to our salvation, since we have a great cause for humility, but find no matter at all for exaltation and pride.

Others have come to great humility through the continual exercise of virtues and the meditation on death, which they must necessarily undergo. For whom would constant meditation on death not humble, even if he were most proud? Or who has so fierce a spirit that he would not gradually lay aside swelling pride if he thought more diligently that he must die in a short time? Therefore the conscience of a monk ought never to drive away from itself the memory of future death, so that he may remain humble and, confident in humility, may not become proud.

[segment 91]
*All things. 19. Fol. 47.*

Follow humility at all times, with all your strength and as much as you can; flee all pride and ostentation, for unless you greatly humble your souls, you will not enter into the kingdom of heaven. Always walk before Jesus in the simplicity of your heart, having a pure conscience in all things, lest your humility become bestial; for it contributes nothing to salvation for a sinner to acknowledge himself publicly and, while persevering in evil, not to amend his fault.

For the true servant of Christ sanctifies his humility by good works, and amends every guilt of his past life by continual penitence. Every day he recalls to memory the evil that he remembers having committed, and groans that he has gravely offended the Lord. He walks very cautiously and so laments past sins committed that he in no way commits further things that must be lamented.

The monk who is truly humble follows the Lord Jesus with a pure conscience, and fears nothing in this world more than offending the teacher whom he imitates. Whatever he does and wherever he goes, he always thinks of the Lord Jesus as present, and by continual meditation examines how he may please him alone. For this is true and Christian humility, and humble Christianity: to wash away sin in true compunction, and to preserve the conscience in all purity at all times, not only through fear, but rather through love of Christ.

The true humility of monks continually does whatever good it can for the honor of God, and thinks that it will do nothing which it knows displeases Christ. The true humility of monks neglects none of the things that God has commanded, and always flees as deadly the things that he has forbidden.

The penitent monk, humble in compunction of tears and serving the Lord in the fervor of charity, is a companion of the holy angels, a friend to the citizens of heaven, and very formidable to demons. For demons fear and flee nothing more than the humility of the servants of Jesus Christ, because, since they themselves are most proud by their vicious will, they are most violently tormented by the devotion of the humble. For where true humility is present, demons cannot remain; for as ice is dissolved by fire, so malignant spirits are scattered by the presence of a humble monk.

O venerable mother and preserver of all virtues, humility: yours is the kingdom, yours the power. You confer the kingdom on the humble; you bestow the glory of eternal felicity on the meek. Without your guidance no one comes to heaven; he who follows you does not fear hell. Whoever gathers works of virtue without you is like one who carries dust into the wind. You are the seasoning of all good works, for just as food in the mouth of the eater is not savory without salt, so without your companionship no one is established as a friend of God.

Humility made the Son of God man. Humility will make the servant of Christ equal to the holy angel. An angel, through pride in his own judgment, became a devil; and the Christian man through humility becomes an angel. Humility opens heaven to the meek servants of God, and pride plunges exalted men down to hell. Humility makes friends of God, and pride makes companions of demons. Humility illuminates the mind to true wisdom, and pride blinds the understanding of the one whom it has possessed. Humility purges the man whom it possesses of all disordered affections, and pride fills its worshiper with all crimes and filthiness.

Humility makes its lover a servant of God, but pride makes him a slave of the devil. Humility makes a man wise in the law of the Lord, but pride renders a man foolish in the knowledge of the Most High. Humility brings the poor man into fellowship with God, and pride makes the rich man a member of the devil. Humility is the mother and nurse of all virtues, and pride is the source of all vices. Humility, with charity mediating, covers a multitude of sins; but pride alone brings in the fullness of all vices.

Humility makes the good works of monks most pleasing to God, but pride renders the pursuits of the proud idle before the Creator. Humility receives from the Lord whatever it has asked; but God almighty resists the prayers of the proud. Humility always has, in the mercies of the Lord, a pious confidence with gladness of mind; but pride, by the sting of despair, generates disturbance in man. Humility always has joy of spirit in the love of Christ; but destructive fear always occupies pride. Humility, the fosterer of good works, neglects none of the spiritual exercises; but pride despises all pursuits of salvation. Humility justifies sinners; but pride condemns its followers. Humility makes one near to God; but pride makes one alien from the grace of God.

*b ii*

[segment 92]
--- Page 101 ---
Homily XIX. Fol. XLVII.

At all times, with all your strength and as much as you can, flee all pride and display, because unless you greatly humble your souls, you will not enter the kingdom of heaven. Walk always before the Lord Jesus in the simplicity of your heart, having a pure conscience in all things, lest your humility become bestial; for it contributes nothing to salvation for sinners to acknowledge themselves publicly and, by persevering in evil, not to amend their fault. For the true servant of Christ sanctifies his humility with good works, and amends all the guilt of his past life through continual repentance. Daily he recalls to memory the evils he remembers having committed; he groans deeply that he has offended the Lord. He laments greatly, and so bewails his past offenses that he does not commit further things that must be lamented.

The monk who is truly humble follows the Lord Jesus with a pure conscience, and fears nothing in this world more than offending the teacher whom he imitates. Whatever he does and wherever he goes, he always thinks of the Lord Jesus as present, and by continual meditation considers how he may please him alone. For this is truly Christian humility and humble Christianity: to wash away sin in true compunction, and to preserve the conscience in all purity at all times, not only from fear of punishments but rather from love of Christ.

The true humility of monks continually does whatever good it can for the honor of God, and does not think of doing anything that it knows displeases Christ. The true humility of monks neglects none of the things God has commanded, and always flees as deadly the things he has forbidden. The penitent monk, humble in the compunction of tears and serving the Lord in the fervor of charity, is made a companion of the holy angels, a friend to the citizens of heaven, and very fearsome to demons. For demons fear and flee nothing more than the humility of the servants of Jesus Christ, because, since they themselves are most proud by their own vicious will, they are most violently tormented by humble devotion. Where true humility is present, demons cannot remain; for as ice is dissolved by fire, so malignant spirits are scattered by the presence of a humble monk.

O humility, venerable mother and preserver of all virtues: by your power the kingdom is conferred on the humble; by your glory eternal happiness is granted to the meek. Without your guidance no one comes to heaven; whoever follows you does not fear hell. Whoever gathers works of virtue without you is like one who carries dust into the wind. You are the seasoning of all good works, for just as food in the mouth of the eater is not savory without salt, so without your company no one will be established as a friend of God.

Humility made the Son of God man; humility will make the servants of Christ equal to the holy angels. An angel, through pride and by his own choice, was made a devil; and the Christian man, through humility, becomes an angel. Humility opens heaven to the meek servants of God, and pride plunges haughty men down to hell. Humility makes friends of God, and pride makes companions of demons. Humility enlightens the mind toward true wisdom, and pride blinds the understanding of the one it possesses. Humility purges the person it possesses from all disordered affections, and pride fills its worshipper with all crimes and filthiness.

Humility makes its lover a servant of God; pride, however, makes him a slave of the devil. Humility makes a man wise in the law of the Lord; pride, however, renders a man foolish, far from the knowledge of the Most High. Humility introduces the poor man into fellowship with God; pride makes those rich in their own minds belong to the devil. Humility is the mother and nurse of all virtues, and pride is the source of all vices. Humility, by means of charity, covers a multitude of sins; pride alone brings in the fullness of all vices.

Humility makes the good works of monks most pleasing to God; pride makes the efforts of the haughty useless to the Creator. Whatever humility asks from the Lord, it receives; but almighty God resists the prayers of the proud. Humility always has a devout confidence in the mercies of the Lord, with cheerfulness of mind; pride, however, with the goad of despair, generates disturbance in a person. Humility always has joy of spirit in the love of Christ; but destructive fear always occupies pride. Humility, strong in good works, neglects none of the spiritual exercises; pride despises all pursuits of salvation. Humility justifies sinners; pride condemns its followers. Humility makes a person most like God; pride makes him alien from the grace of God.

b iiij

[segment 93]
On the true humility of monks.

Humility obeys the commands of God with a willing and zealous heart; pride resists the precepts of the Most High. Humility accumulates the merits of everlasting happiness; pride, however, prepares for itself the very heavy torments of hell. Humility grants security at the moment of death; pride brings dreadful fears and calamities to the dying. Humility frees the souls of its servants from every anguish in the fearful judgment of God; pride, however, accuses before that same judge all the wretches whom it has deceived in the world. Humility will place God’s servants at the judge’s right hand; pride will set the damned on the left. Humility will plead the causes of its own and prevail in judgment; pride will hand over its guilty followers to the devil. Humility will repay its friends with the rewards of eternal happiness; pride will render to all its wretches the punishments of perpetual damnation. Humility will be crowned in heaven without end; pride will suffer inexpressible torment with the demons forever among those below. Humility, crowned with Christ, will reign in heaven; pride, condemned with the devil, will be tormented in punishments. Humility, joyful and glorious, will sit forever at the right hand of God; pride, sorrowful and disgraced, will lie without end in the torments of hell.

Then the humble will exult in glory; all the proud, under excessive pain, will cry out forever in punishment. The humble will stand in the sight of God and of all the saints in great joy and everlasting exultation, receiving for the voluntary poverty of this life unfading riches; for lowliness, a kingdom; for sorrow, joy; for the love of God, dominion; for contempt of the world and voluntary chastening of the flesh, heaven. But when the proud and arrogant, set in hell, see the great glory of the humble whom they despised in the world, they will marvel at the sudden change of the saints, and will be troubled with dreadful fear; groaning from the excessive anguish of spirit, the proud will say in their fury: “These are the poor and humble monks, once clothed in vile garments, cast aside by men, abstaining from meats and from every impurity of the flesh, whose habit we once held in derision and as a likeness of death, and whom we counted wretched, without reputation and without honor. Behold how they have been numbered among the sons of God, and their lot is among the saints. Therefore we have strayed from the way of truth; the light of justice has not shone upon us, and the sun of understanding has not risen for us. What now has pride profited us, by which we once despised the humble? And what has the boasting of divine things in the habit brought us in this world? All things have passed away, and because of our stubbornness we have been numbered among the demons, since we always hated humility in the habit, and now suffer inexpressible torments.”

But the humble will live forever; with God is their reward, and their thought is with the Most High. My dearest brothers, always think beforehand that these things will undoubtedly be true in the future; therefore take up humility of mind, flee every pride, and strive to preserve purity, so that you may merit to obtain eternal rewards. Amen.

On the true obedience of monks. Homily 11.

If the humility of monks is true and perfect, it brings forth every fullness of the holy virtues. But if it lacks the splendor of virtue, true humility was never present in the mind. For first of all it brings forth true obedience, and profound subjection in all things for the love of God. For the monk who is truly humble while he journeys in the flesh strives to show himself in all things a disciple of our Lord Jesus Christ; for love of him, content with every lowliness and extremity, obeying his abbot in all things, he rejoices to be subject. Humble obedience for the love of Christ is a great virtue: it will open heaven to the monk and by its strength close hell. For obedience restores the peace of paradise between God and man. True obedience in a monk is this: it is rendered to the commands of superiors not from slavish fear but from the inward affection of charity; not from fear of punishment but from love of justice, persevering even to death and working good in cheerfulness of mind. These are those most beautiful wings of the mind by which a monk flies from earth to heaven: one wing humility spreads, the other the obedience of subjection carries. A monk who has not been adorned with these wings has utterly lost the dignity of his name and merit. He should not be called a monk, but a lying and most deceitful apostate, who, living in the monastery with the others, is without the obedience of humble subjection.

For a monk without obedience is a monster of the devil; and he who resists the commands of his superiors will inherit the punishments of hell. From the beginning the disobedience of the first man destroyed the way of eternal happiness, which the obedience of Jesus Christ restored. For he was made obedient to the Father for us, even unto death, and by his most innocent passion he restored to us the happiness we had lost. Hence the way to salvation is humble obedience, as our holy lawgiver Benedict cries out in the Rule: “Listen, my son, to the precepts of the master, and incline the ear of your heart; willingly receive the admonition of a loving father and carry it out effectively, so that by the labor of obedience you may return to him from whom you had departed by the sloth of disobedience.”

Obedience is the tree of eternal salvation, which grows in the garden of monastic humility. Obedience is the dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit and the quiet tomb of one’s own will. Obedience is the merit of heavenly happiness and the single lesson of monastic discipline. Obedience is the destruction of all arrogance and pride, and the pursuer of all vices. Obedience conquers every malice and extinguishes every disordered pleasure of the flesh. Obedience makes the conscience pure and clean, and preserves true humility in the mind. Obedience is the remedy for the soul’s wound and the foundation of all monastic observance. Obedience is the faithful guardian of all good works and the discipline of a sincere mind. Obedience is the beginning of mortification and the glorious ornament of monks. Obedience is the preserver of peace and concord, without which the vigor of monastic discipline perishes. Obedience always directs the keen edge of the mind toward God; and he who is truly mortified in spirit despises all the consolations of this world. Obedience makes the monk close to God and far removed from every defilement of soul. Obedience is the adornment of the soul and the garment of beauty; without it no one will come to the kingdom of eternal brightness. Obedience is the mother of victory; and whoever knows how, for the honor of God, to be subject in humility will obtain the crown of martyrdom in heaven. Obedience distinguishes the monk from the world; and he who follows it with his whole heart will be great before the Most High. Obedience is the consummation of Christian perfection; in its humble workshop, the more a monk advances, the holier he becomes. Obedience is sure security at the hour of death, for when a monk is about to depart from here, he will fly forth the more securely, the more ready he has been for obedience to commands. Obedience is the rewarder of the humble and the chief emblem of merits. Obedience is the rule of Christian gentleness and the strongest inner nourishment of charity. Obedience is the shelter of chastity and the ornament of most pure innocence. Obedience is the wisdom of monks; and to submit to the commands of prelates is the highest philosophy of cloistered men.

Obedience and humility are the wealth and riches of monks; and to possess continually in the mind the memory of the Lord’s passion is a sweetness greater than all the feasts of the world. Obedience and humility make the monk; but he who is rebellious and proud wears the monastic habit without cause. Obedience in the mind is the voluntary mortification of living members; its merit is of such dignity that a man has nothing greater that may be offered in honor of God. Let each one offer for the love of God what he can: without doubt, he has offered more than all who has slaughtered his own will for God through obedience. It is indeed a great good to distribute one’s resources for Christ’s love to the use of the poor, and to relieve by alms the poverty of the needy. But by as much as the whole outweighs the part, as free choice of the will is nobler than necessity, and as heaven stands apart from earth, by so much more has he offered who, for the love of God and by laying aside his will, has forever denied himself. For whatever temporal substance you have offered to God is not yours but another’s, and is of less value than what is proper to you, both in esteem and in reward. You have one thing proper to yourself which, offered to God, is pleasing above all things. What is this, if not free will, which the almighty Creator placed in your power? A good will is greater than all riches, for nothing richer can be offered to God, nothing will be judged more acceptable.

This most precious gift, my brothers, you once offered to God when, denying yourselves and all worldly things for Christ’s honor, you put on the monastic habit. I beseech you, be mindful of the prayers of your holy profession, and let that solemn promise never depart from memory, which each one of you made in the sight of God before us, saying: “I, brother Johannes, promise stability, conversion of my ways, and obedience according to the Rule of Saint Benedict, before God and his saints, in this monastery which was built in honor of blessed Martin, in the presence of lord Johannes, abbot.” Behold the bond by which we have voluntarily bound ourselves to obedience; behold

[segment 94]
Homily XX. Fol. XLVII.

...the obedience of subjection. For a monk without obedience is a monster of the devil; and the man who resists the commands of his superiors will inherit the punishments of hell. The disobedience of the first man lost, in the beginning, the happiness of eternal life; the obedience of Jesus Christ restored it. For he was made obedient to the Father for us, even unto death; and by his most innocent passion he restored to us the happiness that had been lost.

Therefore humble obedience is said to belong to salvation, as our holy lawgiver Benedict cries out in the Rule: “Listen, O son, to the precepts of the master, and incline the ear of your heart; gladly receive the admonition of a loving father, and effectively embrace it, so that by the labor of obedience you may return to him from whom you had withdrawn by the sloth of disobedience.”

Obedience is the tree of eternal salvation, which grows in the garden of monastic humility. Obedience is the dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit, and the tomb of every self-will. Obedience is the summit of simplicity and merit, and the one proof of monastic discipline. Obedience is the destruction and everlasting ruin of all pride, and the correction of every vicious sinner. Obedience conquers every malice and extinguishes every disordered pleasure of the flesh. Obedience makes conscience pure and clean, and preserves true humility in the mind. Obedience is the remedy for the wounded soul and the foundation of the whole of monastic observance.

Obedience is the faithful guardian of all good works and the discipline of a sincere mind. Obedience is the beginning of monastic life and the glorious ornament of the monk. Obedience is the conservatory of peace and concord; without it the strength of monastic discipline perishes. Obedience always directs the mind’s gaze toward God; and the man who is truly monastic in spirit despises all the consolations of this world. Obedience makes the monastic man near to God and far removed from every filth of the soul. Obedience is the adornment of the soul and the garment of beauty; without it no one comes to the eternal brightness of the kingdom.

Obedience is the mother of victory; and the man who has learned to be humbly subject for the honor of God will obtain the crown of martyrs in heaven. Obedience separates the monk from the world; and whoever follows it with his whole heart will be great before the Most High. Obedience is the consummation of Christian perfection; in its humble workshop, the more a monk advances, the holier he becomes. Obedience is certain security at the hour of death; for the more prepared a monk has been for obedience to the commandments, the more safely he will fly forth when he is about to depart from here.

Obedience is the reward of the humble and the special mark of merits. Obedience is the form of Christian gentleness and the strongest nourishment of inward charity. Obedience is the shadow of chastity and the ornament of innocent purity. Obedience is the wisdom of monks; and acquiescence in the commands of prelates is the highest philosophy of cloistered men. Let obedience and humility be the monk’s resources and riches; and to possess continually in the mind the memory of the Lord’s passion is the greatest sweetness, above all the feasts of the world.

Obedience and humility make the monk; but the man who is rebellious and proud bears the monastic habit without cause. Obedience is, in the mind, the living voluntary mortification of the members; its merit is of such great dignity that a human being has nothing greater which may be offered for the honor of God. Let each person offer for the love of God what he can; without doubt, the man who, through obedience, sacrifices his own will for God offers more than all.

It is indeed a great good to distribute one’s possessions openly for the love of Christ for the use of the poor, and to relieve the poverty of the needy with alms. Yet as much as the free choice of the will is nobler, in comparison with all necessity, than heaven is distant from earth, so much more does that man offer who, for the love of God, has of his own free will denied himself forever.

Whatever of external substance you offer to God is yours, yet it is not yours but another’s, and it is of lesser value both in estimation and in reward than your own self. You have one thing of your own which, offered to God, pleases him above all things. What is this, if not the free will which the almighty Creator placed in your power? But a good will is greater than all riches; whoever offers this to God is prepared to offer something unspeakably acceptable.

This most precious thing, my brothers, you once offered to God when, wishing to deny all worldly things for his honor, you put on the monastic habit. Remember the words of your holy profession, and let that solemn promise never depart from memory, which each one of you made in the sight of God before us, saying: “And I, brother Johannes, promise stability, conversion of my way of life, and obedience according to the Rule of Saint Benedict, before God and his saints, in this monastery which is built in honor of blessed Martin, in the presence of lord Johannes, abbot.” Behold, you see that we are bound to voluntary obedience; behold...

[segment 95]
--- Page 104 ---

Homily XX.
Fol. LIX.

O lover and student of your own salvation, consider the form of obedience which you are to follow. The abbot bears the office of Christ’s vicar in the monastery; whether he is good or bad, he deserves the obedience of all his subjects, not by his own merit but in the name of Christ. For we understand the saying, “He who hears you hears me, and he who despises you despises me,” not only of the apostles, but of any prelate in the Church of God who has been appointed guardian and ruler of souls.

Therefore, if you have an abbot who is good, suitable, and holy, hear his voice as the command of God, since he bears God’s place; and whatever he commands you, be obedient to him in all things. If his teaching is for you a rule of life, and his holy conduct an example of purer discipline, then he is a friend of God and the vicar of Jesus Christ: for the one reason he deserves that you love him, and for the other that you obey him in all things when he commands.

But if you have obtained a shepherd who is rude, unlearned, or certainly less suitable, take care not to sin by wishing rashly to examine his actions. Rather, consider that your sins deserve to have a useless shepherd. Consider him not according to what he is, but according to what he is called; honor the shepherd for love and reverence of him whose vicariate marks him out. Remember at all times that the obedience which you promised to God must be shown purely and simply to his vicar; for if the abbot is bad in his way of life, nevertheless he does not lose the vicariate of Christ unless he is deposed in fact. Nor does the shepherd’s wicked and blameworthy life absolve you from the obedience promised in religion.

The sense of our saying must be maintained: bad monks do not deserve to have a good abbot. Do not think, beloved brothers, that this example is being hurled at you. For I do not believe myself to be a bad shepherd, although I cannot deny that I am rude and unlearned; nor have we admitted any suspicion of evil about you, since indeed we seek nothing more than to nourish and enlarge brotherly charity in all things.

Frequently monks come to us, wandering either with reason or without cause, who for the most part turn the matter of their own instability back upon their abbots. But I, who desire you to be instructed in all things holy and useful, exhort and warn your charity in fatherly fashion that hereafter you should not lend your ears to these wanderers, who slander the characters of their own shepherds and strive to hide their own crimes by accusations against others. This is not the necessary integrity of monastic life: to turn the cause of the rebellion and disobedience of subjects back upon the head of the shepherd.

He who is a true monk does not rashly judge another’s affairs; and he who is a son of humble obedience does not investigate causes for rebellion. But he who grants consent to the passions of the flesh can never be at rest; and he who scrutinizes another’s life is a despiser of his own salvation. A monk founded in the love of God does not pass judgment on the command of his superior; and he who always beholds Christ as present venerates his own shepherd as an angel of God. Boldness makes a monk degenerate, and presumption has cast many into prison.

A wanton monk, too lascivious and insolent, has lost the purpose of holiness; and unless he is recalled to his senses by the hard chastisement of the flesh, he has already set his foot toward hell. He who examines the command of his superior with curious rashness is not obedient; and he who resists an order is not a true disciple of Christ. For true obedience does not murmur, does not hesitate, is not sad, does not seek a byway, does not delay, does not excuse itself, nor show the least sign of lack of resignation. At the superior’s command, for the love of Christ, it always rejoices; it runs to meet it gladly, and obeys cheerfully with haste.

For what else is a monk than a constant combatant, who, armed with the virtues of humility and obedience, always keeps watch in the arena of struggle, and, victorious under a threefold battle-line, fights more strongly against himself? Blessed is that monk who, doing violence to himself, has learned to overcome the will of the flesh, and by the continual practice of spiritual warfare climbs the steep mountain of true obedience. Truly blessed is that monk who, having despised all the consolations of the world, has placed his whole self in the love of Jesus Christ, and now desires nothing more among visible things. It is not for monks to seek riches, honors, and pleasures in this world, nor is it fitting for the soldier of Christ to think of anything except Christ. O monk three and four times blessed, who for the love of Christ mortifies his own will until the end of life, and perseveres humbly in true obedience.

How great a good humble obedience is before God, my brothers, no one can sufficiently declare, for divine testimony wished to prefer it to all sacrifices. It is obedience that reconciles the soul to God, and through the shedding of blood crowns the martyrs.

[segment 96]
Homily XX. Folio XLIX.

For you who love your own salvation and are zealous for sound obedience, consider whom you are to follow. The abbot who governs the monastery as Christ’s vicar, whether he is good or bad, deserves the obedience of all his subjects not by his own merit, but in Christ’s name. For we understand the saying, “He who hears you hears me, and he who rejects you rejects me,” not only of the apostles, but of every prelate in God’s Church who is appointed guardian and ruler of souls.

Therefore, if you have an abbot who is good, suitable, and holy, listen to his voice as to the command of God’s representative, and be obedient in all things, whatever he commands you. But if his teaching is a rule of life for you, and his holy conduct an example of purer formation, he is God’s friend and the vicar of Jesus Christ: for the one reason he deserves to be loved, and for the other, to be obeyed in everything he commands.

But if your pastor is negligent, ignorant, or certainly less suitable, beware, I beg you, of wishing rashly to examine his actions. Rather, think that your sins deserve to have such a pastor over you. Consider not what he is, but what he is called, and honor the pastor for the love and reverence of him whose vicariate he bears. Remember at all times to show purely and simply to his vicar the obedience that you promised to God; for if the abbot is bad in his conduct, still he does not lose the vicariate of Christ unless he is removed from office. Nor does your pastor’s blameworthy life absolve you from the promised obedience of religion.

The saying of our word must be feared: reprobate monks do not deserve to have a good abbot. This example will not be turned against you, holy brothers in Christ; for I do not believe myself to be a bad pastor, though I cannot deny that I am negligent and ignorant. Nor have we ever admitted any evil suspicion about you, since we seek nothing more than to nourish and enlarge fraternal charity in all things.

Monks often come to us, wandering either with reason or without cause, who very often lay the grounds of their instability upon their abbots. But I, who desire you to be instructed in everything holy and useful, exhort your charity paternally and warn you in Christ not hereafter to lend your ears to those who detract from the character of their pastors and strive to hide their own crimes by denouncing others. It is not the necessary integrity of monastic discipline to turn against the head of the pastor a cause for rebellion and disobedience among subjects.

The true monk does not rashly judge another’s affairs; and one who is a son of humble obedience does not set forth causes of rebellion. But one who yields consent to the passions of the flesh can never be at rest; and one who scrutinizes another’s life has contempt for his own salvation. The monk founded in the love of God does not judge the command of his elder; and one who always beholds Christ as present venerates his pastor as an angel of God.

Audacity makes a monk degenerate, and presumption casts many into ruin. A pernicious and insolent monk has lost the purpose of holiness; and unless he is recalled to his senses by harsh chastisement of the flesh, he has already set his foot toward hell. One who discusses the elder’s command with curious rashness is not obedient; and one who is saddened by an order is not a true disciple of Christ. For true obedience does not murmur, does not delay, is not saddened, does not seek difficulties, does not tarry, does not make excuses, and does not consent to the least sign of irreverence. For love of Christ it always rejoices at the elder’s command, runs to meet it gladly, and obeys cheerfully with haste.

For what else is a monk but a constant combatant, armed with virtues, who always watches in the zealous contest of humility and obedience, and, under a triple battle-line, fights more bravely against himself after conquering? Blessed is that monk who, doing violence to himself, has learned to overcome the will of the flesh and, through continual practice of spiritual warfare, ascends the arduous mountain of true obedience.

Truly blessed is that monk who, despising all the consolations of the world, has placed his treasure in the love of Jesus Christ and desires nothing further among visible things. This is the divinity of monks: to despise honors and pleasures in this world, and to think of nothing else but Christ. O monk three and four times blessed, who, with his own will mortified from the beginning to the very end of life for the love of Christ, perseveres humbly in true obedience.

How great a gift humble obedience is before God, my brothers, no one can sufficiently express, since divine providence has willed to prefer it to all virtues. Obedience is what reconciles the soul to God and, through the shedding of blood, crowns martyrs.

[segment 97]
On the true obedience of monks.

For the monk who, for God's sake, of his own free will binds himself to perpetual obedience in religion, if he lives rightly according to his vow, certainly undertakes the next thing to martyrdom. Obedience, then, is martyrdom without the shedding of blood; and voluntarily to subject oneself to a man for God's sake is a great torment of the flesh. The soul that is firmly loved in humility and obedience will without doubt attain the crowns of martyrdom. It is a hard work and an exceedingly laborious contest for a free man to despise himself, and to resign himself wholly, and his own will, forever under the command and power of another. But let us look with faithful eyes upon the reward of this contest, and we shall reckon all the hard and rough things of this life to be like a certain childish game, which already is striving toward blessedness and afterwards will appear as nothing. For, as the apostle, lover of Christ, says: "The sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come, which shall be revealed in us." Therefore the crown that once gave the saints martyrdom for Christ, true humble obedience will prepare today for the lovers of Christ.

For the monk who is truly humble and obedient for Christ dies in secret without fear, since one who has served faithfully under the command of his superior is freed from every demand of accounting. For he who lives at the nod of his superior and walks in all the commandments in humility can commit nothing that should be carried before the judge. He is defended by the testimony of the abbot, under whose direction he lived rightly without complaint.

Consider, brothers, the law of obedience according to which you promised to live, and know that what had been voluntary was made necessary when you solemnly consented in the profession of monastic consecration. It is no longer lawful for you to withdraw from your holy purpose, nor can you revoke the vow once uttered. If you do everything according to the holy Gospel of Christ and the rule of father Benedict that you vowed, let there be no doubt of your salvation: you will be blessed, and you will receive from the Lord the reward due to the saints. But if, forgetful of your promises, you violate the vow of holy purity made to God; if, after spiritual consecration, you live according to the flesh, then your condition is now such by your own law that you are most certainly to be condemned by the Lord, since no one under Christ the judge of all can mock God, to whom you consecrated yourselves.

For it is much better to promise nothing than to violate a vow. Therefore the loss of your salvation is set in this one thing: that what you once promised to God according to the rule of Saint Benedict, you should not persevere in until death in all purity of mind and body. For a Christian man who has not made the vow of monastic profession can be saved by living according to the Gospel; but one who has once become a monk will not be saved unless he has observed until death all the things required for monastic life.

Listen then, O monk, to the word of Benedict to the end. For if you live as a monk, O monk, you will be saved; but if you live otherwise, you will not be saved, but will receive your place among apostate spirits, because you have mockingly despised the God whose servant you are. Now therefore call yourself back to yourself; examine carefully the perfection of your whole life, how you have fulfilled the vows that you promised and assumed. For the third fearful sentence of first examination now constrains not only the conscience but also the judgments of others. Always think on that soul in which, for the sake of the outward monastery, the first entrance was made, so that whenever you find yourself growing sluggish from the fervor of holiness, you may be able to renew in your mind the purpose of your first beginning. And so you will be able to be a holy and good monk, if in all things and always everywhere you keep obedience and humility.

The monk's true obedience gathers all the virtues into itself, and humility preserves them gathered. Forget yourself, and be ready to obey the command of your superior; and however far you advance in the arena of holy struggle, do not lift up your soul in any estimate of perfection. For what are our righteousnesses before God, and what good can we do that he has not given? For God, who is all in all, is himself sufficient and alone the perfection of our goods; he has no need of ours, since he is the highest and unchangeable good. In comparison with him, all our justifications are less than nothing. Therefore the monk placed under an abbot, truly obedient in humility, walks securely; and if he perseveres to the end, fighting and conquering, he will be gloriously rewarded.

Your humility of obedience truly delights me, brothers, when you present yourselves as faithful to the almighty Lord; nor in this matter do I regard myself as set over you, but as your companion, and the humble, though unworthy, servant of you all. Therefore, in the things that are yours and pertain to your salvation, receive me as speaking and enjoining not my own private and personal precept, but rather the common precept of the Lord of all.

[segment 98]
--- Page 107 ---

Homily XXI.
Fol. L.

... for love of him, unworthy men have set me, an unworthy man, over you as abbot. For my part, I shall not be troublesome to you if you run well; but if anyone strays from the way of truth, I am compelled, even unwillingly, to oppose him with all my strength.

Let the monk always think about his entrance into the monastery, and never forget the first purpose of his profession. In all your works, O monk, always remember that you are not long-lived upon the earth, but will shortly depart from this life, and perhaps will die when you do not expect it. Therefore stand by the purposes of inviolate holiness, and always be watchful in good works, so that when the Lord comes and calls you, he may find you prepared. Remember that you are a pilgrim on earth and have here no lasting city; but just as a traveler hastens toward the end of his journey, so you hasten swiftly toward the thing to which you draw nearer every day without interruption. Therefore, while you are healthy, put your house and the confidence of your soul in order, so that soon you may not be afraid; for rarely does he die well who is not prepared as one who is to die each day. O how secure a monk departs from this life, who, keeping his conscience in all purity, has lived at all times, without rebuke, according to the integrity of the rule of our most holy lawgiver, even unto death.

Let your mind continually hold in memory our Lord Jesus Christ as if present, indeed as never absent; and let no hour pass in which you do not think with thanksgiving upon him suffering for you. Stand in humility and true obedience, and no adversities will ever prevail against you. Just as trees, deep-rooted and sending great roots into the earth, are not easily torn from their place, so the monk who possesses charity and obedience in humility, together with the other virtues, is by no means overcome by demons. Obedience, brothers, is a great virtue, which brings the other virtues into the mind and, through the humility of service, preserves them once brought in. May our Lord Jesus Christ himself, who for us became obedient unto the death of the cross, enlighten your hearts by his grace and sweetly incline your minds at all times to the observance of the regular life: he who lives and reigns with God the Father in the power of the Holy Spirit, God, for all ages of ages. Amen.

On Continence and True Chastity: Homily XXI.

My very dear brothers, I have obtained from God a slender grace, by which, as your pastor, I may be found worthy and fit at once in life and in speech. For I know, nor does it escape me, how dangerous a thing the governance of souls is for a young man and beginner such as I am, when one who has not yet learned to direct and govern himself according to the norm of justice presumes to undertake the instruction of others. I blush to open my mouth among you for teaching, since in a short time and before maturity I am compelled to be a teacher of discipline. Yet since this has pleased you, and you wished to have me, a young man, as master, pardon me, for necessity compels me to fulfill, as best I can, the office committed to me. Help me with your prayers, I beg you, brothers, so that the mercy of God may supply the strength that the rashness of age denies.

For I desire you to walk before God in all purity of mind and body, so that you may please him to whom you handed yourselves over from the beginning. As leader of the journey you have undertaken toward heaven, find our Lord Jesus Christ, who, born of Mary ever most modest virgin, perpetually preserved the most perfect chastity of mind and body. Follow his commandments, in whose service you were consecrated, so that you may preserve not only in the body the continence commanded to you, but also in a mind brighter than sapphire and carbuncle guard perpetual chastity. For chastity is a most beautiful virtue, especially fitting for cloistered men; and when, under the yoke of reason, the impulse of every lust boils up, chastity is the ordered and conquered habit of the vices within the bodily fervors of honor. An incontinent and wanton monk, who is seduced by the spiritual lust of the flesh, is accounted to have withdrawn entirely from God and gone over to the demons.

There are also three things without whose observance no one should be reckoned firmly among the monks. The first gift is the obedience of humble and true subjection; the second, the renunciation of all property, as the Rule commands; the third, the unsullied keeping of perpetual purity and chastity. Whatever monk violates any of these has corrupted the apostasy of his order. A monk is lifted up by supports, strength, and spiritual work; and if one of these has been taken away, then, without doubt, he is blemished. Chastity adorns monks, indeed everyone, and leads them to the highest glory of the angels.

[segment 99]
On Continence and True Chastity.

Nothing is more beautiful than chastity: it makes clean what was conceived from unclean seed; it also makes a member of the household out of an enemy, and an earthly human being into an angel. Chastity is the most beautiful of all virtues; when preserved worthily for love of Christ even unto death, it is rewarded with an inestimable prize. Chastity is nearest to the fellowship of angels, the highest medicine of the Christian mind. Chastity is the lovable household companion of Jesus Christ, the eternal king, a wondrous beginning of future incorruption. Chastity is the fullness of heavenly grace, through which every corruption of nature is restored and renewed. Chastity is the most powerful of all virtues; through it, in fragile flesh, nature conquers in a wondrous contest. Chastity imitates the angelic life, and even in the midst of mud is not touched by any sprinkling of stain. Blessed is the person who has deserved to receive the true gift of chastity from the Lord God, and to keep it inviolate unto death in Christian humility.

Chaste is the one who, for love of Jesus Christ, pure in mind and body, abstains from every defilement of the flesh; who, by the speech of the Holy Spirit, sweetly burns in the charity of God; who desires absolutely nothing in this world, but always longs to be and to live with Christ. Chaste is the one who, mindful of his profession of spiritual warfare, subdues the motions of the flesh by reason, and strongly restrains at once, from their beginning, all the little sparks of budding lust. Chaste is the one who, as soon as a carnal thought begins to knock at the mind, implores divine help with tears, and does not cease from prayers until he senses that the fire of burning desire has been utterly extinguished. Chaste is the one who flees every occasion and touch of lust; who makes a covenant with his eyes so that he does not even think of a woman; and who sets a guard over his senses, lest any foul or immodest things be thrust upon them. Chaste is the one who avoids the company of the wanton and always turns his eyes away, lest he behold vanities. Chaste is the one who detests the presence of all misery and who at all times holds his own frailty suspect, with fear and watchfulness. Truly chaste is the one who, in mind and body, imitates angelic nature and admits no corruption in holy conduct.

Chastity is a gift of grace, and not a work of nature or human industry. Chastity makes a humble monk most pleasing to God, since one who lives in mortal flesh without corruption is a companion of the angels. Whoever has attained chastity is always truly humble, and whoever understands its nature does not boast in himself over its possession, but in the Lord who gave it. For no one is chaste by his own strength, since fragile man is not sufficient to conquer nature unless he who created nature and all things grants it. As often, therefore, as nature is conquered through chastity, God manifestly works together with man. Chastity itself has, in those subject to it, a difficult beginning, until the movement of unlawful thought is brought into contempt. Chastity overcomes fornication by fleeing rather than by struggling against it, since lust is girded about with so many subtle weapons that it must be avoided before it gathers strength.

Therefore, whenever anyone’s mind is touched by the motions of lust, let divine help be invoked without delay. Let no deliberate space be given to unclean thought, but as soon as it begins to be felt, let it be brought into contempt by the remedy of forgetfulness. No conversations at all are to be held with the fraudulent enemy who is always laying snares; nor are counsels of the flesh to be discussed with unclean thoughts. For unless you crush the serpent’s head, it slips away; unless you at once break the motions of lust from the beginning, it is strengthened. Hence the holy apostle of Christ, Paul, teaches that one must fight against any vices, but commands fornication alone to be fled. We carry about with us the materials of victory against ourselves; therefore he wills that we flee the enemy.

Chastity is nourished by fasting; it is shipwrecked by gluttony. Therefore, if you love chastity, love fasting, so that it may have necessary nourishment. One who is chaste follows the industry of the bee; and one who is truly a monk, as though crowned with all flowers, is adorned with all virtues. Not everyone who is ignorant of lusts is chaste, but the one who, in pure and clean flesh, for the love of Christ alone, also strives for holiness of mind. Otherwise, what does purity of dwelling profit, if the inhabitant is foul and unclean? Blind paganism once had its virgins, sacrificing in their garments; but because they did not offer the modesty of the body to the true God, nor did they preserve right faith in the mind, they descended into the depths of hell with the demons whom they worshiped. For chaste is the one who, for love of the Creator, restrains the flesh from every lust, and, holy in mind through virtues, offers to God himself a soul adorned with all virtues.

Outside the faith of Jesus Christ today there is no true chastity, nor any that is meritorious for eternal salvation. Although you live chastely, O monk, pure and holy, and as long as the spirit remains in the body, do not presume to have confidence in chastity from clay. The food of chastity is fasting and abstinence, and, in every action, the support of wholesome continence. But when you have attained all things, do not trust in yourself, because the one whom we know to have eaten nothing fell from heaven. Mortification of the body is both very necessary and very useful for preserving the beauty of chastity; but however much you have advanced in either, do not trust in yourself. Instead, always watchful, hoping in the Lord’s help, be afraid. For unless you keep yourself continually and most earnestly in the fear of the Lord, the house of your conscience will quickly be overthrown. He runs in vain who presumes to guard chastity by his own strength. For unless the Lord causes the dwelling of chastity to be built, the one who builds the wall of abstinence labors in vain. And unless you become, with all your strength, a co-worker with divine grace, you will by no means obtain the beauty of chastity. For God will not make you chaste without your will, nor will you attain this great gift unless he, through his mercy, comes to the aid of your weakness.

Therefore it will be your part, O monk, to offer the desire for chastity with good will, and, in humble confidence, to await its sure increase from the Lord. Apply fasting, and the Lord’s help will not be lacking. Chastity is a gift of grace, not of nature; and to live in the flesh as though beyond the flesh belongs to angelic strength, not human. Nevertheless, it is not difficult for a person of good will to preserve chastity, especially one who is separated from the tumult of worldly people and lives solitary in the midst of monks. A monk, too, who enters a monastery of good reform while young and innocent, if he keeps his mind continually occupied in the fear of the Lord, is less assailed by lust. For the experience of lust is pernicious, since, as someone truly said, it seeks to conquer souls, not to satisfy the veins. But a monk who is a lover of chastity can easily extinguish the heat of lust if, fleeing idleness and fullness, he chooses to occupy himself continually in spiritual exercise. For among all vices none assaults chastity more greatly or more dangerously than fullness and idleness. If a monk overcomes these two evils, he will easily preserve chastity.

Hence the most holy princes of monks, wishing to cut away the causes of lust, established for us a spare and meager diet according to a fixed measure, by which the body is sustained for necessity and is not fattened for lust. They also decreed that scantiness of sleep was useful for monks, because immoderate rest stretches the flesh no less than gluttony. But they wished idleness, as the most pernicious plague of the soul, to be far from cloistered persons, dividing the time most wisely so that a monk would never be found idle. Hear these things, brothers, and grasp them in your mind, you who carry a great treasure in a fragile little body. Always flee idleness; always do something good, so that whenever the tempter, the devil, approaches, he may find you usefully occupied. For truly, one who is idle lies open to the snares of the tempter; but one who is occupied in good work has closed every entrance to snares. Therefore the mind must be occupied with continual exercise, so that it is never found idle. Blessed is that monk who occupies his mind at all times in good exercises and never sleeps in the sleep of torpor and sloth. Blessed is the spiritual soldier of Christ who always keeps watch in the fear of the Lord and immediately restrains, from the beginning, all the disordered motions of the flesh.

For it is not enough to guard true chastity unharmed if someone plays with unclean thoughts as though deliberating with them. It belongs to strong men to crush the first motions of wicked thoughts, because where delay creates danger, one must fight strongly at once. There must always be vigilance against the adversary of our salvation, who is never conquered more quickly than if he is choked off immediately from the beginning. Continual exercise is needed, brothers, because a mind always occupied in holy studies is like a very high tower of strength, in which the soldier of Jesus, set as a most watchful guard, fears no hostile snares. Therefore I beseech you, my dearest brothers, do not consume time in idleness, lest you increase in yourselves the material of vices, you who are not ignorant of the wiles of the malignant spirit. Preserve continence in all things, and apply temperance, the guide of virtues, to all your works. Always consider how you ought to conduct yourselves in all religious holiness before the Lord [unclear].

[unclear] Jesus Christ, a person's chastity is not true nor meritorious for eternal salvation. If you wish, O monk, to live chastely, purely, and [unclear], then as long as you remain in this body of clay, do not presume to have confidence in your chastity.

The nourishment of chastity is fasting and abstinence, and devout prayer is the firm support of salutary continence. But when you have done everything, do not look to yourself, for one fell from heaven who, as we know, ate nothing. Mortification of the body is very necessary and useful for preserving the beauty of chastity; but however much you have advanced in both, do not trust yourself, but always watch, hope in the Lord's help, and fear. For unless you keep yourself continually and most earnestly in the fear of the Lord, the house of your conscience will soon be overturned.

He runs in vain who presumes to guard chastity by his own strength. Unless the Lord builds the dwelling of chastity, he labors in vain who constructs the wall of abstinence. And unless you have cooperated with all your strength with divine grace, you will in no way obtain the beauty of chastity. For God does not make you chaste without your will, nor will you attain this great gift unless he comes to the aid of your weakness through his mercy.

Therefore, O monk, offer the desire for chastity with a good will, and with humble confidence await sure increase from the Lord. Apply effort, and the Lord's help will not be lacking. To live chastely in the flesh belongs to angelic strength, not human strength. Nevertheless, it is not difficult for a person of good will to preserve chastity, especially one who is separated from the useless company of secular people and lives among monks. A monk, too, who enters a monastery of good reform while young and innocent, if he continually occupies his mind in the fear of the Lord, is less attacked by lust.

For experience of lust is ruinous, since, as someone truly said, Venus wishes to conquer souls, not to satisfy them. But a monk who is a zealous lover of his own salvation can easily extinguish the heat of lust if, fleeing idleness and satiety, he wishes continually to occupy himself in spiritual exercise. Among all vices, nothing attacks chastity more dangerously than satiety and idleness. If a monk overcomes these two evils, he will easily guard chastity.

For this reason the most holy leaders of monks, wishing to cut off the causes of lust, established for us a frugal diet in fixed measure, so that the body might be sustained for necessity and not unchastely stirred to lust. They also decreed restraint in sleep as useful for monks, because excessive rest nourishes the flesh no less than gluttony. They wished idleness, as a most deadly plague of the soul, to be far from cloistered men, most wisely arranging the time so that a monk would never be found idle.

Hear these things, brothers, and grasp them in your mind, you who carry a great treasure in a fragile little body. Flee idleness; always do some good work, so that whenever the tempting devil approaches, he may find you usefully occupied. For the one who is idle lies open to the snares of temptation; but the one occupied in good work has closed every entrance to those snares. Therefore the soul must be occupied in continual exercise, so that it is never found idle.

Blessed is that monk who at all times occupies his mind in good exercises, and never dozes in the sleep of torpor and sloth. Blessed is the spiritual soldier of Christ who always keeps watch in the fear of the Lord and restrains every disordered movement of the flesh immediately at its beginning. For no one who knowingly plays with unclean thoughts is able to guard true chastity. The first movements of wicked thoughts must be resisted, because where delay creates danger, one must fight strongly at once.

One must always keep watch against the adversary of our salvation, who is never defeated more surely than when he is choked off immediately at the beginning. Continual exercise is needed, brothers, because the mind always occupied in holy studies is like a very high tower of strength; stationed there, the most watchful soldier of Jesus Christ fears no hostile ambushes.

Therefore I beseech you, my dearest brothers, do not spend time in idleness, lest you open within yourselves the materials of vices, which the crafty evil spirit knows well. In all your works apply temperance, the guardian in all things and the guide of virtues. Always consider how you ought to walk before the Lord in all religion and holiness.

[segment 101]
--- Page 110 ---

All things, XXI. Folio LII.

For unless the mind has first been blinded, the will will not yield to the demon. The spirit of fornication first darkens the mind gravely; then, until it has hurled it into sin, it fights against one already blinded. But after it has cast the monk into the mire of unspeakable crime, it sets two obstacles in his way so that he may not rise again to penance.
