For either it subtly tries to lighten the gravity of the sin, so that he does not fear a great punishment, or it brings back to the fallen man’s memory the severity of judgment, so that he falls into the pit of despair. By these snares the sinful soul is caught; and the longer it lies in the depth of misery, the more hardened it becomes.

Flee idleness, O cloistered men, and above all avoid gluttony and the satiety of the belly, because the monk who fills his belly with food and drink provides material for the demon of fornication to do harm. Put on true contrition of heart, and restrain illicit movements of the flesh through tears of prayer. For no one is able rightly to conquer his body who has not first taken up the goad of contrition. But he cannot attain the grace of holy contrition who has despised perfectly to deny himself. Yet whoever has perfectly denied himself for the love of Christ, already overcoming the impulse of the flesh, will more easily obtain contrition. For one who is deadened in will desires nothing carnal, nothing base. But who among us, brothers, would dare to say that he has attained this grace of mortification? Who among us does not feel himself weak? We run in the stadium of spiritual warfare, and in daily combat we fight against vices. We have received no assurance of peace, even if at times we have peace. The more familiar to us the weakness of the flesh is, the harsher are the battles of chastity. Therefore we must keep greatest watchfulness when we carry about a familiar enemy and endure, as it were, a domestic instigator of evil.

Therefore as long as we live in this mortality, let us always fear the strongest enemies of chastity. Let us avoid everything that excites lust; let us flee as much as we can from sloth and idleness. For satiety of bread and idleness are truly the strongest enemies of chastity; unless they are driven far away by abstinence and by the work of salutary exercise, they threaten chastity with imminent shipwreck. Guard your hearts with watchful keeping, O dearest ones, and always keep watch in mind against your adversary. Arm yourselves with the breastplate of perfect faith in Jesus Christ, so that in work and belief you may be true Christians. Always be of one mind in brotherly charity, and in the observance of true regular discipline be concordant without pretense. Love fasting and continence; meditate without interruption. Flee idleness as a pernicious plague of the soul, and exercise yourselves continually in holy works. Love silence, and do not flee monastic solitude, if you desire to obtain true purity of mind and body. Keep continual guard over your eyes, ears, and speech, lest sin enter the soul through the windows of the body. Flee conversations with women, and avoid even the touch of holy women as you would a burning fire. No one safely looks willingly on what brings the sting of death to the covetous.

Always behold our Lord Jesus Christ dying for you with the faithful eyes of the mind through love; be occupied every day in his saving evangelical doctrine; give humble thanks for his passion and death alike. If you do these things with persevering continuance, in true simplicity of heart, until death, we trust that you will be rewarded in the company of the saints.

*On the brotherly charity of monks. All things, XXII.*

Let us hear, my dearest brothers, our Lord Jesus Christ commanding in the holy Gospel: Whatever you wish people to do to you, do the same also to them. This is indeed a brief but solid commendation of brotherly charity, without which no one can ascend to the love of God. For no one can love God who is convicted of not loving his fellow Christian. Hence the blessed apostle John says: Everyone who hates his brother is a murderer; and you know that no murderer has eternal life abiding in him. Therefore if anyone says, “I love God,” and hates his brother, he is a liar. For this reason Paul, the lover of Christ, exhorts us, saying: Bear one another’s burdens, and so you will fulfill the law of Christ. And Peter, the prince of the apostles, admonished us in this way: Before all things, he says, have constant mutual charity among yourselves, because charity

[segment 102]
Homily XXI. Fol. LI.

For unless the mind has first been blinded, the will does not yield to the demon. The spirit of fornication first gravely darkens the mind; afterward it fights until it has hurled it, blinded, into sin. But afterward, when it has cast the monk down into the mire of unspeakable crime, it obstructs him in two ways, lest he rise again to repentance. For either it subtly tries to make light of the gravity of the sin, so that he may not fear a great penalty, or it brings the severity of judgment back to the fallen man’s memory, so that he may fall into the pit of despair. By these snares the sinful soul is caught; and the longer it has lain in the depth of misery, the more hardened it becomes.

Flee idleness, O cloistered men, and avoid gluttony and the excess of the belly with the greatest care, because the monk who stuffs his belly with food and drink provides the demon of fornication with matter for doing harm. Put on true contrition of heart, and restrain the illicit motions of the flesh through tears of prayer. For no one is able rightly to conquer his body who has not first taken up the goad of contrition. But he cannot attain the grace of holy contrition who has not first learned perfectly to despise and deny himself. Whoever, however, has perfectly denied himself for the love of Christ, already overcoming the assaults of the flesh, will more easily obtain the grace of contrition. For he who has been mortified in will desires nothing carnal, nothing base.

But who among us, brothers, would dare say that he has reached this grace of mortification? Which of us does not know himself to be weak? We run in the arena of spiritual warfare, and in daily combat we fight against vices. We have received no assurance of peace, even if at times we have peace. The more familiar to us is the weakness of the flesh, the harder are the battles of chastity. Here we must be especially watchful, since we carry about a familiar enemy and endure, as it were, a domestic instigator of evil. Therefore, so long as we live in this mortality, let us always fear the corruptor of chastity. Let us beware of everything that arouses lust, and as much as we can, let us flee sloth and idleness. For fullness of bread and idleness are the strongest enemies of true chastity; unless they have been driven far away by abstinence and the labor of salutary exercise, they threaten immediate shipwreck to chastity.

Keep your hearts with every guard, O my dearest brothers, and always keep watch in spirit against your adversary, who never sleeps. Arm yourselves with the breastplate of perfect faith in Jesus Christ, so that in deed and belief you may be true Christians. Always be of one mind in brotherly charity, and be united without pretense in the true observance of regular discipline. Love fasting and continence; pray without ceasing. Flee idleness as the pernicious plague of the soul, and continually exercise yourselves in holy works. Love silence, and do not flee monastic solitude, if you desire to obtain true purity of mind and body. Keep continual guard over your eyes, ears, and speech, lest death enter the soul through the windows. Flee conversations with women, and avoid even the touch of holy women as the burning of fire. No one safely looks willingly upon what brings the sting of death to the desirous man. Always behold with faithful eyes of the mind, through love, our Lord Jesus Christ dying for you on the cross; occupy yourselves daily in a salutary way with his evangelical teaching; give humble thanks for his passion and likewise for his death. If you do these things with continued perseverance in true simplicity of heart until death, we trust that you will be rewarded with the fellowship of the saints.

On Brotherly Charity among Monks.
Homily XXIII.

Let us hear, my dearest brothers, our Lord Jesus Christ commanding in the holy Gospel: Whatever you wish men to do to you, do the same also to them. This is indeed a brief but solid admonition of brotherly charity, without which no one can ascend to the love of God. For no one can love God who is shown not to love his fellow Christian. Hence the blessed apostle John says: Everyone who hates his brother is a murderer, and you know that every murderer does not have eternal life abiding in him. Hence, if anyone has said, “I love God,” and hates his brother, he is a liar. Therefore Paul, the holy lover of Christ, exhorts us, saying: Bear one another’s burdens, and so you will fulfill the law of Christ. And Peter, prince of the apostles, admonished us thus: Before all things, he says, have continual mutual charity among yourselves, because charity covers a multitude of sins.

[segment 103]
--- Page 112 ---

[segment 104]
--- Page 113 ---

On the brotherly charity of monks.

What is immortal is what one is bound to love in another, and the regard of charity must be directed toward the soul rather than toward the body. Since by the use of reason and understanding we are distinguished from brute animals, let the gaze of our love be referred to the mind, by which we understand, not to the body, which we have in common with beasts. And let all affections of the flesh be far from holy monks, who are not ignorant of the mutual and sincere rule of charity. Let love toward one another be pure, upright, and conceived from the fear of God alone, so that no advantage of any corruptible thing may intervene, but the simple intention of charity may remain.

True charity, simple and pure, does not seek the byways of evil conspiracies, nor arm itself with the murmur of contention for plots of iniquity. Whoever possesses the true charity of brotherhood in his heart keeps peace in all things and raises no matter or causes of destructive dissension. Charity, rendering what is uniquely its own, does injury to no one, and whatever it does not wish done to itself, it transfers to no one else’s person.

Do not suppose that you have true brotherly charity when you love one another for the sake of the flesh or for the advantage of any temporal thing; for where the cause of love is not the Lord, the affection of the one who loves is always either partly or wholly vicious. But he will rightly love his brother who loves him for the love of God and because of the common sharing in the kingdom which we await.

Therefore, as Saint John the bishop says, we have been baptized into one body of the Church, so that we may not be dissolved from that harmony and friendship toward one another which belongs to one body, but may preserve charity and mutual agreement perpetually. Just as no one outside the unity of the catholic Church can obtain salvation, so it is believed that no one can be saved without brotherly charity.

Charity, then, is necessary for you before all things for salvation; without it the remaining works of the virtues are altogether fruitless. For there can be no good, no saving merit for life, that has not taken its origin from it. Charity is a certain road to the compendium of virtue; if anyone enters upon it by the straight path, he will need no guide to direct him. Pure, simple, and upright charity is not accustomed to injure a neighbor or to inflict evil on anyone.

Charity is accustomed to expend upon all the affection which it grants to its own interests, and whatever it hopes will conduce to its own salvation, it desires this to be common to all people in Christ Jesus. Charity performs the office of love without any disturbance, and although it loves all people most greatly, it is nevertheless provoked for no one into bitter zeal. It is always solid, always constant, always stable and firm, because it is neither touched by passions nor agitated by movements of unrest. It presents itself as common to all, and so bestows its love upon each that it seems to view its own things as belonging to all.

In those things that pertain to the reason of the mind and soul, charity does not neglect itself; but in those things that concern the advantage of the body, it often sets the causes of others before its own utilities. Great indeed is the command of true charity, which compels a person to make another’s misfortune his own.

And so you, my brothers, who have solemnly pledged yourselves to Christ’s service, who through the steep path of obedience pant after eternal life: if you do not wish to run in vain in the arena of so laborious a contest, take care before all things, we exhort you, always to preserve brotherly charity inviolate within yourselves. For unless you have sincere and perfect charity toward one another, you labor without fruit in regular observance. Abstinence from meats will confer nothing upon you for the salvation of souls; the renunciation of all private property will profit nothing toward obtaining eternal happiness, if brotherly love does not hold a place in your hearts.

Desiring, therefore, to be saved with Christ in truth, love one another mutually; and, as Christ loved you, so we beg you to love one another. Whatever each one of you desires to be done for himself, let him, without envy and for the love of God, always bestow that very thing upon his brother. Let divine love conquer among you all quarrels of the flesh and all contentious disputes, and let destructive dissension of the flesh find no place where the fullness of brotherly charity ought continually to dwell.

Unless all of you in the monastery are united according to the law of Christian institution, you must always necessarily abstain from injuring one another. But if through contentions and insults you wound one another mutually, you are certainly neither monks nor true Christians. For it is fitting that all cloistered men and monks be outstanding in the observance of charity, and be far removed from envy, hatred, and rancor. Therefore let the monk remain stable in the purpose of holiness unto death; let him be faithful in the service of God, so that also in love

[segment 105]
--- Page 114 ---

Omnia .xxiii. Fo. Liiii.

of the Creator he may sweetly burn, and never depart from purity of conscience. For whoever perfectly loves the Lord God with his whole heart, and preserves his conscience clean and pure from every pollution of sin, always shows sincere charity to his neighbor. But the one whom envy burns, or whom hatred stirs up against a brother, is plainly shown neither to love God truly nor to have a conscience pure from sin. Therefore everyone who pursues his brother with hatred and indignation is neither a true monk nor worthy of the name Christian, but by merit and way of life a minister and companion of demons.

Far, then, from this holy congregation of yours let hatreds depart. Let there be among you no destructive contests of contention. Let no zeal of bitterness be found here, and let no word of displeasing elation be heard. Let the concord of brotherly unity flourish here continually; let the innocence of Christian purity adorn the consciences of each, so that we may live in this brief time in such a way that after death we may glory with Christ in eternity.

Omnia .xxiiij.

Nothing is so just, nothing so holy, nothing in human actions can be so honorable, as always to love God above all things with the whole heart, the whole mind, and the whole strength, and for love of him to fear neither death nor any kinds of punishments. There is no greater cause for any action in us than divine love; for since God is the author of all things, nothing is more justly loved than he, and since he is the greatest of all goods, nothing is loved more fruitfully than he.

God first loved us: we were the lowest, he the greatest; we were little, he almighty; we were weak. Therefore, my brothers, let us love the Lord God with our whole heart, whole soul, and whole strength, by whom we are most sweetly loved, who not only made us in his image and likeness, but, what is the proof of the greatest love, mercifully called us to the kingdom of eternal happiness through the teaching and death of his only-begotten Son, our Lord Jesus Christ.

No bodily mother ever loved her only son so tenderly, sweetly, and strongly as almighty God loved us, who not only wished to become man for our instruction, but also deigned to undergo death in the flesh he had assumed for our salvation. No greater love ever existed, if we rightly consider who it was who endured such things for us. For love of us, he who was free from the debt of death dies voluntarily; and he who was always immune from every sin, innocent and just, is willingly condemned for our sins.

By the most just reasoning, then, we are commanded to love the Lord our God with our whole heart, who created us from his goodness alone when we did not exist, and mercifully delivered us when we had perished. The love of God is the greatest of all virtues, because whatever good, whatever virtue is done in this world, if it is not done from charity toward God, it is deprived of reward. Charity toward God purifies the conscience from every fault, since he who truly loves the Lord his God with his whole soul, his whole heart, and all his strength undoubtedly grants no place in his conscience to vices. For the heart which the love of God occupies is entered by no uncleanness of vices.

The love of God is an unfailing treasure: whoever possesses it is richer than all the wealthy of the world; whoever lacks it is more wretched than all the poor. Hence the holy apostle of Christ says: To those who love God, all things work together for good, because nothing harms those who love him.

Happy is the person in whose heart, besides the love of Christ, no bond of earthly affection dwells; who sighs after the remembrance of no human being; in whose conscience purity resides, and divine love always burns most ardently. Love him, my brothers, who for love of you descended into the womb of the Virgin. Always carry him in your hearts, who by his death opened heaven for you. Let the love of Christ, strong as death, be in you at all times, brothers, so that your life may be conformed to Christ's teaching, and, if ever it becomes necessary, you may not fear to die for love of him, just as he first deigned calmly and kindly to undergo death for us.

For the love of Christ makes the monk near to God, and whoever is always fervent in the charity of the Lord will obtain the fellowship of the angels. The love of Christ makes a person very strong against all assaults of demons, and no less in adversities than

[segment 106]
*On a Man’s Charity Toward God.*

in prosperity, long-suffering and at the same time tranquil. The love of Christ teaches one truly to despise the world, and to reckon all its delights and all the consolations of the flesh as nothing. The love of Christ quickly suppresses the illicit motions of the flesh and brings the purest chastity into the mind. The love of Christ sweetly lifts the mind with desire toward heavenly things, and, separated from earthly things, preserves it in spiritual exercise.

The love of God lightens everything hard and rough, because where Christ is truly loved, tribulations and any adversities at all are counted as delights. The love of God is always diligent in good works and is never idle. The love of God despises present things and strongly raises the mind to eternal things. The love of God neither seeks honors nor pursues riches, but, joined to Christ by desire, covets absolutely nothing except him. The love of God covers a multitude of sins and remits all offenses to the penitent. The love of God at once justifies the impious; and the more love grows, the more perfectly, swiftly, and strongly perfection rises up.

The love of God is a tower of strength, which all ministers of diabolical wickedness cannot approach. The love of God is the consummation of all righteousness and the end of monastic discipline. The love of God perfects the monk and makes him consummate in all good works. The love of God is the sweet yoke of inward sweetness, by which every foulness of earthly corruption is driven from the mind. The love of God honors souls and makes them pure and immaculate from all stains. The love of God perfectly illumines the mind in every knowledge of heavenly honors, and purges its affection from all unclean vanities.

The love of God makes those who truly possess it most learned, and wonderfully instructed in every Scripture. The love of God renders the monk fearsome to all enemies, and grants angelic power to those who persevere. The love of God defends a man in dangers, because whoever truly loves God fears no hostile things. The love of God makes the one who possesses it secure and safe from demons at the hour of death, and joins the soul to the heavenly companies. The love of God leads its possessor back to the heavenly fatherland, and grants eternal glory to those who love God.

The love of God is the greatest of all honorable works and the one foundation of all virtues in the faith of Christ. The love of God is the best ointment, in which all wounds of languishing souls are healed. The love of God is an abundant remedy for poverty and want, and a consolation more effective than every possession of riches. The love of Christ far surpasses all teaching of this world, and instructs those who possess it most perfectly in every knowledge necessary for salvation. The love of God prepares a delightful dwelling in the heart of man for the Holy Spirit, and preserves those who persevere in all purity.

The love of God is the swiftest of all teachers, because what carnal persons learn over time and with much study, this the Holy Spirit gives in a moment to those who love God. The love of God gives knowledge to little ones and makes rustics more learned than the educated. The love of God is stronger than all things and by its power rules over all things. The love of God commands demons by a single word, and drives them by the sign of the cross from the bodies of the possessed. The love of God bestowed eternal blessedness on the holy spirits in heaven, whom the grace of the Creator confirmed in love.

The love of God beatifies honorable men baptized in the faith of Jesus Christ in eternal happiness, because no one could ever reach the fellowship of angels unless the persevering love of God were proved to have inflamed him. For the love of God alone makes works both good and meritorious, because God receives from us only that gift of a good work which the flame of charity has made worthy in his sight. Whatever is done outside charity is judged unworthy in God’s sight.

Now attend, my brothers, to our admonitions, and consider carefully how necessary it is for you to love God above all things, if you desire to reach his kingdom and to triumph happily with him forever. Let none of you deceive his soul with empty words, so that he believes he truly loves God while he either scorns or neglects to keep his commandments. You have heard the Lord Jesus saying in the holy Gospel: “Whoever loves me will keep my words.” And, as the blessed pope Gregory says, the performance of the commanded work is the proof of the love that has been enjoined. For then we truly love the Lord our God with our whole heart, when, having despised worldly pleasures, we perfectly keep all his commandments even unto death. Likewise, then we love him above all things, when for love of him, not according to the flesh but according to

[segment 107]
--- Page 116 ---

[segment 108]
--- Page 117 ---

*On a person’s charity toward God.*

loved whenever, besides him, something is loved together with him. The love of God then proceeds rightly when nothing is mixed with it that could weaken it. Let each of you strive, as much as he can, to love the Lord his God above all things with the whole strength of his mind, because love in God alone is what will open for you the gate of paradise. For the more ardently you now love God, the greater the glory with which you will be received in the heavenly homeland.

Happy is that monk in whose conscience, besides the love of Christ, no image of any occupation resides; who, waking, thinks of nothing but Christ; sleeping, dreams of Christ; living and dying, sighs most steadfastly for Christ. O how well and securely he dies who has always been diligent in the exercise of divine love, and at all times zealous in observing the commandments of God. For only the love of God is admitted into the kingdom of heaven; and the more fervent the mind has been in loving, the more blessed it will become in glory. He who loves much receives much; and he who now loves less will then also receive less in glory. Therefore, as much as each one can, let him love with his whole heart, with his whole soul, and with his whole strength, because our God is not to be loved tepidly or sluggishly, but fervently, wisely, and sweetly.

Now the love of God grows and is increased by spiritual exercises, just as a burning fire spreads by the addition of wood. For if the wood is withdrawn, the fire is extinguished; and if spiritual exercises are interrupted, charity is soon shipwrecked. But true love of God is never idle; rather, at every time it is delightfully occupied in those things that nourish and preserve charity. For the lover of God never forgets his beloved, but always meditates on his presence and beauty; and, in his prayers, most devoutly marvels at Jesus speaking to him. The true lover of Christ meditates day and night on the law of the Lord, and does with the greatest diligence everything he understands to be pleasing to God.

O how sweetly he burns whom the love of God has truly inflamed; how pleasantly he glows, whose mind the flame of the Holy Spirit has set on fire. Blessed is that monk who, full of divine love, withdraws himself wholly from earthly vanities and rests in the shadow of heavenly desire. O if only we had a love toward God as strong, whole, and vehement as mad and foolish lovers have for their corruptible mistresses; out of desire for them, empty men often become disgracefully insane and fear no scandals of this world. But the love of God is stronger than all things, if it has truly been rooted in us.

Whoever truly loves the Lord God always keeps in his memory his most bitter passion, because nothing causes the love of God so perfectly in the human heart, nothing so induces true compunction, as the never-interrupted remembrance of God’s benefits. Therefore let the written sequence of Christ’s passion always be in your memory, and let this word never depart from your mouth: O crucified Jesus, my love, by the power of your most innocent death, have mercy on me, and make me your faithful servant.

*On Serenity of Conscience.*

*Homily XXIV.*

With great solicitude, dearest brothers, we must keep watch, and with continual meditation take care that we always preserve our conscience, whose testimony we cannot escape, clean and unstained from every contagion of sin. For there is nothing more beautiful, nothing more pleasant, nothing finally more precious than a good conscience; when the mind has within itself a manifest testimony of rectitude, it suffers no gnawing of destructive unrest. An upright and good conscience is an incredible exultation of the mind and a sure consolation in every adversity. The monk who has obtained the testimony of a good conscience neither refuses to live nor fears the coming of future death.

A good conscience is always joyful, always glad, always fearless, and truly tranquil in the peace of Jesus Christ. In a good conscience there is always peace, piety, goodness, light, virtue, honor, joy, gladness, sweetness, and delight without dissipation. Conscience is the storehouse of the soul, since whatever is placed in it is reserved for the provision of the life to come. A good conscience is the best treasure; if anyone neglects to have it, he must be judged the most wretched and unhappy of all people. A good conscience is the dwelling place of the Holy Spirit and the earthly paradise of all graces. A good conscience does not fear the slanders of the wicked, and a mind conscious of rectitude despises the lies of rumor. A good conscience is always cheerful, and he who

[segment 109]
*On the charity of a person toward God.*

[unclear] is loved whenever anything apart from him is loved together with him. Love of God then advances rightly when nothing is allowed into it that could weaken it. Let each of you, as much as he can, strive with the whole strength of his mind to love the Lord his God above all things, because love of God alone is what will open for you the gate of paradise. For the more ardently you now love God, the greater the glory in which you will be received in the heavenly homeland.

Happy is that monk in whose conscience, apart from the love of Christ, there remains no imagining of any other occupation; who, waking, thinks of nothing but Christ; sleeping, dreams of Christ; living and dying, sighs most steadfastly after Christ. O how well and securely he dies who has always been diligent in the exercise of divine love, and at all times zealous in observing the commandments of God. For love of God alone is admitted into the kingdom of heaven; and the more fervent the mind has been in loving, the greater its reward will be in the kingdom of heaven. He who loves much receives much; and he who now loves less will also then receive less in glory. Therefore let each person, as much as he can, love with his whole heart and with his whole soul and with all his strength, because our God is not to be loved lukewarmly or lightly, but fervently, wisely, and sweetly.

The love of God grows and is increased by spiritual exercises, just as a burning fire spreads when wood is supplied. For truly, if the wood is taken away, the fire is also extinguished; if spiritual exercises themselves are interrupted, charity is soon shipwrecked. True love of God is never idle, but at every time is delightfully occupied in those things that nourish and preserve charity. The lover of his beloved God never forgets him, but always meditates on his presence and beauty; and now, in most devout prayers, he speaks to Jesus; now, in the holy Scriptures, he marvels at him speaking to himself. The true lover of Christ meditates day and night on the law of the Lord, and does with the greatest diligence all the things that he understands to be pleasing to God.

O how sweetly he burns whom God has truly set aflame; how pleasantly he glows whose mind the flame of the Holy Spirit kindles. Blessed is that monk who, full of divine love, wholly withdraws himself from earthly vanities and rests in the unity of heavenly desire. O if only we had love toward God as strong, whole, and vehement as mad and foolish lovers have toward their corruptible mistresses, from desire for whom they are often shamefully driven mad, and fear no scandals of this world. But the love of God is stronger than all things, if it has truly been rooted in us. Whoever truly loves the Lord God always keeps his most bitter passion in his memory, because nothing so produces perfect love of the Lord God in the heart, nothing so truly brings on compunction, as the never-interrupted recollection of God’s benefits. Therefore let the written sequence of Christ’s passion always be in your memory, and let this word never depart from your mouth: O crucified Jesus, my love, by the power of your most innocent death have mercy on me and make me your faithful servant.

*On Serenity of Conscience.*

*Homily 24.*

Most beloved brothers, we must keep watch with great solicitude, and must take care with continual meditation that we always guard our conscience, whose testimony we cannot escape, clean from every contagion of sin and unstained by intimate custody. Nothing is more beautiful, nothing more pleasant, nothing finally more precious than a good conscience, when the mind has in itself a manifest testimony of rectitude; it suffers no remorse of destructive unrest. An upright and good conscience is an incredible exultation of the mind and a sure consolation in every adversity. The monk who has obtained the testimony of a good conscience neither refuses to live nor fears the coming of future death. A good conscience is always glad, always joyful, always fearless, and truly tranquil in the peace of Jesus Christ. In a good conscience there is always peace, piety, goodness, light, virtue, honor, joy, gladness, sweetness, and delight without dissipation. Conscience is the treasury of the soul, because whatever is placed in it is reserved as provision for the life to come. A good conscience is the best treasure; if anyone has neglected to have it, he must be judged the most wretched and unhappy of all men. A good conscience is the dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit and the earthly paradise of all grace. A good conscience does not fear the detractions of the impious, and a mind conscious of rectitude scorns the lies of rumor. A good conscience is always cheerful, because

[segment 110]
--- Page 119 ---

On the Serenity of Conscience.

From this there is no doubt that many have erroneous consciences, because they love the pleasures of their own flesh too much. Every proud, lustful, unclean person, and everyone entangled in great or public crimes, clouds his own conscience with deliberate perversity; and how erroneously he thinks about the future repayment of merits he often makes clear both by words and by deeds. A malicious affection perverts the judgment of uprightness, and the disordered desire of the flesh clouds the understanding.

Sometimes, moreover, excessive and immoderate faintheartedness makes the conscience err; this evil is born partly from ignorance, and partly generated from one’s own sensuality. In such scrupulosity, however, the monk should follow the counsel either of the abbot or of some more experienced elder, and should not rashly defend the opinion of his own head. Excessive scrupulosity is dangerous, because it hurls the conscience into error and unhappily trips up many. For everything that is done against conscience is said to build toward hell.

Excessive perplexity of mind has also harmed many and has cast them headlong into various errors. But he who is truly a humble monk brings himself to the examination of his ruler. For whoever, in doubtful matters that arise, makes use of the counsel of his superior is not easily tripped up by the snares of demons. Indeed, the more the consciences of monks have been exposed to the examination of a wise prelate, the more secure they will be, and the more clearly they will shine.

Therefore, dearest brothers, care must be taken above all that you have consciences free from every error, then pure from all vice, and finally adorned with the holy works of all the virtues. Let the monk, then, who desires to advance in truth guard his heart with every vigilance, and as far as possible place a barrier before his senses, nor allow anything deadly to enter. But if, by any occasion, he recognizes that he has fallen into some sin, let him at once strive to erase it by true repentance, and not grant time to the wicked seed in which it may grow stronger for the worse. While the wound is small, let a remedy be applied, for if it is neglected it grows without measure.

Therefore, my brothers, resist strongly the first movements of demonic suggestion, and overcome the enemy while he is small and weak. But if the sensuality of the body has become altogether wanton, let it be brought back to obedience by harsh mortification, just as we know our holy father Benedict did, when he rooted out the slippery insolence of the flesh with the stings of nettles and brambles. Then, if you diligently call back to memory the dreadful future punishments of the wicked, you will easily overcome the illicit movements of the flesh. For there is no one who does not fear pain more than he loves pleasure.

You should also often recall to mind the joys of heavenly happiness, to which you desire to come; for if you think on them faithfully, you will strongly quench the fire of the flesh. Whatever delights in the world is corruptible and momentary; but the joys of eternal delight remain without end forever. It is foolish, therefore, and a matter of extreme madness, to choose a passing advantage and to despise that good which remains forever. He is judged foolish among men who prefers dung to gold; but before God he is held most foolish who puts temporal pleasure before everlasting goods.

He who loves a serene conscience should flee idleness, since, as Scripture says, every idle person is in desire, and idleness has taught much malice. The soul must be occupied with good exercises, and the fire of holy conduct, which burns with charity, must be nourished by continual practice. For the idle man lives neither for himself nor for his neighbor, but is always given over to his belly or to sleep; he opens his mind to vices, and keeps it closed and empty of virtues. Idleness, the kindler of lust, quickly extinguishes the fervor of spiritual devotion.

Abstinence also is especially necessary for the monk above all, so that his conscience may remain clean. For, as Saint Jerome says, a full belly easily foams into lust, and he who feeds his servant delicately will find him rebellious. The lover of serenity of conscience should be constant in holy prayers, since, as dogs flee a stone, so demons flee devout and continual prayer. Let him also diligently beware of acedia, together with sadness of mind; and as soon as he feels any temptation creeping in, let him have recourse to the consolation of the Scriptures. Truly, the mind that the word of God has filled, base thought will not enter.

Whatever we do, whatever we think, whatever we undertake, let us always know that the presence of God is there, and we shall easily overcome disordered delight. A good conscience never forgets the presence of God, but always meditates that its judge is present.

[segment 111]
[Page 120]

Hence there is no doubt that it comes about that many have erroneous consciences, because they love too much the pleasures of their own flesh. Every proud, lustful, and impure person, and anyone entangled in great or public crimes, clouds his conscience by deliberate perversity; and how erroneously he thinks about the future repayment of merits he frequently shows both by words and by deeds. A malicious affection perverts the judgment of rectitude, and the disordered concupiscence of the flesh clouds the understanding. Sometimes, moreover, excessive and immoderate faint-heartedness makes the conscience err; this evil is born partly from ignorance and partly from one’s own sensuality.

In this scrupulosity, however, let the monk follow the counsel either of the abbot or of some more experienced elder, and let him not rashly defend the opinion of his own head. Excessive scrupulosity is dangerous, because it casts the conscience into error and unhappily causes many to stumble. For everything that is done against conscience is said to build toward hell. Excessive perplexity of mind has also harmed many and driven them headlong into various errors. But he who is truly a monk submits himself in humility to the examination of his rector. For whoever, in doubts that arise, makes use of his superior’s counsel is not easily overthrown by the snares of demons. For the more the consciences of monks have been exposed to the examination of a wise prelate, the more secure and more brightly shining they will gleam.

Therefore, dearest brothers, care must be taken first of all that you have consciences free from every error; then pure from all vices; and finally adorned with the holy works of all the virtues. Therefore let the monk who desires to advance in truth guard his heart with every watchfulness; and, as far as can be done, let him place a barrier before his senses and not permit anything deadly to enter. But if by any occasion he recognizes that he has fallen into some sin, let him at once strive to wipe it away by true penance, and let him not grant time to the wicked seed in which it may grow stronger for the worse. While the wound is small, let a remedy be applied; for if it is neglected, it grows beyond measure.

Therefore, my brothers, strongly resist the first motions of demonic suggestion, and overcome the enemy while he is small and weak. But if the sensuality of the body has become altogether wanton, let it be brought back to obedience by harsh mortification, just as we know our holy father Benedict did when he rooted out the slippery wantonness of the flesh by the prickings of nettles and thorns. Then, too, if you diligently recall to memory the dreadful future punishments of the impious, you will easily overcome the unlawful motions of the flesh. For there is no one who does not fear pain more than he loves pleasure.

Also bring often to mind the joys of heavenly blessedness, to which you desire to come; for if you faithfully consider them, you will strongly lessen the fire of the flesh. Whatever delights in the world is corruptible and momentary; but the joys of eternal pleasure remain without end forever. It is therefore foolish, and a matter of extreme madness, to choose a passing advantage and to despise that good which remains eternally. He is judged foolish among men who prefers dross to gold; but he is held most senseless before God who sets temporal pleasure before everlasting goods.

He who loves a serene conscience should flee idleness, since, as Scripture says, every idle man is in desires, and idleness has taught much malice. The soul must be occupied with good exercises, and the fire of holy conduct, which burns with charity, must be nourished by continual use. For the idle man lives neither for himself nor for his neighbor, but is always given over to the belly or to sleep; he opens his mind to vice and keeps it closed and empty of virtues. Idleness, the kindler of lust, quickly extinguishes the fervors of spiritual devotion.

Abstinence also is especially necessary for a monk, so that his conscience may remain clean. For, as Saint Jerome says, a filled belly easily foams into lust; and he who nourishes his servant delicately will find him disobedient. The lover of serenity of conscience should be constant in holy prayers, since, as dogs flee a stone, so demons flee devout and continual prayer. Let him also carefully beware of acedia together with sadness of mind; and as soon as he senses some temptation creeping in, let him have recourse to the consolation of the Scriptures. Indeed, no foul thought will enter the mind which the word of God has filled. Whatever we do, whatever we think, whatever we perform, let us always know that the presence of God is at hand, and we shall easily overcome disordered delight. A good conscience never forgets the presence of God, but always meditates that its judge is present.

[segment 112]
Homily XXV. Fol. LVII.

Act now, my brothers, so that you may have consciences pure from every crime; and if anyone feels himself burdened in some fault, let him return without delay through true penitence to necessary purity. For unless you have consciences clean and good at all times, you live as apostates, not as monks.

On Continual Preparation for Death. Homily XXV.

The highest philosophy for cloistered religious, my dearest brothers, is continual meditation on death. Hence a certain wise man, considering the usefulness of this philosophy, admonished every mortal, saying: “Remember your last things, and you will not sin forever.” Meditation on death draws people back from the pleasures of the flesh and brings contempt for all corruptible things. Meditation on death despises present things through voluntary poverty and drives the mind toward desire for future things. Meditation on death banishes pride far away and plants true humility in the heart of the one meditating. Meditation on death makes the monk solicitous about the observance of regular discipline and never permits him to be idle. Meditation on death cuts away all the vanities of the world and preserves the mind in the highest temperance. Meditation on death makes one despise the vain and transitory honors of the world and think of nothing except the future judgment of God. Meditation on death does not fear the poverty of this life, because it longs with its whole desire for everlasting riches. Meditation on death most patiently tolerates all the adversities of this world for the love of Christ, because it hopes for nothing except the heavenly reward. Meditation on death makes the monk humble and obedient in all the commandments of righteousness. Meditation on death makes the monk quiet and stable in the congregation, and well concordant with all in Christ. Meditation on death makes the monk quiet and peaceable, and tranquil without any disturbance of mind. Meditation on death drives out all dissoluteness and frivolity, and preserves the monk mature and wholesomely sorrowful in all things. Meditation on death makes the monk solitary and continually devout in holy prayers. Meditation on death hastens penitence and brings true compunction. Meditation on death withdraws desire from every vanity and keeps the conscience serene and most pure. Meditation on death makes a person despise all the consolations of this world and prepare daily for departure. Meditation on death teaches one to flee idleness and makes a person keep watch continually in the fear of the Lord. Meditation on death does injury to no one, because it always fears the strict accounting of judgment. Meditation on death softens anger and all disordered motions, because when a person thinks that he will soon die, nothing external disturbs the mind. Meditation on death makes the heart contrite and humble, because intent remembrance of future things permits no one to be proud. Continual meditation on death makes departure safe at the last, because it has long made the one who has meditated prepared and ready. Meditation on death especially befits cloistered religious and monks, whom renunciation of all earthly things and regular profession itself have already made dead and buried to the world. Meditation on death makes the monk run prudently in the course of spiritual warfare and daily renew the purpose of holy fervor. Continual meditation on death is very useful to cloistered religious, because it powerfully represses the vices of the flesh and grants no entrance to perverse thoughts of the mind. For the holy bishop Augustine says: Fear of future death necessarily shakes the mind and, like a nail of the flesh, fastens all the motions of pride to the wood of the cross. And the holy champion of the Church, Jerome, says: Nothing will benefit you so much for temperance in all things as frequent thought of the shortness of life; its end is certain, but nothing is more uncertain than its hour. Therefore meditation on death corrects all intemperance and sets in order the character of the one meditating, stable in beautiful variety. Meditation on death gives a person true wisdom and perfects the highest philosophy among cloistered religious. For Plato too held the opinion that the whole life of wise men is a meditation on death, because we are all born on this condition, that we should depart again from here through death. For Scripture says: We all die, and like waters we flow down into the earth, which do not return any more. And the holy apostle of Christ, Paul, says: It is appointed for men to die once

[segment 113]
[Page 122]

On continual preparation for death:

The continual meditation on necessary death makes a man never trust in earthly things, but await his own departure in every place and time with foresight. Meditation on death always expects, in every place, the end of life, because it is wholly ignorant of the moment of dying. Whoever meditates daily on the coming of future death disposes himself for dying, in every circumstance, with all the diligence he can. Nothing is more certain for us than death; all of us are ignorant of the hour of dying. Therefore, brothers, as earnestly as we can we admonish your charity that you always think upon death, and that with full foresight you diligently prepare yourselves for dying, so that when the hour of departure comes you may not fear.

Let no one promise himself a long life in this world, because nothing deceives mortals so much as hope of longer life. What does length of age accomplish in men except that it makes them liable for more offenses, and always less disposed for dying? The longer we live in this world, the more we fear death. Longer life accumulates greater debts of spiritual obligation, so that a man always fears the more to come before the sight of the strict judge, the more he recognizes himself bound before the Lord to render account in many matters. For the soul leaves its body uneasily when it is stirred there by the heavier stings of its conscience. But he who is pure from every evil of iniquity, and adorned for salvation with the necessary beauty of virtues, does not fear to die, because through death he goes to eternal life.

Therefore, O monk, live now as you wish to die, because death cannot be evil when the integrity of a good life has gone before. Whoever loves the Lord Jesus above all things, and despises all the goods of this world for love of him, and truly keeps his words, dies securely. He departs securely from this life who always lives in the love of God without stain of conscience. He dies securely whose life is defiled by none of the pleasures of this world. The death of such a one is a most sweet sleep, and a tranquil entrance into eternal life. But the death of sinners is very evil, for an evil life, contrary to the divine ordinances, has gone before. For one who lives in sins, contrary to God, death becomes a dreadful entrance into hell. He who has lived well does not die badly; rarely does he die well who is shown always to have lived badly.

He dies securely who loves God with a pure mind above all things, and keeps himself undefiled by this wicked world at all times. This hour is to be desired by all the upright in heart, because it brings an end to their distress and restores life in heaven. This hour is the abandonment of the body, the escape from a dark prison, and the laying down of a heavy burden. This hour is the end of present evils, the limit of sadness, and the grave of every earthly pleasure. A good life in the faith and charity of Jesus Christ makes this hour precious and most desirable to the servants of God. Now therefore, brothers, struggle to anticipate death with holy works, and by zeal for virtue make pleasant for yourselves what nature has willed to be necessary for all. Remember that we are all necessarily mortal, and that we shall pass from this world when we do not expect it. For, as a certain venerable bishop said: whether we wish it or not, all of us in the very midst of life are borne away, and we are drawn where life, when completed, calls us.

Therefore, brothers, while we have time, let us do whatever good we can, lest, overtaken by death, we grieve too late that we have been too negligent. Time alone is ours; all other things are another’s. The labor of our spiritual warfare, even if it seems somewhat hard to lukewarm minds, is nevertheless very brief, and with life it will quickly be ended; but the reward of good works that will be given to us remains forever. Let meditation on swiftly approaching death never desert you, but let the consideration of the strict judgment, which no one can escape, continually pierce your minds with compunction in the love of God.

Behold how many monks have gone before us in this monastery of Sponheim, living and dying, through almost four hundred years from its first foundation, of whom today no memory at all remains among us. Where then are the brothers who preceded us in this monastery? Just as there is now no memory of them among us, so too, after we have passed away, we shall be in everlasting oblivion among those who come after. Let us think on these things beforehand, dearest brothers, while we live, lest we fall into death unprepared. As we prepare daily for death, so daily, according to our strength, let us make ourselves ready; for he dies securely who in life is adorned with good and holy works.

Let not the hope of longer life deceive you, for the longer we live in this world, the shorter will be the life that remains. Behold, as we tend daily toward death, we die; daily one part of life is taken from us, and the longer our life advances, the shorter the remaining time of living becomes. All the time that we have lived is wholly lost when it has passed; what we live today we divide moment by moment with death; how much time for living remains to us in the future we do not know. Why then do we trust in the vanity of this life, when we are soon to die? Let us continually think, brothers, that we shall soon come to the place where all our elders have gone before us. We are all born under this law: that we may die soon.

Let death seem terrible to him whose whole life is polluted in the pleasure of the flesh and who stores up no good in heaven. But we who place all our hope in Christ Jesus our Savior, and who despise all the pleasures of the world for love of him, why should we fear death? Yet, since death is terrible, indeed most terrible of all terrors, we shall be able to drive out indiscreet fear of dying if we prepare ourselves daily as though we were to die. It is one thing to have fear of death, another to have trembling; for even the Lord Jesus himself felt the trembling of present death, to show that he had taken on the truth of both natures. Therefore continual meditation on death is altogether necessary for us weak ones, that a mind not yet perfected in the charity of Christ may be stirred, as if by a violent impulse, toward the grace of compunction.

For the memory of future death, like a nail driven into wood, pierces the mind with useful fear. The memory of death introduces difficult penitence and true sobriety of mind. The memory of death is necessary for beginners, whose conversion begins from servile fear, but is gradually lifted up through filial fear into charity. The memory of death and of the strict examination of God’s future judgment is especially necessary for those whom evil custom and long habit draw into sin. And if there are among you some who are too lukewarm and negligent in the spiritual warfare, we admonish and exhort them paternally to remember that they are mortal, and thereafter to live in such a way that they do not fear to die. For it is very dangerous to live in a state in which a man would fear to die.

One must live in such a way that we may await death securely; and this can happen only if, preserving ourselves in all purity of mind and body, we continually burn in the exercises of divine charity. Let us live, so far as can be done, innocently, according to the rule of our holy father Benedict, which we have professed, and we shall have good hope that almighty God will have mercy on us, for he does not allow a man of good will to perish. Let us always think of God as present to us everywhere: he sees all things, knows all things, understands all things, rewards all things. And let us live in that purity with which we promised to serve his most holy majesty.

The end of the twenty-fifth homily of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Sponheim, to the monks.

Here ends the first book of exhortations to the monks, written with my own hand, Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Sponheim, on the day of the month of June, in the year of Christians 1486, in the fourth indiction, in the thirty-third year of my age. I dedicated this work to my predecessor, now abbot of Seligenstadt, most dear to me.

[segment 114]
--- Page 123 ---

Letter.

Johannes Trithemius, abbot of the monastery of Sponheim, though unworthy, to the reverend father lord Johannes of Colenhausen, abbot of Seligenstadt and formerly of Sponheim, my very dear predecessor: with prayers, a mind prompt and ready to please.

I recently sent to you, venerable teacher, my first book of exhortations to monks, containing twenty-five homilies. After I learned that it had been approved by your examination, I judged that this second book also, which I now send, could confidently be inscribed to your name. In the former book I gathered, for the instruction of the brothers, twenty-five homilies that I had delivered before them; in this one I have arranged sermons in number according to the order in which they were spoken in their presence.

I do not fear to undergo the examination of your paternity, since I have always had you as a gentle teacher, and I have long found you to be a sincere lover of truth. For you flourish with an intellect both gentle and deep, able easily to discern between true and false, and, where necessity requires it, to correct everything sufficiently. Therefore I pray that, according to the gentleness innate in you, you patiently bear the rudeness of my intellect, for I am both a little child in knowledge of the Scriptures and a novice in the rank of prelates. Practice in the Scriptures will give me facility, as I trust, and continued solicitude will give progress. The management of household affairs and the poverty of the place often draw me back from salutary study and carry my mind elsewhere, contrary to the intention of my will.

Only on feast days and Sundays is leisure granted me for writing; on these, out of reverence for God, I customarily admit no cases involving the peasants. Sometimes also, after matins has been sung according to custom, I am accustomed to devote what remains of the night to the study of letters, and to compose something by lamplight, as far as I can, for the instruction of my brothers. For I desire in all things to follow in your footsteps, and to be diligent in the praiseworthy work of teaching.

I submit these sermons to your examination, and with humble supplication I request that the file of your mastery correct them. I have gathered a few out of many into order, since I did not commit them all to writing, nor did too great an extension of the book seem to me either necessary or useful. For on feast days, when we have respite from the tumult of worldly business, we devote ourselves to reading according to the rule of the Scriptures. And lest I seem to occupy the place of pastor in vain, what I have composed by lamplight at night I am accustomed to speak after None in place of the regular study, and to recite from a little paper before the brothers in chapter. For I consider that this solicitude of mine not only profits the brothers for edification, but also helps me toward skill in composition.

Farewell, venerable teacher, and pray for us. From Sponheim, on the twelfth day of the month of September, in the year of Christians 1486.

[segment 115]
--- Page 124 ---

John Trithemius, abbot of the monastery of Sponheim, though unworthy, to the reverend father Lord Johannes de Colenhausen, abbot of Seligenstadt and formerly of Sponheim, my dearest predecessor: with prayers, a mind ready and prepared to please.

Recently, venerable teacher, I sent you the first book of my Exhortations to Monks, containing twenty-five homilies. After I learned that it had been approved by your examination, I judged that this second book also, which I now send, should confidently be inscribed to your name. In the former book I collected, for the instruction of the brothers, twenty-five homilies which I had delivered before them; in this one I have arranged seven sermons according to the order in which they were spoken in their presence.

I do not fear to undergo the examination of your paternity, since I have always had you as a gentle teacher, and long ago found you to be a sincere lover of truth. For you are vigorous in intellect, both gentle and profound, able easily to distinguish true from false and, where necessity requires, to amend everything sufficiently. Therefore I pray that, according to the gentleness innate in you, you may patiently bear the rudeness of my talent, since I am both a little child in knowledge of the Scriptures and a novice in the order of prelates. Exercise in the Scriptures, I trust, will give me practice, and continued concern for writing will give me progress.

The occupation of household business and the poverty of the place very often draw me back from salutary study and carry my mind elsewhere, against the purpose of my will. Leisure for writing is granted me only on feast days and Sundays; on these, out of reverence for God, I am accustomed to admit no cases of the country people. Sometimes also, after the customary chanting of matins, I am accustomed to spend what remains of the night on the study of letters, and, as far as I can, to work by lamplight on something for the instruction of my brothers.

For I desire in all things to follow your footsteps and, if I can, perpetually to observe the praiseworthy custom of teaching those subject to me which you introduced. I know that I am bound to this by my office; and although I am rude and unlearned, nevertheless I willingly wish, according to my powers, to be diligent both in learning and in teaching. I submit these sermons to your examination, and with humble supplication I ask that the file of your mastery may correct them. I have gathered a few out of many into order, since not all were to be entrusted to writing, nor did an excessive extension of the book seem to me either necessary or useful.

For on feast days, when we have respite from the tumult of worldly business, we devote ourselves to reading according to the rule of the Scriptures. And lest I should seem to occupy the place of shepherd in vain, what I am accustomed to work out by night I am accustomed, after none, in place of the regular study, to speak and to recite from a small sheet before the brothers in chapter. For I find that this concern of mine not only profits the brothers for edification, but also helps me toward practice in composition. Farewell, venerable teacher, and pray for us. From Sponheim, on the twelfth day of the month of September, in the year of the Christians 1486.

[segment 116]
*Sermon I. Folio LIX.*

In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ begins the second book of exhortations to monks, by Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Sponheim, afterwards of [unclear]. How cautiously monks must live: Sermon One.

Many indeed, most beloved brothers, are the things necessary for you to consider in this life, if you desire to arrive at the glory of eternal salvation. For Scripture bears witness that your adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour; and we know that he must be resisted with great vigilance in the faith of our Lord Jesus Christ through works of charity. Therefore let no one presume upon his own strength or trust in vanity, for unless you humbly keep yourselves in the fear of the Lord, you will quickly fall into the snare of the devil. “The greater you are,” Scripture commands, “humble yourself in all things,” for God resists the proud and gives grace to the humble.

A monk ought to be humble toward God and a true despiser of this world. A monk must always be solicitous in the observance of regular discipline, and continually zealous in the fear of the Lord. A monk must be chaste and modest in mind and body alike, so that he may serve God, just as he promised, in all purity. A monk ought not to live according to his own will, but according to the Rule and according to the direction of his pastor, conformed to the norm of cenobitic institution. A monk ought to love busy and holy silence, and to occupy his mind usefully in the reading of the Scriptures. A monk must at all times, even unto death, be solicitous that he may be perfect in the observance of the commandments of God and of the Rule.

But so that you may know what things are necessary for you who live in holy religion, for perfection, I shall describe for you a certain path of virtues, by which one must direct the way to the kingdom of heaven. All the virtues, however, cohere with one another by the bond of kinship, so that he who possesses one perfectly seems to have them all, and he who lacks even one is said to possess none at all.

The first of all virtues necessary for salvation is the most holy Catholic faith, separated from every pollution of error and heresy, and originally rooted in the doctrine of our Lord Jesus Christ. For, as Scripture bears witness, without faith it is impossible to please God; and he who does not believe in the only-begotten Son of God is already judged, and the wrath of God remains upon him. Therefore faith is the origin of all virtues, from which all pursuits of salvation arise.

True and right faith in God begets fear of the Lord, for if we believe that God is just, we shall rightly confess that he is to be feared. Right faith brings forth hope and confidence, because when we believe God to be merciful, we hope that by his goodness we can attain salvation. Faith begets charity, because if we believe God to be the highest good, without doubt we love him. For who would not love what he does not doubt to be the supreme good? Faith is the substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen, and the single foundation of our salvation. Faith joins the soul to God, and through the mediation of charity makes it the friend of the eternal King.

For the most holy bishop Augustine says: There are no greater riches, no treasures, no honors, no substance of this world greater than the Catholic faith, which saves sinful men, enlightens the blind, cures the sick, baptizes catechumens, justifies the faithful, restores penitents, increases the just, crowns martyrs, preserves virgins, widows, and married persons in chaste modesty, ordains clerics, consecrates priests, and places believers with the holy angels in the eternal inheritance of the heavens.

But where sound faith is not, there justice cannot be, because the just man lives by faith; and he who lacks the merit of justice will have no portion with Christ. Faith is the beginning of human salvation, without which no one can reach the number of the children of God, nor possess any share in the kingdom of God. Faith is the foundation of all virtues; whatever is done outside faith is without doubt built for Gehenna. Christian faith is a most beautiful lamp illuminating the soul, and through the observance of the commandments leading it into eternal happiness.

Faith first brings the mind back to the knowledge of God; afterward it gives the precepts of living unto obedience; and when a man has kept the commandments of God, hope of attaining salvation follows; then charity is made firm in hearts. Faith gives knowledge to the soul; knowledge brings forth action in charity; and to these two beatitude is promised in eternity. One must take the greatest care that there be no error in knowledge, because when right faith is corrupted by the falsehood of heresy, no action brings one to the harbor of salvation. Therefore let there be rectitude and truth in faith, justice and sincerity in action, and no iniquity will have dominion.

For faith without works is dead, and merits nothing toward the salvation of eternal happiness. To have faith without the works of charity is to imitate the demons, who, although they are far removed from all charity, nevertheless believe that God is just and tremble. Hence, concerning believers who do not show the works of charity, the Lord speaks through the prophet Isaiah and afterward through himself in the holy Gospel, saying: “This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me.” He who praises Christ with his mouth but without works is like a man who doubts in faith. For if he truly believed, he would certainly fear God. Faith without works is dead, like a body without spirit, which, removed from the sight of the living, is hidden in a tomb. If you believe well and live badly, you slay yourself with your own sword, because for him who knows the good and does not do it, it is sin.

Beware, my brothers, lest you admit error in faith, and take the greatest care that there be no wickedness in action. Follow the faith which holy Church commends to us; live the life which the Gospel and the cenobitic norm command.

Then have hope in the manifestation of God at all times most certain, but never separated from fear of the Lord. Hope is the certain expectation of future goods through the grace of the merciful God, and the solicitude of meriting the crown of justice through the exercise of charity. Hope is the sure expectation of future beatitude, which the grace of God works in us, accompanied by the rectitude of a good will. Hope is that by which someone hopes to come to the life of eternal happiness which he desires, and, through the mediation of God’s grace, also composes and fits his own will to meet it.

Hope concerns goods that are difficult, future, and possible, of which no one hopes for the contrary. For no one hopes for evil, nor for what is easy, nor for what is present or past; nor do we fail to know that hope is not of impossible things. Hope is the moderator of all labors, and the one consolation in the midst of tribulations. For the hope of future goods brings rest in this life to those laboring for Christ, just as, for those contending in the arena, the expectation of the crown through continual victory lessens the pain of wounds. Hope strengthened the martyrs in torments, and made them, in the power of Christ, stronger than all their persecutors. Hope of eternal beatitude preserves the Church, and gave the holy confessors and virgins true charity in Christ. Hope powerfully raises up the soul cast down by grief; and the higher it has lifted itself through love, the less it feels the adversities of this world. Blessed is the man who trusts in the Lord, because hope does not confound, as the holy apostle promised, by which we have been saved.

But so that hope may not be corrupted into presumption, fear of the Lord is necessary for our salvation, so that him whom we believe to be kind and merciful we may not doubt also to be just and upright. For the most blessed Pope Gregory says: Nothing is safer for us than to tremble humbly under hope, lest an incautious mind either cast itself into the depths of vices by despairing, or, by exalting itself, fall presumptuously from hope. For the more humbly it trembles in hope before the eyes of the strict judge, the more robustly it stands in him.

Fear of God corrects the mind and keeps it pure from all malice. Fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom, because on the way of God one begins from fear, so that little by little one may arrive at the strength of charity. Fear of the Lord composes the monk, and makes him zealous in the observance of regular discipline. Fear of the Lord guards the Rule, and preserves true purity of mind and body. Fear of the Lord neglects none of the things that are commanded, but is solicitous for all. The commandments of God are walls. A city without the protection of walls is quickly captured by enemies, and a monk who does not have the fear of God is easily overcome by demons. For the fear of God alone is what corrects minds, drives away crimes, preserves the innocent, and makes the monk persevering in the purpose of holiness.

From fear, little by little, charity arises; and when, with God’s help, it has been truly rooted in the mind, overcoming servile fear, it now casts it out. The root of all good things is charity, since whatever good is done in this life is governed by charity alone, so that it may please God. Charity is the greatest of all virtues, without which all the deeds of mortals are nothing. For the most holy apostle of Christ says: “If I speak with the tongues of men and of angels, but do not have charity, I have become like sounding brass or a clanging cymbal. And if I have prophecy, and know all mysteries and all knowledge, and if I have all faith, so that…”

[segment 117]
so that I might move mountains, but do not have charity, I am nothing. And if I distribute all my goods as food for the poor, and if I hand over my body so that I may burn, but do not have charity, it profits me nothing.

Consider yourselves diligently now, my brothers, and question your consciences, whether you have the true charity of God, without which all other things avail nothing for salvation. For you have heard the Apostle’s clear judgment, by which he says that charity is necessary for salvation. Let no one trust in the wisdom of this world; let no one vainly exalt himself over the natural gifts he has received; let no one presume upon bodily continence, since, if charity is lacking, the observance of the other things contributes nothing to salvation.

But lest anyone presume upon a false estimate of himself, the holy Apostle set forth certain signs and operations of charity, saying: Charity is patient, is kind. Charity does not envy, does not act perversely, is not puffed up, is not ambitious, and does not seek what is its own; it is not provoked, does not think evil, does not rejoice over iniquity, but rejoices together with the truth. It bears all things, believes all things, hopes all things, endures all things. Charity never fails. Have the charity of God within you, my brothers; unless you keep it, you labor in vain by contending.

Humility, I have told you more often, is necessary for you above all: it is not only a great virtue, but also the nurse of the remaining virtues and the chief guardian of them all. For the great Pope Gregory says: Whoever gathers virtues without humility is like one carrying dust into the wind. For humility is the greatest virtue of monks, since they are bound to be humbler than others living in the world, the more they are obliged by profession to strive and tend toward greater perfection. Humility is the stable foundation of the virtues; whatever is built upon it will be overturned by no whirlwind of storm. Through pride the devil fell from heaven; through humility man ascends. We are called monks in vain if we have not been humble; for just as fire, if it lacks the fuel of wood, is extinguished, so a monk without humility is judged very unworthy of his name. By Christian humility the devil is conquered, and, as if struck by a nail, is overcome as he flees. No more splendid jewel is seen in the monastic adornment than humility; if a monk despises it, he has denied both his profession and the humble Christ.

Prudence succeeds humility. It always directs all things according to the rule of reason and does nothing except what is right, honorable, and praiseworthy. Prudence knows what it ought virtuously to seek, and it well knows what it ought to avoid according to the right judgment of reason. True and perfect prudence is that by which we seek that highest, unchangeable good, and order all things to their due and proper end. Prudence preserves the observance of regular discipline stable in its strength, and leads wandering monks back to the complete restoration of their lost purpose. Prudence directs a monk on the right way of salvation and does not allow him to decline to either side. Prudence preserves peace and concord among the brothers, and orders right action in each matter.

Prudence is very necessary for you, my brothers, so that you may know in every action what you should take up and what again should be avoided. True prudence, not of the flesh but of the spirit, teaches you to despise the world and to desire only those things that are future and eternal. Prudence teaches a man to stand firm within himself, and not to decline from the purpose of holiness either in prosperity or in adversity. Prudence teaches how to order present things well, recalls past things to memory for instruction, and foresees future things so that nothing may happen unexpectedly. Prudence usefully and fruitfully corrects those who offend, and composes everything that it either does, says, or thinks, according to the judgment of reason, toward its due end.

Therefore be prudent as serpents and simple as doves, because prudence without goodness is malice, and simplicity without rational prudence is called folly. For the most holy Paul, Apostle of Christ, says: The prudence of the flesh is death. Be prudent, lest you be deceived by the fraud of others, but simple, lest you deceive anyone. For this is the office of the prudent person: neither to be capable of being deceived, nor to wish to deceive anyone. Prudence acts by discerning virtues; for one is called prudent from providing beforehand.

Justice accompanies prudence, and it is the most splendid of all the virtues. For without justice all the remaining virtues are nothing. Justice is the preserver of religion, the mother of grace, the nurse of observance, the nourisher of truth, the begetter of peace, and the origin of piety.

[segment 118]
*Berno. I. Folio LX.*

“... so that I might move mountains, but if I have not charity, I am nothing. And if I should distribute all my goods as food for the poor, and if I should hand over my body so that it burns, but have not charity, it profits me nothing.” Consider carefully, now, my brothers, your own selves, and ask your consciences whether you have true charity; without it all other things avail nothing for salvation. For you have heard the clear judgment of the Apostle, by which he says that charity is necessary for salvation. Let no one trust in the wisdom of this world; let no one vainly exalt himself over the natural gifts he has received; let no one presume upon bodily continence, since, if charity is lacking, the observance of the other things contributes nothing to salvation. But lest anyone presume a false opinion of himself, the holy Apostle set forth certain signs and operations of charity, saying: “Charity is patient; it is kind. Charity does not envy, does not act perversely, is not puffed up, is not ambitious, does not seek what is its own, is not provoked, does not think evil, does not rejoice over iniquity, but rejoices together with the truth. It bears all things, believes all things, hopes all things, endures all things. Charity never fails.” Have the charity of God in you, my brothers; unless you keep it, you labor in vain by striving.

Humility is very necessary for you, as we have often said; it is not only a great virtue, but also the nurse of the other virtues and the foremost guardian of them all. For Pope Gregory the Great says: “He who gathers virtues without humility carries dust into the wind.” Humility is indeed the great virtue of monks, since they are bound to be so much humbler than the rest who live their lives in the world, as by profession they are bound to strive and tend toward greater perfection. Humility is the stable foundation of the virtues; whatever is built upon it will be overturned by no whirlwind of tempest. Through pride the devil fell from heaven; through humility man ascends. In vain are we called monks if we have not been humble, for just as fire is extinguished if it lacks the fuel of wood, so a monk without humility shows himself very unworthy of his name. By Christian humility the devil is conquered, and, as if struck by a nail, he is overcome as he flees. No more splendid jewel is seen in monastic adornment than humility; if a monk has despised it, he has denied both his profession and the humble Christ.
