Prudence follows upon humility; it always directs all things to the standard of reason and does nothing except what is right, honorable, and praiseworthy. Prudence knows what it ought virtuously to seek, and knows well that it ought to live according to the right judgment of reason. True and perfect prudence is that by which we seek that highest, unchangeable good, and order all things to their due and right end. Prudence preserves the observance of regular discipline stable in vigor, and brings observing monks back to the complete restoration of the purpose they had lost. Prudence directs the monk in the right way of salvation, and does not allow him to decline to either side. Prudence preserves peace and concord among brothers, and orders right action in each matter. Prudence, O my brothers, is very necessary for you; by it you will know in every action what things are to be undertaken by you, and what in turn are to be avoided. True prudence, not of the flesh but of the spirit, teaches you to despise the world, and to desire only those things that are future and eternal. Prudence teaches a man to stand firm in himself, and neither in prosperity nor in adversity to decline from the purpose of holiness. Prudence teaches how to order present things beautifully; it brings past things back to memory for instruction; and it foresees future things, lest anything happen unexpectedly. Prudence corrects offenders usefully and fruitfully, and composes all that it either does, says, or thinks, according to the judgment of reason, toward its due end. Therefore be prudent as serpents and simple as doves, because prudence without goodness is malice, and simplicity without rational prudence is called foolishness. For the most holy Paul, Apostle of Christ, says: “The prudence of the flesh is death.” Be prudent, lest you be circumvented by the deceit of others, but simple, lest you circumvent anyone. For this is the duty of the prudent man: neither to be capable of being deceived, nor to wish to deceive anyone. A prudent man does not abandon the judgment of reason, and does nothing about which he must afterward be ashamed. For from foreseeing one is called prudent.

Justice accompanies prudence; it is the most splendid of all the virtues. For without justice all the remaining virtues are nothing. Justice is the preserver of religion, the mother of grace, the nurse of observance, the nourisher of truth, the begetter of peace, and the origin of piety.

[segment 119]
[unknown]

That monks must live cautiously.

Justice is the virtue which distributes and preserves for each person what is his own. It neglects private advantage in order to preserve common advantage and equity; without it no commonwealth can long subsist in peace. For the wise man says in Ecclesiasticus: “A kingdom is transferred from nation to nation because of injustices, injuries, insults, and various deceits.” The order of justice looks first to God, then to the fatherland, thereafter to parents, and afterwards to all people in common, according to the distinctions of ranks and the merits of dignity. To God, in the first place, justice dictates that we owe religion, faith, worship, devotion, acts of thanksgiving, and true love from the whole heart, under obedient submission. Then to ourselves we owe purity of mind and body, and continence with zeal for perfection, so that through obedience we may be permitted to return to him by whom we were created. In the third place, to the fatherland we owe advantage, honor, and defense, and, as far as we can, the procuring of its interests together with justice itself. Fourth, we are bound to our parents by honor and obedience, and in their need by care and every kind of support. Fifth, to our prelates and superiors we owe obedience and humble subjection for the love of Christ. Sixth, prelates owe teaching and nourishment to their subjects, and correction to those who offend. In the seventh place, however, we are bound to give honor to elders and princes, and blameless subjection for the love of God. Eighth, we owe reverence to venerable persons, and honor to priests. Consequently, we are bound to equals by friendship, to inferiors by condescension, to benefactors by reciprocation, to friends by fellowship, to enemies by patience, to the poor by mercy, to the wretched by consolation, and to all by charity. This, then, is true justice: by it we love God with the whole heart for his own sake, and our neighbor in God as ourselves, and enemies for God’s sake, distributing to each person what is owed to him by right and providing for the advantage of all.

But since excessive justice easily falls into sin, temperance is necessary for you in all things; it alone makes people perfect. Temperance is the virtue which, by reason, discerns and conquers the impulses of the soul and every lust, lest impulse in action exceed the equity of the law. Temperance is a most excellent virtue: it governs pleasures and preserves all things in due order. Apply temperance, and the discretion necessary to the virtues, to all your actions, words, and also thoughts. Whatever good you do with discretion, accept it under the name and merit of virtue; but what is done without discretion must be called vice. Discretion is the moderator of all virtues and the greatest ornament of prudence and justice. Discretion is necessary not only for prelates but also for subjects, since it gives order to virtues, grants moderation to affections, and bestows most beautiful grace and honorableness on all human counsels. Hence a certain wise man says: “There is a measure in things; there are, finally, fixed limits, beyond which and short of which the right cannot stand.”

For this, brothers, strength of soul is necessary for you, through which you may become not only steadfast in adversities but also prompt and cheerful in difficult things. For who, without the greatest strength of soul, could despise this world with its pleasures, and persevere in holy monastic religion under the laws of obedience even unto death? Strength of soul receives its powers from the charity of Christ, and stands constant in desire for virtue no less in adversities than in prosperity. Strength walks the way of uprightness without fear, and to enemies it seems terrible and fearsome with a certain holy pride. Strength of soul despises small injuries and easily overcomes all the pleasures of the flesh. Strength lies hidden in prosperity, is shown in adversity, and is openly declared through patience. Strong of soul is not the man who avenges injuries brought upon him, but the man who patiently tolerates them; if he has received his adversary into his power, he considers that he could have exacted vengeance. The strength of monks is to despise the world, to conquer the flesh, to spurn every honor and pleasure of the world, and to place all hope and confidence in God alone. The measure of strength is to be neither timid nor rash, since the one walking on the right way turns aside neither to the right nor to the left. That monk must be judged strong who is temperate in all things, moderate, and just. Hence the most holy pope Gregory says: “The strength of the just is to conquer the flesh, to oppose their own pleasures, to extinguish the delight of the present life, to love the hardships of this world for eternal rewards, to contemn the enticements of prosperity, and to overcome the fear of adversity in the heart.” The saints are made strong in battle, because they subdue the flesh, are strengthened by virtues, fortify the spirit, despise earthly things, and seek heavenly things. They can be killed, but they cannot be bent, nor can they endure falsehood. Through weakness they fear [unclear], nor, when harmed, do they ever withdraw from the truth.

[segment 120]
[Page 128]

Sermon I. Folio LXI.

The monk, therefore, who is strong with true virtue neither ventures rashly nor fears without counsel. For fortitude together with constancy is the royal road; from it the rash and obstinate man turns aside to the right, while the fearful, faint-hearted, and timid man turns aside to the left. The monk who is constant and strong in spirit never withdraws from the purpose of holiness, but is neither cast down by adversities nor lifted up by prosperity. Hence the most holy bishop Ambrose also says: “The fool is changed like the moon. But the wise man is not broken by fear, is not altered by power, is not puffed up by prosperity, and is not submerged by sorrows.” For where there are wisdom and virtue, there are constancy and fortitude of spirit. The wise man, therefore, is always the same in spirit; he is neither diminished nor increased by changes of affairs, nor does he waver like a little child, so as to be carried about by every wind of doctrine, but he remains perfect in Christ, founded in charity and rooted in the perfection of faith.

Lastly, my dearest brothers, perseverance is necessary for you in every good and virtuous work; without it no one ever comes to the crown of glory. For the Lord says in the holy Gospel: “He who has persevered to the end will be saved.” Take away perseverance, and virtue will have no prize, service no reward, nor will a benefit merit grace. For perseverance alone deserves to receive the crown of praise for good works; without it, it profits nothing to have begun the good. No virtue has merit to which perseverance has not given increase. The kingdom of eternal blessedness is not promised to those who begin the good, but to those who persevere in the good even unto death, because not in the morning, not at midday, but when evening had come, the denarius was paid to those laboring in the vineyard.

For it is not the man who began well who is to be praised, but the one who continued well to the end is to be honored. Therefore, my brothers, I am accustomed earnestly to admonish you, and I must never cease, that you diligently attend to your calling and walk cautiously in all religion and holiness, as befits sworn soldiers of Christ. For now is the time for doing good, because when the hour of death has arrived it will utterly cut off every capacity for working. And therefore, as long as you live sound and unharmed, do good; always consider the future recompense, and grow daily in spiritual progress. Seek the Lord while he can be found. Let the life of Christ the Savior be your imitation; you are bound, by the foundations of your institution, to follow him not by bodily steps, but by your powers, and by likeness of affections. Walk cautiously in all things; always place the fear of God before the gaze of your mind in true humility. For great purity is required of you who have consented, in mind and body, to the holy warfare of Christ. It now belongs to you, brothers, to despise the world and all things that are in the world for God’s sake with constant fortitude; to overcome all movements of fleshly pleasures; to flee sin; and to occupy yourselves continually in good and holy works. Your life also, so far as human weakness permits, ought to imitate angelic purity, and to flee and detest all depravity. The holy angels are never idle, but are always attentive, without weariness or tedium, to the praises of God. The holy angels love God above all things, and do not withdraw from his charity even for a moment. In imitation of them, the monk ought to be assiduous in the service of Christ, and always occupied in good works according to the Gospel and the Rule. The monk ought to have most fervent charity for God, and true, orderly, and sincere love of neighbor. For burning love toward God perfects the monk and does not allow him to be idle. For the four-times most holy pope Gregory says: “The love of God is never idle. For it works great things if it exists; but if it refuses to work, it was never true love of God.” For if the mad poet spoke truth concerning carnal love, saying, “let him love who does not wish to become idle,” how much more strongly does the most chaste love of God impel the mind it possesses toward arduous things. Love God, brothers, with the whole heart, as far as the strength of desire can extend itself, and you will most easily be able to observe all the things commanded you. For nothing is difficult to one who loves; rather, burning love makes all hard things, all grave and arduous things, easy.

What the ordering of a good monastic life is: Sermon Two.

We have said that the institution of our most holy father Benedict, which by the customary term we call religion or order, shone forth as most holy at the beginning of its foundation, and was very greatly and manifoldly spread throughout the whole church of the Latins. Hence for very many years in this same order many wise, learned, and most holy men flourished,

[Page 129]

What the ordering of a good monastic life is.

who magnificently illuminated the whole Church. For there are those who write that from this monastic religion of ours fifteen thousand six hundred persons, men as well as nuns, have been enrolled in the catalogue of the saints. This multitude of saints in our order, brothers, is set before us for imitation, not for vain glory. How can their multitude conduce to our salvation, if we do not imitate the way of life in which they went before us? Whenever we wish usefully to praise the most holy streams of our order, let us hasten by holy conduct to come into their fellowship. Then indeed we shall glory fruitfully in the multitude of saints of our order, if, by living rightly, we earnestly strive to increase their number. For this reason we set before you so great a multitude of saints: so that, when you see before you in this very order many who have overcome the world and the flesh, you may at last rise up from your lukewarm way of life. The virtue that God’s mercy bestowed on them he will not deny to you, if you ask in true charity. They overcame the world because they loved Christ with complete devotion, despising all things for love of him. They despised all things, and the Lord Jesus provided for them whatever seemed necessary for the sustenance of life.

This fervor of devotion among monks endured for many years after the first institution of the order, with great constancy, and converted the souls of many thousands of people to the Lord. But nothing in human affairs is stable; nothing among perishable things is perpetual. Holy religion brought riches to monks, until in time the daughter suffocated the mother. For, as Scripture says, all who wish to become rich fall into the temptations of the devil. The beloved grew fat and kicked back; made gross and enlarged by earthly possessions, he abandoned the holy pursuits of his first institution and went after vanities and false madnesses. Abundance of temporal things made him poor and miserable in spiritual riches.

This pitiable deformity of our order began almost two hundred years after the passing of the divine father Benedict, and grew more and more with time, until at last the harmful poison spread through the whole order. Yet this deformity did not seize the order suddenly, but through the succession of many years it occupied almost the whole of it, although almighty God never left it wholly destitute of good men, but always kept for himself some who in no way bent their knees to Baal. For men of mercy arose, whose just deeds will never be handed over to oblivion; pitying the ruin of the order, they put their shoulder beneath the labor and, with the Lord bestowing his aid, restored it in many places. Many different partial reforms in the order indeed flourished very often at various times, but there was never any general reform that brought the whole order back to the integrity of regular observance. Finally, we now see almost all the partial restorations, which we have said were many, collapsed again.

Most recently, however, namely in the year 1420 of the Christians, when the general council was being celebrated at Constance, a certain new and up to that time latest partial reform took its beginning in Germany. From a certain monastery in the region of Saxony, then indeed the poorest of all, called Bursfelde, it receives the word of its name. The first author and prince of this most holy reform was Johannes Rode, abbot of the distinguished monastery of Saint Matthias at Trier. Having been appointed general visitor of the order by the aforesaid Council of Constance, lest he himself should be judged reprobate while preaching to others, he first displayed reform in himself. For he reformed both himself first of all and the monks of the said monastery; he issued constitutions conforming to the Rule, and obtained confirmation of the holy work from the Apostolic See. Among all the monasteries of the order, however, Bursfelde, a humble and rather poor monastery, first received this reform, which it afterwards spread to Reinhausen and to a few neighboring monasteries of Saxony.

At length, when Johannes Rode, abbot of Saint Matthias and prince of our reform, had died, and when no suitable successor for extending the business that had been begun was found in that same place, Johannes de Hagen, abbot of Bursfelde, who had already reformed certain monasteries, a holy man and very zealous for the order, was chosen at the insistence of Jakob, archbishop of Trier. Content with his poverty, he did not consent to leave Bursfelde, lest he seem to have preferred riches to poverty. He therefore remained in his own monastery, and another was appointed in the place of the first reformer. Since this man seemed comparable to his predecessor neither in learning nor in zeal, he was able to accomplish little for the continuation of the business that had been begun, nor to extend the reform among many. Meanwhile it happened that Nicolaus Cusanus, cardinal priest of the holy Roman Church,

[segment 121]
Page 130

What the good ordering of monastic life is.

who magnificently illuminated the whole Church. For there are some who write that from this monastic religion of ours fifteen thousand seven hundred men and nuns have been entered into the catalogue of the saints. This multitude of saints in the order, brothers, is set before us for imitation, not for vain glory. What can their multitude contribute in the end to our salvation, if we do not imitate the way of life in which they went before us? Whenever we desire profitably to praise the most holy men of our order, let us hasten by holy conduct to come into their fellowship. For then we shall glory fruitfully in the multitude of the saints of our order, if, by living rightly, we strive to increase their number. Therefore we set before you so great a multitude of saints, so that, when you see that many before you in this very order conquered the world and the flesh, you may at last rise up from your lukewarm manner of life. The virtue that God’s mercy granted to them he will not deny to you, if you ask in true charity.

They conquered the world and loved Christ with total devotion, despising all things for love of him. They despised all passing things, and the Lord Jesus provided whatever seemed necessary to them for the support of life. This fervor of devotion among monks endured after the first institution of the order for many years in great constancy, and converted the souls of many thousands of people to the Lord. But nothing among human things is stable; nothing among perishable things is everlasting. Sacred religion brought riches upon the monks, until in time the daughter suffocated the mother. For, as Scripture says, all who wish to become rich fall into the temptations of the devil. The beloved grew fat and kicked; fattened and enlarged by earthly possessions, he abandoned the holy pursuits of the first institution, went after vanities and false follies, and an abundance of temporal things made him needy and wretched in spiritual riches.

This pitiable deformity of our order began almost two hundred years after the passing of the divine father Benedict, and with time it grew more and more, until at last the harmful poison spread through the whole order. Yet this deformity occupied the order not suddenly, but through the succession of many years, until it had almost wholly taken possession of it; although almighty God never left it wholly without good men, but always reserved for himself some who by no means bent their knees to Baal. For men, and women too, arose whose righteousness is never handed over to oblivion; pitying the ruin of the order, they put their shoulder under the labor, and, the Lord generously granting aid, restored it in many places.

Indeed, many varied particular reformations often flourished in the order at different times, but there was never any general one that brought the whole order back to the integrity of regular observance. Finally, we now see that the particular restorations, which we have said were many, have almost all collapsed again. Most recently, however, namely in the Christian year 1420, when the general council was being celebrated at Constance, a certain new and, up to now, last particular reformation took its beginning in Germany. It took the word used as its name from a certain monastery in the regions of Saxony, then indeed the poorest of all, which is called Bursfelde.

The first author and leader of this most holy reformation was Johannes Rode, abbot of the distinguished monastery of Saint Matthias at Trier. Having been appointed general visitor of the order by the aforesaid Council of Constance, lest, while preaching to others, he himself should be judged reprobate, he first displayed reformation in himself. For first of all he reformed both himself and the monks of the said monastery; he issued constitutions conforming to the Rule; and he obtained confirmation of the holy work from the apostolic see. But among all the monasteries of the order, Bursfelde, a humble and rather poor monastery, first received this reformation, which it afterwards extended to Reinhausen and to a few monasteries of Saxony neighboring it.

When at last Johannes Rode, abbot of Saint Matthias and leader of our reformation, had died, since in that place no suitable man was found for extending the undertaking begun, Johannes de Hagen, abbot of Bursfelde, who had already reformed certain monasteries, a holy man and very zealous for the order, was elected at the urging of Jacob, archbishop of Trier. Content with his poverty, he did not fully consent to leave Bursfelde, lest he seem to have preferred riches to poverty. He therefore remained in his own monastery, and another was appointed in the place of the first reformer. Since he seemed comparable to his predecessor neither in learning nor in zealous affection, he was able to accomplish little for the continuation of the undertaking begun, nor to enlarge the reformation among many. Meanwhile it happened that Nicholas of Cusa, presbyter cardinal of the holy Roman Church and legate of the apostolic see, came into Germany. Since he burned with great zeal for our order, he established and granted many things for its reformation. Hearing, moreover, the good fame of the aforesaid abbot in Bursfelde and his most fervent purpose for regular discipline, he called him to himself and appointed him general reformer of the order; and through his industry he nobly reformed many things.

On this occasion at last he instituted an annual chapter for the preservation of the reformation; he transferred the presidency from Saint Matthias to Bursfelde; and he commanded that all the things he had instituted for the good of the reformation be confirmed by the apostolic see. The first abbot of Bursfelde in the reformation was Johannes, a monk appointed from Reinhausen; the second was the aforesaid Johannes de Hagen; the third was Theodericus, whom we have seen; and the fourth is Johannes, who has recently been appointed and is now in office. Brothers most beloved, we briefly touch upon the origins of our reformation so that you may recognize with how much labor it began, and so that you may persevere constant unto death in the holy observance of the Rule.

Among all the reformations of our order that preceded it, none was more conformable and agreeable to the one Rule of the divine father Benedict, and none more alien to foreign aids. We have read the statutes of almost all other reformations, but we judge none more suited than ours to the Rule and to the purity of Benedict’s intention. This Bursfelde reformation is most holy in the order; it teaches true obedience and guards subjection in humility. In this holy religion monks live poor and without personal property; they live humbly; they live obediently; they live chastely. O venerable union of monks, in which twin charity rules, obedience serves, humility obeys, prayer keeps watch, perseverance fights, and joyful chastity triumphs. This is the school of regular discipline, the instruction of Christian humility, which directs conduct toward uprightness and restrains the vices of the flesh. In this arena of virtues no one is permitted to follow his own will against the Rule; no one is forbidden to do good. In this holy union men live according to the purity of the Rule, and if anyone begins to wander from the path of uprightness, the care of the elders soon calls him back to the observance of justice. The Rule of the most holy father Benedict is read publicly three times in the year, so that no one may try to excuse himself from observance under the shadow of ignorance.

There are two things that promise me great hope for the perseverance of this holy reformation: the annual chapter and the diligent biennial visitation of the monasteries. But if either of these is neglected, the reformation soon falls into danger. Therefore think now, most beloved brothers, and take the most diligent care you can, lest you occupy this monastery of the divine bishop Martin uselessly under the title of so venerable a reformation, since unless you live purely, holily, and blamelessly according to the Rule and the statutes of the order, you trust in the name in vain.

We shall speak to you a few true admonitions for salvation, through which the zealous observance of our Bursfelde reformation will possess both name and dignity by merit. First, before all else, direct all your efforts and all your desires toward monastic perfection; and whenever you sense yourselves growing lukewarm, renew them by attentive remembrance of the Lord’s passion and most innocent death. Although we are not bound to be perfect by necessity of salvation, nevertheless we are bound to strive for perfection with all our desires. For when a monk casts away the striving for perfection, he falls into the pit of defection. The second thing that the sacred custom of our reformation has instituted for you to keep strongly preserves the striving for perfection in the desire already conceived. Therefore, when you rise at the signal for Prime, do these things in secret. Examine and discuss the course of the coming day, considering prudently what must usefully be done. Set in your hearts how cautiously you wish to live for the whole day, and arm your souls with patience against all adversities that may occur. Whatever you do, complete it in the fervor of charity, and always order all words and deeds to the praise of God.

The third thing we judge useful and necessary for you is that with most devout prayers you always strive in all things to implore the help of God. And it seems very fitting to us, as we have often said and always admonish, that each of you should at least once a day keep remembrance of the Lord’s passion, and once give thanks to God for the many benefits received from him. So that this may be done uniformly by each one of you, listen to this brief form that we have ordained; if you wish, write it down for yourselves: I adore, praise, bless, and glorify you, Lord Jesus Christ, Son of the living God, savior and giver of life to the souls of all the faithful; and I give you thanks from the depth of my heart for your most holy incarnation and birth from the most pure virgin Mary; for your circumcision; for your flight into Egypt; for your seven years of exile there; and for [unclear].

[segment 122]
--- Page 131 ---

What constitutes a good ordering of monastic life.

for your return afterward into the land of your temporal birth. I give thanks to you, most sweet Lord Jesus; I praise, bless, and adore you for all your labors, journeys, and weariness; for hunger, thirst, cold, nakedness, poverty, and want; and for the necessities, injuries, insults, mockeries, and slanders that you endured throughout your whole life for us, willingly and not under compulsion. And as often as you moved your hand to work, your foot to walk, or opened your mouth to speak, so often I bless, praise, glorify, and humbly adore you; and with all the powers of my heart I always give thanks to you, mediator of God and men, most sweet Jesus Christ.

I give thanks to you, Lord Jesus Christ, most merciful king of the heavens, for your most holy teaching, for the establishment of your faith and of the evangelical sacraments, for the calling of the nations, for the justification of sinners, and for all your wonderful works and powers, and for all your benefits, which you have bestowed either on me particularly or on all the rest together with me generally.

I give thanks to you, Lord Jesus Christ, most kind savior, for the charity that you had toward the human race, and for your whole most bitter passion and all the sufferings that you endured for us wretched sinners. I give thanks to you, Lord Jesus; I praise, bless, and adore you for the grief and sorrow of your most innocent heart, which you endured for us at the time of your impending passion, when you said to your disciples who followed you: “My soul is sorrowful even unto death.” I beseech you, by this sorrow of your heart, O God, have mercy on me now and at the hour of my death; and remove from me all melancholy and disordered grief, granting me at all times a salutary sorrow for my sins.

I give thanks to you for the sadness of your most holy heart, when, placed in agony, you prayed to the Father three times that he would take from you the chalice of the passion; and, having poured out sweat under wondrous anguish, you resigned yourself wholly to his will for us. I give thanks to you, O king of heaven and earth, for your captivity, for the insults, injuries, and mockeries that you endured throughout the whole time of your passion for the salvation of us all.

I give thanks to you, Lord Jesus; I praise, bless, and adore you bound to the column, for the very harsh scourging of your most tender body, for the outpouring of your most sacred blood, and for the immense pain of your wounds. I adore you, Lord Jesus Christ, crowned with thorns, and I give thanks to you for the piercings and pains of your most holy head, for the blows, spittle, and all the mockeries cruelly inflicted on your majesty by the impious. I adore you, Christ Jesus, Son of the living God, bearing the very heavy weight of your cross. I adore you stripped of your garments before the multitude. I adore you stretched out by your hands and feet with iron nails upon the cross, and fastened to it with exceeding cruelty.

I adore you, Jesus Christ, eternal king, hanging and dying on the cross for me; and I give thanks for the pains of your wounds; I give thanks for the shedding of your most precious blood; I give thanks for your most innocent death, which you voluntarily endured for our sins and the sins of the whole world. I give thanks to your boundless goodness because you called me, your servant, to the faith; because you caused me to be baptized in the name of the holy Trinity; and because from the vanity of the world you deigned to call me to serve you in true tranquility of mind in this holy congregation.

I beseech you by your most holy name, O most sweet Jesus, mediator of God and men; I beseech you by the pain of your wounds and by the merits of your most bitter passion; I beseech you by the shedding of your most holy blood and by the power of your most innocent death: grant me pardon for all my sins; pour into my heart the sweetness of your most fervent love; give me perseverance with the advancement of all virtues; and have mercy on me now and at the hour of my death, guarding me today and at all times, in your mercy, loving Jesus, from every sin and from danger of mind and body, in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. Amen.

Which of you, brothers, is unable to say this brief prayer once each day, or a similar one if he has a better? This prayer is not long, nor wrapped in foreign expressions, but brief and plain, gathering together the life and passion of Christ in a concise summary. A Christian person, and especially one enclosed in the cloister, is exceedingly ungrateful to God if he does not at least once a day recall the Lord’s passion and devoutly render acts of thanksgiving. But if this prayer seems too long to any of you, in the eighth homily of our Exhortations, recently delivered to you, we gave a shorter one, to which anyone wearied may have recourse. Before all things, beloved, take care that you are not ungrateful for so many benefits of God. Let this be

[segment 123]
--- Page 132 ---

Let this act of thanksgiving be made after matins, because that is the most suitable time for prayer, and the silence of the night’s rest usually increases devotion. For I say what is attested as true: no prayers are more effective than those which are offered to God the Father with remembrance of the Lord’s passion and with gratitude.

Fourth, what we have judged necessary for you for regular observance is that you keep watch over the custody of the outward senses, because no one can sufficiently guard the city of his heart if he scorns to close its gates. Love silence, and flee every disturbance of this world. Apply holy prudence to everything you do, and love the secrecy of quiet. For a monk who loves public and wandering excursions has never been a true lover of observance.

Fifth, we admonish you to flee idleness, destructive to the soul, because this pestilential poison nourishes the gravest vices and always begets unclean thoughts. Therefore whoever, as a lover of virtues, loves his own salvation; whoever desires to reach eternal happiness: let him flee idleness as from the face of the devil, if he wishes to avoid sin.

Sixth, for purity of regular observance, by the necessity of salvation, it is required that the renunciation of property be kept inviolate by all until death. For a monk who has private property prepares for himself a road to hell. But whoever receives and spends money in the monastery should be a public minister; no private person should dare to presume this. The possession of riches is not fitting for monks, who are commanded to imitate the Lord Jesus in poverty of spirit. Let us hear the holy apostle of Christ saying: “Having food and clothing, with these let us be content.” By God’s mercy we provide you with necessities, not as much as the desire of disordered pleasure demands, but as much as nature, content with little, requires. Therefore, just as it is not lawful for you to possess anything without the abbot’s consent, so, since we supply everything, nothing is lacking that is necessary.

Seventh, what makes a monk is humble obedience to commands. For he cannot be a true monk who scorns obedience and refuses to his superiors the humble subjection which he promised. This disease of disobedience arises from elation and pride of mind, just as pus is produced from a bad ulcer. But what does Scripture say? “Pride is hateful before God and men,” and is the beginning of every transgression. The warfare of monks is the imitation of Christ, who obeyed the Father even unto death.

The eighth place is adorned by chastity, a most beautiful virtue; if it is scorned, it is now inevitable that the whole integrity of regular observance suffer shipwreck. For a monk, by necessity of his salvation, is commanded to live chastely until death and always to abstain from every venereal pollution. For these are the three principal vows of monastic life; to live contrary to their observance is certain damnation for monks. Indeed, the profession of monks, under sentence of eternal damnation, commands them to live without property, under obedience to a pastor, and in perpetual chastity. If one of these three is removed, monastic integrity is violated, and transgression condemns the one who scorns it.

In the ninth place, therefore, we admonish every person dedicated to God always to beware of his own will as of a venomous serpent, because unless he has it suspect at all times, he easily falls into the pit of damnation. Attend to the Rule, which does not permit monks power over either their own body or their own will. No one sins unwillingly. If you have perfectly submitted your will in all things to God and to your abbot, you will easily avoid sin.

The tenth thing that can preserve in you, my dearest brothers, the integrity of regular observance is that in all adversities and oppositions you have most steadfast patience for the love of God. For patience is a great virtue, which by another name we call endurance, without which no one will come forth the victor over his temptations. Therefore the Lord said in the holy Gospel: “In your patience you will possess your souls.” What is patience except to bear calmly both the injuries done by one’s own people and all other adversities, out of reverence and love for God? Believe me, brothers; I speak the judgment of the apostle: patience is very necessary for you, so that, doing the will of God, you may receive the promise. Let the Lord Jesus be for you the living example of patience, whom you should follow with all your strength.

Eleventh, my brothers, take care that your hearts not be weighed down with gluttony, because a monk should not eat and drink to satiety, but, for love of abstinence, should always stop with a certain appetite and hunger. For the wise man says: “He who loves feasts will be in poverty, and he who loves wine and fat things will not become rich.” Love fasting; cherish abstinence; preserve continence and frugality in all things. These are the weapons of cloistered persons; these are the insignia of the spiritual warfare of monks.

[segment 124]
They do not overcome demons. An abundance of food is accompanied by useless talk, and a belly filled with wine stirs up unclean suggestions of the flesh and of lust. If you wish to be chaste, be abstinent. It is most shameful for a monk to serve his belly and gluttony, when for the love of God he has renounced all the pleasures of the world at once.

The twelfth thing that makes a monk useful and vigorous in regular observance is that he be most diligent in the service of God. For truly, whoever is a true servant of God, as all monks ought to be, is always fervent and diligent in the work of his Creator; and because he truly knows that God is present everywhere, he prepares himself with complete devotion for dutiful service to him. For it is written: Cursed is the man who does the work of God negligently. Therefore, dearest brothers, when you approach the service of the highest and unchangeable God, sing psalms sweetly, wisely, fervently, and cheerfully; and with as much devotion and purity of mind as you can, diligently praise your Creator and Savior. Let all sluggishness be far from the hearts of those who sing psalms; let no drowsiness be admitted while praising, because, since God is present in the midst of those who sing, it is altogether fitting that fervor of devotion be present in their souls. At every time of divine service let all things be clean, all things pure, all things filled most fully with the fervor of charity, so that improper thoughts may have no place, and all things may be done as they ought.

When the day has been completed and compline has been sung according to custom, as soon as a monk has reached the secrecy of his cell, let him examine his conscience as to what business he has occupied himself with. This is the excellent and praiseworthy custom of the brothers of the Bursfeld reform: that no one should presume to lay himself down on his bed after compline unless he has first taken the greatest care to examine his conscience concerning the faults or negligences committed during the day. But if, after making this examination, you find that you have offended in anything during the day, you know from long practice what must be done. Hidden faults must soon, on the next day, be wiped away by contrition and humble confession; public faults, however, must be referred to the chapter of faults, which is held daily. This is the custom of the reformed order; this is the institution of holy reform: that we never permit the faults of delinquent brothers to grow, but, as soon as they begin to arise, we take care to cut them away. The soul must be cleansed daily; the resolve of holiness must be renewed daily; for just as we cannot remain in the same state without change, so it is necessary for us, by continual exercise, to rouse the mind more frequently to the former fervor of charity.

What are the marks of true humility.

Sermon III.

Let none of you, most beloved brothers, deceive himself by his own estimate of himself; let no one mislead his own heart by foolish and empty presumption. Not all who wish to appear humble are humble before God, nor are those who keep silence under injuries always truly patient. For the roots of true virtues are hidden in the secrecy of the heart, and not in the mere display of outward showing. For we discern some among you who desire rather to be considered humble than truly to be so, and who show many signs of pride, but either no signs of humility or certainly very few. We feel intense compassion, as we rightly should, for those who err so greatly: they desire to have the name of virtue, but they despise virtue itself. Whom, I ask, do you miserable men deceive? Are you not shamefully destroying yourselves by leading yourselves astray, you who, despising truth, love a lie? But so that you may know how proudly you live, we shall set out in order the marks and signs of true humility. Some of these remain hidden in the heart alone; others, breaking forth outwardly as well, ought to be manifest to all who behold them. Let this be for a monk the true mirror of perfect humility, in which, looking each day, he may examine himself.

The first mark of true humility, then, each person can recognize in himself when he can regard himself as lower and viler than all people, not with feigned speech, but by the humble judgment of the heart. This sign of deep humility is the greatest: that a monk, always advancing toward a better way of life, believe himself to be a useless and unworthy servant of God. True humility belongs to the heart, not to speech; it is a striving of the mind, not merely an outward bodily display. That monk is truly humble who loves God above all things, who always keeps himself pure in all things from every transgression of the commandments of God and of the Rule, who does great works of piety and justice, and concerning himself, in his own judgment, truly...

[segment 125]
--- Page 134 ---
Sermon III. Fol. LXIII.

He bears continual contrition in his heart; and, always compunct in spirit, praying and groaning to the Lord with tears, he seeks pardon and mercy. Nor does he pray only for his own sins, but also for the sins of the whole world. The greatest remedy against exaltation of mind is the remembrance of one's own faults.

The third sign of humility is that he desires to keep all the good works he does privately with himself, and hides his own progress out of zeal for humility. For the man who is humble does not vaunt himself emptily, nor does he seek praise from men, since he hopes for the glory of eternal happiness. Whoever among you considers himself humble and a true monk should not despise, for God's sake, this sign of profound submission and abasement.

The fourth sign of true humility is that, voluntarily descending into the abyss of contempt for himself, he judges all his fellow brothers, even those openly sinning, better than himself before God. Mindful of his own evils and forgetful of his good deeds, he wisely considers them; but passing over others' evils in silence, he makes known at fitting times the good things they have done. This is the condition of true humility: always to admire and proclaim the good works of others, and prudently to depreciate and hide one's own for the avoidance of pride.

The fifth sign of humility is obtained by the man who is quiet in his own simplicity and does not speak of others' defects, unless he is compelled either by reason of office or by the command of charity. Yet prudent humility preserves moderation in correction, so that the man whom it cannot make better by correcting him more severely it strives to admonish gently and meekly. It also sometimes wisely passes over rebukes, and waits for and observes a more opportune time.

The sixth sign of humility is shown when a monk, out of love of humility, is slow and not ready to accuse and reprehend the sins of others. For he who is truly humble always considers his own weakness, and silently weighs in secret how many evils he himself has committed against God's justice. Hence he greatly fears to reprehend the deeds of others, because he recognizes that he is a sinner and therefore unworthy to judge others. We except the prelates and rulers of monks from this rule of humility, insofar as we know that they are obliged to correct those subject to them.

The seventh sign of true humility is that he gladly receives corrections and amendments for his faults and negligences, and is not embittered by any resentment against the one who reproves him. Whenever the humble man is corrected by anyone, he rejoices in spirit with the prophet, saying: Let the just man correct me in mercy, and he shall rebuke me; but let not the oil of the sinner fatten my head. But Scripture says of the proud: They have hated the one who reproves in the gate. The man who is truly humble loves the one who corrects him, but flees the flatterer.

The eighth sign of true humility is clearly shown when, once corrected, he does not defend, deny, palliate, or excuse his fault, but confesses it with simple shamefastness. When a truly humble monk is corrected, he does not murmur; he does not protest that he has exceeded the measure of fairness; but immediately, falling to the ground before the judge, he cries out most meekly: I have sinned; I confess my fault; I beg pardon; I promise amendment.

The ninth sign of monastic humility makes those who have it gentle, sweet, and modest in speech, and not clamorous in voice. A monk whom you see clamorous and headlong in speaking you will judge not humble, but proud and rash. The humble man, however, speaks modestly and almost under silence, and is always found readier to listen than to speak before others. It is shameful for a monk to raise his voice too much while speaking, since, being crucified to the world, he is commanded to love silence.

The tenth sign of perfect humility is possessed by the man who is accustomed to interpret everything he hears about his neighbor in the better part. For our Savior says in the holy Gospel: Do not judge, and you will not be judged. And again: With what measure you have measured, it will be measured back to you. The holy apostle also says: Do not judge before the time, until the Lord comes, who will both illuminate the hidden things of darkness and make manifest the counsels of hearts. Who are you, who judge another's servant? To his own lord he stands or falls, and not to you.

The eleventh sign of holy humility is that he despises no man in his heart on account of any offenses whatever, but sets all before himself by the merit of holiness. He also considers all who sin publicly to be better than himself, and therefore condemns no one. He always thinks within his heart about what evil he himself has done, and humbly weighs that the good deeds of others outweigh his own. Therefore he spurns no one, depreciates no one, contemns no one, since he desires all to be saved in the Lord.

[segment 126]
*Sermon III. Folio LXIII.*

he bears them continually in his heart; and, always contrite in spirit, praying and groaning to the Lord with tears, he seeks pardon and mercy. Nor does he pray only for his own sins, but also for those of the whole world. The chief remedy against the mind’s exaltation is the remembrance of one’s own offenses.

The third mark of humility is that he desires to keep hidden in secret all the good works he does, and conceals his own progress out of zeal for humility. He is not vainly proud, but humble; nor does he seek praise from men, because he hopes for the glory of eternal happiness. Whoever among you considers himself humble and a true monk should not, for God’s sake, disdain this sign of profound submission and abasement.

The fourth indication of true humility is that, voluntarily descending into the abyss of self-contempt, he considers all his brethren, even those openly sinning, better than himself before God; forgetting his own good deeds, he wisely considers his evils, while passing over the evils of others in silence and, when fitting, making known whatever good they have done. This is the condition of true humility: always to admire and proclaim the good works of others, and to make little of and hide one’s own, prudently and secretly, for the avoidance of pride.

The fifth sign of humility, once attained, is that he is quiet in his simplicity and does not speak about the defects of others unless compelled either by the reason of his office or by the command of charity. Yet prudent humility preserves moderation in correction, so that the one whom it cannot make better by correcting him more severely, it endeavors to admonish gently and meekly. It also sometimes wisely passes over rebukes, and waits for and observes a more opportune time.

The sixth sign of humility appears when a monk, out of love of humility, is found slow and not ready to accuse and rebuke the sins of others. For he who is truly humble always considers his own weakness, and silently weighs in secret how many evils he himself has committed against God’s justice. Hence he greatly fears to rebuke the deeds of others, because he recognizes himself as a sinner and therefore unworthy to judge others. From this norm of humility we except the prelates and rulers of monks, insofar as we know them to be bound to the correction of their subjects.

The seventh sign of true humility is that he gladly receives corrections and amendments for his faults and negligences, and is embittered by no grief against the one who rebukes him. Whenever the humble man is rebuked by someone, he rejoices with the prophet in spirit, saying: Let the just man correct me in mercy and rebuke me; but let not the oil of the sinner fatten my head. Of the proud, however, Scripture says: They hated the one who rebuked in the gate. He who is truly humble loves the one who rebukes him, but flees the flatterer.

The eighth indication of true humility is clearly shown when, having been rebuked, he does not defend, deny, cloak, or excuse his fault, but confesses it with simple shamefacedness. The truly humble monk, when he is rebuked, does not murmur; he does not protest that the measure of fairness has been exceeded, but at once, falling to the ground before the judge, cries out most meekly: I have sinned; I confess the fault; I ask pardon; I promise amendment.

The ninth indication of monastic humility makes one mild, sweet, and modest in speech, and not loud in voice. The monk whom you see to be loud and hasty in speaking you will judge not humble, but proud and rash. The humble man speaks modestly and almost under silence, and is always found readier to listen than to speak before others. It is shameful for a monk to raise his voice too much when he speaks, since, crucified to the world, he is commanded to love silence.

He possesses the tenth indication of perfect humility who is accustomed to interpret everything he has heard about his neighbor in the better sense. For our Savior says in the holy Gospel: Do not judge, and you shall not be judged. And again: With what measure you have measured, it shall be measured back to you. The Apostle also says: Do not judge before the time, until the Lord comes, who will both illuminate the hidden things of darkness and make manifest the counsels of hearts. Who are you, who judge another’s servant? To his own lord he stands or falls, and not to you.

The eleventh indication of holy humility is that he despises no man in his heart on account of any excesses whatever, but places all before himself in the merit of sanctity. He also considers all who offend publicly to be better than himself, and for that reason condemns no one. He always thinks within himself, in his heart, what evil he himself has done, but humbly weighs the good deeds of others as surpassing his own. Hence he spurns no one, makes little of no one, despises no one, and desires all to be saved in the Lord.

The twelfth sign of perfect true humility is that he does not desire to be praised by men, but whenever he is commended by someone, he blushes and groans inwardly with sorrow of mind. But if he hears the praises of others recited in the convent, rejoicing with their praise and exulting, he becomes cheerful in spirit. He who is perfectly humble cannot hear his own praises without bitterness of heart, because he has placed his hope wholly in heaven and despises all the glory of this life.

The thirteenth indication of true humility is not only to despise praises and favors in the truth of the mind, but also prudently to avoid those by whom he sees himself being praised. Those who are truly humble flee their praisers, and whenever they are praised, they fall silent and answer nothing. Esteem for a great reputation is burdensome to the humble man, and he is confounded within himself all the more, the more he is proclaimed by men. For true humility, when blamed, makes progress; when praised, it incurs danger.

The fourteenth perfect sign of monastic humility is that, in corrections and detractions, if he suffers any, he conceives spiritual joy and a great increase of divine love. When he is wronged, he is pierced with compunction and is stirred all the more toward contempt of the world. Whenever he is reproved injuriously by someone, if he is humble, he judges himself justly corrected. For he does not consider his own innocence in the accusation, but humbly and contritely weighs how gravely he has offended God in other matters.

The fifteenth indication of perfect humility is that he never resists the one correcting him, but preserves silence in all tranquility of spirit. He does not throw back into his reprover’s face even the faults very well known to him, nor does he seek any grounds for argument. He does not complain that an insult is being done to him; he offers no rash excuse in opposition, nor is he accustomed to rebuke words with words, but, as though mute, to keep silence in all things.

The sixteenth sign of profound humility is that he despises all the consolations of the world and seeks no friendships of mortals. For true humility, which is undertaken for the love of Christ, delights nowhere except in Christ alone. But one cannot remain in the love of Christ unless he has learned to deny both his own will and the world with all its disordered pleasures. Thus the Lord says in the holy Gospel: If anyone comes to me and does not hate his father and mother, wife and children, brothers and sisters, and even his own soul, he cannot be my disciple.

The seventeenth sign of humility is that he does not divide himself among many things; he does not greatly seek the assistance and benefits of men, but humbly remains in his own moderation, or even in extremity. For benefits received from men he is not accustomed to applaud much, but shows himself grateful in few and simple words. Little gifts weaken the spirits of the infirm and often render the unwary useless and excessive in speech. Indeed, he who is truly humble does not delight in the conversations of givers, because they often impede the purpose of purity. The love of Christ does not love publicity.

The eighteenth sign of humility is that he sympathizes from the heart with all who suffer for the love of Christ, patiently bears the defects and weaknesses of others, and prays for the amendment of those who offend. For true humility shows the bowels of pity and compassion toward weak sinners; but false and simulated humility, because it is truly pride, swells against the fragile with harsh and indiscreet severity, and often becomes a cause of danger when in correcting it exceeds the rule of charity. But he who is truly humble always fears to exceed the due measure of correction, and therefore inclines more to mercy than to excessive severity.

The nineteenth indication of true humility perseveres with a mind free from all envy, and favors and prays for all men. He envies no one the gifts of divine kindness; he favors the grace of God in all and rejoices with the progress of others. He judges no one unworthy of God’s benefits; he thinks all better than himself. He considers the advantage of his neighbors his own, because he loves all as himself.

The twentieth sign of perfect humility recalls to memory, with bitterness of heart, the multitude of his sins, and therefore considers himself unworthy of every benefit and grace of God. Hence, seeking the depth of humility, he imitates the publican of the Gospel, who, having the memory of his offenses, did not wish to lift his eyes to heaven. For the monk who is truly humble for the love of Christ always bears in his heart the holy sorrow of compunction and penitence for the evils he has done.

The twenty-first indication of cenobitic humility is that he trusts the judgment of others more than his own, and totally subjects his own will, for Christ’s sake, to another’s command. Let those among you who follow the judgment of their own head consider whether they can or wish to apprehend this sign of monastic humility. For today we discern few observers of true monastic humility, though we see many monks professing it. O times, O morals, in which we all promise humility, and very few of us truly take care to preserve inviolate what has been promised.

The twenty-second sign of cenobitic humility is that he abominates and despises beautiful, new, and costly garments, and is content to have all clothing cast off by others.

[segment 127]
--- Page 136 ---
Sermon M. Fol. LXV.

...he longs to put on. He willingly uses cheaper garments, and takes very great delight in those things which others despise. Beautiful and ornately arranged garments do not befit a monk, but the cheapest ones, by which he may merely defend the body from the harm of inclement weather. For all fastidiousness is forbidden to cloistered persons, who, by reason of the profession they have made before God, are bound to contempt of the world. Humility flees lofty things; it seeks what is lowly.

The twenty-third sign of monastic humility shows a monk voluntarily given over to servile duties and services for the brothers, and ready for all things whatsoever he has been ordered to do. For true humility obeys even inferiors with the readiest will, and is never indignant against one who commands. As much as he is able, he desires to serve all for the love of Christ, because he faithfully loves God with his whole heart. For whatever service he renders to the brothers, he firmly believes it all to have been shown to Christ.

The twenty-fourth sign of humility a monk then obtains in truth, when for the love of God he delights in taking care of the lower offices in the monastery, which the rest despise. We read that many once ascended to this grade of true humility, and we do not doubt that they were of great merit before God. For the Lord says in the holy Gospel: Everyone who humbles himself will be exalted; for God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble. The humble monk imitates Christ; the proud one, the devil.

The twenty-fifth sign of monastic humility is, in any gathering of people, always to seek the lower place for sitting, and not to prefer oneself to an equal or an inferior. It is not for monks to contend proudly over preeminence of place, nor does it befit a soldier of Christ to wish to surpass others in honor. For the true monk, who loves humility, is content with the lowest place and desires to excel no one.

The twenty-sixth sign of humility belongs especially to him who, though learned and educated, because of the humble abasement of himself, hides all doctrine and knowledge. For the monk who is truly humbled is always found readier to listen than to speak. For the divine priest Jerome says: The monk has the office not of one teaching but of one mourning, who either laments himself or the world, and fearfully awaits the coming of the Lord. The arrogance of monks is hateful both to God and to men, and is always liable to sin.

The twenty-seventh sign of humility is perfectly attained by the one who, out of love for regular discipline, delights more in being silent than in speaking. The humble lover of Christ loves silence, and, lest he offend in speech, is accustomed to speak very little. Humility is rarely found among the talkative, and he who is quick to speak casts down his own soul. A loquacious and garrulous monk is not free from pride, and the fool multiplies words. Whoever desires, in the spiritual contest, to overcome his adversary through humility must not neglect to restrain his tongue from much speaking and from every idle word. It is written in the book of Proverbs that in much speaking sin will not be lacking.

Whoever has obtained the twenty-eighth sign of perfect humility will avoid laughter and all disordered gestures of those who guffaw. Mourning, not laughter, especially befits a monk, as one who is dead to all the levities of the world. But the wise man says: The fool raises his voice in laughter; but the wise man will scarcely laugh silently.

The twenty-ninth sign of perfect humility takes its beginning from the fear of the Lord, for whose honor, as much as he is able, he flees and detests every sin. For everyone who does not fear to sin was not truly humble. But the monk who follows Christ according to his strength in true humility is accustomed to flee every sin, and, as much as he can, to exercise himself in the pursuit of virtues. Humility confesses its sin, and as often as it has offended even in the least things, it does not rest until it has amended everything through true penance. He who is truly humble is always found zealous to preserve cleanness of conscience. For the nourishment of humility is charity, which does not permit the one who has it to be idle.

The thirtieth sign of true humility shows a monk confirmed in the purpose of holiness, and makes him subject to all the brothers, even the younger ones. Whatever the pastor commands, he obeys simply; he never debates the commands of a senior, nor asks the reason why he orders it so. If impossible things are commanded, he is ready in spirit; for any hard and arduous things whatsoever, he is prepared without contradiction. He is directed by the counsel of his pastor: he believes him, trusts him, obeys him, and willingly is governed by his command.

You have now heard, my dearest brothers, the thirty signs of true humility. O good God, where are their works? Which of us will boast that he has true humility, so that he suffers no defect in these signs? These, alas for shame, are...
