[segment 129]
*On the arduous way that leads to life.*

...our own times, in which charity has grown very cold; the integrity of holy religion has perished; the devotion of almost all has breathed its last. Our monks seek holiness without the merit of virtues; they wish to have merit without the exercise of labor. Therefore a saint is not found today in the monastery. The fervor of holy compunction in many is either exceedingly rare or lukewarm; for where one lives for flesh and blood, he does not possess the fervor of inward charity. It is not enough for you, O monk, who boast of observance, to have nothing of your own, not to touch a woman, to remain in a monastery under obedience to a pastor, not to eat meat, and in the other external matters of regular discipline to live within the monastic institution, unless with the utmost diligence you also strive to crown your inward life with true charity toward God and neighbor. For charity is the end of regular observance, for the acquisition of which all the other things have chiefly been instituted. Therefore, without the charity of God all things profit you nothing; for it is itself the one and only thing that distinguishes between good and evil works. O monk, monk, whoever you are who live lukewarmly in the monastery without the fervor of charity, do you think that the outward apparatus of discipline can save you? Charity is what sanctifies the one who has it; charity commends the other virtues and all good works to God. Have charity, and all the works of piety that you do will be pleasing to God.

*On the arduous way that leads to life. Sermon 4.*

You have heard, dearly beloved brothers, our Lord Jesus Christ saying in today’s reading from the holy Gospel: “How narrow is the gate and strait the way that leads to life, and few are they who find it.” Is not Christ himself the way, who had said, “I am the way, the truth, and the life”? And how does he now say that it is strait and narrow, when he himself is the one who had said: “Take my yoke upon you and learn, for I am meek and humble of heart. For my yoke is sweet, and my burden light”? What else is the yoke of Christ than the strait and narrow way to the kingdom of God? How, then, is it both light and narrow? Let your charity hear. The yoke of the Lord Jesus is sweet to the good, hard and unbearable to the reprobate. The way of Christ to life is broad and pleasant to the humble who are exercised in charity, but narrow and bitter to the proud. For nothing is so hard, nothing so terrible, nothing so arduous, that it is not easy and pleasant if God is loved. Again, nothing is so light, nothing so easy, nothing so small, that it is not heavy and burdensome if it is turned toward an evil will.

Therefore the Lord said: “Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart.” If you wish to bear my yoke upon you, first learn the meekness and humility of my heart, since unless you are meek and humble, you will by no means be able to carry my burden, as though it were a heavy weight and an unbearable load. I am a humble way; I do not receive the proud. I am a narrow gate; I do not admit the swollen. Unless the soul has despised all worldly things, unless it has inflicted the necessary force upon the pleasures of the flesh, it cannot enter through me into the kingdom, like an animal burdened with sins. I am a humble and meek way, and I receive the humble; I am a strait doorway, and I admit the poor in spirit and the clean of heart.

To eternal life, brothers, we strive by the narrow way of truth; it is necessary that in time we provide for ourselves all things that are necessary for so arduous a journey. There are, moreover, four things especially necessary for travelers, without which the journey is dangerous and one rarely arrives at the goal. The first thing that we say is necessary for travelers we believe to be knowledge of the roads. For one who tries to walk far through trackless places without knowledge of the roads is sometimes entangled in dangerous errors. Therefore let the prudent traveler either first learn the road, or take to himself a companion skilled in the journey, lest he either fall into the hands of robbers or depart from the path of the right road.

Therefore, desiring to return to the homeland of the heavenly kingdom by a very narrow way known to few, we must have as guide and companion of the journey Jesus Christ himself, who called himself the way and the truth. Outside Christ there is no true knowledge; and whoever neglects to have Christ as the companion of his journey runs in vain and without fruit. Therefore let us learn the way of life, dearly beloved brothers, without knowledge of which no one reaches the homeland; no one reaches eternal life. Let us firmly believe that our Lord Jesus Christ is the one mediator between God and men, true God and man, outside whose faith no one will be saved, however innocent he may seem to be in his way of life. For he himself says in the holy Gospel: “He who believes in the only-begotten Son of God has eternal life in...”

[segment 130]
[Page 139]

... remaining in himself. But he who does not believe in the Son: the wrath of God remains upon him. What does it mean that wrath remains upon him, except that the first, original sin is not removed from him? For after the establishment of the New Testament, original sin is removed from those who believe in him by faith in Christ alone. Circumcision was given to the fathers as a sign of future salvation; nevertheless none of them could enter the kingdom of heaven before Christ the Savior rose from the dead. Therefore outside faith in Christ there is no salvation, since, as he himself said, he who has not believed has been judged and will be condemned. We remain in Christ through faith and love, because he is the way of salvation, the truth without error, the life without death. If we have the Lord Jesus as the guide of our journey, we walk confidently.

Secondly, it is necessary for wayfarers to pass through the lands of their journey without injury to anyone; for if they commit theft or robbery, or any other similar sin against the commonwealth, in the end they will come to be punished with penalty and will not reach the intended destination of their journey. But we, brothers, set on the road by which there is return to the homeland of the heavenly kingdom, must take most diligent care that we commit nothing, in word, deed, or thought, contrary to the justice of God, but render to each one his right and due: to God first of all piety in worship and charity; to our neighbor love; and to ourselves the holiness of perfect purity. Indeed, if we live otherwise than Christ taught, do we remain on the straight way of salvation which leads to the kingdom? We shall remain on the way if we keep the commandments of God; we shall walk in the truth if we love the Lord our God himself with all the strength of our mind; we shall come to life if, turning aside from evil, we continually persevere in the practice of good.

Thirdly, it is necessary to have provision for the journey, because if wayfarers lack travelling supplies, they will not be able to reach the place intended. But a prudent wayfarer, before he leaves his house, reckons within himself the time and length of the journey, and prepares travelling supplies and expenses according as necessity requires. A great and hard journey remains for us; we must prepare provisions for many years, lest we fail on this road on which we go. The travelling provision of this journey of ours to the supercelestial homeland is not gold, not silver, not the deceitful riches of the world, but faith in Christ and the love of God. The holy virtues are the unfailing travelling provision of the soul for the kingdom of heaven; strengthened by them, it walks on the way of God in gladness. The soul faithful to Christ and accompanied by the holy virtues gladly completes the hard road of this long pilgrimage. To love God above all things, to exercise oneself constantly in good works, to keep one's conscience in all purity, to do injury to no one: this provides the soul with true travelling provision for the everlasting homeland in heaven.

Fourthly, it is advised as necessary for wayfarers that they not forget their proposed purpose before they come to the end of the journey they have begun. For he must be called a foolish wayfarer who is called back from his journey by children's games and forgets where he was tending. And we, dearest brothers, who have taken up the way of monastic religion, must always think upon our end, since death hastens every day even when we do not notice it, and presents the unprepared as well as the prepared before judgment. Meanwhile let us do good while it is permitted, as much as we can, and let us not delay to be converted to the Lord with all our heart. Christ is the way by which we proceed to the kingdom of God; Christ is the truth in which, remaining, we do not err; Christ is the life for which we hope. Hear, brothers, what he himself says in the holy Gospel: Whoever wishes to come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. This is the way upon which you entered at the beginning of your monastic profession; if you wish to proceed on it rightly and laudably, you will have to love Christ above all things, living purely in regular observance. It is yours, brothers, to love nothing in this world and to chastise the flesh for the love of God. It belongs to monks to despise all the delights and pleasures of the present life and to desire nothing other than the everlasting feasts. It belongs to monks to imitate Christ according to their powers and to shape their life according to the evangelical pattern of purity. To imitate Christ is the highest perfection. The faithful minister of Christ follows his Lord, and where Christ is, there his minister also will be. Therefore whoever among you is a lover of his own salvation, desires to come after Christ to the kingdom of heaven, and wishes to keep and preserve until the end those things which he promised at his entrance into religion, let him keep these things. First, then, before all else, let the monk learn with faith and devotion [unclear].

[segment 131]
--- Page 140 ---

[unclear] to know, and at all times to be piously and soberly occupied with the holy Scriptures. Whatever holy mother Church of the faithful has established as to be believed or observed, let him faithfully and firmly believe and keep, and let him in no way depart from her determination. All who remained outside the ark were once destroyed by the divine flood; Noah alone, with those who were in the ark, escaped safely. The ark prefigured the Church, and the event itself signified a mystery. Therefore there is no salvation for human beings outside the Church, which our Lord Jesus Christ built upon the most firm rock. Every unbeliever is not in the Church; therefore he will necessarily perish, just as those once did who were not in the ark. For all who do not believe in the Lord and Savior of the world, Jesus Christ, Jews, pagans, gentiles, Saracens, together with heretics and schismatics who have withdrawn themselves from the unity of the Catholic Church, have placed themselves outside the ark of salvation; if they persevere in error, without doubt they are to be damned forever with the demons.

Since, therefore, faith is the foundation of salvation, without which it is impossible to please God, the monk must know what he ought to believe, lest it happen that he err. Let him learn the Gospel and the constitutions of the Church; for it says, “He who does not know will not be known.” It is most shameful for a monk not to know the rules of the faith, and to be wholly ignorant of what he is bound to believe for salvation. We do not say that you ought to examine by curious argument the things proposed to you for belief, since, as Saint Pope Gregory says, faith has no merit where human reason supplies proof. But this we admonish: that the monk ought to be studious in the divine Scriptures, so that he may know what holy mother Church commands him to believe and may beware of positions and errors contrary to the faith. Therefore let every monk, as a soldier of Jesus Christ, be firm and stable in the Catholic faith, sober and studious in the Scriptures, and constant in good works.

The sixth thing that we believe necessary for a monk walking in the way of eternal salvation is that he should strive to know himself. For knowledge of oneself is the best of all branches of knowledge, and therefore it is rightly to be preferred to all the studies of mortals. Yet the beginning of this saving knowledge is exceedingly difficult, because very few are those who do not love the world. For no one who follows the world and loves the vanities that pass away can be a knower of himself. Nothing hinders a person more from knowledge of himself than the person himself. He who is fed by the pleasures of his flesh, who is lifted up into pride by the vain knowledge of this world, whether by riches or by honors, is not permitted to come to knowledge of himself. Indeed, to know many things and neglect oneself is not to be wise, but rather to be mad. It contributes to the accumulation of a graver damnation to learn many things and shamefully to be ignorant of oneself.

Let each of you, therefore, consider what he is, whence he is, and what he is going to be. A human being, in himself, is certainly nothing; whatever he is, he is from God and for God. What is a human being but a wayfarer and pilgrim, as it were for one day upon the earth, who today is alive in the world, but tomorrow, snatched away by death, is seen no more? Our whole life, every action, every day, every hour, shows how much we are nothing: by living we die, by acting we are corrupted; as time runs on, we fail and are changed. I compare our unstable, wretched, and weak race of human beings, how windy and vain it is in itself, with those who have gone before us to death. Truly he speaks rightly who compares us to the leaf of a tree. The wind seizes a leaf from the tree and casts it to the ground; in its place another new one afterward grows, subject to a similar fall and succeeding to the same fate. But what has fallen to the ground is dissolved into the ground and never afterward returns to the tree. Hence a certain wise man of the Greeks says: “As is the race of leaves, so too is the race of men.” The race of leaves is very like the race of human beings, for it flourishes for a time and is dissolved into the earth; neither the leaf returns to the tree nor the human being to life.

Now follows the third thing, which I think very necessary for a monk walking in the way of God. The person who understands himself to be a wayfarer in this life of very brief duration truly shows himself humble in all things. For why should earth and ashes be proud? He who truly knows himself cannot be exalted. My brothers, we have no cause at all for self-exaltation and pride, but much and continual cause for humility, since indeed we are earth and a certain compacting of dust, and after a few days are to return again into dust. But because true humility belongs to few, the way of salvation is therefore called narrow and is found by few. By humility one comes to the kingdom, as the Lord says in the holy Gospel: everyone who humbles himself for God’s sake will be exalted, and he who exalts himself will be humbled.

[segment 132]
*Sermon III. Fol. LXVII*

Fourth, for the monk travelling toward heaven it is necessary that he be free from every defilement, since no one is admitted into the kingdom of God unless he is commended by the utmost purity of conscience. In holy virtues and good works conscience must be cleansed, for by them the gate of heaven is opened, and for the peace of God the love of the world is despised. Hence Peter, the most Christian chief apostle, says in his second epistle: “For this very reason, add virtue to your faith; and to virtue, knowledge; and to knowledge, abstinence; and to abstinence, patience; and to patience, piety; and to piety, love of the brethren; and to love of the brethren, charity. For if these things are present in you and abound, they will not leave you empty or without fruit in the knowledge of our Lord Jesus Christ.” Faith, adorned by works of true charity, is one strengthening force for virtue as it stands firm, and the way of the good, which leads souls withdrawn from the flesh not into the fabulous Elysian fields of the poets, but into the angelic choirs.

Fifth, the traveller on the arduous road of the heavenly kingdom must follow the will of God in all things by keeping his commandments. Whatever is said or done must be examined piously and carefully, to see whether it agrees with God and with the ordinances of the Church. But if it is found to be contrary either to God or to the Church, let him flee it at once, lest he fall into sin. It belongs to a wise man to commit nothing unexamined, and before he acts to consider everything carefully by prudent counsel. Whoever on a difficult road wishes to be safe and secure must, according to his strength, imitate the life and conduct of our Lord Jesus Christ. This is the light for all toward eternal life.

Sixth, in our judgment it is necessary for the traveller to love salutary study of the scriptures, to be diligent in reading, and not to be given to vain superstitions, but simple, pure, and upright in mind. It is not fitting for monks to wander without discrimination among the writings of the Gentiles, nor to accommodate divine things shamefully to the fables of the poets. Whoever follows vanity will never be wise, and whoever seeks curiosities will quickly fall away. Let the monk flee every reading of vain superstition, because whatever does not lead to knowledge of the divine scriptures must at all times be avoided by our traveller.

Seventh, in our judgment it is very necessary for the traveller that he prudently direct his desires and thoughts and guard them from every disordered concupiscence. Our unruly little ass must be restrained by the bridle of discipline, lest the flesh draw the spirit headlong into a snare. Whoever thinks that he is walking in the sight of him who always sees all things preserves his conscience pure and watches over his conduct with the greatest diligence. He is always vigilant in the fear of God; he always chastises his little body with fasting and abstinence, for love of the heavenly homeland. The monk who recognizes himself as a pilgrim and stranger in this world never forgets the heavenly homeland, for he sees nothing on the road of the present life in which his soul may take delight. A prudent traveller considers none of the sights along the roads; nothing among passing things delights him in his pilgrimage, but at every time he sighs for the end of the journey. He always thinks sweetly of his homeland; he bears it grievously that his pilgrimage is prolonged or delayed; and therefore he desires to have nothing in this world except the Lord Jesus, in whose sight he earnestly hastens to appear.

Eighth, in our judgment, the perfection of divine charity is necessary for the traveller. For no one will be able to come to the heavenly homeland who departs from the path of charity. The charity of God is the way to God; if anyone neglects to keep it burning within himself, he has wandered miserably from the true road of the heavenly homeland. Scripture says: God is charity; and he who remains in charity remains in God, and God in him. But he who is not in charity has already withdrawn far from the way of salvation; and walking in the darkness of sins, he will never come to the kingdom.

*How Sin Is to Be Fled*

*Sermon V.*

You have heard, my very dear brothers, what Saint John the Apostle says about sinners: “Whoever commits sin is from the devil, for the devil sins from the beginning.” A truly fearful and greatly dreaded sentence. If all who commit sin are from the devil, and we all offend in many things, how many shall we find immune from sin? Yet he says afterward: “Everyone who is born of God does not commit sin, because his seed remains in him; and he cannot sin, because he is born of God.” But he had said before: “If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us.” What does this apparent contradiction mean, that no one of the sons of God is without sin, and yet he who is born of God does not commit sin? There is no contradiction here, because both are true: no one is without sin, since no one is without the law of sin; and he who is born of God does not commit sin, because through the law of the mind, that is, through charity, which is the seed of God, he does not commit sin. For charity covers the multitude of sins; without it there is no good work, and it alone has power to destroy every sin. Hence the holy bishop Augustine says: No saint and no just man lacks sin; yet because of this he does not cease to be just or holy, if he holds holiness with affection. But those sins of the just are not to be thought mortal or enormous, but small and venial, without which life is scarcely lived; for every mortal sin must be far from the just man, if he is to retain the name of justice. Mortal sin separates a person from God, kills the soul, and damns it eternally with the demons. By God’s grace we can avoid this pernicious kind of mortal sin, and by uprightness of will we ought diligently to do so. For this reason, we monks have been made mindful of our deed, so that, living most cautiously under the command of saving obedience, we may not fall into the pit of mortal sin. Therefore the monk must always keep watch in good works, so that he may live free from mortal crime. But whoever on the way of God feels the flesh troublesome and contrary to him must make it subject and, as it were, obedient to the spirit by the remembrance of punishments and by fear.

First, therefore, the monk who loves his own salvation must flee the deceitful world and never permit himself to be caught by the flatteries of the flesh. For what is the world but a great, deep, and restless sea, in which there are as many crawling evils as there are evil desires of men not ordered toward the good? O world, deceitful, deceptive, and unclean, you shamefully deceive those who cling to you; you submerge all who follow you in the depth of hell. How happy are those who despise you for the love of Christ and, making a useful separation from your vanities, depart from you in time. Whatever we perceive in the world is vain, and every lover of the world is made vain and will shortly perish. Think that you will die soon, for nothing so draws people back from sin as frequent and intense meditation on death. For Scripture says: “Remember your last things, and you will not sin forever.” Thinking on the necessity of dying strikes the mind with fear and restrains it from vice.

Second, what persuades one to flee sin is fear of the future judgment, at which all people must be presented. No one can escape this dreadful judgment, but we must all appear there. Two judgments hang over us to be feared, and we shall be able to evade neither of them; necessity compels us to appear at both. One is called particular, in which immediately after death the soul is judged according to its merits, either to glory or to punishment. The other is called general, which will be at the end of the world, where we shall all be judged publicly by the only-begotten Son of God. Each judgment is exceedingly terrible and greatly to be feared by all mortals. Hence the prophet Joel says: “The day of the Lord is great and very terrible; and who will endure it?” Likewise in Ecclesiastes King Solomon says: “All things that are done under the sun God will bring into judgment, for every error, whether it be good or evil.” Likewise in Isaiah it is read thus: “The Lord will come to judgment with the elders of his people and its princes.” And in the book of the prophet Amos it is written thus: “Woe to those who desire the day of the Lord. What is it to you? For that day of the Lord is darkness and not light.” Likewise Zephaniah, prophet of the Lord, foretelling the bitterness of the future judgment, said: “The voice of the day of the Lord is bitter; there the strong man will suffer tribulation. That day is a day of wrath, a day of tribulation and distress, a day of calamity and misery, a day of darkness and gloom, a day of cloud and whirlwind. In the fire of the Lord’s zeal the whole earth will be devoured, because he will make a consummation with haste for all who dwell on the earth.” Keep always in memory, most dear brothers, this exceedingly terrible day, to be dreaded by all mortals, which you believe will certainly come. For the most holy bishop Ambrose says: Nothing is more profitable for an honorable life than to believe that he will be the future judge whom neither the secrets of minds deceive nor dishonorable things delight. For, as Saint Christopher says, we must all be made manifest before the tribunal of Christ, so that each one may receive the things proper to the body, according as he has done, whether good or evil. How greatly that day of the future and most terrible judgment is to be feared, the holy priest Jerome shows when he says: Whether I eat or drink, or do anything else, that voice always seems to sound in my ears: “Arise, you dead, and come to judgment.” Whenever I think of the day of judgment, I tremble in my whole heart and body. Therefore, if there is any joy in the present life, it must be conducted in such a way that the bitterness of the future judgment never withdraws from memory.

[segment 133]
--- Page 142 ---
Sermon V.
Folio LXVIII.

What shall we wretched people say on that day, or where shall we stand before the strict judge, if, as blessed Peter the apostle says, even the just man will scarcely be saved? If Jerome, a just and most holy man, feared the judgment to come, O good God, where will unhappy Trithemius, a sinner, appear? If the just man fears, where will the sinner stand? For what shall we say, or what shall we answer, when we are gravely accused on every side? All the elements will stand against us as testimony; earth and heaven alike will accuse us. Even if all these were silent, each person’s own conscience will be a grave accuser. He who knows all things is ignorant of nothing, for there all things to be judged will be made manifest.

There the evils we have done will be present, accusing us gravely; around us on every side there will stand countless demons as witnesses against us. We shall see open before us the dreadful abyss below; above us we shall behold the judge in wrath; and from every side the utmost danger will hang over us. My brothers, always turn over in the memory of your heart these dangers of the examination to come, and carefully consider how dreadful it is to fall under the future sentence of the judge. In that terrible hour pure hearts will avail more than the sharp words of rhetoricians; a clean conscience will profit a person more than the money of the whole world, for he who will judge all things is not deceived; indeed, he is moved neither by price nor by prayers.

O what incredible horror will invade all the wicked when they are about to hear that fearsome sentence: “Go, accursed ones, into the eternal fire prepared for the devil and his angels.” O worst of all evils, O deepest sorrow of all sorrows: to be separated from the fellowship of the saints and to be tortured forever with demons. Truly the day of judgment is dreadful and fearsome to all, because it is exceedingly bitter; there the remorse of conscience does not die like a worm; there one once damned to punishment is never freed.

Therefore the monk who desires to obtain everlasting salvation should always keep himself, as far as he can, clean from every sin, and that voice, “Go, accursed ones, into the eternal fire,” should never depart from his memory.

Hence the third thing that we consider very useful for turning away from sin is the careful thought of the bitterness of the punishments of eternal damnation. For there is no temptation of carnal pleasure so violent that it will not immediately be overcome and fail, if the one tempted recalls in diligent meditation what the damned suffer in hell. What do the riches once possessed now profit the damned? What do past pleasures contribute to the luxurious placed in hell? What do most empty honors contribute to the proud, or once-broad power to the arrogant? Consider, brothers, what those already damned now suffer in the lower world, and, lest similar things befall you, persevere continually in good and holy works.

If you fear hell, flee sin. For hell will harm no one if a person remains in the faith of Christ and free from sins; nor will he burn in punishment who had not first burned here in guilty desire. Hell is a place to be feared, in which there is no redemption, but whoever has once entered there damned will never again come out to salvation. The wretched cry out in torments and in anguish, placed in everlasting horror, saying: “What now does our pride profit us? What do riches, honors, dignities, luxury, gluttony, and all past carnal pleasures profit us, now that we are damned forever? Behold, all things have passed like a shadow, and all have vanished like the brief dream of a single night. But we have been assigned to these eternal torments, in which we know most certainly there is no redemption.”

In hell, as Augustine testifies, even if there will be the goad of penitence, nevertheless there will be in the damned no correction of the will; in them iniquity will be so blamed that the equity of justice can in no way be loved or desired by them. There the devouring flame of hell burns those whom carnal delight now pollutes, and each person is burned so much more in torments as he delights more here in the flesh against God. In the lower world, as Gregory bears witness, there will be intolerable cold, inextinguishable fire, the immortal worm of conscience, unbearable stench, palpable darkness, inestimable pains, scourges without interruption from those who strike, the dreadful sight of demons, confusion over crimes committed, and despair of all goods. But of all the punishments in hell, that one must be considered by far the gravest: that there the damned are forever deprived of the vision of eternal light.

Here the most blessed bishop John Chrysostom says: “Gehenna is an intolerable thing, and one that all people abhor. For who does not know that that punishment in hell is very horrible? But if someone were to posit a thousand, indeed ten thousand, Gehennas, all that he says is nothing compared with this: for the soul to be exposed to Christ, to be thrust away from the glory of the saints, and to hear that terrible voice from the mouth of the judge of all: ‘Depart from me, workers of iniquity, for I do not know you,’ and, ‘Go, accursed ones, into the eternal fire prepared for you and for the devil.’”

[segment 134]
*Sermon V.*
*Folio LXVIII.*

What shall we wretched ones say on that day, or where shall we stand before the strict Judge, if, as the apostle Peter says, scarcely will the righteous be saved? If Jerome, a righteous and most holy man, feared the judgment to come, O good God, where will Trithemius, an unhappy sinner, appear? If the righteous man has reason to fear, where will the sinner stand? What, then, shall we say, or what shall we answer, when we are gravely accused on every side? All the elements will stand as testimony against us; earth and heaven together will accuse us. And even if all things were silent, each person’s own conscience will be a grave accuser. He who knows all things is ignorant of nothing, for there all things will be manifest to the Judge. The evils we have done will be there, gravely accusing us; innumerable demons will stand around us as witnesses against us. We shall see beneath us that dreadful chaos lying open, above us the Judge angry against us, and on every side eternal danger threatening us.

My brothers, keep these dangers of the future examination always revolving in the memory of your heart, and diligently consider how dreadful it will be to fall under the Judge’s future sentence. In that terrible hour, pure hearts will be worth more than the artful words of rhetoricians; a clean conscience will profit a person more than the money of the whole world, since he who is to judge all things is not deceived; indeed, he is moved neither by price nor by prayers. O what incredible horror will invade all the wicked when they are about to hear that fearful sentence: “Go, accursed ones, into eternal fire, which has been prepared for the devil and his angels.” O worst of all evils, O deepest sorrow of all sorrows: to be separated from the fellowship of the saints and to be tormented forever with demons. Truly the day of judgment is dreadful and fearful to all, because it is exceedingly bitter: there the synteresis of conscience dies by the worm that does not die; there one once consigned to punishment is never freed.

Therefore the monk who desires to attain eternal salvation should, so far as he can, always keep himself clean from every sin, and that voice, “Go, accursed ones, into eternal fire,” should never depart from his memory.

Hence the third thing which we judge very useful for turning away from sin is to think diligently on the bitterness of the punishments of eternal damnation. For there is no temptation of carnal pleasure so vehement that it will not at once be overcome and fail, if the tempted person carefully recalls what the damned now suffer in hell. What do riches once possessed now profit the damned? What do past pleasures confer on the lustful placed in hell? What do the world’s vainest honors confer? What does power once broad confer on the proud? Think, brothers, what those already damned now suffer below; and lest similar things happen to you, persevere continually in good and holy works. If you fear hell, flee sin. For hell will harm nothing if a person has remained in the faith of Christ, free from sins; nor will he burn in punishment who previously here had not burned in guilt.

Hell is a place to be feared, in which there is no redemption; but whoever, once damned, enters there will never again come out to salvation. The damned, wretched in torments, in anguish and everlasting horror, say: “What now does our pride profit us: riches, honors, dignities, luxury, gluttony, and all past carnal pleasures? What do they now profit us, damned forever? Behold, all things have passed away like a shadow, and everything has vanished like the brief dream of a single night. But we have been seized by these eternal torments, in which we know most certainly that there is no redemption.” In hell, as Augustine testifies, even if there will be the goad of penitence, nevertheless there will be no correction of the will in the damned; their iniquities will be blamed by them in such a way that the equity of justice can in no way be loved or desired by them.

There, then, the consuming flame of hell burns those whom carnal delight now pollutes; and each person is burned in torments so much the more as here in the flesh he takes greater delight against God. In the lower regions, as Gregory testifies, there will be intolerable cold, inextinguishable fire, the immortal worm of conscience, intolerable stench, palpable darkness, pains beyond measure, lashes without interruption, the dreadful sight of demons striking them, confusion over committed crimes, and despair of all good things. But of all the punishments in hell, that one must be judged by far the gravest: that the damned are deprived forever of the vision of eternal light.

Here the most blessed father John Chrysostom says: “Gehenna is an intolerable thing, and all people abhor it. For who does not know that punishment of hell to be very horrible? Yet if anyone should set down a thousand, indeed ten thousand gehennas, the whole of what he says is nothing compared with this: for the soul, exposed before Christ, to be driven away from the glory of the saints, and to hear that terrible voice from the mouth of the Judge of all: ‘Depart from me, workers of iniquity, for I do not know you’; and, ‘Go, accursed ones, into eternal fire, which has been prepared for you and for the devil.’”

[segment 135]
*How sin is to be avoided.*

O voice by far the most terrible of all terrible things; O sentence to be feared and exceedingly dreaded by all mortals: to be hated by Christ for one’s deserts, to be reproached by the judge, to be separated from the fellowship of glory, and to retain no further hope of restoration. Consider now, brothers, the punishments of the impious, most certainly to come, and compare all the pains of this life, all the torments of this passing age, and all the imaginable tortures of this world with those everlasting punishments of the damned; and they will be as nothing compared with something, imagination compared with truth, a dream compared with waking. You will easily overcome the temptations of the flesh and the law of sin if you continually keep thoughts of these things in memory. O eternity, what an inconceivable time; being without end, an age without time. Blessed is he who now, in time, foresees how he may fare well in eternity after this very brief life.

The fourth thing that can draw a person back from sin is to have continual remembrance in the heart of future and everlasting happiness. Truly the sufferings of this time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come, which will be revealed in the saints, where there will be no discord, no dissonance at all, but the highest peace of all and the everlasting concord of the rejoicing saints. In the heavenly blessedness of the saints there is true joy without sadness, peace without discord, goodness without malice, knowledge without end, light without darkness, everlasting life without pain. There is sweetness without bitterness, rest without labor, praise without reproach, love without fault, brightness without cloud, satisfaction without weariness. Eternity remains without tedium; goodness abounds without defect; happiness continues without misery. For where God is all in all, perfect and full joy will be present to everyone.

There the soul, or holy mind, is joined to its first principle through love; the intellect is perfectly illuminated for the knowledge of the highest good; and from these two things the enjoyment of eternal happiness is poured into those who rejoice. To know and love God in the kingdom of eternity is to possess, by enjoyment, the very glory of happiness. For this was man created: that he should understand God his creator to be the highest good; that, understanding him, he should love him above all things; and that, loving him, he should enjoy and possess him forever. This is the end of the monastic institution; this is the destination of the Christian faith: so that God, whom we now know through faith and love with our whole heart through works of charity, we may then, in that life, understand perfectly and have as the perpetual object of our enjoyment. For the attainment of this everlasting happiness, dearest brothers, all good things must be done, even with the greatest labor; all adversities must be endured most patiently, since the very brief exercise of virtue brings forth for us an eternal reward. Hear what Saint John the bishop says on this matter: “If every day,” he says, “we had to endure torments; if, finally, we had to bear Gehenna itself for a little time, so that we might be able to see Christ reigning in eternal glory and be joined forever to the number of his saints, nothing sorrowful that could happen to us would, by any comparable reckoning, be worthy, if by it we might deserve to possess the glory of so great a good.”

But one does not arrive at the highest good except through the greatest labors, since, as truth itself says in the holy Gospel, “the kingdom of heaven suffers violence, and the violent seize it.” The fifth thing that can draw a person back from all sins is to consider the dignity of the human condition, which by the natural guidance of reason detests vice and loves virtue. Every human being who lives according to the command of reason is a lover of virtue; but he who has chosen to become filthy in the dung of vices lives like a beast, not like a human being. But for you, my brothers, vices must above all be detested and virtues loved, since for this reason you have despised the world, that you might live profitably in the monastery, a life estranged from sins and adorned with holy virtues. Therefore virtues must be cultivated by you, not images of virtues; I say cultivated not with incense, not with sacrifice, but with love and with the purpose of a most upright will. For to cultivate virtues is nothing other than to embrace them in the soul and, for the love of God, to keep them inviolate even unto death. For as Lactantius says, the whole duty of virtue is not to sin; and therefore there is no true virtue except that which, through faith in Christ, tends to that end where the highest good of man is, than which nothing can be better. But he is not virtuous who does not love God, since charity is the sum of all virtues. Therefore, if you love God with your whole heart above all things, you are truly virtuous. Virtue is the one way of the good, by which, through faith and love of Jesus Christ, one comes to the kingdom. He who truly loves God does not sin, out of love of virtue. Consider your dignity, my brothers, and flee sin, since everyone who is swollen with pride, who is defiled by lust, who is infected by the other plagues of the soul, does not live as a human being, but is confounded in himself and proceeds into the punishment of eternal death. For the more deeply anyone slips into sin, the farther he withdraws from God and the nearer he approaches to hell. But since all human beings naturally seek the good, it is most shameful to love sin, since every sinner abandons the good and turns himself toward evil.

[segment 136]
--- Page 144 ---

*Sermon V.*

Sixthly, it strongly draws the mind back from sin if, by habit, it always remains occupied in meditation on the Lord’s Passion. Who will be able to devote his efforts to the pleasures of the flesh, if he has considered Jesus Christ, the ruler of the world, hanging for him on the cross? The death of Christ freed the world from eternal death; whoever carries this benefit with him in memory will easily abstain from every wickedness of the vices. Finally, if anyone considers, with the whole attention of his mind, that God is present to him in every place, knowing and understanding all things, he will without doubt always abhor every sin. For God is everywhere with us, not only present, but truly most inward and most present; no secret escapes him, nothing remains hidden from him, but everything lies plainly manifest to him. He searches the hearts of all; he knows the most secret thoughts, since he alone has true knowledge of all things. For who among mortals, in the presence of so great a majesty, which he believes sees all things, would dare without the greatest shame, if he weighs who and how great he is who sees? Therefore, whenever some wicked thought troubles the mind, whenever the disordered motions of the flesh disturb the soul, think of the Lord God, who is the avenger of evils, as present; recall to memory the bitterness of the Passion of Jesus Christ; devoutly implore the aid of heavenly grace. He is harder than stone whom these things, diligently considered, have not been able to call back from the intention of sinning.

The seventh thing that calls the mind of the studious monk back from the desire of sinning is that, by continual reading, he should go through the passions of the holy martyrs. For indeed, whoever often recalls to memory the unconquered constancy of the holy martyrs of Christ cannot fail to become stronger and more robust in resisting sins. For this reason the brave deeds of the saints were committed to writing: so that, whenever we who are weak read such things, we may rise up to imitate those who have gone before. Do not look, brothers, to those who with proud rashness deny credence to the histories of the saints, and think that the glorious things narrated about the most blessed friends of Christ are fables. For the virtues and good qualities that they do not find in themselves, they scorn to believe that the saints of God possessed. For there are many, especially among those who think themselves great because of their learning, who reject the histories of the holy fathers far too rashly; and since they themselves cannot enter upon the arduous path of virtue, they invent with windy loquacity that no one, or only a few, could have done so. Whatever is read before them about the lives of the saints, whether lofty or great, they cry out that it is invented and fabulous; and, being themselves placed in the darkness of vices, they judge that those whose histories they mock went before them just as they themselves live. But the prudent and devout monk will not be moved by the sloth of the proud, since he well understands that an effective power of exhortation is present in the histories of the saints. For, to confess meanwhile what I feel about myself, I am moved more toward the devotion of inward fervor by the life of one saint than by many curious sermons, because, as the most holy pope Leo says, examples are more powerful than words, and it is more to teach by deed than by voice. But just as not all things please all people, so not all things are suited to all; we advise each person to read more frequently and more zealously that by whose reading his mind is stirred to greater perfection.

The eighth thing that most powerfully draws a man back from sin is charity, concerning which the holy apostle says: Charity is patient, is kind; it does not act perversely, is not puffed up, is not ambitious, does not seek the things that are its own, is not provoked, does not think evil, does not rejoice over iniquity, but rejoices together with the truth; it bears all things, believes all things, hopes all things, endures all things. Charity never falls away. He who loves God flees sin; but he who is a servant of crimes assuredly does not love God. A lover of God does not commit sin; and he who truly loves his Lord and always beholds him as present does not work iniquity. God is the highest and unchangeable good; for love of him sin must both be fled and detested, since one who fears to sin only from fear of punishments does not ascend to the heights of charity. For a slave does not sin through fear of the rod; but a son withdraws from evil out of love for his father, lest he offend his father’s heart. Let us avoid sin, my brothers, and live in the sight of the Lord with as much purity of soul as we can, since just as deadly poison, once taken, kills the body, so sin kills the soul of the offender. Hence the wise man admonishes us in Ecclesiasticus, saying: Flee sins as from the face of a serpent, because if you come near to them, they will receive you. The teeth of a lion are the teeth of sin, killing the souls of men. Like a two-edged sword is the iniquity of man; for its wound there is no healing. In Ezekiel also

[segment 137]
--- Page 145 ---

Sermon V. Folio LXIX.

Sixth, you will draw your mind back from sin very strongly if you keep it always occupied, as a matter of habit, in meditation on the Lord’s passion. For who will be able to devote himself to the pleasures and works of the flesh, if he considers that Jesus Christ, ruler of the world, suffered on the cross for him? Christ, by his cross, freed the world from perpetual death; whoever has fixed so great a benefit in his memory will easily abstain from every wicked deed. Finally, if anyone has understood and considered with his whole inner mind that God is present to him in every place, knowing and understanding all things, he will without doubt always abhor every sin. And indeed God is everywhere present to us, not only most present, but truly most intimate and most near; no secret is hidden from him, nothing remains concealed from him, but everything is present and manifest to him. He searches the depths of all things; he knows the most secret thoughts; he alone has true knowledge of all things. What mortal, in the presence of so great a majesty, which he believes sees all things, would dare without the greatest shame to be anything other than he judges that he ought to appear? Therefore, whenever some troublesome thought disturbs the mind, whenever disordered movement of the flesh unsettles the soul, think that the Lord God, who is the avenger of evils, is present everywhere; recall to memory the bitterness of the passion of Jesus Christ; fill your soul with the sweetness of heavenly grace. Harder than stone is the man whom these things, diligently considered, cannot call back from readiness to sin.

The seventh thing that recalls the mind of a holy monk from the desire of sin is the continual reading of the passions, deeds, and martyrdoms of the saints. For truly, whoever often calls to memory the unconquerable constancy of Christ’s holy martyrs cannot fail to become readier and stronger for resistance. Therefore the brave deeds of the saints were once committed to writing so that we, weak men, reading them, might rise up to imitate those who have gone before. Let the brethren not regard those who, with proud rashness, deny faith to the histories of the saints and think the glorious things narrated about Christ’s most blessed friends to be fables. For the virtues and good things that they do not find in themselves, they refuse to believe that God’s saints possessed. Indeed, many, and especially those among them who judge themselves great because of their learning, rashly reject the histories of the holy fathers; and since they themselves cannot enter upon the hard road of virtues, they claim with reckless loquacity that no one, or only very few, could do so. Whatever they read about the lives of the saints as arduous or great, they cry out that it is invented and fabulous. As they themselves live, placed in the darkness of vices, so they suppose those histories which they mock to have proceeded. But a prudent monk and lover of honor is not moved by the sloth of the proud, since he well understands how great an efficacy for exhorting holiness lies within history.

Meanwhile, it is clear enough that, if I may say so, I am moved by the devoted affection of holy fervor toward one thing: that, amid the many writings of the curious, the deeds of the saints should be read. For, as most holy Pope Leo says, examples are stronger than words, and it is more to teach by deed than by voice. But just as not all things please everyone, so not all things suit everyone. Each person should be advised frequently and diligently to read that by whose reading his mind is stirred to greater progress.

The eighth thing that most strongly draws a man back from sin is charity, concerning which the holy apostle says: Charity is patient, is kind; it does not act perversely; it is not puffed up; it is not ambitious; it does not seek what is its own; it is not provoked; it does not think evil; it does not rejoice over iniquity, but rejoices with the truth; it bears all things, believes all things, hopes all things, endures all things. Charity never fails. Whoever loves God flees sin; but whoever is a servant of crimes certainly does not love God. Love of God does not cause a man to sin; and whoever truly loves his Lord and always turns back to him in repentance does not work iniquity. For God is the highest and unchangeable good; for love of him sin is to be fled and detested. For whoever, through fear of long-lasting punishments, is afraid to sin, has not climbed to the summit of charity. A servant does not sin through fear of the blow, but a son withdraws from evil because of perfect love, lest he offend his father’s heart.

Let us flee the snares of sin, my brothers, and with as much purity of soul as we can let us live in the sight of the Lord, because just as literal venom, when taken, kills the body, so sin, when committed, kills the soul. Hence the wise man admonishes us in Ecclesiasticus, saying: Flee sin as from the face of a serpent, for if you come near it, it will seize you. The teeth of a lion are the teeth of sin, killing the souls of men. All iniquity is like a two-edged sword; there is no healing for its wound. In Ezekiel also it is said: The soul that has sinned, that soul shall die. With all solicitude we must always keep watch, lest by meditating on sin we be divided from God. For charity fixed in God is the form for overcoming every sin, because, as the most holy evangelist John says, everyone who is born of God does not commit sin; but whoever sins...

[segment 138]
--- Page 146 ---

How Sin Is to Be Fled.

He does not imitate God, but the devil, the first author of sin. Let us love God, and sin will not have dominion in us; let us love Christ, and we shall do nothing contrary to our salvation. Nothing is better, nothing stronger against sin, nothing safer, than to love God, who first loved us and washed us from all sins in the blood of his only-begotten Son.

For we are bound to God, and we are obliged to love him with all the strength of our heart for many reasons. First, God is to be loved supremely by us for no other cause than for himself, who is the highest and only good; in him alone is rest and salvation for the soul, and without him there is no salvation, no rest, no blessedness, no happiness, either in heaven or on earth. For since God is the cause, maker, and beginning of all things, he alone is the object of the soul's blessedness; eternal enjoyment of him is reached by charity alone, and one comes to love of him by purity of mind alone. Therefore, since God is the soul's one and highest good, he is to be loved by us for his own sake with all our strength. Outside God there is nothing good, nothing holy, nothing by which the soul can be gladdened forever. For God alone is the center of the soul's blessedness.

Second, we are commanded to love the Lord our God because he deigns to show himself to us as a most loving father. For thus he taught us to pray: "Our Father, who are in heaven, hallowed be your name." Truly God is the father of us all: he created us all, nourishes us, feeds us, and preserves us in being lest we perish. There has never been any father on earth so indulgent that he loved his only-begotten son with as much charity as God almighty, our Father, loves, cherishes, directs, and attends us with fatherly affection. And therefore we are bound to love above all things, with a pure heart, him who gives us both being and life. For he is our Father; and Adam, who was created first of all human beings, did not know us.

Third, we ought to love God above all things because of the likeness: man was made in the image of God, following his Creator by a certain likeness in intellect. God, however, is perfect, infinite, and everlasting intellect; by participating in his goodness through grace, our intellect is made blessed in him who created all things. For although there is no comparison between God and man, nevertheless the rational soul itself has, from the nobility of its created condition, a likeness to its own principle that is of no contemptible dignity. For the prophet says: "I have said: you are gods, and all of you sons of the Most High." The Lord God also said in the beginning: "Let us make man in our image and likeness." Great is the condescension of divine kindness toward our race, and we ought to remember it at all times with a pure mind in the observance of charity.

Fourth, we ought to love our God above all things because of his inestimable munificence, when we recognize that he has bestowed so many benefits upon us, who did not deserve them. When we did not exist, he first established us in being by his goodness, and ordained us for a share in his glory, which will never end. Thus Scripture says of man's nobility: "You made him a little lower than the angels; with glory and honor you crowned him, and you set him over the works of your hands." Do you wish to know, brothers, in what respect man is less than an angel? Attend carefully to what we say.

There are two intellects created from the beginning, the angelic and the human. The angelic intellect, immediately from the beginning of its creation, was made full and perfect in the actuality of all knowable things, and it knew and comprehended by nature all that it ought to know. But the human intellect first exists in potency toward all knowable things, ignorant of all things; in the end, however, through many actions, it finally acquires knowledge of all things, like the angelic intellect. For the perfect consummation of nature which belongs to the angelic intellect from the beginning will be granted to the human intellect at the end. Hence between these two created intellects there is plainly only this difference: the angelic intellect is what it can be, while the human intellect is to receive, at the end, that is, after the death of the flesh, the likeness of angelic perfection.

For the angelic intellect, which was created first, was created without any action of its own, immovably, substantially, and by nature, full and perfect in knowledge of all things. Those angels who adhered by love to their principle, namely God, have been confirmed in eternal happiness; but those who, glorying in themselves, withdrew in affection from God are perpetually miserable and unhappy. The human intellect, therefore, because it was created at the beginning in ignorance and privation of all things, must necessarily come at last, by action, motion, and art, to the knowledge of things set before it.

As long as we are in the body and are pilgrims away from the Lord, our intellect, because of its excessive commerce with the body, is confused as though placed in the darkness of ignorance, and is by no means illumined to perfect knowledge of future things. But after the soul has been separated from the body, then the intellect will rejoice in angelic perfection. Therefore we are distant from the angels only in proportion as we share in bodily impurity.

Fifth, God is to be loved by us above all because he first loved us and washed us

[segment 139]
Sermon VI. Folio LXX.

...from our sins in the blood of his only-begotten Son. The Creator most greatly loves his creature, which he brought forth from nothing; it is fitting that the creature itself should most greatly love its Creator, by whose goodness it was brought into being. God loves us with the greatest charity, than which none greater can be spoken or conceived; and he loves us so infinitely that he neither ought nor could love more, since in God all things are perfect, and in him there is no capacity for change or shadow of alteration. Consider now, my brothers, with how great a fervor of mind and with how great a desire of heart the devout person ought to love him, by whom he is loved even without measure in the highest degree of love. And let us give back our soul in love for him above all things, as much as we can: for he is worthy that we love him with all our strength. Yet however much we love him, we shall by no means come to that measure by which he himself loves us. For he loves us as worthily as possible; nor can his love toward us grow greater, since he already perfectly loves his creature. But however much we love him, we can always make further progress in loving, and love him more ardently even to the point of death.

O we weak and sluggish people, we who are committed to a prison of clay: what shall we repay to so great a lover, who has gone before us by so great a degree of love, when there can be no comparison of the creature with the Creator? The sixth thing that deservedly ought to stir us to the love of God is the consideration of his supreme kindness toward us: that, after so many thousands of men had been passed over and left in error, he mercifully called us in Europe, along with a few others, to the knowledge of the Christian faith. Of all benefits after the death of Christ, I judge this to be the chief: that he caused us to be Christians in the faith. For outside the faith of Christ no one will be saved. Christ is proclaimed here, but in many places he is utterly unknown. How many kingdoms there are today in Asia and Africa, how many peoples, how many islands of the sea, where there is no knowledge of Christ at all and no faith; where every religion is in error and without the merit of salvation, even if some outside Christianity seem to adore and worship one God. For Christ is the Savior of faithful souls; whoever has not believed in him has already been judged, and without doubt will be condemned. How shall we ever be able to offer God worthy thanks, because he established us, so to speak, to be born in the Catholic faith, and by the blood of his only-begotten Son freed us from the destruction of death? Therefore let us be mindful of so great a benefit, and let us take care to use it in such a way that we are not judged for ingratitude. Let us bless God the Father at every time, because he has poured out such great spiritual goods upon us, and has not permitted us to be born removed from the unity of the Catholic Church.

The seventh thing that is believed able to produce the love of God in our hearts is considered in the promise of eternal happiness. For if we consider how great are the goods in heaven which he has promised to give us, then love for him will deservedly burn sweetly in our hearts. He has promised us his whole self, he who alone is the highest and wholly unchangeable good. Since he is indivisible, he will grant himself to be enjoyed by all in such a way that nevertheless he will be possessed whole by each. He loves all by whom he is loved, and he loves each by whom he is held. The more we now love God with a pure mind, the more there we shall receive what was promised.

On the Knowledge of Spiritual Grace. Sermon Six.

The holy apostle of Christ Paul teaches us that we cannot always believe every spirit, but that promptings must be diligently examined, whether they are from God, who is light, or whether something hostile rises up against us from the side of darkness. For since our adversary is very cunning, he never leaves anything untried, and devises all manner of ways by which he may catch us off guard. He attacks those who are already making progress in one way, and deals in another way with those who still live in the torpor of negligence. The more we have advanced in the commandments of God, the more necessary it is that we keep watch against the devil plotting against us. Indeed, he pursues those who flee temptations, and rages with envious malice against those who desire to please God. He is accustomed to assail secretly, under the appearance of good, those whom he knows he cannot overcome by open assault. He is accustomed to suggest many things to those making progress and still insufficiently grounded in the fear of God, by which he very often tries to trip up unguarded minds under the appearance of good. Against his snares the best remedy is this: that the monk submit all the thoughts of his heart to the examination of the elders. For the demons hate public light, and are ashamed that the thoughts which they suggest should come to the knowledge of spiritual elders. Therefore when any of you thinks that he stands firm in good, let him be anxious lest he be tripped up by the devil. If there is among you someone who in years and in habit...

[segment 140]
--- Page 148 ---
On the knowledge of spiritual grace.

Young men, both in years and in monastic conversion, who consent to their own sensual appetites more than is expedient, are mocked by demons in various ways, as if under the appearance of good. They seek sanctity without merit; they desire to have merit without the necessary exercise of virtues. They seem to themselves great and outstanding before God in devotion, yet in their animal passions they are not mortified.

You are in error, O wretched men, and you shamefully deceive yourselves, who think to build the tower of sanctity without the foundation of most humble subjection and obedience. First the disordered desires of sensuality must be mortified; the animal passions must be restrained: anger, murmuring, the spite of indignation, and all impatience. For as long as vices dominate you, whatever gifts of private singularity you offer are not pleasing to God. All things are empty where conscience is not pure.

The way of life of a young monk is suspect to us, however devout it may seem, if the humility of voluntary subjection does not commend it. For a monk without humility and obedience cannot be good, even if by fasting and prayer he hunts for sanctity every day. The devil deceives many in the secrecy of elation and pride, whom he permits continually to keep vigil, fast, and pray. For just as no works without humility and charity are pleasing to God, so the fasts, vigils, prayers, and studies of monks without these are not to be feared by the devil. This does not move the devil, because he is not tormented by it. He does not reckon much the vigils of the self-exalted, nor does he heed the fast of the swollen with pride, nor fear the prayers of the proud.

But if he finds you humble; if he finds you cheerfully obedient in all things to the commands of your elders; if he observes that you do great things and think humble things about yourself; if he weighs that charity toward God and neighbor burns in you, then certainly he is moved against you, because his envy is tormented by your virtues. He turns against you all the weapons of his cunning that he can, and with all his strength he tries to overturn your purpose of holiness. For nothing in a monk displeases demons more, nothing torments them more violently, than if for love of God he has truly humbled himself in all things and has always kept the service of monastic institution under the guard of obedience with a cheerful spirit.

You must beware the promptings of demons, you who think yourselves something great without the merit of true virtues. I see that you are unwilling toward the commands of your elders, impatient and murmuring under correction, quick to rebuke another's life, but exceedingly slow in considering your own defects. These are the surest signs of the promptings of demons, and manifest indications of necessary mortification despised and of self-will. You believe yourselves to be perfect and holy practitioners of great religion and inward devotion, yet you have neither brotherly charity nor true humility, which are necessary in you as monks. You seek sensible devotion in prayers, and with tearful eyes already think yourselves God's friends.

What then? Do tears in prayers make a person holy, when prayer is made with a certain elation of mind and without true humility? God gives tears; the soul struck by adversities gives them; the devil, deceiver of the self-exalted, also gives them. God makes the mind humbled in true charity fruitful, like a meadow, with sweet tears; nature moves one to weep in adversities; the devil pours little streams of waters upon the proud under the likeness of devotion.

Humility is the one virtue which, in the breadth of charity, makes human hearts truly devout. Nothing that you do in your private singularity profits you, O monk, if you do not have true humility together with the fervor of twofold charity. For to rise before others for the nocturnal lauds, to say long prayers in private beyond the custom of the order, to continue a fast beyond the rule's prescription while others are lawfully eating, to remain in seclusion while the rest go out to common labor: two things make such acts meritorious. If they are lacking, all will be altogether fruitless. Indeed, whatever work of a monk charity and humble obedience do not commend will be judged empty of merit before God and will lack reward.

But you who scorn the precepts of your elders with haughty brow, who do not do what they command but what pleases you by your own rashness, who set your own inventions before the common holy exercises of the order, are still far removed from the way of life of the saints. It is not permitted you to abandon by your own rashness the things that belong to monastic institution, and to prefer to necessary things the new and empty things that please you. Whatever work you do is ungrateful to God if you despise that to which you are bound.

The inspiration of divine grace teaches humility, persuades obedience, and follows true charity in all things. It keeps the commandment; it admits no pride; it despises no one, but pursues the perpetual integrity of regular discipline. But the prompting of the devil, under the appearance of good, craftily trips up the unwary monk and makes him subject in all things to his own judgment. For everything that pertains to the preservation of monastic integrity, he falsely says is badly instituted and contrary, and therefore to be set aside in favor of his own pursuits.

[segment 141]
On the recognition of spiritual grace.

...they despise all the consolations of the present life. The soul that has God present within itself through love despises all comforts of the flesh; it desires to come to its Lord through hard and bitter things. It does not love the riches of this world; it spurns transitory honors and dignities; it resolutely contemns the pleasures of the flesh. It meditates always on the law of the Lord and never forgets the most bitter Passion of Jesus.

Who among you, brothers, is there who does these things, who detests all the little consolations of this age, who thinks of nothing else, seeks nothing, desires nothing, except the charity of God? Whoever has obtained this sign of the presence of the Holy Spirit, if he perseveres to the end of life, will be saved.

The eighth indication of a good course, and the true sign of the presence of the Holy Spirit, we receive when we humbly conform our wills in all things to the divine ordinances. This degree of spiritual progress separates the monk from all cares of the world and calls him wholly to inward things. For his will is peacefully subjected, by the affection of the mind, to the divine will, and is separated far from all desires that war against the spirit.

Whoever has received this gift of the grace of the Holy Spirit should first give thanks with humility, and should not vainly exalt himself because of the gift; rather, the more he has advanced, the more he should learn to humble himself in all things. Often, however, a man deserves to lose the grace of God because he neglects to preserve it in the vessel of true humility. Therefore almighty God sometimes withdraws for a short time from those making progress the grace of sensible devotion, so that humility may persevere in the mind and the fervor of desire may burn more strongly for seeking further. But since there are many causes in human beings for which the grace of God is sometimes withdrawn, it will be worthwhile for them to be stated in order.

First, then, the grace of God is withdrawn from a person because of sin, when the conscience is stained by the ugliness of vices. For our God, as Scripture says, is jealous: either he alone is loved, or he is not loved with any companion. Therefore, as often as the mind turns back in disordered desire either to itself or to anything else, it is alienated and removed from its source, which is God; and so it deserves to lose the grace it had, because by consenting to sin it was unwilling to keep that grace inviolate.

Hence the holy apostle of Christ, Paul, exhorts us not to receive the grace of God in vain. For it is then received fruitlessly and in vain when the mind of the recipient is made unworthy by the filth of vices. The little vessel into which we receive the grace of God is our conscience. When it is cleansed from vices and adorned with holy virtues, it becomes a worthy dwelling of the Holy Spirit and produces great progress in us. But if the vessel of our conscience has been defiled by the corruption of sin, immediately the Spirit of God withdraws with all the gifts of grace.

Is not our Lord Jesus Christ the lover and faithful bridegroom of the soul, which he purchased for himself by the outpouring of his own blood? With what face, with what rashness, then, can you, O man, despise so great a lover and savior of yours, and love anything else in this world? God, who is your creator, loves you supremely and by his death has freed you from everlasting destruction. What will you repay him, O mortal man, from whom you have received such great benefits? He asks nothing of you, seeks nothing, demands nothing, except gratitude, affection, and charity. Guard innocence and purity of conscience, lest you lose the impulse of grace.

The second cause, indeed, on account of which grace is withdrawn from a person is called the pride of ingratitude. God, as Scripture testifies, resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble. By the just judgment of God, the grace of the Holy Spirit is lost whenever the one who has it is lifted up into pride. For everyone who humbles himself will be exalted, and he who exalts himself will be humbled. Hence almighty God again says: Upon whom will my Spirit rest, except upon the humble and quiet, and the one who trembles at my words? The more anyone has humbled himself in true purity of conscience for the love of Christ, the more fit he will be to receive the grace of the Holy Spirit. But as often as the mind is raised up in pride, the spirit of devotion is deservedly taken away, as from an unclean vessel.

Therefore, my brothers, let no one grow proud over the grace of the Holy Spirit that he has received; let no one vainly exalt himself, because unless we are humble we shall by no means be able to share in the mercies of the Lord. For, as the holy lover of Christ, Paul, says: he who thinks himself to be something when he is nothing deceives himself.

Third, the grace of devotion is withdrawn from a person because of sloth...
