--- Page 150 ---

*On the knowledge of spiritual grace.*

... they despise the comforts of the present life. The soul that has God present within itself through love despises all consolations of the flesh; it longs to come to its Lord through hard and bitter things. It does not love the riches of this world; it spurns passing honors and dignities; it firmly contemns the pleasures of the flesh. It always meditates on the law of the Lord and never forgets the most bitter passion of Jesus Christ. Who among you, brothers, does these things: who detests all the little consolations of this age; who thinks of nothing else, seeks nothing else, desires nothing else than the charity of God? Whoever has obtained this sign of the presence of the Holy Spirit, if he perseveres to the end of life, will be saved.

We receive the eighth indication of a good course, and the true sign of the presence of the Holy Spirit, when we humbly conform our wills in all things to the divine ordinances. This degree of spiritual progress separates the monk from all cares of the world and calls him wholly to inward things. For his will is subjected, by the affection of a peaceful mind, to the divine will, and it is far separated from all desires that war against the spirit. Whoever has received this gift of the grace of the Holy Spirit should first give thanks with humility, and should not vainly exalt himself because of the gift; rather, the more he has advanced, the more he should learn to humble himself in all things. For a man often deserves to lose the grace of God because he neglects to keep it in the box of true humility. Therefore almighty God sometimes withdraws the grace of sensible devotion from those making progress for a short time, so that humility may persevere in the mind and the fervor of desire may burn more ardently to seek more. But since there are many causes in people for which the grace of God is sometimes withdrawn, it will be worthwhile to state them in order.

First, the grace of God is withdrawn from a person because of sin, when the conscience is stained by the foulness of vices. For our God, as Scripture says, is jealous: either he is loved alone, or he is not loved with any companion. Therefore, whenever the mind turns back by desiring either itself or something else in a disordered way, it is removed, alienated from its source, which is God. Hence it deserves to lose the grace it had, because by consenting to sin it refused to keep it inviolate. Therefore the holy apostle of Christ, Paul, exhorts us not to receive the grace of God in vain; for it is indeed received fruitlessly and in vain when the mind of the recipient is stained by the filth of vices. The little vessel into which we receive the grace of God is our conscience; when it is purged of vices and adorned with holy virtues, it becomes a worthy dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit and generates great progress in us. But if the vessel of our conscience has been defiled by the rottenness of sin, immediately the Spirit of God departs with all the gifts of grace.

Is not our Lord Jesus Christ the lover and faithful spouse of the soul, which he purchased for himself by the pouring out of his own blood? With what face, with what rashness can you, O man, despise this lover and your savior, and love anything else in this world? God loves you supremely: he is your creator, and by his death he freed you from everlasting destruction. What will you repay him, O mortal man, from whom you have received such great benefits? He asks nothing from you, seeks nothing, demands nothing, except gratitude, affection, and charity. Keep innocence and purity of conscience, lest you lose the prompting of grace.

The second cause for which grace is withdrawn from a person is called the pride of ingratitude. For God, as Scripture testifies, resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble. By the just judgment of God, the grace of the Holy Spirit is lost whenever one is lifted into pride by what he possesses. For everyone who humbles himself will be exalted, and whoever exalts himself will be humbled. Hence almighty God again says: “Upon whom shall my Spirit rest, if not upon the humble and quiet, and the one who trembles at my words?” The more anyone humbles himself for the love of Christ in true purity of conscience, the more fit he will be to receive the grace of the Holy Spirit. But whenever the mind rises into pride, spiritual devotion is deservedly taken away from the unclean vessel. Therefore first, my brothers, let no one grow proud of the grace of the Holy Spirit which he has received; let no one vainly exalt himself, because unless we have been humble, we will by no means be able to share in divine inspirations. As the holy Christ-loving Paul says: “He who thinks himself to be something, when he is nothing, deceives himself.”

Third, the grace of devotion is withdrawn from a person because of laziness and acedia, when the mind is hardly exercised at all in good works. For, as Scripture says, idleness is the enemy of the soul, and everyone who follows idleness will be in want. The wise man also says: “Desires kill the lazy man; his hands have refused to work at anything.” Therefore, just as fire is extinguished without fuel, so the spirit of devotion grows weak without the continual exercise of charity. Let no one, then, presume upon his own powers in this life; let no one settle down securely in his former way of life, because, I say, as long as someone can be made better from worse, he can also be made worse from better. For our trial is swift...

[segment 143]
On the Knowledge of Spiritual Grace

Third, if you do not wish to lose the grace of inward compunction, strive to love the quiet of solitude. For divine Scripture says: “Upon whom shall my spirit rest, unless upon the humble and quiet one?” And again in another place it says: “In silence and in hope shall your strength be.” For the grace of inward compunction is quickly lost when the secrecy of solitude is not loved. Therefore let the monk who wishes to possess the grace of God learn gladly to remain with himself in his cell.

Fourth, one must take the utmost care that you do not despise any person in your heart, however bad he may be, because the grace of compunction is withdrawn by the just judgment of God whenever a rash judgment concerning one’s neighbor is not avoided. For the Lord says in the holy Gospel: “Do not judge, and you will not be judged; do not condemn, and you will not be condemned.”

Fifth, the monk who does not wish to lose the grace of inward devotion should strive to exercise himself in the practice of all the virtues. Let him always keep watch in the fear of the Lord, always occupy himself in the exercise of divine love, be continually diligent in good works, and at all times, as much as he can, be devoted to the memory of the Lord’s passion. For attentive meditation on Christ’s passion strengthens a person against all vices and preserves the grace of compunction.

Sixth, be constant in holy prayers, because one who neglects the practice of prayer cannot sufficiently guard the grace of compunction. For the Savior says in the holy Gospel: “Watch and pray, that you may not enter into temptation.” The Lord’s help is ready for those who call upon him; but the person who neglects to pray loses grace. But if you have lost the grace of devotion in any way, you will quickly recover it by the following six remedies.

First, consider that without the grace of God you cannot make progress toward salvation, and that in all the good things you are going to do you have the greatest need of his mercy. Therefore recognize that you are unworthy of the grace of God for doing good, and know that, because of the multitude of your sins, you are unworthy. Call upon him, because he is near; and make every time that is granted to you as free from the common exercises of the brethren pass usefully and fruitfully. The grace of God is necessary for you to do good; it is acquired by the breadth of charity, guarded by humility, and recovered by the practice of saving penitence. The Lord is with you as long as you are with him in good will. But in order that you may be able to recover the lost grace of devotion, you must submit yourself to God in true humility.

Second, for the recovery of the lost grace of compunction it seems necessary that you do not in anything raise yourself against the Lord, but conform your will in all things to the divine ordinance. We say in the Lord’s Prayer, “Your will be done”; it is fitting that we always willingly agree with that prayer in mind and affection.

Third, whoever desires to recover the lost grace of devotion must strongly despise every pleasure of this world for the love of God. For it cannot happen that one recovers the spirit of inward compunction while he opens the dwelling place of his heart to earthly joys and pleasures. Therefore you must cast away every consolation of the present life, if you desire to have the divine spirit of love with his gifts.

Fourth, you are commanded to withdraw yourself from the vain cares and occupations of this world on account of your purpose, and at all times to occupy your mind delightfully in the service of God. For, as the Lord says, no one can serve God and mammon; but whoever desires to serve the Lord in truth of heart must despise all the occupations of the world.

Fifth, we advise that you remember always to occupy your heart in meditation on Christ’s passion, because there is no shorter way for recovering the delay of compunction, nor any manner of prayer more effective for obtaining from the Lord whatever we ask.

Sixth, strive daily to cleanse your conscience through true penitence; and whenever you have fallen into any fault, immediately recover yourself through contrition. If you take care to observe these things with good will, you will recover, by the Lord’s mercy, the lost grace of compunction.

On the Nine Degrees of Ascent

Sermon VII

Beloved brothers, we describe for you the ladder of the nine degrees by which the human spirit ascends into heaven. And although one is higher than another, nevertheless all and each direct one to the kingdom of God, and they are distinguished from one another according to the merits of those who ascend. Therefore attend diligently to what we are about to say, and consider how much progress you have made in spiritual advancement. All who come to the kingdom of God have ascended by the degrees of this ladder. The more each person has advanced in the love of God, the higher he has already ascended.

The first degree is nearest to hell, and [unclear]

[segment 144]
On the knowledge of spiritual grace.

Third, if you do not wish to lose the grace of inward compunction, you must love the pursuit of solitude. For divine Scripture says: Upon whom will my spirit rest, except upon the humble and devout? And likewise it says elsewhere: In silence and in hope shall be your strength. For the grace of compunction is generally lost quickly when one does not love the leisure of solitude. Therefore let the monk remain gladly shut up in his cell with himself, if he wishes to possess the grace of God.

Fourth, one must take the greatest care not to despise in one’s heart any persons, however evil, because the grace of compunction is withdrawn by the just judgment of God whenever one does not guard against rash judgment of one’s neighbor. For the Lord says in the holy Gospel: Do not judge, and you will not be judged; do not condemn, and you will not be condemned.

Fifth, the monk who does not wish to lose the grace of inward devotion should see to it that he exercises himself in the cultivation of all the virtues. Let him always keep vigilant in the fear of God; let him occupy himself in the exercise of divine love; let him be constantly intent on good works; and let him remain devout in the memory of the Lord’s passion, so far as he can, at all times. For diligent meditation on Christ’s passion strengthens human beings against all the vices and preserves the grace of compunction.

Sixth, be constant in holy prayers, because it is not enough to keep the grace of compunction if one interrupts the practice of prayer. For the Savior says in the holy Gospel: Watch and pray, that you may not enter into temptation. Divine help is near in your calling upon him. Whoever neglects to pray loses grace.

But if you lose the grace of devotion in any way, you will quickly recover it by the following remedies. First, consider that without the grace of God you cannot make progress toward salvation. Know that in all good things you are about to do you most greatly need his mercy. Therefore recognize that you are unworthy of the grace of God for doing good, although you are unworthy because of the multitude of your lost years. Call upon him while he is near, and ask that every time which he grants you free from the crowd of exterior occupations may be spent usefully and fruitfully. The grace of God is necessary for you in doing good; it is acquired by breadth of charity, preserved by humility, and recovered by the practice of saving penance. It is with you when you have long been with him in good will. Therefore you will be able to recover the lost grace of devotion whenever you subject yourself to God in true humility.

Second, for the recovery of the lost grace of compunction, it seems necessary that you lift yourself inwardly against yourself and conform your will in all things to the divine ordering. Since we seek God’s will in the prayer of compunction, it is fitting that in that very prayer we should always be in agreement with him by the mind and affection of a willing heart.

Third, whoever desires to recover the lost grace of devotion must strongly condemn every pleasure of this world for love of God. For it cannot happen that he receives the spirit of inward compunction when he fills the dwelling of his heart with earthly joys and pleasures. Therefore you must cast aside every consolation of the present life if you wish to have the spirit of divine love and the charisms that follow it.

Fourth, you are commanded to withdraw yourself from the vain cares and occupations of this world, and to occupy your mind delightfully at all times in the service of God. For, as the Lord says, no one can serve God and mammon; and whoever truly desires the familiar presence of God in the truth of the heart must necessarily despise all occupations of the world.

Fifth, we advise that you remember always to occupy your heart in meditation on Christ’s passion, because there is no other preparation more suitable for recovering the grace of compunction, nor any manner of prayer more effective for hoping for whatever is sought from the Lord.

Sixth, you should strive to cleanse your conscience daily through true penance; and whenever you have fallen into any fault, you should soon repent with a contrite heart. If you take care to keep these things with a good will, then, with God taking pity, you will recover the lost grace of compunction.

On the nine degrees of ascent.

Sermon VII.

We shall hasten, very dear brothers, to set before you the nine degrees by which the ascent of human beings into heaven is made. And although one is higher than another, nevertheless each is directed toward the kingdom of heaven and distinguishes the merits of those who ascend according to their merit. Therefore attend most diligently to what is said here, and consider in which degree of spiritual progress you stand. All the saints, whoever have come to the sight of God, have ascended by the degrees of this ladder. The more anyone has advanced in the love of God, the higher he has already ascended. The greatest degree, because it is lowest, is the first, and it is more remote from the sublimity of the heavenly fatherland. Those placed in it are said to withdraw from vices and to begin to make progress in the fear of God; neglecting venial sins, they withdraw from mortal sins. Indeed, they flee the heavier sins, being raised to a certain exercise of the virtues; yet through the weakness of a bad habit they do not attend to the smaller sins. Hence they often fall, through frailty, into the pit of mortal sin, because they have not guarded against venial sins as occasions of graver ones.

[segment 145]
--- Page 153 ---

...has not guarded against them. For no fault is so small to one making progress that it does not become greater if neglected. Although we cannot wholly avoid venial sins, nevertheless we ought to strive with all our strength that they not multiply in us. But the one who, by the Lord’s mercy, advances daily in this degree toward better things falls less often into danger; and whenever such persons have fallen, they rise again through penitence. Yet their condition is close to danger: just as a garment, often torn and mended, is at last made useless, so a person who falls more often by sin is frequently stifled by evil habit. If any depart this life from this degree through the Lord’s mercies, they will be saved, but not without the very grievous punishments of purgatory. Therefore one must hasten toward higher things, so that they may guard, as far as they can, not only against mortal sins but also against venial ones.

They are believed to ascend the second degree who strive, as far as they can, to avoid venial sins as well as mortal crimes. Yet because of the commerce of the weak body, they do not persevere in their purpose, but often fall into various negligences of this life, although they do not commit mortal sins. We lukewarm people think that we have somehow ascended to this degree, because, having been placed under the observance of regular discipline, we keep our minds, with the Lord’s help, from the graver sins. For we are all too lukewarm; many of us live negligently, and we are slow to perform the holy exercises of virtue.

Yet there are many set in the climate of this second degree whom servile fear possesses; and if that alone is present, it by no means brings one near to the kingdom of God. For to attain the felicity of heaven above, such servile fear without charity does not suffice, since he is a servant who does not sin through fear of punishment, and if he does not do evil, he does not do so from love of God. It is good, however, to begin from servile fear, provided that little by little one can come from good fear to filial fear. Yet to withdraw from evil out of fear of penalties is by no means sufficient for attaining the kingdom of God, though it prepares those who labor for filial fear. Thus the first degree of those making progress is not to commit wrong from fear of punishment, and not to sin lest they be condemned. For charity alone, discerning all merits, admits into the kingdom of God what is fit to enter, and judges whatever has been done outside it to be unworthy of the kingdom of God. Therefore, whoever has deserved to ascend this second degree, whoever has learned not to sin from fear of punishments, if you wish to press on to the joy of eternal felicity, take care in every way to hasten to higher things. Since, as Scripture bears witness, no one is suddenly made highest, begin from the lower things; yet we judge it dangerous to stand idle too long in the valley after you have set out. Therefore ascend daily as much as you can, and do not persist in the filth of beastly action; for if you do not advance, you fall back; if you do not ascend, you descend.

The third degree includes those who, persevering in a good purpose, keep themselves from every danger of mortal sin and withdraw, as far as they can, even from venial sins. They love purity of conscience and knowingly commit nothing base that could stain it. Yet in this one respect they are lower than those who follow, because they do not strive to withdraw from evil simply for love of God, but because they trust that they will receive the kingdom of God for their merits. Whoever serves God for reward is not in the state of perfection; with the weak he still remains far from the summit of the blessed, in lower things. Whoever truly loves God with a pure mind serves and obeys him neither from fear of penalties nor with an eye to reward, but only for love of him. And therefore all the degrees written above are set before beginners, through which, ascending little by little, they may at last reach perfection. It is good to withdraw from evil out of fear of penalties; it is good to do good out of love of reward; and although this is by no means sufficient for perfection, nevertheless it provides a beginning and a way for those who desire to make progress. For fear customarily leads to filial and chaste love, from whose root perfect charity flows. But afterward, once minds have reached it, they cast out all servile fear and fear of reward.

The fourth degree displays the way of perfection to those ascending, and it is rightly judged higher than the degrees described above. For whatever good is seen in them, those at this degree possess more perfectly. Those ascending this fourth degree, with all zeal, keep themselves not only from the plague of mortal sins, but also from venial sins as far as is possible for them; they eagerly engage in good and holy works, and above all desire to please God. In one respect they surpass those who precede them: they neither withdraw from evil through fear of punishment nor do good in hope of reward, but do all things for love of God alone, and seek nothing other than God’s honor. This is the way set before those striving for perfection; this is the life of holy justice in regular discipline: that we love God with a pure mind above all things, and do all our works for his honor. For the true lover of God neither fears punishment nor thinks of reward, but, walking in simplicity of heart, entrusts his will to the divine ordinance. For because God is the highest good, he must be loved not for the reward of the heavenly kingdom, nor for any other thing, but for his own sake; and he himself, as the chief good, must principally be held in one’s intention.

[segment 146]
On the nine degrees of ascent.

Hasten, my dearest brothers, to climb these degrees of the spiritual ladder by which one ascends, so that you may be able at last to reach the summit of perfection. And unless you, who have been placed under the obedience of monastic perfection, have advanced to this fourth degree, you will scarcely ascend the five that follow. For those who are placed on the fourth degree do good works when commanded, from their own will, though they have not submitted themselves by vow or by monastic profession to its constitutions. And in this one respect they are inferior to those who follow: because a good work is the more acceptable to God, the more it is commended by the merit of obedience.

The fifth degree, then, is climbed by one who guards himself with all his strength from sins, and, being placed under obedience, always remains strong and constant in his purpose of holiness. Loving God above all things with a pure mind, such persons do good works principally for love of him; and in their intention they neither fear punishments nor seek rewards. They humbly obey their prelates for the love of God, and do not withdraw from the purity of regular observance in the practice of penance. And yet there are some whom the weakness of the flesh sometimes makes less stable, and somewhat inclined to waver in their opinions against obedience. At times they try to defend their own exercises and private observances against the determination of their prelates, and to judge themselves more prudent in spiritual examination than their own rectors. Flee this, O monk, because the devil lies hidden in ambush, and those whom he cannot overcome by open assault he attempts to defeat by secret contrivance. For the more learned and perfect a monk has been, the more readily he should obey his prelate without discussion. But if a perfect monk, in matters lawful and honorable, contemns obedience to his abbot, though imperfect, he certainly loses the name of perfection. Therefore each person should take care to apply himself to the pursuit of progress in such a way that he presumes to violate obedience in nothing. For Scripture says: “Obedience is better than sacrifices, and to listen is better than to offer the fat of rams.” Whatever you have offered is another’s; but, says our God [unclear].

The sixth degree, therefore, is especially fitting for those who, living in every purity of mind and body, love the Lord above all things and do every good work they undertake for God’s honor alone, and do not depart from the commands of their prelates. Great is the power of obedience, which doubles the deed of a good work and increases the merit of the one who performs it, as though trees were filled with fruit for God. Let not the words of the Lord concerning obedience trouble you, my dearest brothers, because he preferred the freedom of the will to the subjection of monks, and set a good work done in freedom before the merit of obedience. For this is the monk who obeys from the necessity of his profession: yet that will is good which, for love of God, bound itself to obedience, and this necessity commends it in many ways.

For there is one necessity by which a good person is compelled to do something, where the will has not consented but is always reluctant. This necessity diminishes the praises due to virtue, because the good work was never in the will. And there is another necessity, arising from an inward inclination of the mind, which increases rather than diminishes the praise of a good work, because it kindles the will intent upon the work itself. And there is a third necessity, which does not compel without regard to the will. For if you wish to cross a deep river, the fitting necessity requires an instrument. But the necessity by which a monk is compelled to obey his shepherd is not one of punitive coercion, nor of blind inclination, but rather of the third kind, which is ordered to its end from a preceding will. It is therefore necessary for a monk, on account of that end, to do what the abbot commands, so that he may be able to hope for the vow that he made for love of God. And since that end is most praiseworthy, by which the creature subjects himself to his Creator in obedience out of love, necessity in the monk does not diminish the praise of the good work, but makes it greater. O blessed necessity of monks, which draws from the treasure of a good will and orders it to the end which is God, the unchangeable good. It is an increase of praise, an excellence of merit, and an abundance of reward, to do a good work from obedience, rather than if it stood without vow and profession, in the freedom of one’s own will. For the monk has once committed himself wholly, and all his will, to God, reserving nothing at all for himself. Whatever good he has done under obedience he brings back to the end of the religious life he assumed, for love of keeping it; and no work is compared with this after martyrdom. One who is continent without a vow from the act of Venus performs one virtue, which is called chastity; but one who observes the same thing by reason of a vow proceeds otherwise, because the individual virtues are referred and ordered to the perfection of the whole religious life that he professed. The whole monastic religion is more than one particular virtue. For one who offers a good work that he has done without obedience offers only that, while the rest remains to himself, because he is still in his own will, with greater freedom of acting. But the monk who does anything by the vow of obedience merits more highly on account of God, since he offers both the act [text breaks off].

[segment 147]
Sermon VII. Folio LXXIII

...to God and the power of acting. No one has offered more to God than the monk who has given himself. But some who ascend this sixth degree have hidden within themselves a certain defect of pride, because, while they look back incautiously upon themselves, they do not entrust themselves in all things to the divine will as they ought. This defect takes its beginning from a certain very subtle vanity, when the mind, trusting in its own purity, asks that some secret be revealed to it by the Lord. And in this one respect those who ascend the sixth rung differ from the seventh: those who are devoted to God flee signs of vanity. For signs were given not to believers but to unbelievers; hence the monk who asks a sign from the Lord shows that he has not yet perfectly subjected himself to the divine will.

Those ascend the seventh degree who most zealously keep all the prescribed things, and, subject to God in all things, live without the stain of vanity. They flee every sin, both venial and mortal, as far as they can; they do works of virtue for the love of God alone; they obey their pastors in all things; they seek no miracles of vanity. Whatever almighty God has ordained concerning them, they receive gratefully; they withdraw in no respect from the observance of monastic purity. Yet in one respect they are inferior to those who follow, because perhaps they suffer some defect unknown to them, by whose impulse they sometimes commit something through their own sensuality.

Whoever has deserved to ascend the eighth degree concedes nothing further to sensuality. Here is the mind’s sweetest rest; here is the perfect tranquillity of the soul. In this degree nothing vicious is left of beauty; nothing is found here that can sadden the soul. Here all things are clean, all things pure, all things burning most sweetly with the fervor of charity. O if we might strive to ascend to this degree; O if we might someday deserve to reach this harbor of tranquillity. The more one who stands on this holy rung begins to taste the sweetness of inward delight, the more, hastening toward what lies ahead, he desires still more. O desires of holy love always to be proclaimed; O venerable exercises of perfection, where the clean conscience of one loving God shines without sins, where the wicked battles of vices have ceased, and great tranquillity has followed.

Those ascend the ninth degree who, already consummated in the eighth, keep all the prescribed things purely and simply for the love of God, and desire nothing other than to praise, bless, and glorify God. Whoever has ascended to this degree of perfection, as though forgetful of himself, resigns himself wholly to God and humbly submits. He reckons himself unworthy of all God’s benefits; he cares nothing at all for himself, because he has already learned to entrust himself wholly to God and humbly to be subject in all things to his ordering. Hence he neither seeks miracles nor asks for any consolations in this life, but with the holy apostle of Christ truly cries: “For me to live is Christ, and to die is gain.” “The world is crucified to me, and I to the world.”

For the true monk, as a lover of Christ, has offered himself wholly to God out of charity, and has despised the world with all its riches, honors, and vanities. Hence the world is said to be crucified to him, in which there is nothing that seems able to pierce his mind. Happy are those monks who serve the Lord God with a pure conscience in true charity; who run without offense under the rule of humility and obedience; who ascend to this last degree of perfection. These are true monks, who seek nothing other than the honor of God.

Indeed, my brothers, although monks are not bound by necessity of salvation to be perfect, otherwise very few would be saved, yet we are all bound with good will to strive toward perfection, and with all the powers we can to long for this ninth degree. For it is very contrary to the monastic profession to be unwilling to make progress, and it is a kind of apostasy, after the vow of perfection, to wish to remain in imperfection. For the vices that attack us are enemies of our salvation, and until they either conquer or are conquered, they do not cease to pursue us. Let us imitate the history of the ancient battle, when the sons of promise destroyed their enemies not in one day but successively. For it is difficult to conquer all things at once, unless the charity of God suddenly overshadows the mind. Let us wage war in every way against all the vices that attack us. Today let us conquer gluttony; when it has been rightly laid low, let us overcome sloth; and so let us deal with the rest, that at last we may come out victors over all. The Lord will be present to us as we fight, and God will give victory to his lovers. Amen.

End of the second book of exhortations to monks by Johannes Trithemius, abbot, on the first day of September, 1486.

These two books were printed at Strasbourg by Johann Knoblouch, printer, at the expense of Johann Haselberger of Reichenau, diocese of Constance, in the year of the Lord 1516, on the twenty-fifth day of the month of August.
